KEY

  ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Green step                          always written, in this order
  ----------------------------------- ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Amber step · when…                  written only when the “when …” condition in its title is met

  A · condition                       variant paragraphs: write the one whose condition the facts match, never more than one

  [red]                               the fact to find in the paper: the bracket names exactly what to look for

  [X, or Y]                           the facts decide which; write one, never both

  → N                                 next step: follow the arrow exactly

  Quotation marks                     verbatim from the case, the slides or the CLA; never paraphrase
  ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

BREACH OF DUTY

Approach for breach of duty under the CLA

The flow chart as given in Class 8, Class 9 and Class 18.

+--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| Step 1 Identify the risk of harm (Dederer)                                                       | Does the CLA apply? S 3B                                                                                                                                           |
|                                                                                                  |                                                                                                                                                                    |
| Step 2 Address 3 elements in s 5B(1):                                                            | Step A Reasonable foreseeability of risk of harm → CLA s 5B(1)(a) and (b)                                                                                          |
|                                                                                                  |                                                                                                                                                                    |
|   ▪ a) Foreseeable risk (D knew or should have known);                                           |   ▪ Identification of negligent wrongs (Dederer)                                                                                                                   |
|                                                                                                  |                                                                                                                                                                    |
|   ▪ b) Not insignificant (probability); and                                                      |   ▪ RF- D knew or ought to have know (Wyong) Risk of injury arising from the wrongs                                                                                |
|                                                                                                  |                                                                                                                                                                    |
|   ▪ c) Reasonable person (to respond the risk)                                                   |   ▪ Risk of injury not insignificant; (Wyong)                                                                                                                      |
|                                                                                                  |                                                                                                                                                                    |
|   ▪ In determining 5B(1)(c) - whether a RP would have taken precautions against the risk of harm | Step B1 Standard of care → s 5B(1)(c) [and (2)] + Common Law                                                                                                       |
|                                                                                                  |                                                                                                                                                                    |
| Standard of care is RP in D's position                                                           |   ▪ What is the SoC (reasonable response to the risk of harm)? Who is the reasonable person?                                                                       |
|                                                                                                  |                                                                                                                                                                    |
|   ▪ Consider factors in s5B(2) (amongst other relevant things) and s 5C;                         | Step B2 Can the D use CLA ss 5O + 5P to avoid liability?                                                                                                           |
|                                                                                                  |                                                                                                                                                                    |
|   ▪ 5O and 5P for professional negligence;                                                       |   ▪ Is D a professional?; Is D “providing professional services”?                                                                                                  |
|                                                                                                  |                                                                                                                                                                    |
|   ▪ Common law regarding children, mentally ill, learners, and professionals.                    |   ▪ Is peer professional opinion about the service “widely accepted”? s 5O(4); Is the opinion “irrational” s 5O(2)?                                                |
|                                                                                                  |                                                                                                                                                                    |
|                                                                                                  |   ▪ NB: s 5P in ‘failure to warn’ cases → s 5O doesn’t apply                                                                                                       |
|                                                                                                  |                                                                                                                                                                    |
|                                                                                                  | Step B3 Calculus of negligence: CLA s 5B(1)(c) and (2)(a)-(d)                                                                                                      |
|                                                                                                  |                                                                                                                                                                    |
|                                                                                                  |   ▪ (a) the probability that the harm would occur if care were not taken: Dederer, Romeo; (b) the likely seriousness of the harm: Paris v Stepney Borough Council; |
|                                                                                                  |                                                                                                                                                                    |
|                                                                                                  |   ▪ (c) the burden of taking precautions to avoid the risk of harm: (Woods); (d) the social utility of the activity that creates the risk of harm: (E v Red Cross) |
|                                                                                                  |                                                                                                                                                                    |
|                                                                                                  |   ▪ Tentative conclusion                                                                                                                                           |
+==================================================================================================+====================================================================================================================================================================+
+--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

BREACH OF DUTY: SCAFFOLD

1 The second element of Negligence- Breach

“[P]’s claim is not excluded by s 3B of the Civil Liability Act 2002 (NSW) (‘CLA’), the claim being [why no s 3B exclusion applies on the facts: not an intentional act done with intent to injure, not a motor accident, dust, tobacco or other excluded category], so the CLA is the starting point, not the common law (Adeels [11]); [P] must prove [D]’s conduct fell below the required standard of care (‘SoC’).”

→ 2 · more than one person’s act or omission is complained of → adopt this paragraph for each defendant in one sentence and walk the run again for each

2 Step 1: Identify the risk of harm (Dederer)

“The negligent wrongs are [each act or omission complained of, listed] (Dederer). For [the negligent wrong], the risk of harm is that [the hazard, the true source of potential injury] would expose [the class of persons P belongs to] to ‘[the injury]’ by [how the injury came about] (Coles [20]), for it is ‘only through the correct identification of the risk that one can assess what a reasonable response to that risk would be’ (Dederer [59]). Framed at that level, and not as the absence of the precaution alleged, the risk of harm names the ‘true source of potential injury’ (Dederer [60]) and ‘the general causal mechanism of the injury sustained’ (Perisher Blue [98], in Coles [22]).”

A · risk favours P

  “The negligent wrongs are [each act or omission, listed] (Dederer). For [the negligent wrong], the risk of harm is that [the hazard] would expose [the class of persons P belongs to] to ‘[the injury]’ by [how the injury came about], and it is ‘only through the correct identification of the risk’ that the reasonable response can be assessed (Dederer [59]).”

B · risk favours D

  “The negligent wrongs are [each act or omission complained of, listed] (Dederer). [D] frames the risk of harm for [the negligent wrong] more narrowly: the risk of harm is that [the narrower hazard D points to] would expose [the class of persons P belongs to] to ‘[the injury]’ by [how the injury came about], and that framing is right because [why the narrower framing is right], it names the ‘true source of potential injury’ (Dederer [60]), and it is ‘only through the correct identification of the risk’ that the reasonable response can be assessed (Dederer [59]).”

One risk and one precaution for each wrong in issue; run 3 to 11 for each wrong. Number the wrongs, not the limbs, after the first: open each subsequent wrong “Turning to ‘[the next wrong]’, the risk of harm is …” and drop the inner Firstly to Fourthly, so the ordinal spine runs once · → 3

3 s5B(a)- Foreseeable of risk of harm

“Firstly, [what put the risk of harm before D] means the risk of harm was ‘a risk of which the person knew or ought to have known’ (CLA s 5B(1)(a)), a risk being foreseeable unless ‘far-fetched or fanciful’ (Wyong). Doubleday is [analogous, or distinguishable], the foreseeable risk there being injury to a child of seven if she used the trampoline ‘without adult supervision’ (Bryson JA [11]); [why the risk of harm here is like or unlike]. Section 5B(1)(a) is [satisfied, or not].”

A · foreseeable

  “Firstly, [what put the risk of harm before D] made this ‘a risk of which the person knew or ought to have known’ (CLA s 5B(1)(a)), foreseeable unless ‘far-fetched or fanciful’ (Wyong). Doubleday is analogous, its foreseeable risk being injury to a child of seven using the trampoline ‘without adult supervision’ (Bryson JA [11]), and [why this risk is like that one]. Section 5B(1)(a) is likely satisfied.”

B · not foreseeable

  “Firstly, [what D was not told or could not see] left the risk of harm outside [D]’s contemplation, so it was not ‘a risk of which the person knew or ought to have known’ (CLA s 5B(1)(a)), a risk being foreseeable only where not ‘far-fetched or fanciful’ (Wyong). Doubleday is distinguishable, the foreseeable risk there being injury to a child of seven using the trampoline ‘without adult supervision’, being plain to its defendant (Bryson JA [11]), whereas [why D was not so placed]. Section 5B(1)(a) is arguably not satisfied.”

the sequence or manner of injury was odd or bizarre → B1, and cut the Doubleday sentence from the block 3 paragraph so the comparison is made once, at B1 · [D] warned, prohibited or relies on [P]’s own carelessness → B11 (at 11) · → 4

4 S 5B(1)(b) – risk not insignificant

“Secondly, [the fact bearing on probability] gives the risk of harm [a higher probability of eventuating, or less], and ‘not insignificant’ (CLA s 5B(1)(b)) asks a higher probability than ‘not far-fetched or fanciful’ (Wyong), though the risk does not have to be substantial; it refers to the probability of the risk of harm eventuating, not to the seriousness of the resulting harm [P] suffers. Section 5B(1)(b) is accordingly [satisfied, or not].”

A · risk not insignificant

  “Secondly, [the fact bearing on probability] gave the risk of harm a higher probability of eventuating than ‘not far-fetched or fanciful’ (Wyong), which is what ‘not insignificant’ asks, though the risk does not have to be substantial (CLA s 5B(1)(b)). Gazis is distinguishable, its risk ‘foreseeable, but quite unlikely to eventuate’ ([89]), whereas [why this risk was more than that]. Section 5B(1)(b) is satisfied.”

B · risk insignificant

  “Secondly, [the fact bearing on probability] leaves the risk of harm short of the higher probability that ‘not insignificant’ requires (CLA s 5B(1)(b)), more than ‘not far-fetched or fanciful’ (Wyong), though the risk does not have to be substantial, and the limb concerns the probability of the risk of harm eventuating, so [the grave injury] cannot supply it. Gazis is analogous, its risk ‘foreseeable, but quite unlikely to eventuate’ and one that ‘could be dismissed as insignificant’ ([89]), as [why this risk of harm was as unlikely] shows this one may be. Section 5B(1)(b) is not satisfied and breach fails there.”

(b) fails → breach fails and the run ends · otherwise → 5

5 S 5B(1)(c) – precautions

“[Both (a) and (b) being satisfied, or (b) being satisfied though (a) is arguable], thirdly, the precaution [P] alleges is [the precaution P alleges], and the question is whether ‘in the circumstances, a reasonable person in the person’s position would have taken those precautions’ (CLA s 5B(1)(c)): the reasonable person (‘RP’) in [D]’s position and not [P]’s, particular qualities ignored, ‘the man on the Bondi tram’, and with reasonable resources (PQ v Australian Red Cross). Only a precaution the risk of harm called for ‘as a matter of reasonable precaution’ answers the claim (Adeels [38]). [D] [is, or is not] ‘a person practising a profession’ (CLA s 5O(1)); the SoC is accordingly that of [the RP in D’s position, or the ordinary skilled person].”

A · ordinary standard

  “Thirdly, the precaution [P] alleges is [the precaution P alleges], and whether ‘in the circumstances, a reasonable person in the person’s position would have taken those precautions’ (CLA s 5B(1)(c)) is asked of the reasonable person (‘RP’) in [D]’s position, particular qualities ignored. [D], [why D does not practise a profession], is not ‘a person practising a profession’ (CLA s 5O(1)); the SoC is that of the RP in [D]’s position.”

B · professional standard

  “Both (a) and (b) being satisfied, thirdly, the precaution [P] alleges is [the precaution P alleges], and the question is whether ‘in the circumstances, a reasonable person in the person’s position would have taken those precautions’ (CLA s 5B(1)(c)): only a precaution the risk of harm called for ‘as a matter of reasonable precaution’ answers the claim (Adeels [38]). [D], [why D practises a profession], is ‘a person practising a profession’ (CLA s 5O(1)), so the SoC is that of the reasonable [member of D’s calling], ‘the ordinary skilled person exercising and professing to have that special skill’ (Rogers 483), and the reasonable person (‘RP’) against whom [D]’s response is judged carries that skill.”

D is a child → B3 · D has a mental illness or disability → B4 · D was inexperienced, unlicensed or unqualified → B5 · D held out a special skill but does not practise a profession → B6; where D practises a profession, B6 has no work and the skill is answered by s 5O. If D is a professional and there is peer professional opinion in evidence → B7; if D practises a profession but no peer professional opinion is in evidence, s 5O(1) cannot be established, because it must be ‘established that the professional acted in a manner’ so accepted and the onus is D’s: say so in a clause and → 6 · a warning, advice or information about a risk from a professional service is alleged → B9 · a warning is alleged and [D] is not a professional → B16 · otherwise → 6

6 Responding to the foreseeable risk – the ‘calculus of negligence’ CLA s 5B (2)

“Whether the RP would have taken [the precaution P alleges] is answered through CLA s 5B(2), the court ‘is to consider the following (amongst other relevant things)’, (a)–(d), which do not alter the common law (RTA v Refrigerated Roadways [177]), judged ‘looking forward at the prospect of the risk of injury’ (Vairy per Hayne J).”

→ 7 (→ 8 → 9 → 10 → weigh them at 11; where more than one wrong is in issue, score each matter for each wrong) · Where time is short, run 7 to 10 as single clauses inside one sentence, every case comparison keeping its because-clause: cut the case, never the reason

7 Bolton v Stone [1951] AC 850 / s 5B(2)(a) – probability risk

“Firstly, [what made the harm likely, or unlikely, if care were not taken] made ‘the probability that the harm would occur if care were not taken’ [high, or low] (CLA s 5B(2)(a)). Bolton v Stone is [analogous, or distinguishable]: a ball had been hit over a seventeen-foot fence six to ten times in thirty years, and where the risk is very small or insignificant and the RP would not take steps to eliminate it, it is right to neglect it; [why this risk is like, or unlike, that one]. (a) tells [for, or against] the precaution.”

A · probability high

  “Firstly, [what made the harm likely if care were not taken] made ‘the probability that the harm would occur if care were not taken’ high (CLA s 5B(2)(a)). Bolton v Stone is distinguishable, its risk eventuating only six to ten times in thirty years despite a seventeen-foot fence, whereas [why this risk was more frequent]. (a) tells for the precaution.”

B · probability low

  “Firstly, [what made the harm unlikely if care were not taken] kept ‘the probability that the harm would occur if care were not taken’ low (CLA s 5B(2)(a)). Bolton v Stone is analogous: a ball cleared its seventeen-foot fence only six to ten times in thirty years, and where the risk is very small or insignificant and the RP would not take steps to eliminate it, it is right to neglect it; [why this risk was as infrequent as that] puts this case with it. (a) tells against the precaution.”

C · Bolton v Stone both ways

  “Firstly, Bolton v Stone cuts both ways. It is analogous because [what made the harm unlikely if care were not taken] matches a risk eventuating only six to ten times in thirty years, ‘very small or insignificant’ and ‘right to neglect it’; yet [what made the harm likely if care were not taken] weakens the analogy, [what shows D expected the harm to eventuate]. The better view is that ‘the probability that the harm would occur if care were not taken’ was [high, or low] (CLA s 5B(2)(a)), though finely balanced.”

there is a history without injury, or a like accident before → B2 · → 8

8 Paris v Stepney Borough Council / s 5B(2)(b)-the likely seriousness of the harm

“Secondly, [what the injury risked actually was] made ‘the likely seriousness of the harm’ [grave, or slight] (CLA s 5B(2)(b)). Paris is [analogous, or distinguishable]: the employer there knew the workman had one eye, and the duty ‘must … be related to both the risk and the degree of the injury’, so ‘the known circumstance that a particular workman is likely to suffer a graver injury than his fellows’ had to be taken into account (Lord MacDermott 387). [why D did, or did not, know P stood to suffer graver injury]; (b) accordingly tells [for, or against] the precaution.”

A · harm grave

  “Secondly, [the injury risked] was [the injury], so ‘the likely seriousness of the harm’ was grave (CLA s 5B(2)(b)). Paris is analogous: the employer knew its workman had one eye and stood to suffer ‘a graver injury than his fellows’ (Lord MacDermott 387), as [what D knew of P’s vulnerability] shows here. (b) tells for the precaution.”

B · harm slight

  “Secondly, [what the injury risked actually was] went no further than [the injury], so ‘the likely seriousness of the harm’ was slight (CLA s 5B(2)(b)). Paris is distinguishable: it was the employer’s knowledge of the workman’s one eye that made ‘the known circumstance that a particular workman is likely to suffer a graver injury than his fellows’ a matter it had to weigh (Lord MacDermott 387), and [why D knew of no such vulnerability]. (b) tells against the precaution.”

C · Paris both ways

  “Secondly, Paris cuts both ways. It is analogous because [what D knew of P’s vulnerability], the employer there knowing the workman had one eye and owing him goggles; yet [why D knew of no such vulnerability] weakens this, nothing particular to [P] having been before [D]. The duty must in any event ‘be related to both the risk and the degree of the injury’ (Lord MacDermott 387). On balance ‘the likely seriousness of the harm’ was [grave, or slight] (CLA s 5B(2)(b)), though the point is arguable.”

[P] is a child, is elderly or is otherwise likely to suffer graver injury → B14 in place of this block’s paragraph, so Paris is compared once · → 9

9 Woods v Multi-sport holdings (2002) 208 CLR 460 / s5B(2)(c)- burden of taking precautions

“Thirdly, [what the precaution would have cost or required] made ‘the burden of taking precautions to avoid the risk of harm’ [light, or heavy] (CLA s 5B(2)(c)). Woods is [analogous, or distinguishable]: the plaintiff there was ‘an adult who chose, for his personal enjoyment, to play’ indoor cricket, no headgear having been ‘designed for the game’ nor ‘worn by players elsewhere’ (Gleeson CJ); [why the precaution here was, or was not, onerous]. (c) tells [for, or against] the precaution.”

A · burden light

  “Thirdly, [the precaution P alleges] would have required no more than [the small money, time or trouble it involved], so ‘the burden of taking precautions to avoid the risk of harm’ was light (CLA s 5B(2)(c)). Woods is distinguishable, no headgear there having been ‘designed for the game’ nor ‘worn by players elsewhere’ (Gleeson CJ), whereas [why this precaution was narrow and cheap]. (c) tells for the precaution.”

B · burden heavy

  “Thirdly, [what the precaution would have cost or required] reached [the wider consequence], not this risk of harm alone, so ‘the burden of taking precautions to avoid the risk of harm’ was heavy (CLA s 5B(2)(c)), the burden extending past money to time, distance and communication (NSW v Mikhael [82]). Woods is analogous, its plaintiff ‘an adult who chose, for his personal enjoyment, to play’, no headgear having been ‘designed for the game’ nor ‘worn by players elsewhere’ (Gleeson CJ), as [why this precaution was equally broad] shows here. (c) tells against the precaution.”

C · Woods both ways

  “Thirdly, Woods cuts both ways. It is analogous because [why this precaution was equally broad], its plaintiff ‘an adult who chose, for his personal enjoyment, to play’ a game for which no headgear had been ‘designed for the game’ nor ‘worn by players elsewhere’ (Gleeson CJ); yet [why this precaution was narrow and cheap] weakens this, one measure here answering the whole of the risk of harm. On balance ‘the burden of taking precautions to avoid the risk of harm’ was [light, or heavy] (CLA s 5B(2)(c)), though it is a close thing.”

the precaution was cheap or simple → B13 · the same precaution would have to be repeated across other sites or occasions → B12 for s 5C. If a second precaution is alleged, run 9 again for it, costing each separately against the same risk · → 10

10 E v Australian Red Cross Society (1991) 31 FCR 299 / s 5B(2)(d)- social utility

“Fourthly, [the social value of D’s activity] means ‘the social utility of the activity that creates the risk of harm’ tells [for, or against] the precaution (CLA s 5B(2)(d)). E v Red Cross is [analogous, or distinguishable]: anti-HBc testing there would have meant ‘the likely loss of 5% of all donations, on top of the 1.47% already lost’, so the precaution’s ‘possibly serious effect on the blood supply’ answered the risk of harm (Wilcox J); [why the precaution here would, or would not, cost that benefit].”

A · utility does not save D

  “Fourthly, [the precaution P alleges] is part of doing [the activity] properly, so ‘the social utility of the activity that creates the risk of harm’ does not save [D] (CLA s 5B(2)(d)). Unlike E v Red Cross, where testing cost a ‘possibly serious effect on the blood supply’ (Wilcox J), it costs no like benefit. (d) tells for the precaution.”

B · utility tells for D

  “Fourthly, [the precaution P alleges] would have cut down [the social value of D’s activity], because [why the precaution here would cost that benefit], so ‘the social utility of the activity that creates the risk of harm’ tells for [D] (CLA s 5B(2)(d)). E v Red Cross is analogous: anti-HBc testing there would have meant ‘the likely loss of 5% of all donations, on top of the 1.47% already lost’, and no breach was found because of the ‘possibly serious effect on the blood supply’ (Wilcox J). (d) tells against the precaution.”

→ 11

11 Weighing up the ‘calculus’

“[The probability finding and the seriousness finding] [outweigh, or are answered by] [the burden finding and the utility finding] because [why one side of the balance outweighs the other], ss 5B(2)(a) and (b) being weighed against (c) and (d) (RTA v Refrigerated Roadways [176]). The duty is ‘not that of an insurer but a duty to act reasonably’ (Romeo per Hayne J 488), judged looking forward, for a calculus ‘undertaken looking back on what is known to have happened’ ‘becomes separated from standards of reasonableness’ (Vairy per Hayne J, in Dederer). Dederer is [analogous, or distinguishable], its low probability, high burden of taking precautions, high gravity and no social utility producing no breach; [why the facts here match, or differ]. Therefore, the better view is that [D] [did, or did not] breach the duty owed to [P].”

A · breach established

  “[The probability finding and the seriousness finding] outweigh [the burden finding and the utility finding] because [why one side outweighs] (RTA v Refrigerated Roadways [176]); the duty is ‘not that of an insurer but a duty to act reasonably’ (Romeo 488), and the RP would have taken [the precaution P alleges]. Therefore, the better view is that [D] breached the duty owed to [P].”

B · no breach

  “[The probability finding and the seriousness finding] are answered by [the burden finding and the utility finding] because [why that side of the balance outweighs the other], ss 5B(2)(a) and (b) being weighed against (c) and (d) (RTA v Refrigerated Roadways [176]); the duty is ‘not that of an insurer but a duty to act reasonably’ (Romeo per Hayne J 488). Therefore, the better view is that [D] did not breach the duty owed to [P].”

C · Mulligan both ways (only where B17 was not run; after B17 conclude with A or B)

  “Mulligan found no breach in failing to warn, the Council there having had no notice of any earlier accident and no recommendation to sign that place, and the finding was unanimous. Here [the probability finding and the seriousness finding] sit close against [the burden finding and the utility finding] (RTA v Refrigerated Roadways [176]), but [why the facts here differ from Mulligan’s] supplies the notice Mulligan’s council lacked and the analogy fails. Therefore, the better view is that [D] breached the duty owed to [P], because [why the balance tips to breach].”

a failure to warn is complained of → B17 before concluding · D warned, prohibited or relies on P’s own carelessness → B11 before concluding (skip B11 where B16B or B16C already carried the Dederer warning analysis, and where B16B carried Nagle’s submerged rocks do not repeat them) · a further wrong or precaution remains unscored → 7 (run the calculus for it, opening it “Turning to ‘[the next wrong]’ …” and without repeating the ordinals) · there is a further defendant → 1 · Otherwise the run ends; if breach is established the next run is causation

B1 Doubleday v Kelly: s5B(a)- Foreseeable of risk of harm, not the sequence · when the facts make the manner or sequence of the injury odd

“That [the way the injury came about] was unusual does not by itself [defeat, or decide] s 5B(1)(a); [what made the general class of injury plain to D] shows whether the class of injury was foreseen even where its mechanism was not. Doubleday is [analogous, or distinguishable]: the roller skates were ‘a bizarre complexity but not an important one’, the foreseeable risk being that the child ‘would not use the trampoline in a competent way and would injure herself by falling off’ (Bryson JA [11]); [why the risk of harm here is, or is not, like that one]. Section 5B(1)(a) accordingly [stands unaffected, or fails].”

A · class of injury foreseen

  “That [the way the injury came about] was unusual does not negate foreseeability; [what made the general class of injury plain to D] shows the class of injury was foreseen even where its mechanism was not. Doubleday is analogous: the roller skates were ‘a bizarre complexity but not an important one’, the foreseeable risk being that the child ‘would not use the trampoline in a competent way and would injure herself by falling off’ (Bryson JA [11]), and [D] faced the like risk from [the activity]. Section 5B(1)(a) stands unaffected.”

B · harm of a different class

  “That [the way the injury came about] was unusual is here decisive; [how the harm differs in kind from the foreseeable one] puts the harm in a class [D] could not foresee, not an odd route to a foreseeable one. Doubleday is distinguishable: the roller skates were ‘a bizarre complexity but not an important one’ (Bryson JA [11]) because the risk of the child falling off the trampoline was plain throughout, whereas [the activity] carried no risk of [the harm] at all. Section 5B(1)(a) fails.”

C · Doubleday both ways

  “Doubleday is analogous because [the way the injury came about], like the roller skates that were ‘a bizarre complexity but not an important one’ (Bryson JA [11]), is an odd detail within a plain risk, there, that the child ‘would not use the trampoline in a competent way and would injure herself by falling off’; yet [what suggests a different class of injury] tells against it, the class of injury itself having to be foreseen. On balance the unusual sequence does not negate s 5B(1)(a), although the contrary is open.”

→ 4

B2 s 5B(2)(a) – probability risk: years without accidents (Romeo, Dederer, Vairy) · when the facts give a history of the thing running without injury, or a like accident before

“[D] [knew of no earlier accident, or knew of one], and ‘years of experience without accidents may tend to confirm an occupier’s assessment that the risks of harm were negligible’ (Romeo per Kirby J); in Dederer no one was injured until the plaintiff’s dive, probability being ‘in truth very low’ (Gummow J). A known earlier accident bears more on foreseeability under s 5B(1)(a) than on probability, for ‘[e]ven the fact that another similar accident had occurred at the same place previously does not necessarily bear on the probability of the given accident occurring’ (SVE [10.75] Note 5). Vairy, dismissed 4:3, assists [P] only on the dissent’s reasoning, the Council’s knowledge of an accident 15 years before and an unactioned recommendation to sign deciding it for Gleeson CJ, McHugh and Kirby JJ, the majority finding no duty to warn.”

A · probability very low

  “[How long the thing ran without injury] passed without incident, and ‘years of experience without accidents may tend to confirm an occupier’s assessment that the risks of harm were negligible’ (Romeo per Kirby J). Dederer is analogous, no one being injured until the plaintiff’s dive and probability ‘in truth very low’ (Gummow J), as [what confirms D’s assessment that the risk of harm was negligible] shows here; Bolton v Stone points the same way. Probability under s 5B(2)(a) is low.”

B · earlier accident known

  “[The earlier accident and how D learned of it] deprives [D] of the comfort that ‘years of experience without accidents may tend to confirm an occupier’s assessment that the risks of harm were negligible’ (Romeo per Kirby J). Vairy assists [P] only on the dissent’s reasoning, knowledge of an accident 15 years earlier and an unactioned recommendation to sign, the majority, 4:3, finding no duty to warn; [how closely the earlier accident matched this one] narrows that gap, so probability under s 5B(2)(a) is not low.”

C · Dederer both ways

  “Dederer is analogous because no one was injured on [the structure or place] until [P], probability there being ‘in truth very low’ (Gummow J); yet [the earlier accident and how D learned of it] points the other way, displacing the comfort of ‘years of experience without accidents’ (Romeo per Kirby J). That accident bears more on foreseeability than on probability (SVE [10.75] Note 5), and brings the case only as close as Vairy’s dissent, the majority finding no duty to warn despite like knowledge. Probability remains low, though not without difficulty.”

→ 8 (continue the calculus; if the precaution alleged was also cheap or simple, or s 5C is engaged, those points are picked up at 9)

B3 ‘Reasonable person’-Children: an objective standard with subjective aspects · when d is a child

“[D] being [age], his conduct is judged by the standard of an ordinary child of that age: in McHale a twelve-year-old was judged ‘not in terms of the foresight and prudence of an ordinary person, but of the foresight and prudence of an ordinary boy of twelve’. Yet childhood is no complete answer: ‘a minor who engages in dangerous adult activities … must conform to the standard of the reasonably prudent adult’ (Fleming, in McHale). Thus [the child standard governs, or the adult standard applies].”

A · child standard governs

  “[D] being [age] and [the activity] being ordinary childhood play rather than an adult undertaking, his conduct is judged as McHale judged the twelve-year-old, ‘not in terms of the foresight and prudence of an ordinary person, but of the foresight and prudence of an ordinary boy of twelve’. That ‘a minor who engages in dangerous adult activities … must conform to the standard of the reasonably prudent adult’ (Fleming, in McHale) does not reach him, because [why the activity is ordinary childhood play]. The child standard likely governs.”

B · adult standard applies

  “[D] being [age], childhood is no answer: [the activity] is a dangerous adult activity, and ‘a minor who engages in dangerous adult activities … must conform to the standard of the reasonably prudent adult’ (Fleming, in McHale). McHale, which judged a twelve-year-old by ‘the foresight and prudence of an ordinary boy of twelve’, is distinguishable because [what makes it a dangerous adult activity] took [D] outside the pursuits of an ordinary child of that age. The adult standard likely applies.”

C · McHale both ways

  “McHale is analogous because [D], at [age], was doing what children of that age do, the twelve-year-old there being judged by ‘the foresight and prudence of an ordinary boy of twelve’; yet [what makes it a dangerous adult activity] points the other way, for ‘a minor who engages in dangerous adult activities … must conform to the standard of the reasonably prudent adult’ (Fleming, in McHale). The better view is that the child standard governs, [the activity] being play and not an adult undertaking, although the point is arguable.”

D also has a mental illness or disability → B4 · D was also inexperienced, unlicensed or unqualified → B5 · D also held out a special skill → B6 · otherwise → 6

B4 ‘Reasonable person’-Mental illness and disability · when d has a mental illness or disability and could not foresee harm to others

“[D]’s [condition] ‘had no effect on the standard of care owed by him to the plaintiff, which … is to be judged by the standard of the ordinary and reasonable person’ (Carrier per McPherson JA), and [D]’s inability to foresee harm is no answer. Nor does the analogy with childhood assist, for ‘unsoundness of mind is not a normal condition in most people’ and, coming ‘in different varieties and different shades or degrees’, it would be ‘impossible to devise a standard by which the tortious liability of such persons could be judged as a class’ (Carrier). The RP standard stands unmoved, and [is, or is not] met.”

A · standard not met

  “[D]’s [condition] ‘had no effect on the standard of care owed by him to the plaintiff, which … is to be judged by the standard of the ordinary and reasonable person’ (Carrier per McPherson JA): ‘unsoundness of mind is not a normal condition in most people’, and it would be ‘impossible to devise a standard by which the tortious liability of such persons could be judged as a class’ (Carrier). Measured against that unmoved standard [D] fell short: [the precaution P alleges] was open and the RP would have taken it.”

B · standard met

  “[D]’s [conduct] is what the ordinary and reasonable person would have done faced with [the situation], and that is the whole question: [D]’s [condition] ‘had no effect on the standard of care owed by him to the plaintiff, which … is to be judged by the standard of the ordinary and reasonable person’ (Carrier per McPherson JA), no analogy with childhood assisting since unsoundness of mind ‘comes in different varieties and different shades or degrees’ (Carrier). The RP standard, standing unmoved, is met quite apart from [the condition].”

→ 6 (open the calculus)

B5 ‘Reasonable person’- Skills: Inexperienced drivers · when d was an inexperienced, unlicensed or unqualified driver, or doing a like activity with an unqualified standard

“[D] being [inexperienced, unlicensed or unqualified], the SoC remains that of the reasonable driver: ‘That standard is not to be further qualified, whether by reference to the holding of a licence to drive or by reference to the level of experience of the driver’ (Imbree per Gummow, Hayne and Kiefel JJ [25]), Imbree overturning Cook v Cook, which had qualified it. [P]’s knowledge goes to contributory negligence and voluntary assumption of risk. The reasonable driver standard accordingly [is, or is not] met.”

A · reasonable driver standard not met

  “[D’s manoeuvre] was one no reasonable driver would attempt, and [D]’s inexperience does not lower the measure: ‘That standard is not to be further qualified, whether by reference to the holding of a licence to drive or by reference to the level of experience of the driver’ (Imbree [25]), Imbree overturning Cook v Cook. That [P] knew of [D’s inexperience or want of licence] goes to contributory negligence and voluntary assumption of risk, not to the SoC. The reasonable driver standard is not met.”

B · reasonable driver standard met

  “[D’s manoeuvre] answers the reasonable driver standard because [the road or traffic condition D faced] left a reasonable driver no better course, and the standard sits no higher for want of a licence: ‘That standard is not to be further qualified, whether by reference to the holding of a licence to drive or by reference to the level of experience of the driver’ (Imbree [25]), Imbree overturning Cook v Cook. [P]’s knowledge of [D’s inexperience] goes to contributory negligence and voluntary assumption of risk. The standard is met.”

D also held out a special skill → B6 · otherwise → 6

B6 ‘Reasonable person’- Skills: Professionals · when d held out a special skill or competence, whether or not D practises a profession

“[D] having held out [the skill], ‘the standard of reasonable care and skill required is that of the ordinary skilled person exercising and professing to have that special skill’ (Rogers 483); nor does Bolam assist, being rejected because ‘it is for the courts to adjudicate on what is the appropriate standard of care’ (Rogers). That standard [is, or is not] met because [why D’s conduct meets or falls short of the skilled standard].”

A · below the skilled standard

  “[D]’s [conduct] falls below the SoC his held-out [skill] sets, ‘the standard of reasonable care and skill required is that of the ordinary skilled person exercising and professing to have that special skill’ (Rogers 483), carrying ‘the knowledge and experience to be expected of’ the class of defendant (Wagon Mound (No 2), SVE [10.175]), because [why D falls short]. Nor can [the practice D relies on] settle it, ‘it is for the courts to adjudicate on what is the appropriate standard of care’ (Rogers). The standard is not met.”

B · skilled standard answered

  “[The step D took that the skilled standard called for] answers the SoC, for [D], having held out [the skill], is held to no more than ‘the ordinary skilled person exercising and professing to have that special skill’ (Rogers 483); that Bolam is rejected, ‘it is for the courts to adjudicate on what is the appropriate standard of care’ (Rogers), does not lift the measure above that person. The standard is met, [the step D took] leaving nothing further required.”

D is a professional and there is peer professional opinion in evidence → B7 · a warning is alleged → B9 · otherwise → 6

B7 5O Standard of care for professionals · when d practises a profession, liability is said to arise from a professional service, and there is evidence of peer professional opinion

“[D], a professional sued over [the professional service], may avoid liability under s 5O: the onus being [D]’s (Gould [30]), it must be ‘established that the professional acted in a manner that (at the time the service was provided) was widely accepted in Australia by peer professional opinion as competent professional practice’ (CLA s 5O(1)). If so, [D] ‘does not incur a liability in negligence’ (CLA s 5O(1)). Because [why the peer evidence does or does not establish that manner of acting], s 5O [is, or is not] established.”

A · s 5O established

  “[The practice D relies on] is a manner of acting that ‘was widely accepted in Australia by peer professional opinion as competent professional practice’ (CLA s 5O(1)), because [why that practice was widely accepted by peers], and [D], bearing the onus (Gould [30]), discharges it. Subject to the opinion standing under s 5O(2)–(4), s 5O is accordingly established.”

B · s 5O not established

  “[The peer professional evidence D relies on] rises no higher than [the bald statement the expert made], and the onus being [D]’s (Gould [30]), it fails to establish that [D] ‘acted in a manner that (at the time the service was provided) was widely accepted in Australia by peer professional opinion as competent professional practice’ (CLA s 5O(1)), because [why that falls short of a widely accepted manner of acting].”

→ B8 (test the evidence) · If s 5O is made out the run ends; if not, ss 5B and 5C apply in full, → 6 to open the calculus

B8 s 5O(2)–(4): peer professional opinion (Gould, Dean v Pope, Sparks) · when the peer-professional evidence must be tested, or more than one body of opinion is in the field

“The evidence [D] relies on is [what the expert actually said], and s 5O asks whether it establishes ‘a widely accepted manner of acting’, not ‘“a” specific practice such as might be set out in a manual or the like’ (Dean v Pope per Ward P [233]), for ‘professional practice in this context refers to the manner in which professionals practise their profession, not to a particular protocol, procedure or process’ (Brereton J [314]). Firstly, differing opinions do not prevent reliance (CLA s 5O(3)); secondly, opinion need not be universally accepted (CLA s 5O(4)); thirdly, it cannot be relied on if ‘irrational’ (CLA s 5O(2)), and is not so ‘merely because one peer, or a body of peers, does not share that opinion’ (Gould [30]). The opinion accordingly [is, or is not] one s 5O accepts.”

A · opinion one s 5O accepts

  “[The peer professional evidence D relies on] establishes what a significant body of practitioners did in [D]’s circumstances, and that is enough: s 5O asks for ‘a widely accepted manner of acting’, not ‘“a” specific practice such as might be set out in a manual or the like’ (Dean v Pope [233]). Differing opinions do not prevent reliance (CLA s 5O(3)), universal acceptance is not required (CLA s 5O(4)), and the opinion is not ‘irrational’ (CLA s 5O(2); Gould [30]). The opinion is one s 5O accepts; [D] ‘does not incur a liability in negligence’ (CLA s 5O(1)).”

B · opinion s 5O does not accept

  “Sparks is analogous: ‘a bald statement by a practitioner, however well qualified, without reference to the specific factors giving rise to a claim of negligence may well not persuade the court that there is a relevant standard identified in the evidence’ (Basten JA [87]). What is missing is not a manual but ‘a widely accepted manner of acting’ itself (Dean v Pope [233]). The opinion is not one s 5O accepts.”

C · Sparks both ways

  “Sparks is analogous because [the peer professional evidence D relies on] may be no more than ‘a bald statement by a practitioner, however well qualified, without reference to the specific factors giving rise to a claim of negligence’ (Basten JA [87]); yet [the expert’s account] describes what practitioners in fact did in [D]’s circumstances, and s 5O asks for ‘a widely accepted manner of acting’ (Dean v Pope [233]); nothing here is ‘irrational’ (CLA s 5O(2); Gould [30]). On balance the opinion is one s 5O accepts.”

satisfied → s 5O answers the claim and the run ends · not → 6

B9 5P Division does not apply to duty to warn of risk · when the complaint is of a warning, advice or information about a risk of death or injury from a professional service

“Nor can s 5O answer [the failure to warn of the risk]: the Division ‘does not apply to liability arising in connection with the giving of (or the failure to give) a warning, advice or other information in respect of the risk of death of or injury to a person associated with the provision by a professional of a professional service’ (CLA s 5P), so the failure to warn is tested against Rogers, not s 5O.”

A · s 5P excludes the Division

  “[What P says D failed to tell her] is ‘a warning, advice or other information in respect of the risk of death of or injury to a person associated with the provision by a professional of a professional service’, to which the Division ‘does not apply’ (CLA s 5P). Section 5O therefore cannot answer [the failure to warn of the risk]; the failure to warn is tested against Rogers.”

B · treatment, not warning

  “[What P says D did badly] impugns [D]’s performance of [the procedure], not the giving or withholding of ‘a warning, advice or other information in respect of the risk of death of or injury to a person’ (CLA s 5P), so s 5P does not displace s 5O for that complaint: the manner of treatment answers to s 5O, while any warning allegation continues to answer to Rogers.”

→ B10

B10 Rogers v Whitaker: a material risk · when a failure to warn is alleged, the warning half of the Rogers slide

“[P] [‘incessantly’ questioned [D] as to possible complications, or did not]. ‘The law should recognise that a doctor has a duty to warn a patient of a material risk inherent in the proposed treatment’, subject to the therapeutic privilege (Rogers). A risk is material where, ‘in the circumstances of the particular case, a reasonable person in the patient’s position, if warned of the risk, would be likely to attach significance to it’, or where the practitioner is or should reasonably be aware that the particular patient would (Rogers); thus [material, or not].”

A · material risk

  “[P] ‘incessantly’ questioned [D] as to possible complications, so [D] was or should reasonably have been aware that this particular patient would attach significance to [the risk] (Rogers). The duty ‘to warn a patient of a material risk inherent in the proposed treatment’ is subject to the therapeutic privilege, which does not assist [D] because [why therapeutic privilege does not excuse D]; the risk is material in any event, ‘a reasonable person in the patient’s position, if warned of the risk, would be likely to attach significance to it’. The risk is material.”

B · not a material risk

  “[P] did not question [D] as to possible complications, and nothing put [D] on notice that this particular patient would attach significance to [the risk] (Rogers). Nor is the risk material on the ordinary test, ‘a reasonable person in the patient’s position, if warned of the risk, would be likely to attach significance to it’, because [what P stood to lose and how likely it was] leaves it both remote and slight; alternatively, the therapeutic privilege excuses [D] because [why therapeutic privilege excuses D]. The risk is not material.”

→ 6 (open the calculus)

B11 Reasonable care, not prevention (Dederer) · when d or another warned, prohibited or fenced the conduct, the risk was obvious, or D says P was careless of their own safety

“[D] owed reasonable care that [the structure or place D controlled] be safe ‘for users exercising reasonable care for their own safety’ (Dederer per Gummow J, quoting Brodie). [D] having [warned or prohibited], a prohibition is ‘one form of notice, perhaps the most effective form of notice’ (Nagle), and its failure is no answer: ‘the question is always the reasonableness of the warning, not its failure’ (Dederer per Gummow J). The risk of harm was [obvious, as the cliffs at Casuarina Beach were ‘perfectly obvious to any reasonable person’, or hidden, as the submerged rocks in Nagle were hidden from the ordinary users of the Basin] (Romeo per Kirby J).”

A · obvious risk, care discharged

  “[The visible feature of the danger] put the risk of harm before anyone taking care, and [D] owed reasonable care only ‘for users exercising reasonable care for their own safety’ (Dederer per Gummow J, quoting Brodie). [D] prohibited [the conduct], a prohibition being ‘one form of notice, perhaps the most effective form of notice’ (Nagle), and that it did not stop [P] assists nothing: ‘the question is always the reasonableness of the warning, not its failure’ (Dederer). The risk of harm, like Romeo’s cliffs ‘perfectly obvious to any reasonable person’ (Kirby J), was obvious; care was discharged.”

B · hidden risk, care not discharged

  “[The feature that hid the danger from view] concealed the risk of harm, and [P] was a user ‘exercising reasonable care for their own safety’ (Dederer per Gummow J, quoting Brodie). [D] warned only of [the other danger], so the warning was unreasonable, not merely ineffective, ‘the question is always the reasonableness of the warning, not its failure’ (Dederer), no prohibition (Nagle) being given. The risk of harm was hidden, as the submerged rocks in Nagle were hidden from the ordinary users of the Basin (Romeo per Kirby J); care was not discharged.”

C · Romeo both ways

  “Romeo is analogous because [the visible feature of the danger] made [the structure or place D controlled] as plain as the cliffs ‘perfectly obvious to any reasonable person’ (Kirby J), [D] owing reasonable care only ‘for users exercising reasonable care for their own safety’ (Dederer per Gummow J, quoting Brodie); yet [what shows the danger lay concealed] is the strongest counter, closer to Nagle, whose submerged rocks were hidden from ordinary users. [D] having warned, ‘the question is always the reasonableness of the warning, not its failure’ (Dederer). On balance the risk of harm was obvious.”

Return to 11 and conclude.

B12 s 5C: Other principles · when the facts give another site or occasion, an alternative way of doing the thing, or later remedial action

“Firstly, [the precaution P alleges] is costed across [every like site D is responsible for]: the burden ‘includes the burden of taking precautions to avoid similar risks of harm for which the person may be responsible’ (CLA s 5C(a)), tested by the proposition that all equivalent sites for which [D] is responsible would have to be so treated (Romeo per Kirby J). Secondly, that [the alternative] was open does not ‘of itself give rise to or affect liability’ (CLA s 5C(b)); thirdly, nor does [the remedial step D took afterwards] ‘of itself … constitute an admission of liability’ (CLA s 5C(c)). Section 5C thus tells [for, or against] [D].”

A · s 5C tells for D

  “[The precaution] must be costed across [every like site D is responsible for], not [the one place] alone: the burden ‘includes the burden of taking precautions to avoid similar risks of harm for which the person may be responsible’ (CLA s 5C(a)), and [why repeating the precaution everywhere is costly] makes that substantial, resources demanded here being diverted from sites of equal or greater priority (Romeo per Kirby J). That [the alternative] was open does not ‘of itself give rise to or affect liability’ (CLA s 5C(b)). Section 5C tells for [D].”

B · s 5C tells against D

  “[The precaution], even costed across [every like site D is responsible for] because the burden ‘includes the burden of taking precautions to avoid similar risks of harm for which the person may be responsible’ (CLA s 5C(a); Romeo per Kirby J), remains slight because [why repeating the precaution everywhere is cheap]. That [the alternative] was open does not ‘of itself give rise to or affect liability’ (CLA s 5C(b)); nor does [the remedial step D took afterwards] ‘of itself … constitute an admission of liability’ (CLA s 5C(c)). Section 5C tells against [D].”

→ 10 (continue the calculus)

B13 s5B(2)(c): a real risk cheaply eliminated (Wagon Mound (No 2)) · when the precaution alleged cost little, was simple, or was already to hand

“[The precaution] would have cost [the money, time or trouble involved]. A reasonable man ‘would only neglect such a risk if he had some valid reason for doing so’, weighing it against the difficulty of eliminating it, for ‘a person must be regarded as negligent if he does not take steps to eliminate a risk which he knows or ought to know is a real risk’ (Lord Reid, in RTA v Refrigerated Roadways [185]), qualified only where ‘it is justifiable not to take steps to eliminate a real risk if it is small’ and the circumstances make it ‘right to neglect it’ ([185]). The burden thus [does, or does not] answer the probability and gravity.”

A · burden no answer

  “[The precaution] would have cost [the money, time or trouble involved] only, and [why elimination presented no difficulty] leaves [D] without the ‘valid reason’ a reasonable man needs to neglect ‘a real risk’ (Lord Reid, in RTA v Refrigerated Roadways [185]). Unlike the headgear in Woods, [the precaution P alleges] was readily available and directed at this risk of harm alone; unlike Bolton v Stone, [how often or how nearly this risk arose] made this one real. The burden does not answer the probability and gravity.”

B · burden answers

  “[What stood in the way of taking the precaution] made elimination difficult and costly, the ‘valid reason’ on which a reasonable man may neglect a small risk (Lord Reid, in RTA v Refrigerated Roadways [185]); the burden extends past money to time, distance, communication and ‘less tangible burdens’ (NSW v Mikhael per Beazley JA [82]). As in Woods, [why no equivalent precaution existed here] left none reasonably available. The burden answers the probability and gravity.”

C · Bolton v Stone both ways

  “Bolton v Stone is analogous because [how rarely or remotely this risk arose] made the risk of [the harm] small; yet [the money, time or trouble involved] is the strongest counter, a reasonable man needing ‘some valid reason’ to neglect it (Lord Reid, in RTA v Refrigerated Roadways [185]), and [the precaution P alleges] asked little of [D]. Nor is [the alternative precaution] a clean answer, even cheap precautions creating risks, as catch-points risked derailing runaway trucks (Caledonian Collieries 225). On balance the burden does not answer the probability and gravity, though the point is arguable.”

s 5C is engaged → B12 · otherwise → 10

B14 s 5B(2)(b): the likely seriousness of the harm (Paris v Stepney) · when [P] is a child, is elderly, or is otherwise likely to suffer graver injury than others, and the seriousness limb must be argued in…

“Secondly, [P] being [the description], the risk of harm is weighed accordingly: the duty ‘must … be related to both the risk and the degree of the injury’, so ‘the known circumstance that a particular workman is likely to suffer a graver injury than his fellows’ must be taken into account (Paris per Lord MacDermott 387). Dederer is [distinguishable, or analogous], according to whether [D] could assume of [P] the care Dederer expects of ‘users exercising reasonable care for their own safety’ (Gummow J). The seriousness limb (CLA s 5B(2)(b)) therefore tells [for, or against] the precaution alleged.”

A · seriousness tells for the precaution

  “Secondly, [P] being [the description], and [D] knowing of [the vulnerability], the duty ‘must … be related to both the risk and the degree of the injury’, ‘the known circumstance that a particular workman is likely to suffer a graver injury than his fellows’ being taken into account (Paris per Lord MacDermott 387). Dederer is distinguishable: [why D could not assume of P the care Dederer expects] means [D] cannot assume the care of ‘users exercising reasonable care for their own safety’ (Gummow J). The seriousness limb (CLA s 5B(2)(b)) tells for the precaution.”

B · seriousness tells against

  “Secondly, [P] being [the description], nothing took the risk of harm beyond the ordinary: [the vulnerability] never came to [D]’s notice, and it is ‘the known circumstance that a particular workman is likely to suffer a graver injury than his fellows’ that must be taken into account (Paris per Lord MacDermott 387). Dederer is analogous: [what shows P was able to take care for herself] entitled [D] to assume of [P] the care of ‘users exercising reasonable care for their own safety’ (Gummow J). The seriousness limb (CLA s 5B(2)(b)) tells against the precaution alleged.”

C · Dederer both ways

  “Secondly, Dederer is analogous because [P’s own conduct or capacity here] showed her well able to exercise the care [D] may assume of ‘users exercising reasonable care for their own safety’ (Gummow J); yet [the description] is the strongest counter, ‘the known circumstance that a particular workman is likely to suffer a graver injury than his fellows’ being taken into account (Paris per Lord MacDermott 387), and [D] knew of [the vulnerability]. On balance the seriousness limb (CLA s 5B(2)(b)) tells for the precaution.”

→ 9

B16 A warning as the precaution alleged, [D] not being a professional · when the precaution [P] alleges is a warning and [D] is not ‘a person practising a profession’ (CLA s 5O(1))

“The precaution [P] alleges is a warning, yet [D] ‘does not owe a duty of care to another person … to warn of an obvious risk to the plaintiff’ (CLA s 5H(1)), an obvious risk being one that ‘in the circumstances, would have been obvious to a reasonable person in the position of that person’ (CLA s 5F(1)), including risks ‘patent or a matter of common knowledge’ (CLA s 5F(2)). A low probability of occurring does not stop a risk being obvious (CLA s 5F(3)), nor need it be ‘prominent, conspicuous or physically observable’ (CLA s 5F(4)). That yields only where [P] ‘requested advice or information about the risk from the defendant’ (CLA s 5H(2)(a)) or [D] is ‘required by a written law to warn the plaintiff of the risk’ (CLA s 5H(2)(b)), the professional limb (CLA s 5H(2)(c)) being closed to [D]; even then no duty is presumed (CLA s 5H(3)). If the duty survives, the warning is weighed under ‘the burden of taking precautions to avoid the risk of harm’ (CLA s 5B(2)(c)). The risk of harm is [obvious, or not], because [what made the risk obvious or kept it hidden].”

A · obvious risk, no duty to warn

  “The warning alleged answers to s 5H: [the plainly visible feature of the risk] made ‘[the risk]’ ‘patent or a matter of common knowledge’ (CLA s 5F(2)), so [D] ‘does not owe a duty of care … to warn of an obvious risk’ (CLA s 5H(1)); [P] never ‘requested advice or information about the risk’ and no ‘written law’ required a warning (CLA s 5H(2)). Woods is analogous, its risk ‘so obvious that reasonableness did not require the respondent to warn players about it’, as [this risk] was. No duty to warn arose.”

B · a warning was required and was not given

  “The warning alleged was required: [the hidden feature of the risk] was known to [D], not ‘obvious to a reasonable person in the position of’ [P] (CLA s 5F(1)), so s 5H(1) leaves the duty intact and the burden of giving a warning was slight (CLA s 5B(2)(c)). Nagle is analogous, the submerged rocks there being hidden from the ordinary users of the Basin so a warning was required (Romeo per Kirby J), as [the feature that hid the danger] hid this one. The RP in [D]’s position would have warned; [D] did not.”

C · a warning was given, and it was reasonable

  “The warning [D] gave was a reasonable response: Dederer is analogous, the authority there having ‘erected signs warning of, and prohibiting, the very conduct engaged in’, a prohibition being ‘one form of notice, perhaps the most effective form of notice’ (Nagle, in Dederer). That [P] proceeded anyway is no answer, ‘the question is always the reasonableness of the warning, not its failure’, and a further sign would not have ‘told him anything he did not already know’ (Dederer). [The warning] was reasonable, ‘and the law demands no more and no less’ (Dederer).”

→ 6 (open the calculus) · On causation later, what [P] would have done if warned is determined subjectively (CLA s 5D(3))

B17 Mulligan: no breach in failing to warn · when the precaution alleged is a warning and the weighing has been done

“Mulligan is [analogous, or distinguishable]: the Council there had no notice of any earlier accident and no recommendation to sign that place, and the High Court unanimously found no breach in failing to warn a young man who dived into the water; [why this case is like or unlike Mulligan’s], so a warning [was, or was not] required.”

A · Mulligan distinguishable

  “Mulligan is distinguishable: its Council had no notice of any earlier accident and no recommendation to sign that place, and the High Court unanimously found no breach in failing to warn. Here [what D knew of the danger] gave [D] the very notice Mulligan’s council lacked, calling for a warning at [the single known danger]; nor, unlike Vairy’s majority, could [D] say it could not mark ‘every point in its municipal district from which a person could enter a body of water’ (Hayne J [137]). A warning was required.”

B · Mulligan analogous

  “Mulligan is analogous: its Council had no notice of any earlier accident and no recommendation to sign that place, and the High Court unanimously found no breach in failing to warn a young man who dived into the water. [D] is in the like position, [the absence of any earlier accident or recommendation D can point to] leaving it without notice, and [what made the risk plain to P] left the risk one [P] could see for herself. No warning was required.”

Return to 11 and conclude.

CAUSATION AND REMOTENESS

Factual causation, then Scope of Liability -Introduction [composed from two slide headings: Class 10’s factual causation slides and Class 11’s Scope of Liability -Introduction slide]

The flow chart as given in Class 10, Class 11 and Class 18.

+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| Step 1 identify the harm: ‘The harm must be of a kind that is recognised by law as damage’ (CLA s 5)                                                                                                                                           |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| Step 2 restate the negligent act(s)                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| 1 What is factual causation: the ‘But For’ test: would the damage have not occurred but for the act or omission of the D?                                                                                                                      |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
|   ▪ If the damage would have occurred anyway, then the act or omission is not a cause.                                                                                                                                                         |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
|   ▪ s 5D(1)(a): the negligence was a necessary condition of the occurrence of the harm.                                                                                                                                                        |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| 2 Exceptional Cases: when the ‘but for’ / ‘a necessary condition’ test fails, causation may still be established through s 5D(2) ‘whether or not and why’                                                                                      |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
|   ▪ 1. Materially contributes to Injury                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
|   ▪ 2. Materially increases risk of harm                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| 3 Scope of Liability -Introduction: to establish the scope of liability (legal causation) ss 5D(1)(b) and 5D(4), address the normative question of: is it appropriate for the scope of D's liability to extend to the harm so caused to the P? |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
|   ▪ 1. Is there a novus actus?                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
|   ▪ 2. Is the injury not too remote (ie RF)?                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
