CAUSATION AND REMOTENESS: SCAFFOLD

1 What is factual causation

“The harm is ‘[the injury sued for]’, which is [personal injury, damage to property or economic loss] and so ‘harm of any kind’ recognised by law as damage (CLA s 5). The negligent wrongs are [each act or omission complained of, in the same words used at breach]; causation divides into ‘factual causation and scope of liability’ (Adeels [42]) and is asked of each wrong separately; each wrong must be ‘a necessary condition of the occurrence of the harm’ (CLA s 5D(1)(a)), ‘nothing more or less than the application of a “but for” test’ (Wallace [16]), [P] bearing the onus ‘on the balance of probabilities’ (CLA s 5E).”

A · factual causation satisfied

  “Firstly, but for [D]’s ‘[the negligent wrong]’, [what would have happened absent it], so the wrong was ‘a necessary condition of the occurrence of the harm’ (CLA s 5D(1)(a)), and [the evidence proving the counterfactual] discharges [P]’s onus (CLA s 5E). Against that, [the rival cause the facts leave open, or that none is left open]; [why the probabilities nonetheless favour D’s wrong being necessary, or that the point does not arise]. Strong is analogous: Woolworths had no system of periodic inspection and cleaning, the probabilities favoured detection had one existed, and likewise [why the probabilities here favour the harm being avoided had D not been negligent]; a wrong ‘necessary to complete a set of conditions that are jointly sufficient’ need not be the sole cause ([20]). Therefore, the better view is that s 5D(1)(a) is satisfied here.”

B · factual causation fails

  “Firstly, even without [D]’s ‘[the negligent wrong]’, [what would have happened anyway], so the wrong was not ‘a necessary condition of the occurrence of the harm’ (CLA s 5D(1)(a)), and [P], bearing the onus (CLA s 5E), cannot bridge [the gap in proof that the harm would not have happened anyway]. Adeels is analogous: it ‘was not shown to be more probable than not that, but for the absence of security personnel …, the shootings would not have taken place’ ([53]), and likewise [why the harm would have come regardless]. Therefore, the better view is that s 5D(1)(a) is not satisfied here.”

Run the but for limb once for each wrong; the wrongs commonly divide, some passing and some failing. Number the wrongs, not the limbs, after the first: open each subsequent wrong “Turning to ‘[the next wrong]’, but for …” and drop the repeated Firstly · the breach is a failure to warn, or causation turns on what P would have done → B1 · two or more acts or events would each be sufficient to bring about the harm, or the negligence looks like a mere necessary pre-condition rather than a cause (the decapitation example, Deane J in March), or a superseding cause is in play → B13 for the full rigour · the but for test is met → 2 · it fails for the whole harm or for a disputed part of it → B2 · multiple conjunctive factors produced the harm and the contribution of one is unclear → B3 · it fails only because the precaution had a chance below 50% of working → B5, which defeats only the allegation resting on that chance; to put s 5D(2) to that same allegation in the alternative → B2 · Where more than one defendant may answer for the same harm in sequence, an original wrongdoer and a treating party whose later conduct made the injury worse, run this whole section once for EACH defendant separately; in the treating party’s own run, its conduct is assessed at 1 and 2 in the ordinary way, and in the original wrongdoer’s run, whether the later conduct broke that defendant’s own chain is asked at 4, and at B6 where it is medical treatment

2 Scope of Liability -Introduction (legal causation)

“Secondly, the risk of harm that made [D]’s conduct careless was [the risk of harm identified at breach], and ‘[the harm]’ [answers that risk, or does not]. It must be ‘appropriate for the scope of the negligent person’s liability to extend to the harm so caused’ (CLA s 5D(1)(b)), a determination ‘entirely normative’ (Wallace [14]), and the scope of liability ‘normally does not extend beyond liability for the occurrence of such harm the risk of which it was the duty of the negligent party to exercise reasonable care and skill to avoid’ (Wallace [24]). Three matters then confine that scope, taken in order: remoteness, novus actus interveniens (‘NAI’), and the policy-based considerations of s 5D(4).”

A · within scope

  “Secondly, the risk of harm that made [D]’s conduct careless was [the risk of harm identified at breach], and ‘[the harm]’ answers it because [why this harm is that risk materialised], so it is ‘appropriate for the scope of the negligent person’s liability to extend to the harm so caused’ (CLA s 5D(1)(b); Wallace [14]). Wallace is distinguishable: the neurapraxia there materialised a risk Mr Wallace was willing to hazard even if warned ([25]), whereas [why this harm is the very risk of harm D’s care was to avoid, not one P was willing to hazard]. Three matters confine that scope: remoteness, novus actus interveniens (‘NAI’), and the policy-based considerations of s 5D(4).”

B · outside scope

  “Secondly, the risk of harm that made [D]’s conduct careless was [the risk of harm identified at breach], and ‘[the harm]’ does not answer it, [why the harm falls outside that risk]; liability normally does not extend beyond harm the risk of which it was the negligent party’s duty to avoid (Wallace [24]). Wallace is analogous: factual causation was established there, yet liability was denied because the neurapraxia materialised a risk the patient was willing to hazard, and likewise [why P would have hazarded this risk]. Therefore, s 5D(1)(b) is likely not satisfied; remoteness, novus actus interveniens (‘NAI’) and the policy-based considerations of s 5D(4) are addressed in the alternative.”

Three things are then considered, in order: remoteness at 3, a novus actus at 4, and policy at 5 · → 3

3 Remoteness: the boundary beyond which the D should not be held liable

“Firstly, the kind or type of harm must be reasonably foreseeable, ‘the damage is of such a kind as the reasonable man should have foreseen’ (Wagon Mound (No 1)), a third and distinct foreseeability, after the class of persons at duty and the risk of harm at breach (CLA s 5B). The kind foreseeable from ‘[the negligent wrong]’, taken broadly and not in its particulars, was [the kind], the description ‘formulated by reference to the nature of the risk which ought to have been foreseen’ (Jolley per Lord Hoffmann). The risk of harm must be real, one [D] ‘would not brush aside as far-fetched’ (Wagon Mound (No 2) per Lord Reid). Neither ‘the precise character of the damage or the extent of it’ (Pusey per Walsh J 413) nor its manner (Hughes) need be foreseen, and [D] takes [P] as found (Nader). Thus ‘[the injury sued for]’ [is of a foreseeable kind, or differs in kind].”

A · foreseeable kind

  “Firstly, the kind of harm must be reasonably foreseeable (Wagon Mound (No 1)), and the kind foreseeable from ‘[the negligent wrong]’ was [the kind], ‘formulated by reference to the nature of the risk which ought to have been foreseen’ (Jolley per Lord Hoffmann). [What put this risk in D’s contemplation] made it real, one [D] ‘would not brush aside as far-fetched’, as in Wagon Mound (No 2), where oil discharged into Sydney Harbour without justification left a real risk; and only the kind, not its precise character or extent, need be foreseen (Pusey per Walsh J 413). Therefore, ‘[the injury sued for]’ is of that kind.”

B · too remote

  “Firstly, the kind of harm foreseeable from ‘[the negligent wrong]’ was at most [the kind], and [why this risk was unreal] left [D] able to brush the risk of harm aside ‘as far-fetched’, unlike Wagon Mound (No 2), where discharging oil into Sydney Harbour without justification left a real risk (Lord Reid). Though neither precise character nor extent nor manner need be foreseen (Pusey per Walsh J 413; Hughes), [why this harm differs in kind] shows ‘[the injury sued for]’ differing in kind from anything foreseeable (Wagon Mound (No 1)). Therefore, the better view is that it is too remote.”

the manner or extent is unusual but the kind foreseeable → B10 · If the harm is unusual because of something in P, a susceptibility, weakness, belief or reaction, → B11 · the harm is psychiatric injury → B12 · otherwise → 4; if nothing intervened between breach and harm, write ‘Secondly, nothing intervened between breach and harm to sever the chain’ and go straight to 5, so that Thirdly still follows

4 Novus Actus Interveniens

“Secondly, what intervened was [the act or event]. To ‘sever the connexion’ the intervening occurrence ‘must ordinarily be either’ ‘human action that is properly to be regarded as voluntary’, a voluntary human act done with knowledge of its consequences, whether a third party’s or [P]’s own, ‘a fully voluntary act in the sense of one which produced intended consequences’, or a causally independent event ‘so extremely unlikely as to be termed a coincidence’ (Haber per Smith J). An instinctive act in the agony of the moment is not voluntary (Caterson), and nothing severs where the act was reasonably foreseeable within a risk [D] created (Mahony) and was ‘the very kind of thing likely to happen as a result of the defendant’s negligence’ (Chapman, adopted in March per Mason CJ). ‘[The act or event]’ [was, or was not] of that kind; there [is, or is no] NAI.”

A · no novus actus

  “Secondly, ‘[the act or event]’ was neither ‘properly to be regarded as voluntary’ nor a coincidence (Haber per Smith J): it was reasonably foreseeable within the risk of harm [D] created (Mahony) and ‘the very kind of thing likely to happen as a result of the defendant’s negligence’ (Chapman, adopted in March per Mason CJ), because [why the intervention was within D’s risk]. Chapman is analogous, ‘the risk of injury from passing traffic’ on the dark, wet road being ‘real and substantial’ and not ‘remote and fanciful’, as [the like risk here] was. There is likely no NAI.”

B · chain severed

  “Secondly, ‘[the act or event]’ severed the chain: [why the act was fully voluntary] made it ‘human action that is properly to be regarded as voluntary’, done with knowledge of its consequences and no instinctive act in the agony of the moment (Caterson), or else [the separate origin of the event] was ‘so extremely unlikely as to be termed a coincidence’ (Haber per Smith J); nor was it foreseeable within any risk [D] created (Mahony), nor ‘the very kind of thing likely to happen’ (March). Therefore, the better view is that the chain is severed.”

→ 5

5 Values, policy-based considerations

“Thirdly, [the strongest policy fact] frames the final matter: the court is to ‘consider (amongst other relevant things) whether or not and why responsibility for the harm should be imposed on the negligent party’ (CLA s 5D(4)). Fairness [tells for, or against] imposing responsibility because [why fairness tells for or against]. The risk of harm [was, or was not] [P]’s personal responsibility because [why the risk of harm was or was not P’s own]. The class of claimants [is contained, or is indeterminate], so floodgates [do not, or do] open. And liability [would, or would not] help people take proper care in future because [why the ruling would or would not encourage care]. On balance these considerations [warrant, or discount] responsibility, so [D]’s ‘[the negligent wrong]’ [did, or did not] cause ‘[the harm]’ within s 5D.”

A · causation made out

  “Thirdly, fairness tells for imposing responsibility because [why fairness favours P]; the risk of harm was not [P]’s personal responsibility because [why the risk of harm lay with D, not P]; the class of claimants is contained, [what confines it], so no floodgates open; and liability would help people take proper care in future because [why the ruling encourages care], the court weighing ‘whether or not and why responsibility for the harm should be imposed on the negligent party’ (CLA s 5D(4)). Therefore, the better view is that [D]’s ‘[the negligent wrong]’ did cause ‘[the harm]’ within s 5D.”

B · causation fails

  “Thirdly, fairness tells against imposing responsibility because [why fairness favours D]; the risk of harm was [P]’s personal responsibility because [why the risk of harm lay with P]; the class of claimants is indeterminate, [what leaves it open-ended], so floodgates open; and liability would not help people take proper care in future because [why the ruling would not encourage care], the court weighing ‘whether or not and why responsibility for the harm should be imposed on the negligent party’ (CLA s 5D(4)). Therefore, the better view is that [D]’s ‘[the negligent wrong]’ did not cause ‘[the harm]’ within s 5D.”

The run ends · Duty, breach and causation of non-remote damage together establish liability; defences are run only where the question asks for them · a further wrong remains untested → 1 (run it) · there is a further defendant → 1

B1 Section 5D (3) CLA – Failure to warn · when the breach is a failure to warn, or causation turns on what P would have done

“What [P] would have done if warned decides factual causation, and ‘is to be determined subjectively in the light of all relevant circumstances’ (CLA s 5D(3)(a)). [P] cannot give positive evidence of what she would have done, ‘any statement made by the person after suffering the harm’ being ‘inadmissible except to the extent (if any) that the statement is against his or her interest’ (CLA s 5D(3)(b)), the onus remaining hers (CLA s 5E). A causal connection exists ‘if it is probable that the plaintiff would have acted on the warning and desisted’, and none ‘if the plaintiff would have persisted with the same course of action in comparable circumstances even if a warning had been given’ (Chappel per McHugh J). The surrounding circumstances show [P] would have [desisted, or persisted].”

A · P would have desisted

  “[The objective circumstance showing P would have desisted] shows, ‘subjectively in the light of all relevant circumstances’ (CLA s 5D(3)(a)), that [P] would have acted on a warning, a causal connection existing ‘if it is probable that the plaintiff would have acted on the warning and desisted’ (Chappel per McHugh J); her later statement is inadmissible unless against her interest (CLA s 5D(3)(b)). Rosenberg is distinguishable: the highly qualified nurse there could assess the risk herself and was not credible, whereas [why P is differently placed]. Therefore, [P] would probably have desisted, and factual causation is established (CLA s 5D(1)(a)).”

B · P would have persisted

  “[The objective circumstance showing P was set on the course] shows, ‘subjectively in the light of all relevant circumstances’ (CLA s 5D(3)(a)), that a warning would have changed nothing: ‘no causal connection will exist if the plaintiff would have persisted with the same course of action in comparable circumstances even if a warning had been given’ (Chappel per McHugh J). Rosenberg is analogous: the highly qualified nurse there could assess the risk herself and was not credible, as [why P is likewise placed]. Therefore, [P] would probably have persisted, and factual causation fails.”

P would have persisted with the same course of action in comparable circumstances → causation fails for that wrong and the run ends for it · P would have desisted, or not proceeded then → 2 · Where D failed to warn of two or more distinct risks and the harm came from a risk P would have accepted regardless, factual causation may still be established but scope of liability at 2 will likely deny it (Wallace)

B2 Causation in Exceptional Cases · when the but for test in s 5D(1)(a) is not met, for the whole harm or a disputed part of it

“[The feature defeating the but for test] makes this a candidate ‘exceptional case’, where negligence ‘that cannot be established as a necessary condition of the occurrence of harm’ may still establish factual causation ‘in accordance with established principles’, the court considering ‘whether or not and why responsibility for the harm should be imposed on the negligent party’ (CLA s 5D(2)). The established principles are material contribution to injury (Bonnington; Amaca v Booth) and material increase in risk, a defendant being taken to have materially contributed where the negligence increased the risk to [P] and that risk eventuated (Chappel), the wider English exception of McGhee and Fairchild, where mesothelioma sufferers ‘had been exposed to asbestos in successive employments’ (Adeels [57]), standing differently: ‘the Fairchild exception has not to date been recognised’ in Australia (SVE [11.145]). Adeels is [analogous, or distinguishable]: the harm there came of ‘deliberate criminal wrongdoing’ ([57]), and that security ‘might have deterred or prevented’ it was not ‘reason enough’ ([56]). Section 5D(2) [is, or is not] satisfied.”

A · s 5D(2) satisfied

  “[The contribution D’s negligence in fact made] engages material contribution to injury (Bonnington; Amaca v Booth), an established principle on which factual causation may be accepted in an exceptional case (CLA s 5D(2)); it rests on contribution proved, not on the material increase in risk of McGhee and Fairchild, an exception that ‘has not to date been recognised’ in Australia (SVE [11.145]). Adeels is distinguishable: the harm there came of ‘deliberate criminal wrongdoing’ ([57]), where a precaution that ‘might have deterred or prevented’ it was not reason enough ([56]), whereas [why no deliberate third-party wrongdoing stands between D’s negligence and this harm]. Therefore, s 5D(2) is likely satisfied.”

B · s 5D(2) not satisfied

  “[The most the evidence establishes about D’s contribution] falls short of both established principles: no contribution to the injury is proved (Bonnington; Amaca v Booth), and material increase in risk (McGhee; Fairchild) ‘has not to date been recognised’ in Australia (SVE [11.145]), so factual causation is not established ‘in accordance with established principles’ (CLA s 5D(2)). Adeels is analogous: the harm came of ‘deliberate criminal wrongdoing’ ([57]), and that [the precaution P alleges] ‘might have deterred or prevented’ it is not ‘reason enough’ ([56]). Therefore, the better view is that s 5D(2) is not satisfied.”

C · Adeels both ways

  “Adeels is analogous because ‘[the harm]’ came of ‘deliberate criminal wrongdoing’ ([57]) by [the third party], and that [the precaution P alleges] ‘might have deterred or prevented’ it is not ‘reason enough’ ([56]); yet [the fact cutting the other way] does not sit with that, pointing to [material contribution to injury, or material increase in risk]. Section 5D(2) operates only ‘in accordance with established principles’, and the Fairchild exception of material increase in risk ‘has not to date been recognised’ in Australia (SVE [11.145]). Therefore, the better view is that factual causation is not established under s 5D(2), though not without difficulty.”

the factors are conjunctive and the contribution of one is unclear → B3 · the risk was increased and eventuated → B4 · the precaution only ‘might have’ worked → causation fails for that wrong

B3 Materially contributes to Injury · when multiple acts or events led to the harm and the extent of one factor’s contribution is unclear

“The factors here [operated cumulatively, or were disparate] because [why the factors did or did not compound one another]. Where harm has ‘multiple conjunctive causal factors’, [P] need only prove the negligence ‘caused or materially contributed to the injury’ (Lord Watson in Wakelin, in Amaca v Booth [70]), any contribution not within the de minimis exception being material (Lord Reid in Bonnington, in Amaca v Ellis [66]). But a claim fails where the injury is ‘likely to have been caused by one or more of a number of disparate factors, one of which was attributable to a wrongful act or omission of the defendant’ (Wilsher, in Williams [40]). This harm [is, or is not] of the conjunctive kind.”

A · factors operated cumulatively

  “[The factor D’s negligence supplied] compounded [the other factor] because [why the factors compounded one another], so the harm has ‘multiple conjunctive causal factors’ and [P] need only prove the negligence ‘caused or materially contributed to the injury’ (Wakelin, in Amaca v Booth [70]), any contribution above de minimis being material (Bonnington, in Amaca v Ellis [66]). Amaca v Booth is analogous, the exposure there having ‘causally contributed to’ the disease’s development ([53]), as [the factor D’s negligence supplied] did to ‘[the harm]’ here; Amaca v Ellis is distinguishable, no witness there putting the asbestos chance above 23 per cent ([30]), whereas [the evidence here] rises beyond that. Therefore, the better view is that ‘[the negligent wrong]’ materially contributed to ‘[the harm]’, and s 5D(2) is satisfied.”

B · factors disparate

  “[What shows the causes were separate] leaves [the other cause] independent of [D]’s negligence, and a claim fails where the injury is ‘caused by one or more of a number of disparate factors, one of which was attributable to a wrongful act or omission of the defendant’ (Wilsher, in Williams [40]). Amaca v Ellis is analogous, no witness there putting the asbestos chance above 23 per cent ([30]), as [the evidence here] likewise leaves [D]’s factor below even; material contribution suffices only for ‘multiple conjunctive causal factors’ (Amaca v Booth [70]), which these are not. Therefore, material contribution is likely not made out.”

C · Amaca v Ellis both ways

  “Amaca v Ellis is analogous because [the other cause] stands as an independent explanation, no witness there putting above 23 per cent the chance that asbestos caused the cancer ([30]); yet [the fact cutting the other way] tells against it: the factors operated cumulatively, as in Amaca v Booth, where the exposure ‘causally contributed to’ the disease’s development ([53]), and with ‘multiple conjunctive causal factors’ [P] need only prove the negligence ‘caused or materially contributed to the injury’ ([70]). Therefore, the better view is that material contribution is made out, though it is close.”

the connection between the agent and this harm is established → 2 · the agent is merely capable of such harm → causation fails for that wrong

B4 Materially increases risk of harm · when d’s negligence increased the risk of harm to P and that risk eventuated

“[D]’s ‘[the negligent wrong]’ increased the risk of ‘[the harm]’, and that risk [eventuated, or did not]: [the added risk the negligence created]. Whether an increased risk of harm proves causation ‘has not been considered by this Court’ (Strong [26]), and McGhee, requiring only that the breach ‘materially increases the risk of injury’, has been confined: in Hotson and Wilsher the House of Lords ‘reversed awards in favour of the claimant’, ‘demonstrating a desire to confine and/or curtail McGhee’, and Wilsher made plain ‘that McGhee was not to be taken as having established a rule that the burden of proof shifts to defendants’ (SVE [11.145]). Chappel nonetheless accepts material contribution where an increased risk of injury eventuates (Gaudron J 238, McHugh J 244, Gummow J 257, Kirby J 273), and the cause must be probable, not merely possible (Amaca v Ellis [70]).”

A · the risk eventuated

  “[The added risk D’s negligence created] eventuated in ‘[the harm]’, and material contribution is accepted where an increased risk of injury eventuates (Chappel per Gaudron J 238, McHugh J 244, Gummow J 257, Kirby J 273). That the question ‘has not been considered by this Court’ (Strong [26]), and that McGhee stands confined by Hotson and Wilsher (SVE [11.145]), does not stand in the way, because [the expert evidence] makes this cause probable, not merely possible (Amaca v Ellis [70]). Therefore, the better view is that factual causation is established.”

B · increased risk proves nothing

  “[P] was injured instead by [the other risk], [what connects the injury to that other risk], so the risk of harm [D]’s negligence increased did not eventuate, and Chappel, accepting material contribution only where the increased risk of injury eventuates, is distinguishable. Whether an increased risk of harm proves causation in any event ‘has not been considered by this Court’ (Strong [26]), McGhee not establishing ‘a rule that the burden of proof shifts to defendants’ (SVE [11.145]); [the expert evidence] leaves this cause possible, not probable (Amaca v Ellis [70]). Therefore, factual causation is not established.”

C · McGhee both ways

  “McGhee is analogous because [D]’s ‘[the negligent wrong]’ ‘materially increases the risk of injury’, [the added risk] exposing [P] to it, and material contribution is accepted where that risk eventuates, as ‘[the harm]’ did (Chappel per Gaudron J 238, McHugh J 244, Gummow J 257, Kirby J 273); yet [the expert evidence] points the other way, McGhee has been ‘confine[d] and/or curtail[ed]’ by Hotson and Wilsher (SVE [11.145]), and the question ‘has not been considered by this Court’ (Strong [26]). Therefore, the better view is that the increase in risk carries causation, though finely balanced.”

the risk was increased and eventuated → 2 · the cause remains possible only → causation fails for that wrong

B5 Loss of a chance (Tabet v Gett) · when the facts give a chance below 50% that a precaution or treatment would have produced a better outcome

“The chance being less than even, [P] cannot prove ‘[the negligent wrong]’ was ‘a necessary condition of the occurrence of the harm’ (CLA s 5D(1)(a)), the onus being hers (CLA s 5E), and loss of a chance of a better medical outcome is generally not recoverable in tort (Tabet). Malec would instead value [the future loss claimed] as a chance, discounted by its percentage, but that is a matter of quantum, not causation. The allegation resting on the chance accordingly fails; any other allegation stands on its own footing.”

A · chance not recoverable

  “[The expert whose evidence gives the chance] puts it at [the percentage], below even, so [P] cannot prove ‘[the negligent wrong]’ was ‘a necessary condition of the occurrence of the harm’ (CLA s 5D(1)(a)) and bears the onus (CLA s 5E). Tabet is analogous, the High Court there denying recovery for the lost chance of a better medical outcome, generally not recoverable in tort, as [P]’s claim on this allegation is. Malec would value [the future loss claimed] as a chance discounted by that percentage, as quantum, not causation. Therefore, the better view is that the allegation resting on the chance fails.”

B · necessary condition proved

  “[The expert whose evidence gives the chance] puts it at [the percentage], better than even, so [P] proves ‘[the negligent wrong]’ was ‘a necessary condition of the occurrence of the harm’ (CLA s 5D(1)(a)) and discharges the onus (CLA s 5E). Tabet is distinguishable: this is no claim for the lost chance of a better medical outcome, generally not recoverable where the chance falls short of even. Malec touches only [the future loss], discounted by its own percentage as a matter of quantum, not causation. Therefore, the better view is that the allegation stands.”

Causation fails only for the allegation that rested on the chance: if D is alleged to have done or omitted anything else → 1 and run it separately · a conjunctive factor is also present → B2 · Otherwise the run ends · The same divide governs contingencies in damages

B6 NAI – 3rd party: subsequent medical treatment · when the act said to break the chain is medical treatment or advice for the injury D caused

“Where [P] ‘acts reasonably in seeking or accepting the treatment’, ‘negligence in the administration of the treatment need not be regarded as a novus actus interveniens’ (Mahony), the original injury carrying ‘some risk that medical treatment might be negligently given’. The chain breaks only where the treatment is ‘inexcusably bad’, ‘a gratuitous aggravation of the injury’ or grossly negligent (Mahony). Therefore, ‘[the treatment]’ [is, or is not] of that order.”

A · chain unbroken

  “[P] ‘acts reasonably in seeking or accepting the treatment’, [the steps P took to get treated] being reasonable because [why those steps were reasonable], so ‘negligence in the administration of the treatment need not be regarded as a novus actus interveniens’ (Mahony), the original injury carrying ‘some risk that medical treatment might be negligently given’. [What the treating party did wrong] shows ordinary clinical error, not treatment ‘inexcusably bad’ or ‘a gratuitous aggravation of the injury’ or grossly negligent. Therefore, the better view is that the chain is unbroken and [D] answers for the exacerbation.”

B · chain broken

  “[The thing done in treatment] went well beyond the risk the injury exposed [P] to, adding [the further harm]; though ‘negligence in the administration of the treatment need not be regarded as a novus actus interveniens’ (Mahony), the original injury carries no risk of treatment ‘inexcusably bad’, ‘a gratuitous aggravation of the injury’ or grossly negligent, and this was of that order because [why the treatment was of that order]. Therefore, the better view is that the exacerbation results solely from the treatment, and [D] answers for the earlier damage only.”

negligent but not inexcusably bad → 5 · inexcusably bad or a gratuitous aggravation → 5 for the earlier damage only

B7 Novus Actus Interveniens: ‘the very kind of thing likely to happen’ (Chapman v Hearse) · when the act relied on is a third party’s careless act, other than medical treatment

“‘[The act complained of]’ [was the very kind of thing likely to happen, or a coincidence]: a third person’s negligent act will not break the chain ‘if the original wrongdoer should have realized that a third person might so act’ (Haber per Smith J, adopting Chapman). Chapman is [analogous, or distinguishable]: Chapman’s negligence left Dr Cherry attending him in the roadway, dark and wet with poor visibility, and Hearse ran the doctor down, ‘the risk of injury from passing traffic’ being ‘real and substantial’ and not ‘remote and fanciful’.”

A · the very kind of thing likely

  “‘[The act complained of]’ was the very kind of thing likely to happen once [D] left [P] in [the position]: [the danger D created] meant [D] ‘should have realized that a third person might so act’, and such an act does not break the chain (Haber per Smith J, adopting Chapman). Chapman is analogous: Chapman’s negligence left Dr Cherry in the roadway and Hearse ran him down, ‘the risk of injury from passing traffic’ being ‘real and substantial’, as [the like risk here] was. Therefore, the better view is that there is no NAI.”

B · a coincidence

  “‘[The act complained of]’ was a coincidence: [the separate origin of the intervening act] had nothing to do with the danger [D] created, and [D] could not have ‘realized that a third person might so act’ (Haber per Smith J, adopting Chapman) because [why D could not have foreseen this intervention]. Chapman is distinguishable: Dr Cherry stood in a dark, wet roadway where ‘the risk of injury from passing traffic’ was ‘real and substantial’ and not ‘remote and fanciful’, whereas [the position here] carried no such risk. Therefore, the better view is that the chain is severed.”

C · Chapman v Hearse both ways

  “Chapman is analogous because [D], like Chapman, who left Dr Cherry in the roadway before Hearse ran him down, exposed [P] to [the third party]’s carelessness, ‘the risk of injury from passing traffic’ there being ‘real and substantial’; yet [the fact cutting the other way] tells against it, sitting closer to a voluntary act or a coincidence (Haber per Smith J). Therefore, the better view is that ‘[the act complained of]’ was the very kind of thing likely to happen, though the point is arguable.”

D should have realised a third person might so act → 5 · otherwise → 5 for the earlier damage only

B8 NAI: P’s own act · when the act relied on is P’s own act after the injury D caused

“[P]’s own act breaks the chain only if voluntary and unreasonable. An instinctive act in the agony of the moment is not voluntary (Caterson), and ‘if the act relied on is not the conscious act of a sane person it does not break the chain’ (Haber per Lowe J), the suicide there, following severe brain damage and depression, severing nothing. By contrast, in McKew the chain was severed by the claimant’s decision to descend a steep stairway with no handrail, without seeking help, knowing his leg had previously given way, and to jump to save himself. Thus ‘[P’s act]’ answers to [Haber or Caterson, or to McKew].”

A · not voluntary

  “‘[P’s act]’ was not voluntary: [P’s condition or situation at the time] left her no real choice, whether as an instinctive act in the agony of the moment (Caterson) or because ‘if the act relied on is not the conscious act of a sane person it does not break the chain’ (Haber per Lowe J), the suicide in Haber severing nothing. McKew is distinguishable: its claimant chose the steep, handrail-less stairway knowing his leg had given way before, whereas [why P’s hand was forced]. Therefore, the better view is that the chain holds.”

B · voluntary and unreasonable

  “‘[P’s act]’ was voluntary and unreasonable: [what P knew and did before acting] shows a deliberate choice made with [the safer alternative available to P] open. McKew is analogous, the chain there severed by the claimant’s decision to descend a steep stairway with no handrail, without seeking help, knowing his leg had previously given way, and ‘[P’s act]’ was a deliberate choice of that order; this was no instinctive act in the agony of the moment (Caterson), and Haber is distinguishable, its suicide not being ‘the conscious act of a sane person’ (Lowe J). Therefore, the better view is that ‘[P’s act]’ severs the chain.”

C · McKew both ways

  “McKew is analogous because [P], like the claimant who descended a steep stairway with no handrail, without help, knowing his leg had previously given way, went on with [P’s act] though [the safer alternative available to P] was open; yet [the fact cutting the other way] weakens this: an instinctive act in the agony of the moment is not voluntary (Caterson), and an act not ‘the conscious act of a sane person’ does not sever (Haber per Lowe J). Therefore, the better view is that ‘[P’s act]’ answers to McKew, though the contrary is open.”

not voluntary → 5 · voluntary and unreasonable → 5 for the earlier damage only · P’s carelessness before the injury is contributory negligence, run in Defences

B9 Supervening events: Baker v Willoughby; Jobling v Associated Dairies · when what intervened was not an act but a later, independent event that overtook the injury

“‘[The later event]’ [is a second tort on the Baker footing, or a vicissitude]: ‘no general, logical or universally fair rules can be stated’ for supervening events, ‘whether due to tortious, partially tortious, non-culpable or wholly accidental events’ (Jobling per Lord Wilberforce). In Baker the plaintiff, his leg already injured by the defendant, was shot in that leg in an armed robbery and lost it, and the supervening tort was disregarded in assessing the first defendant’s damages; in Jobling a supervening illness was not disregarded, the court accepting ‘the “vicissitudes” argument as the best available’.”

A · a second tort on the Baker footing

  “‘[The later event]’ is a second tort: [how the later event came about] shows [the third party]’s own wrong rather than the ordinary course of life. Baker is analogous: the plaintiff there, his leg already injured, was shot in the same leg in an armed robbery and lost it, yet the supervening tort was disregarded in assessing the first defendant’s damages, as ‘[the later event]’ should be here, and Jobling, accepting ‘the “vicissitudes” argument’ for a supervening illness, does not displace that. Therefore, [D] answers for [the loss] as it stood.”

B · a vicissitude

  “‘[The later event]’ is a vicissitude: [the medical evidence on P’s condition] shows [the condition] would have overtaken [P] regardless of [D]. Jobling is analogous, the supervening myelopathy there not being disregarded, ‘no general, logical or universally fair rules’ being available for supervening events ‘whether due to tortious, partially tortious, non-culpable or wholly accidental events’ (Lord Wilberforce), ‘the “vicissitudes” argument’ being ‘the best available’; Baker is distinguishable, the later event there being itself a second tort. Therefore, the better view is that damages are reduced accordingly.”

C · Baker both ways

  “Baker is analogous because ‘[the later event]’, like the shooting in an armed robbery that took the already-injured leg yet was disregarded in assessing the first defendant’s damages, was a second tort visited on [P] by [the third party]; yet [the fact cutting the other way] points the other way: it would have overtaken her regardless, and Jobling declined to disregard a supervening illness, ‘no general, logical or universally fair rules’ being available (Lord Wilberforce). Therefore, the better view is that it falls on the Baker footing, though not without difficulty.”

Either way → 5

B10 Qualification: Precise manner in which harm occurs and/or extent of harm do not need to be foreseeable · when the harm came about in an unusual way, or is of unusual extent, but is of a foreseeable kind

“Hughes supplies both limbs of this qualification: an eight-year-old boy was severely burnt when a paraffin lamp left beside an unguarded manhole fell in and its escaping vapour unexpectedly exploded. That ‘[the harm]’ is greater than expected does not assist [D]: ‘a defender is liable, although the damage may be a good deal greater in extent than was foreseeable’ (Lord Reid 845). Nor does the odd mechanism, the accident being ‘caused by a known source of danger, but caused in a way which could not have been foreseen’, which ‘affords no defence’ (Lord Reid 845). [D] ‘can only escape liability if the damage can be regarded as differing in kind from what was foreseeable’ (Lord Reid 845), Lord Pearce (857) asking whether ‘[t]he accident was but a variant of the foreseeable’. ‘[The harm]’ [is such a variant, or differs in kind].”

A · a variant of the foreseeable

  “‘[The mechanism]’ is an odd route to the foreseeable harm: [what connects this harm to the foreseeable risk]. Hughes is analogous, the accident there ‘caused by a known source of danger, but caused in a way which could not have been foreseen’, which ‘affords no defence’, and ‘a defender is liable, although the damage may be a good deal greater in extent than was foreseeable’ (Lord Reid 845). Therefore, the better view is that ‘[the harm]’ is ‘but a variant of the foreseeable’ (Lord Pearce 857).”

B · differs in kind

  “‘[The mechanism]’ is not merely an unusual route: [what connects this harm to a different risk] shows the injury came of [the different risk] altogether, and [D] ‘can only escape liability if the damage can be regarded as differing in kind from what was foreseeable’ (Lord Reid 845), no foreseeable risk covering ‘[the harm]’. Hughes is distinguishable: the burns there were the foreseeable injury reached by an odd route, ‘but a variant of the foreseeable’ (Lord Pearce 857). Therefore, the better view is that ‘[the harm]’ differs in kind and is too remote.”

If unusual only in manner or extent, return to the chain → 4, or 5 where nothing intervened · unusual because of something in P → B11

B11 Qualification: P’s vulnerability – the eggshell skull rule · when the nature or extent of the harm is due to a susceptibility in P, or to P’s own weaknesses, beliefs and reactions

“[D] takes [P] as found, ‘with all his weaknesses, beliefs and reactions as well as his capacities and attributes, physical, social and economic’ (Nader per McHugh JA), and in ‘the family and cultural setting’ in which she lived (Kavanagh per Mason P). Foreseeability is asked of the initial injury only: once it is ‘of a kind, type or character which the defendant ought reasonably to have foreseen as a real risk’, the link to the ultimate consequences is forged ‘simply as one of cause and effect’ (Stephenson per Richmond J). Therefore, ‘[P’s condition]’ [is not too remote, or is].”

A · not too remote

  “[P’s response or susceptibility] cannot be held against her: [D] takes [P] ‘with all his weaknesses, beliefs and reactions …’ (Nader per McHugh JA), and in her ‘family and cultural setting’ (Kavanagh). Nader is analogous, the ten-year-old’s rare Ganser Syndrome, developed partly from his family’s response, not being too remote; the initial injury here was ‘of a kind, type or character which the defendant ought reasonably to have foreseen as a real risk’ (Stephenson per Richmond J), so the consequences follow ‘as one of cause and effect’. Therefore, ‘[P’s condition]’ is not too remote.”

B · too remote

  “The eggshell skull rule presupposes an initial injury ‘of a kind, type or character which the defendant ought reasonably to have foreseen as a real risk’ (Stephenson per Richmond J), and [what the initial injury actually was] shows none, unlike the cut hand in Stephenson or the head struck alighting from the bus in Nader; ‘[the harm]’ arose instead from [the other source]. That [D] takes [P] ‘with all his weaknesses, beliefs and reactions’ (Nader per McHugh JA) does not reach so far. Therefore, the better view is that ‘[P’s condition]’ is too remote.”

the harm is psychiatric injury → B12 · Otherwise return to the chain → 4, or 5 where nothing intervened

B12 Kind of injury: injury by shock as a distinct kind (Mt Isa Mines v Pusey) · when the harm sued for is a psychiatric injury

“‘[The condition]’ [is not too remote, or is]: ‘injury by shock is treated as a distinct “kind” or class of damage’ (Pusey per Walsh J 413), and in Pusey the schizophrenia of an employee who went to the aid of a workmate electrocuted and badly burned was unforeseeable, yet nervous shock was not. Liability does not require that ‘the precise character of the damage or the extent of it’ be foreseen (Walsh J 413).”

A · not too remote

  “[What P was exposed to or witnessed] made psychiatric injury a real risk to anyone [in P’s position], and ‘injury by shock is treated as a distinct “kind” or class of damage’ (Pusey per Walsh J 413). Pusey is analogous: the schizophrenia there was itself unforeseeable, yet nervous shock was not, as ‘[the condition]’ stands to the injury by shock foreseeable here, and liability does not require that ‘the precise character of the damage or the extent of it’ be foreseen (Walsh J 413). Therefore, the better view is that ‘[the condition]’ is not too remote.”

B · too remote

  “[What P actually experienced] shows no shock at all, and ‘injury by shock is treated as a distinct “kind” or class of damage’ (Pusey per Walsh J 413): that liability does not require ‘the precise character of the damage or the extent of it’ to be foreseen cannot bridge a difference of kind, and ‘[the condition]’ answers no foreseeable kind of damage here because [why no foreseeable kind covers it]. Pusey is distinguishable, nervous shock there being foreseeable even though schizophrenia was not. Therefore, the better view is that ‘[the condition]’ is too remote.”

C · Pusey both ways

  “Pusey is analogous because ‘[the condition]’, like the schizophrenia that was unforeseeable where nervous shock was not, is an unforeseeable form of a foreseeable kind, [what P was exposed to or witnessed] making injury by shock a real risk; yet [the fact cutting the other way] points the other way, shock being ‘a distinct “kind” or class of damage’ (Walsh J 413) and [P] arguably suffering none. Therefore, the better view is that ‘[the condition]’ is not too remote, though close.”

If injury by shock was a foreseeable kind of harm, return to the chain → 4, or 5 where nothing intervened · If the psychiatric harm differs in kind from what was foreseeable, it is too remote; → 5

B13 Is the ‘but for’ test the correct approach? (March v Stramare) · when two or more acts or events would each be sufficient to bring about P’s injury, or a mechanical but for answer looks unacceptable

“[The competing acts or events] present the ‘well-known difficulty’ arising where there are ‘two or more acts or events which would each be sufficient to bring about the plaintiff’s injury’ (March per Mason CJ). In March a truck was parked in the middle of a six-lane road in the early hours of the morning, parking and hazard lights on, unloaded by forklift as had long been the practice, and the speeding, alcohol-affected plaintiff collided with it. Mason CJ held that the but for test, ‘applied as an exclusive criterion of causation, yields unacceptable results’ which ‘must be tempered by the making of value judgments’, while McHugh J warned that directions to use common sense ‘are invitations to use subjective, unexpressed and undefined extra-legal values to determine legal liability’. The CLA takes the second course: s 5D(1)(a) makes the but for test the test of factual causation, value judgments belonging to scope of liability (Adeels [43]). So s 5D(1)(a) [is, or is not] satisfied, [D]’s wrong [completing, or not completing] a set of conditions jointly sufficient to account for the occurrence of the harm (Strong [20]).”

A · value judgment supplies causation

  “[The competing acts or events] each answer for the harm, [what each act would have brought about alone]: the ‘well-known difficulty’ of two or more sufficient acts (March per Mason CJ), where the but for test, ‘applied as an exclusive criterion of causation, yields unacceptable results’ and ‘must be tempered by the making of value judgments’. March is analogous: the negligently parked truck remained a cause notwithstanding the speeding, alcohol-affected driving that met it, as [D]’s wrong remains a cause notwithstanding [the other sufficient act or event]; under the CLA the wrong was ‘necessary to complete a set of conditions that are jointly sufficient’ (Strong [20]), value judgments belonging to scope of liability (Adeels [43]). Therefore, the better view is that s 5D(1)(a) is satisfied for this wrong.”

B · but for governs, causation fails

  “[The competing acts or events] raise no ‘well-known difficulty’: there is no second act or event ‘sufficient to bring about the plaintiff’s injury’ on its own, and [what shows D’s act added nothing] leaves [the other cause] alone sufficient. The CLA follows McHugh J’s warning against ‘subjective, unexpressed and undefined extra-legal values’ (March), so the but for test of s 5D(1)(a) governs and value judgments are kept to scope of liability (Adeels [43]). Therefore, the better view is that causation fails for this wrong.”

the but for test still fails → B2 for s 5D(2) · it is met notwithstanding → 2

DEFENCES: SCAFFOLD

0 Introduction to Defences

“[D] pleads [the defence pleaded], a defence being ‘a rule that prevents liability from arising even if all of the elements of the tort in which the plaintiff sues are present or that reduces a successful plaintiff’s entitlement to damages’ (SVE 677): a complete defence completely absolves [D]’s liability to pay damages. A partial defence, contributory negligence (‘CN’) being the paradigm, enables apportionment of liability between [P] and [D]. [P] having proved duty, breach and causation, the onus shifts to [D] to establish the defence on the balance of probabilities, the CLA having amended the common law defences and introduced new ones.”

A · a complete defence pleaded

  “[D] pleads [the complete defence pleaded], which if made out completely absolves it of liability to pay damages, unlike contributory negligence (‘CN’), which only enables apportionment of liability between [P] and [D] (SVE 677). [P] having proved duty, breach and causation, the onus shifts to [D] to establish the defence on the balance of probabilities, the CLA having amended the common law defence and added statutory bars beyond it; the defence is tested in full before any damages are assessed.”

B · contributory negligence (a partial defence) pleaded

  “[D] pleads contributory negligence (‘CN’), a partial defence that only enables apportionment of liability between [P] and [D] rather than absolving [D] outright (SVE 677). [P] having proved duty, breach and causation, the onus shifts to [D] to establish it on the balance of probabilities, and three steps then follow: fault (CLA s 5R and the common law), causation (CLA s 5D and the common law), and apportionment (CLA s 5S; Law Reform (Miscellaneous Provisions) Act 1965 (NSW) (‘LR(MP)A’) s 9; Montfroy).”

contributory negligence is pleaded → 1 · voluntary assumption of risk is pleaded → 4 · a dangerous recreational activity is pleaded → 5, whether or not volenti is also pleaded (CLA s 5J(2)) · a defence with any factual foothold that plainly fails → raise and dismiss it in one sentence, never omit it

1 Step 1: Did P take reasonable care for their own safety? (Fault)

“The risk of harm to herself was [the risk of harm to P herself, stated as particularly as at breach], and [P] ‘[P’s act or omission, the precaution she failed to take]’. CN was once a complete defence, Bayley J holding a plaintiff riding ‘as fast as his horse could go’ through the streets of Derby to have suffered his accident ‘entirely from his own fault’ (Butterfield). No longer: [D] must now show her ‘contributorily negligent in failing to take precautions against the risk of that harm’ on negligence principles (CLA s 5R(1)). Firstly, the relevant risk must be identified, as at breach (Hallmark [27]). Secondly, her standard is that of ‘a reasonable person in the position of that person’ (CLA s 5R(2)(a)), objective, excluding idiosyncrasy: ‘no allowances are made if the plaintiff is, for example, dim-witted, clumsy, forgetful, intoxicated or impulsive’ (SVE [14.25]). The s 5B(2) considerations apply to her as they applied to [D], operating differentially between the parties but to the same result (Boral Bricks (No 2) [99]). Thirdly, the matter is determined ‘on the basis of what that person knew or ought to have known at the time’ (CLA s 5R(2)(b)). She [fell short, or not] because [why a reasonable person in P’s position would, or would not, have taken that precaution].”

A · CN established

  “The risk of harm to herself was [the risk of harm], and [P] ‘[P’s act or omission, the precaution she failed to take]’. Firstly, that is the relevant risk, identified as at breach (Hallmark [27]). Secondly, a reasonable person in her position would have taken that precaution (CLA s 5R(2)(a)). Thirdly, she knew or ought to have known of [the danger] at the time (CLA s 5R(2)(b)), because [what put the danger before her]. Butterfield is analogous, its plaintiff riding ‘as fast as his horse could go’ and suffering his accident ‘entirely from his own fault’. CN is likely established.”

B · no CN

  “The risk of harm to herself was [the risk of harm], and although [P] ‘[P’s act or omission]’, [D], bearing the onus, cannot show CN. Firstly, the relevant risk (Hallmark [27]) was [the risk properly framed]; secondly, a reasonable person in her position (CLA s 5R(2)(a)) would have done as she did because [why her conduct was reasonable]; thirdly, she neither knew nor ought to have known of [the danger] at the time (CLA s 5R(2)(b)). Butterfield is distinguishable, its plaintiff riding ‘as fast as his horse could go’, whereas [P] took the care he did not. CN likely fails.”

P was unfamiliar with the place or the thing → that is s 5R(2)(b) and belongs in this block: it is what she knew or ought to have known at the time · P is a child, aged, disabled or has special skill or inexperience → B1 · P met an emergency → B2; if the only precaution was costly or inconvenient → B25 · P is an employee → B3 · a rule was breached → B4 · P was intoxicated → B5, or for a motor accident → B6 · a serious offence → B7 · P was reasonable → 4, or go directly to 5 if the facts give a recreational activity · otherwise → 2

2 Step 2: If so, was P’s negligence a cause of the damage (Causation)

“Her want of care must have caused the damage, not the accident, tested at common law and under s 5D. Firstly, the damage suffered must be partly a result of her own conduct: in Froom the accident was ‘caused by the bad driving’ and the damage ‘in part by the failure of the plaintiff to wear a seat belt’. Secondly, the harm must have been reasonably foreseeable and within the class of risk to which she exposed herself by her own wrong: the worker in Jones v Livox, riding a truck towbar against instructions and crushed when another vehicle ran into the back of it, was in a ‘dangerous position’ that was ‘one of the causes of his damage’ and not ‘mere history’, unlike Denning LJ’s sportsman’s stray shot, which ‘would only be part of the history’. Thirdly, her lack of care must cause or add to the injuries. ‘[P’s conduct]’ therefore [worsened ‘[the injury]’, or did not], because [why P’s want of care did or did not make the injury worse].”

A · cause of damage

  “‘[P’s conduct]’ worsened ‘[the injury]’: firstly, [how P’s want of care made the injury worse] made the damage partly a result of her own conduct, as in Froom, the damage there caused ‘in part by the failure of the plaintiff to wear a seat belt’; secondly, [the harm] was reasonably foreseeable and within the class of risk her own wrong exposed her to, her position, like the towbar rider’s in Jones v Livox, ‘one of the causes of his damage’; thirdly, her lack of care added to the injuries. Causation is likely made out.”

B · mere history

  “‘[P’s conduct]’ did not worsen ‘[the injury]’: [why the harm would have followed unchanged] shows the damage was in no part the result of her own conduct, Froom being distinguishable because no failure of hers played the seat belt’s part there. Nor was ‘[the injury]’ within the class of risk her own wrong exposed her to: unlike the towbar rider in Jones v Livox, whose position was ‘one of the causes of his damage’, hers was ‘mere history’, as with Denning LJ’s sportsman’s shot, ‘only … part of the history’. Her lack of care added nothing, and CN fails.”

C · Froom both ways

  “Froom is analogous because [the shared fact: the precaution that would have reduced the damage], the accident there ‘caused by the bad driving’ and the damage ‘in part by the failure of the plaintiff to wear a seat belt’; yet [the fact that distinguishes this case] pushes her conduct towards ‘mere history’, as with Denning LJ’s sportsman’s shot, rather than ‘one of the causes of his damage’, as with the towbar rider in Jones v Livox. Therefore, the better view is that the analogy holds and ‘[P’s conduct]’ added to ‘[the injury]’, although finely balanced.”

P’s want of care contributed → 3 · the trigger fact is fairly arguable either way → C, then one clause stating the fallback: were causation made out, the reduction at 3 would follow · If the harm would have occurred anyway, CN fails, → 4

3 Step 3 Apportionment for CN

“[D]’s [D’s act or omission said to be a ‘wrong’] enlivens apportionment, a ‘wrong’ being an act or omission that ‘gives rise to a liability in tort in respect of which a defence of contributory negligence is available at common law’ (LR(MP)A s 8). Fault and causation found, the third step asks what reduction is just and equitable (Montfroy). [P]’s carelessness no longer defeats her claim, which ‘is not defeated by reason of the contributory negligence of the claimant’ (LR(MP)A s 9(1)(a)). Instead ‘the damages recoverable in respect of the wrong are to be reduced to such extent as the court thinks just and equitable having regard to the claimant’s share in the responsibility for the damage’ (LR(MP)A s 9(1)(b)). That exercise is a ‘question […] of proportion, of balance and relative emphasis, and of weighing different considerations’ (Pennington), the statute giving the court a wide discretion. A reduction [is, or is not] to be made.”

A · a reduction is to be made

  “[D]’s [act or omission] is a ‘wrong’, one that ‘gives rise to a liability in tort in respect of which a defence of contributory negligence is available at common law’ (LR(MP)A s 8), so apportionment is enlivened. [P]’s want of care no longer defeats her claim (s 9(1)(a)); her damages are ‘to be reduced to such extent as the court thinks just and equitable having regard to the claimant’s share in the responsibility for the damage’ (s 9(1)(b)), an exercise ‘of proportion, of balance and relative emphasis’ (Pennington). A reduction is likely to be made.”

B · apportionment not enlivened

  “[D]’s [act or omission] is no ‘wrong’: [why it gives rise to no liability in tort attracting the common law defence] leaves it outside an act or omission that ‘gives rise to a liability in tort in respect of which a defence of contributory negligence is available at common law’ (LR(MP)A s 8), so s 9(1)(b) has nothing to work upon. Nor does [P] lose by that, her claim ‘not defeated by reason of the contributory negligence of the claimant’ (s 9(1)(a)). Therefore, the better view is that no reduction is to be made.”

→ 3A (compare the parties on Podrebersek’s twin criteria) · the percentage must be defended → B22 for the full rigour · For a second defendant → B23 first, the defendants’ shares being combined for the purposes of [P]’s action against each of them

3A Podrebersek v Australian Iron and Steel Pty Ltd (1985) 59 ALJR 492 [HCA]

“[P]’s departure was [how far P fell short of the reasonable person] against [D]’s [how far D fell short], and [which party’s act mattered more in causing the damage, and why]: apportionment ‘involves a comparison both of culpability, ie of the degree of departure from the standard of care of the reasonable man … and of the relative importance of the acts of the parties in causing the damage’ (Podrebersek 494), and ‘[i]t is the whole conduct of each negligent party in relation to the circumstances of the accident which must be subjected to comparative examination’. The second criterion, the relative importance of the parties’ acts contributing to the harm, is now called causal potency, which ‘must mean … that weight be given to the comparative gravity of the risk that each party created’ (Fleming, in SVE [14.75]). In Podrebersek itself a jury found a workplace plaintiff contributorily negligent and reduced his damages by 90 per cent, and the High Court declined to disturb it. The two criteria [point the same way, or pull apart].”

A · both criteria run against P

  “[P]’s [her departure] is the graver departure, and [why her act mattered more in causing the damage] makes it the more potent cause, so both criteria run against her: apportionment compares ‘culpability, ie … the degree of departure from the standard of care of the reasonable man’ and ‘the relative importance of the acts of the parties in causing the damage’ (Podrebersek 494), on ‘the whole conduct of each negligent party’. Podrebersek is analogous because [the shared fact: how the damage was overwhelmingly of P’s own making, as it was the workplace plaintiff’s there], the jury’s 90 per cent reduction against that plaintiff standing undisturbed. Her share is likely the substantial one.”

B · both criteria run against D

  “[D]’s [its departure] is the graver departure, and [why its act mattered more in causing the damage] makes it the more potent cause, so both criteria run against it (Podrebersek 494). Causal potency gives ‘weight … to the comparative gravity of the risk that each party created’ (Fleming, in SVE [14.75]), and [D] created the graver risk; [P]’s lapse, weighed on ‘the whole conduct of each negligent party’, is slight beside it. Podrebersek is distinguishable, its plaintiff bearing 90 per cent on his own conduct. Therefore, the better view is that [P]’s share is a modest one.”

C · the two criteria pull apart

  “[P]’s [her departure] is the graver departure, yet [why D’s act mattered more in causing the damage] makes [D]’s act the more potent cause, and the criteria pull apart; both must be run, apportionment involving ‘a comparison both of culpability … and of the relative importance of the acts of the parties in causing the damage’ (Podrebersek 494), causal potency weighing ‘the comparative gravity of the risk that each party created’ (Fleming, in SVE [14.75]). Therefore, the better view is that [P]’s share is the smaller of the two, although her greater departure keeps a larger figure open.”

Where a total or near-total reduction is arguable → 3B; write 3B only then · the percentage must be defended → B22 for the full rigour, which also carries the modern pedestrian/motorist authorities and the appellate-deference principle · otherwise → 4

3B CLA – s5S

“[P]’s share is put at [the percentage reduction and the figure it produces], and s 5S decides whether so large a reduction is open at all: ‘In determining the extent of a reduction in damages by reason of contributory negligence, a court may determine a reduction of 100% if the court thinks it just and equitable to do so, with the result that the claim for damages is defeated’ (CLA s 5S), altering Wynbergen v Hoyts, which held that a court could not logically reduce damages by 100%. The Ipp Report concedes a reduction over 90% is likely to be rare in practice, yet SW v Khaja upheld a reduction of 90 to 100 per cent where the plaintiff threatened a taxi driver with a knife in an attempted robbery and was injured as the driver drove off ([103]). This [is one of those rare cases, or is not].”

A · a total or near-total reduction is open

  “[P]’s [what makes this an exceptional case] leaves her with all but the whole of the responsibility, and ‘a court may determine a reduction of 100% … with the result that the claim for damages is defeated’ (CLA s 5S), Wynbergen v Hoyts no longer barring the figure as illogical. SW v Khaja is analogous, a reduction of 90 to 100 per cent upheld where the plaintiff threatened a taxi driver with a knife in an attempted robbery ([103]). The Ipp Report’s rarity concession notwithstanding, this is arguably one of those rare cases.”

B · an ordinary case, the rarity concession holds

  “[P]’s [what keeps this an ordinary case] leaves [D] a real share of the responsibility, so no reduction approaching the whole is just and equitable (CLA s 5S); that a court ‘may determine a reduction of 100%’ does not mean it should, the Ipp Report conceding a reduction over 90% is likely to be rare in practice. SW v Khaja is distinguishable, its plaintiff injured after threatening a taxi driver with a knife in an attempted robbery ([103]), conduct of a different order from [P]’s. The reduction likely stands at [the percentage reduction and the figure it produces].”

C · 100% arguable both ways

  “[P]’s [what makes this an exceptional case] pulls towards the whole, yet [what keeps this an ordinary case] pulls back. Section 5S permits ‘a reduction of 100% if the court thinks it just and equitable to do so’, altering Wynbergen v Hoyts; SW v Khaja shows the figure is real, 90 to 100 per cent upheld against a plaintiff who threatened a taxi driver with a knife ([103]); yet the Ipp Report concedes a reduction over 90% is likely to be rare. Therefore, the better view is a reduction short of the whole, though the higher figure stays open.”

the court determines a reduction of 100% → the claim for damages is defeated and the run ends · the percentage must be defended → B22 first · otherwise → 4

4 Voluntary Assumption of Risk (complete defence)

“[P], [D] says, took ‘[the risk]’ on herself: volenti non fit injuria, there can be no injury to the willing, rests on ‘the fundamental principle of human autonomy’, that ‘a person may not complain of the consequences of his own choices’ (Reeves). Its stringent preconditions make the defence rarely successful, and it almost always travels with contributory negligence, Murray v Harringay Arena, a child struck by a puck while watching ice-hockey, volens without acting unreasonably, being the rare case without it; Division 4 exists to make it ‘easier for the defendant to establish’ (Angel). Three steps follow. Firstly, the question is whether the risk of harm was obvious (CLA s 5F). Secondly, her knowledge is tested subjectively, of [P] herself and not of the reasonable person, and of the type or kind of risk, the standard differing for obvious risks, where awareness of the general risk is presumed (CLA s 5G), and non-obvious risks, where she must have been aware of the specific risk and freely consent to it (Carey). Thirdly, her consent must be freely and voluntarily given (Carey).”

A · volenti squarely raised

  “[P] ‘[what P did in the face of the risk]’, and [D] presses volenti in full: the defence rests on ‘the fundamental principle of human autonomy’ (Reeves), rarely succeeds, and almost always travels with contributory negligence. Firstly, whether ‘[the risk]’ was obvious (CLA s 5F); secondly, her knowledge is tested subjectively, [P] herself, not the reasonable person, and of the type or kind of risk, presumed if obvious (CLA s 5G) and requiring awareness of the specific risk if not (Carey); thirdly, her consent must be an unpressured, informed choice (Carey).”

B · an exclusion or incapacity takes volenti off the table before any step is reached

  “[The statutory exclusion or incapacity] shuts volenti out before any step is reached: ‘[the accident]’ is [a motor accident, a workplace injury, or an injury taken while P was too intoxicated to be conscious of any risk], and a plaintiff so intoxicated as not to be conscious of a given risk cannot voluntarily accept it, though contributory negligence may still run (SVE [14.105]). The defence rarely succeeds even where available (Reeves), and Division 4’s purpose of making it ‘easier for the defendant to establish’ (Angel) is not reached.”

If P was intoxicated to the point of being unable to advert to the risk, no voluntary acceptance is possible, though CN may still run → B5 · a motor accident or a workplace injury is on the facts → B24 for the exclusions · → B8 (for obviousness; for knowledge → B9 if the risk was obvious or B10 if not; for consent → B11 → B29 and B30 if freedom of choice or a genuine belief the risk would not materialise is in issue; if a failure to warn is pleaded → B12; if the harm was unavoidable → B13) · → 5 (whenever the facts give a recreational activity, whether or not volenti is raised (CLA s 5J(2)); Division 5 has severed all links to volens for a dangerous recreational activity)

5 Dangerous recreational activity (complete defence)

“[P] was engaged in ‘[the activity]’ when harmed. Division 5 applies ‘only in respect of liability in negligence for harm to a person … resulting from a recreational activity engaged in by the plaintiff’, and ‘does not limit the operation of Division 4’ (CLA s 5J): where an obvious risk of a dangerous recreational activity materialises and causes harm, no liability follows (CLA ss 5F, 5K, 5L; Singh; Fallas). Three questions decide it in turn: whether the activity is a recreational activity (s 5K); whether it is a dangerous recreational activity (s 5K; Fallas; Jaber); and whether the harm resulted from the materialisation of an obvious risk of it (s 5L; 5F).”

A · the three questions engaged and walked

  “[P]’s ‘[the activity]’ answers Division 5’s gate: her harm was one ‘resulting from a recreational activity engaged in by the plaintiff’ (CLA s 5J(1)), and volenti under Division 4 remains unaffected either way (s 5J(2)). Three questions then decide the defence: recreational activity (s 5K); dangerous recreational activity (s 5K; Fallas; Jaber); and the materialisation of an obvious risk (s 5L; 5F), each a gate to the next.”

B · Division 5 not engaged on the facts pleaded

  “[The feature showing the activity or the harm falls outside the description] takes this claim outside Division 5, which applies ‘only in respect of liability in negligence for harm to a person … resulting from a recreational activity engaged in by the plaintiff’ (CLA s 5J(1)). The three questions, recreational activity, dangerous, obvious risk, are not reached, and s 5L cannot answer the claim; volenti under Division 4 remains available on its own terms, s 5J(2) leaving it unaffected.”

Ask the three questions in order: for ‘recreational activity’ → B14, then for ‘dangerous’ → B15, then for ‘obvious risk’ → B16 · a risk warning is printed → B18; if a waiver is printed → B21 · The verdict is reached only at B17

B28 Risk warnings and incapable persons (s 5M(2), s 5M(12)) · when p is an ‘incapable person’: because of young age or a physical or mental disability, she lacks the capacity to understand the risk warning…

“[P] is an incapable person, one who ‘because of the person’s young age or a physical or mental disability, lacks the capacity to understand the risk warning’ (CLA s 5M(12)), so [D] may rely on a risk warning only if she was ‘under the control of or accompanied by another person … and the risk was the subject of a risk warning to that other person’, or the warning was given ‘to a parent of the incapable person’ whether or not she was under the parent’s control or accompanied by the parent (CLA s 5M(2)). The warning may be given ‘orally or in writing (including by means of a sign or otherwise)’ (CLA s 5M(4)). ‘[The person to whom the warning was given, and P’s relationship to them]’ [satisfies s 5M(2), or does not].”

A · warning given to the right person, s 5M(2) satisfied

  “[P], because of [her young age or disability], lacked the capacity to understand the warning and is an incapable person (CLA s 5M(12)); [D] may still rely on it because [the accompanying person or parent to whom the warning was given] was the person under whose control or in whose company she was, or her parent, and the warning, ‘orally or in writing (including by means of a sign or otherwise)’ (s 5M(4)), was given to that person (s 5M(2)). No further warning to [P] herself was required.”

B · no warning to a qualifying person, s 5M(2) not satisfied

  “[P], because of [her young age or disability], is an incapable person (CLA s 5M(12)), and [why no qualifying person received the warning] shows the warning reached no person ‘under the control of or accompanied by’ her and no parent (s 5M(2)). However given, the warning does not answer [D]’s duty to a plaintiff incapable of understanding it; s 5M does not assist [D] against this plaintiff.”

s 5M(2) is satisfied → B19 · If not, [D] cannot rely on the warning against this plaintiff at all, and the run continues without it, unless a waiver is printed → B21

B27 Gross negligence and the obviousness of the risk · when d’s conduct on the facts was more than merely careless

“[D]’s conduct was [reckless, deliberate or grossly negligent], more than merely careless, and Ipp JA’s distinction is in point: ‘the risk of a person being negligent (causing harm) might be obvious, but in the same circumstances the risk of a person being grossly negligent (causing harm) might not be obvious’ (Fallas [53], quoted in Singh [45]). Against it, the Singh majority held ‘[t]he precise degree of negligence was not material’ (Basten JA [68]) and that the fact a defendant ‘recklessly’ or even ‘deliberately’ caused the contact ‘does not alter its obviousness’ (Leeming JA [137]), reasoning tied to a risk that was an ordinary incident of the contest itself. [The source of this risk] [is, or is not] of that inherent kind.”

A · gross negligence changes the characterisation

  “[D]’s [its conduct] was not merely careless, and [the source of the risk, extraneous to the ordinary conduct of the activity] was no ordinary incident of the activity, so Ipp JA’s distinction applies: ‘the risk of a person being negligent … might be obvious, but … the risk of a person being grossly negligent … might not be obvious’ (Fallas [53], in Singh [45]). Singh is distinguishable, its fall answering ‘the risk of a fall as a result of another jockey’s careless riding’ (Leeming JA [139]). The risk of gross negligence here was not obvious.”

B · the Singh majority’s reasoning applies regardless of the degree of negligence

  “[D]’s [its conduct], though [reckless or grossly negligent], created a risk that was an ordinary incident of the activity: [the source of the risk, inherent in the ordinary conduct of the activity] mirrors the racing contact in Singh, where ‘[t]he precise degree of negligence was not material’ (Basten JA [68]) and conduct ‘recklessly’ or even ‘deliberately’ causing contact ‘does not alter its obviousness’ (Leeming JA [137]). Ipp JA’s distinction (Fallas [53]) is not engaged, and the obviousness of the risk of harm stands unaffected by the degree of negligence.”

→ B17 (for the final characterisation and answer) · Singh per Leeming JA [139] is quoted once: having used it here, B17 refers back to it

B26 Was the risk that materialised an ‘obvious risk’ of that dangerous recreational activity? (s 5F) · when immediately after B16, once s 5L is engaged in form

“The risk is tested by the Singh method: identification of the risk; determination whether the risk, as identified, was obvious; and determination whether the harm resulted from the materialisation of that risk (Singh [194]). An obvious risk is one that, ‘in the circumstances, would have been obvious to a reasonable person in the position of that person’ (CLA s 5F(1)), including ‘risks that are patent or a matter of common knowledge’ (s 5F(2)). Even ‘a low probability of occurring’ does not defeat obviousness (s 5F(3)). ‘[The risk]’ [was, or was not] obvious on that description.”

A · obvious on the Singh method

  “‘[The risk]’, identified on the Singh method ([194]), was obvious: [the feature of the place, thing or activity there to be seen] made it plain to a reasonable person in her position (CLA s 5F(1)), and any ‘low probability of occurring’ does not defeat that (s 5F(3)); ‘[the injury]’ resulted from its materialisation because [the causal link between the risk of harm and the injury]. The risk was likely obvious.”

B · not obvious on the Singh method

  “‘[The risk]’, identified on the Singh method ([194]), was not one that ‘would have been obvious to a reasonable person in the position of that person’ (CLA s 5F(1)): [the defect or hazard that caused the harm] was known to [D] alone, and ‘[the injury]’ came from that concealed source rather than from any obvious risk of the activity. The risk was accordingly not obvious, and s 5L does not answer the claim.”

gross negligence is also on the facts → B27 before the final answer · otherwise → B17

B30 A genuine belief the risk would not materialise defeats consent · when p freely chose to face a perceived and appreciated risk, but may have believed it would not eventuate

“Even full appreciation of a risk does not found consent if [P] genuinely believed the risk of harm would not materialise, for a person cannot agree to accept something they honestly believe will not happen (Carey [82]), and ‘mere agreement’ doesn’t amount to voluntary acceptance of a risk (Carey). Agreement is more readily inferred where the harm follows a positive act than an omission (Carey [81]). In Suncorp v Blakeney a sixteen-year-old who got into a car with a driver he knew was drunk was not volens because he thought the driver ‘seemed alright’. In Canterbury v Taylor a cyclist who fully appreciated the risk of colliding with a footballer on a dual-use ground may well have believed the players ‘quite capable of acting without negligence’, a belief that ‘would negative the proposition that he accepted those dangers’ (Ipp AJA, in Carey [82]). Nor does a plaintiff consent to risks eliminable by another’s proper care, a golfer accepting wet grass but not another player’s careless drive (Ollier). ‘[P’s stated or inferred belief about the risk]’ [negatives consent, or does not].”

A · genuine belief the risk would not materialise

  “[P] genuinely believed ‘[the risk]’ would not materialise: [why P believed the risk would not eventuate], and a person cannot agree to accept something they honestly believe will not happen (Carey [82]), ‘mere agreement’ never amounting to voluntary acceptance (Carey). Suncorp v Blakeney is analogous, its sixteen-year-old passenger thinking his known-drunk driver ‘seemed alright’; and her harm followed [an omission rather than a positive act], making the inference of agreement harder to draw (Carey [81]). Consent is negatived.”

B · no genuine belief the risk would not eventuate

  “Nothing shows [P] genuinely believed ‘[the risk]’ would not materialise: [why P held no such belief], unlike the passenger in Suncorp v Blakeney, who thought his drunk driver ‘seemed alright’, or the cyclist in Canterbury v Taylor, who may well have believed the footballers ‘quite capable of acting without negligence’. Her harm followed ‘[the positive act by which P exposed herself]’, a positive act from which agreement is more readily inferred (Carey [81]); nor was this a risk eliminable by another’s proper care, unlike the careless drive in Ollier. Consent stands.”

If a genuine belief the risk would not materialise is made out, volenti fails; → 5 only where the facts give a recreational activity, otherwise the defences run ends · Otherwise the defence stands

B29 Consent must be an unpressured, free choice · when p adverted to and persisted in the risk, but the facts suggest her choice was constrained

“A free and voluntary choice needs not only knowledge but ‘the absence from his mind of any feeling of constraint so that nothing shall interfere with the freedom of his will’ (Bowater 479). Consent ‘is not to be implied where, notwithstanding knowledge, the person concerned has exposed himself to the danger only because of the exigency of the situation in which he stands’, for ‘[i]f he has no real or practical choice he does not voluntarily consent’ (Joyce 57 per Dixon J). Courts are reluctant to infer consent where the alternative is onerous, such as giving up employment (Smith v Charles Baker) or failing to rescue another (Haynes v Harwood), and in Standfield v Uhr a jockey whose horse shied from a tractor he saw only once the race began was in a ‘dilemma’ with ‘no real or practical choice’. ‘[P’s alternative course, if any]’ [left her a real choice, or did not].”

A · a real choice, freely made

  “[P] had a real and practical choice: ‘[the safe alternative genuinely open to her]’ was open, and no ‘feeling of constraint’ interfered with the freedom of her will (Bowater 479). Standfield v Uhr is distinguishable, its jockey discovering the tractor only once the race began; Joyce is too, [P] facing no ‘exigency of the situation’ (Dixon J 57) and no alternative as onerous as giving up employment (Smith v Charles Baker) or abandoning a rescue (Haynes v Harwood). Her choice was accordingly free.”

B · no real or practical choice

  “[P] had ‘no real or practical choice’: [the exigency or constraint she faced] exposed her to the danger ‘only because of the exigency of the situation’ she stood in (Joyce 57 per Dixon J). Standfield v Uhr is analogous, its jockey in a ‘dilemma’ once the tractor appeared mid-race; her alternative, [the onerous alternative she would otherwise have faced], was as onerous as giving up employment (Smith v Charles Baker) or abandoning a rescue (Haynes v Harwood). Consent fails for want of freedom (Bowater 479).”

a real choice was open and she took it → B30 (test whether she genuinely believed the risk would not materialise) · she had no real or practical choice → consent fails outright and the run ends, or continues on CN alone
