B25 Inconvenience of taking precautions · when the only precaution open to P was costly or inconvenient

“The only precaution open to [P] was ‘[the precaution said to have been open to P]’, and a reasonable person weighs the cost of taking safeguards against the risk of harm they guard against: she would probably use a nearby pedestrian crossing, but not if the nearest crossing is five kilometres away (SVE [14.30]). Caterson is the authority, the plaintiff’s conduct there judged reasonable ‘in the light of the inconvenience had he remained on the train’, the next stop being 80 miles away. ‘[The precaution]’ [was, or was not] disproportionately inconvenient measured against [the risk it would have avoided].”

A · the precaution was disproportionately inconvenient

  “‘[The precaution said to have been open to P]’ was disproportionately inconvenient: [the inconvenience or cost the precaution would have imposed], measured against [the risk it would have avoided], sits with the reasonable person who uses a nearby crossing but not one five kilometres away (SVE [14.30]). Caterson is analogous, the jump there reasonable ‘in the light of the inconvenience had he remained on the train’, the next stop being 80 miles distant. A reasonable person in her position would not have taken it either, and CN fails.”

B · the precaution was not disproportionately inconvenient

  “‘[The precaution said to have been open to P]’ was not disproportionately inconvenient: [the modest cost or delay the precaution actually involved] falls well short of a five-kilometre detour to the nearest crossing (SVE [14.30]). Caterson is distinguishable, its plaintiff’s choice reasonable only because the next stop was 80 miles away, whereas [why P’s alternative was readily available] leaves no comparable inconvenience. A reasonable person in her position would have taken the precaution.”

If P was reasonable, CN fails, → 4, or to 5 if the facts give a recreational activity · otherwise → 2

B1 Step 1-Reasonable Person · when the facts give P’s age, a physical or intellectual disability, or a special skill or inexperience

“[P] is [a child, aged, disabled, specially skilled or inexperienced], and the reasonable person in her position is adjusted accordingly. A child is judged as a ‘child of similar age, intelligence and experience’ (McHale 229 per Owen J), an approach Campbell J took for a nine-year-old’s intellectual disability (Goldsmith v Bisset (No 3)); in Doubleday a seven-year-old who ignored instructions and climbed onto a trampoline in roller skates was not unreasonable by the standard of the reasonable child of the same age. Physical disabilities of old age are taken into account, though even an 83-year-old with limited sight should first look before crossing the road (Smith v Zhang [22], [32]); intellectual disability in an adult does not modify the standard (Hodder (No 2)). A plaintiff with special skills is tested by the ordinarily skilled person in those circumstances, and an inexperienced and unsophisticated one as such (Cam & Bear [83]). She [answers the adjusted standard, or falls short].”

A · child, disability or inexperience allowance answered

  “[P], [her age or disability], is judged as a ‘child of similar age, intelligence and experience’ (McHale 229 per Owen J), or with her [intellectual disability, as in Goldsmith v Bisset (No 3), or physical disability of age, as in Smith v Zhang [22]] taken into account, and [her conduct] is what such a person would do. Doubleday is analogous, its seven-year-old’s trampoline play in roller skates not unreasonable by the standard of the reasonable child of the same age. She answers the adjusted standard, and CN likely fails.”

B · falls short of the modified standard

  “[P], though judged as a ‘child of similar age, intelligence and experience’ (McHale 229 per Owen J), still fell short: [the hazard in front of her] was a danger even such a child would meet with [the precaution P alleges]. Doubleday is distinguishable, its seven-year-old’s play being ordinary childish risk-taking rather than a plain danger ignored; an adult’s intellectual disability does not modify the standard (Hodder (No 2)), and allowance for physical disability did not excuse the 83-year-old who failed to look (Smith v Zhang [32]). She fell short.”

C · Doubleday and the disability cases both ways

  “Doubleday is analogous because [P] too was at play, a seven-year-old on a trampoline in roller skates being not unreasonable by the reasonable child’s standard; yet [the hazard in front of her] does not sit easily with that, being a danger a child of her age would avoid, and [her special skill or experience, if any] is tested by the ordinarily skilled person (Cam & Bear [83]). Therefore, the better view is that she answers the modified standard (McHale 229), although the contrary is open.”

Write only the allowance the facts raise · → 2

B2 The ‘agony of the moment’ principle · when p met an emergency of D’s making and had no time for calm reflection

“[P] met [the emergency of D’s making], and that her decision proved poor avails [D] nothing, the reasonable person in her position being adjusted where she lacked the time for calm reflection. Caterson is analogous: given no warning that the train was about to depart, its plaintiff jumped rather than be carried 80 miles from his son on the platform, and acted reasonably. In Avram a passenger who released his seatbelt and moved away from the point of impact an instant before a high-speed collision was not contributorily negligent, his misjudgement a decision taken in the agony of the moment. And a police officer travelling at speed in a situation of some urgency might be forgiven for not observing a lowered boom gate (Grills [175]–[182]). ‘[P’s predicament at the moment she chose]’ [is comparable, or is not].”

A · agony of the moment

  “‘[P’s predicament]’ forced her choice in the heat of the moment: [the emergency D created] left no time for calm reflection, and the reasonable person in her position is adjusted accordingly. Caterson is analogous, its plaintiff, given no warning, jumping from the departing train rather than be carried 80 miles away, and acting reasonably; Avram is to like effect, the split-second seatbelt release before impact held not contributorily negligent. Her response was reasonable, and CN likely fails.”

B · time for calm reflection

  “‘[P’s predicament]’ was no agony of the moment: [the interval between the danger appearing and P acting] left her time to weigh the danger, and the adjustment applies only where calm reflection was impossible. Caterson is distinguishable, its plaintiff given no warning and facing an 80-mile carriage, whereas ‘[the safe course open to P]’ was open to her; unlike the instantaneous release in Avram, she had a real interval in which to choose. Her decision is judged in the ordinary way.”

C · Caterson both ways

  “Caterson is analogous because [P], like the passenger who jumped from the departing train without warning, acted on an impulse [D] created; yet [the interval before she acted] points the other way, the adjustment holding only where she lacked time for calm reflection. Grills shows the allowance can survive urgency short of a true emergency, the police officer there forgiven for missing a lowered boom gate ([175]–[182]). On balance her decision was made in the heat of the moment, though not without difficulty.”

If P was reasonable, CN fails, → 4, or to 5 if the facts give a recreational activity · otherwise → 2

B3 Employees and employers · when p sues her employer for a workplace injury

“[P] sues her employer and is judged by the conditions of her work: allowance is made for momentary lapses of attention in the performance of repetitive work in distracting conditions, and none is made against employees who have no choice but to comply with the employer’s system of work (Williams v Metcash, no choice as to the location of boxes though a choice in lifting additional ones; Rail Corp v Donald, casual labourers not expected to provide their own system of work). The counter is Kennedy v Queensland Alumina, a worker trained in safety procedures who without excuse did not follow them suffering a 50% reduction; against it, VW v Probuild found no CN where training or communication about the particular risk was lacking. Claims by employees against employers for workplace injuries are in any event generally exempt from the CLA (s 3B(1)(f)). The allowance [is, or is not] made because [why the conditions of P’s work do or do not excuse her lapse].”

A · allowance made, no reduction

  “[P]’s ‘[her lapse]’ was a momentary lapse of attention in repetitive work in distracting conditions, [the repetitive task and the distracting condition], and no reduction is made against an employee who had no choice but to comply with the employer’s system of work (Williams v Metcash; Rail Corp v Donald). Kennedy is distinguishable, its worker trained in safety procedures departing from them without excuse; [P], as in VW v Probuild, was given no training on [the particular risk]. The allowance is likely made, the CLA not governing in any event (s 3B(1)(f)).”

B · no allowance, reduction stands

  “[P]’s ‘[her departure from procedure]’ was a considered departure, not inattention: she was trained in [the safety procedure] and, unlike the casual labourers in Rail Corp v Donald, was not bound to the employer’s system without choice. Kennedy is analogous, its trained worker departing from safety procedures without excuse and suffering a 50% reduction; VW v Probuild does not assist her, training here having been given. No allowance is likely made, the CLA not governing in any event (s 3B(1)(f)).”

C · Kennedy both ways

  “Kennedy is analogous because [P], like the worker there, was trained in safety procedures and departed from them; yet [the repetitive task and the distracting condition] tells against it, her departure being the very lapse of attention that repetitive work in distracting conditions produces, and, as in Rail Corp v Donald, she had no real choice but to comply with the employer’s system of work. Therefore, the better view is that the allowance is made and any reduction is modest, although finely balanced.”

→ 2

B4 Failure to comply with safety regulations · when the facts print a statute, regulation, road rule or sign P contravened

“[P] ‘[P’s act or omission, the precaution she failed to take]’ contrary to ‘[the rule]’, and safety regulations may suggest how the reasonable person in her position would have behaved though they are not determinative, breach of a road rule being evidence of negligence (Verryt [4]). The community standard is such that it is nearly always unreasonable to fail to wear a seatbelt (SVE [14.30]). Froom suggests reducing damages by 25% where a seatbelt would have prevented the damage altogether and by 15% where the failure made ‘a considerable difference’. That guideline is not formally adopted in Australia. The reduction is not avoided simply because [P] was panicked or distressed, though Allen v Chadwick leaves open that it might not apply if her failure was truly involuntary, as where the driver’s own dangerous conduct prevented her from wearing it. The breach [makes her departure plain, or carries little weight].”

A · departure made plain

  “[P] ‘[her omission]’ contrary to ‘[the rule]’, which states what a reasonable person in her position would have done, breach of a road rule being evidence of negligence though not determinative (Verryt [4]); [how the harm was of the kind the rule guards against] leaves the rule’s protection squarely engaged. Failing to wear a seatbelt is nearly always unreasonable, Froom suggesting 25% where the belt would have prevented the damage and 15% where the failure made ‘a considerable difference’; her failure was voluntary, not one forced by the driver’s conduct (Allen v Chadwick). Her departure is plain.”

B · breach carries little weight

  “[P] ‘[her omission]’ contrary to ‘[the rule]’, yet the breach carries little weight: it is evidence of negligence only, not determinative (Verryt [4]), and [the cause of the harm, independent of the rule] made compliance beside the point, the harm following regardless. Nor is the seatbelt standard engaged: [why her failure was involuntary, the driver’s own dangerous conduct preventing it] enlivens Allen v Chadwick’s qualification, and Froom’s 25%/15% guideline, never formally adopted in Australia, does not apply.”

C · Froom both ways

  “Froom is analogous because ‘[the rule]’ addresses the very damage suffered and [P] failed to comply with it, the case suggesting 25% where a seatbelt would have prevented the damage and 15% where the failure made ‘a considerable difference’; yet the guideline was never formally adopted here, and breach of a road rule is evidence of negligence only (Verryt [4]). On balance [the evidence that her omission at most reduced the harm] guides towards the lower figure, [P] not having been prevented from complying (Allen v Chadwick), though the point is arguable.”

→ 2

B5 NB s 50: Intoxication and presumed contributory negligence · when p was intoxicated, ‘under the influence of alcohol or a drug (whether or not taken for a medicinal purpose and whether or not lawfully…

“[P] was ‘[the quoted words showing P’s intoxication]’, and s 50 reaches a person who at the time of the act or omission was ‘intoxicated to the extent that the person’s capacity to exercise reasonable care and skill was impaired’ (CLA s 50(1)). Two consequences follow in order. Firstly, a court ‘is not to award damages … unless satisfied that the death, injury or damage to property (or some other injury or damage to property) is likely to have occurred even if the person had not been intoxicated’ (s 50(2)): unless [P] shows that much, she recovers nothing. Secondly, once the court is so satisfied, ‘it is to be presumed that the person was contributorily negligent unless the court is satisfied that the person’s intoxication did not contribute in any way to the cause of the death, injury or damage’ (s 50(3)), the reduction being ‘25% or a greater percentage determined by the court to be appropriate in the circumstances of the case’ (s 50(4)). The section does not apply where the intoxication was not self-induced (s 50(5)). Nor does drink soften her standard, which is that of an ordinary sober person (Joslyn).”

A · she clears s 50(2), and the 25% presumption then applies

  “[P], ‘[the quoted intoxication words]’, was ‘intoxicated to the extent that the person’s capacity to exercise reasonable care and skill was impaired’ (CLA s 50(1)). She clears the first gate, [the reason the harm would have happened anyway] showing the harm ‘is likely to have occurred even if the person had not been intoxicated’ (s 50(2)); that finding engages the presumption of CN (s 50(3)), her drinking, [how the drinking impaired her care], having contributed in some way to the cause, and the reduction is ‘25% or a greater percentage determined by the court to be appropriate’ (s 50(4)).”

B · she fails s 50(2), and recovers nothing

  “[P], ‘[the quoted intoxication words]’, cannot show the harm ‘is likely to have occurred even if the person had not been intoxicated’ (CLA s 50(2)): [the reason sobriety would have saved her] makes the drink the difference, and a court ‘is not to award damages’ unless so satisfied. Her claim is barred outright, and the presumption in s 50(3) is never reached; her standard was in any event that of an ordinary sober person (Joslyn).”

C · s 50 does not reach her at all

  “[P], though ‘[the quoted intoxication words]’, falls short of the gate: [the impairment fact] does not show her ‘intoxicated to the extent that the person’s capacity to exercise reasonable care and skill was impaired’ (CLA s 50(1)), so neither the bar in s 50(2) nor the presumption in s 50(3) is engaged, and her drinking runs only as want of care for her own safety, judged against the ordinary sober person (Joslyn).”

the court is not satisfied under s 50(2) → no damages and the run ends · it is → 3 with the presumed reduction of 25% or more · If the facts also raise volenti, note that a plaintiff too intoxicated to be conscious of the risk cannot voluntarily accept it, though CN under this block may still stand → 4

B6 Intoxication and motor accidents · when the harm is a motor accident and intoxication is raised

“The harm arises from a motor accident, and of Part 6 only ‘section 49 (Effect of intoxication on duty and standard of care)’ applies to motor accidents (CLA s 3B(2)(f)), so s 50 does not reach the award. Under s 49(1), ‘a person is not owed a duty of care merely because the person is intoxicated’ (s 49(1)(b)), and ‘the fact that a person is or may be intoxicated does not of itself increase or otherwise affect the standard of care owed to the person’ (s 49(1)(c)). Part 7, including s 54, still applies to motor accidents (s 3B(2)(g)), as do ‘Divisions 1–4 and 8 of Part 1A’ (s 3B(2)(a)), but not Division 5 (ss 5J–5N). The intoxication accordingly runs only as want of care under s 5R.”

A · s 50 excluded, s 49 governs

  “‘[The accident]’ is a motor accident, so of Part 6 only ‘section 49’ applies (CLA s 3B(2)(f)) and s 50 does not reach the award: [P] ‘is not owed a duty of care merely because the person is intoxicated’, and her intoxication ‘does not of itself increase or otherwise affect the standard of care’ (s 49(1)(b), (c)). Part 7 and Divisions 1–4 and 8 of Part 1A still apply (s 3B(2)(a), (g)); Division 5 does not. Her drinking runs only as want of care under s 5R, [P’s conduct apart from her drinking] supplying that.”

B · not a motor accident, s 50 applies

  “‘[The accident]’ is not a motor accident: [what puts the accident outside that description], so s 3B(2)(f)’s confinement of Part 6 to s 49 has no work, and s 50 reaches this award in full, the bar in s 50(2) and the presumption in s 50(3) both running. Section 49(1)’s principles about duty and standard of care have nothing to add outside s 3B(2)’s list.”

→ 1 (run the intoxication as want of care) · a serious offence is also on the facts → B7, which survives a motor accident via s 3B(2)(g)

B7 Criminals not to be awarded damages (s 54) · when p’s harm occurred at the time of, or following, conduct of P that may be a serious offence

“[P]’s ‘[the conduct]’ raises s 54, which bars the award on two conditions. Firstly, her injury must have ‘occurred at the time of, or following, conduct of that person that, on the balance of probabilities, constitutes a serious offence’ (CLA s 54(1)(a)), a serious offence being one ‘punishable by imprisonment for 6 months or more’ (s 54(3)). Secondly, that conduct must have ‘contributed materially to the death, injury or damage or to the risk of death, injury or damage’ (s 54(1)(b)). The bar does not apply where [D]’s own conduct ‘constitutes an offence (whether or not a serious offence)’ (s 54(2)), and it operates whether or not [P] ‘has been, will be or is capable of being proceeded against or convicted’ of the offence (s 54(5)). ‘[The conduct]’ [is such an offence, or is not].”

A · serious offence bars damages

  “‘[The conduct]’ constitutes a serious offence on the balance of probabilities, [the maximum penalty the offence carries] making it ‘punishable by imprisonment for 6 months or more’ (CLA s 54(1)(a), (3)), and [how the conduct contributed to the injury or the risk of it] shows it ‘contributed materially to the … injury or damage or to the risk of’ it (s 54(1)(b)). [D]’s own conduct is no offence, so s 54(2) does not lift the bar, and that [P] may never be prosecuted is irrelevant (s 54(5)). Her damages are likely barred.”

B · no serious offence, or no material contribution, no bar

  “Neither condition is met. Firstly, ‘[the conduct]’, carrying [the maximum penalty the offence carries], is not ‘punishable by imprisonment for 6 months or more’ and so is no ‘serious offence’ (CLA s 54(1)(a), (3)); secondly, and independently, [the independent cause of the harm] shows the conduct did not contribute ‘materially to the … injury or damage or to the risk of’ it (s 54(1)(b)). Further, [D]’s own conduct constituting an offence would disapply the section in any event (s 54(2)). Her damages stand.”

s 54 bites → no damages and the run ends · otherwise → 1 · The section applies to a motor accident (CLA s 3B(2)(g))

B8 Step 1: Obvious Risk? s 5F CLA · when voluntary assumption of risk is raised and the risk must be characterised

“‘[The risk]’ must first be characterised: it was an obvious risk only if, ‘in the circumstances’, it ‘would have been obvious to a reasonable person in the position of that person’ (CLA s 5F(1)). Obvious risks ‘include risks that are patent or a matter of common knowledge’ (s 5F(2)), and neither ‘a low probability of occurring’ (s 5F(3)) nor being ‘not prominent, conspicuous or physically observable’ (s 5F(4)) negates obviousness. Diving off a bridge into water nine metres below beside a ‘no diving’ sign was obvious even to a fourteen-year-old boy (Dederer); the raised lip of a concrete slab obscured by shade, invisible until a stride away, was not (Angel). It [was patent, or was concealed].”

A · risk patent

  “‘[The risk]’ was ‘obvious to a reasonable person in the position of that person’ (CLA s 5F(1)): [the feature of the place or thing there to be seen] put it plainly before anyone [in P’s position: her age, experience and familiarity with the place], as diving off a bridge into water nine metres below beside a ‘no diving’ sign was obvious even to a fourteen-year-old boy (Dederer), and its low probability or want of prominence does not negate that (s 5F(3), (4)). The risk of harm was patent.”

B · risk concealed

  “‘[The risk]’ was not obvious: [what concealed the risk from view] hid it from a reasonable person in [P]’s position (CLA s 5F(1)), as the raised slab obscured by shade in Angel was not obvious until a stride away, unlike the plainly visible danger in Dederer. That a risk of low probability or want of prominence may still be obvious (s 5F(3), (4)) does not answer true concealment. The risk of harm was arguably concealed.”

Write only the sub-section the facts raise · the risk was obvious → B9; if not obvious → B10 · a failure to warn is pleaded → B12 · the harm was unavoidable → B13

B9 Step 2: Knowledge: if risk is obvious: presumed knowledge (s 5G(1)) of type/kind (s 5G(2)) · when the risk that materialised was obvious

“The risk of harm being obvious, [P] ‘is presumed to have been aware of the risk of harm …, unless the person proves on the balance of probabilities that he or she was not aware of the risk’ (CLA s 5G(1)), an anomalous reversal of onus of little practical significance, since at common law a plaintiff who denied knowing of an obvious risk would not likely be believed, though it might be otherwise where an obstruction was obscured by glare or shade (SVE [14.120]). Awareness remains subjective, and general awareness suffices: she need only be aware of ‘the type or kind of risk’, not ‘the precise nature, extent or manner of occurrence of the risk’ (s 5G(2)); a cyclist need only know a bicycle wheel might jam in an old bridge’s cracks, not the precise mechanism of the fall (Collins). ‘[P’s own account]’ leaves the presumption [standing, or discharged].”

A · presumption stands

  “[P] is presumed aware of ‘[the risk]’ (CLA s 5G(1)) and cannot discharge the presumption on the balance of probabilities: [why P’s denial of awareness is not credible], nothing resembling the glare or shade that might make it otherwise (SVE [14.120]). Awareness is subjective but general awareness suffices: [P’s prior exposure to this type or kind of risk] gave her awareness of ‘the type or kind of risk’, the precise mechanism being unnecessary (s 5G(2); Collins). The presumption stands.”

B · presumption discharged

  “[P] discharges the presumption: [why P’s denial of awareness is made out] proves on the balance of probabilities she ‘was not aware of the risk’ (CLA s 5G(1)), her position resembling the obstruction obscured by glare or shade that makes such a denial credible (SVE [14.120]). Nor was she aware even of ‘the type or kind of risk’ (s 5G(2)); unlike the cyclist in Collins, who knew wheels could jam in an old bridge’s cracks, [P’s want of any prior exposure to this type or kind of risk] left her ignorant of it altogether. The defence fails at knowledge.”

the presumption stands → B11 · If it is rebutted, the defence fails, → 5 only where the facts give a recreational activity; otherwise the defences run ends

B10 Step 2: Knowledge: if not obvious: did P actually know and understand the specific risk? · when the risk that materialised was not obvious

“The risk of harm not being obvious, the stricter common law test applies, and it is subjective, asking what was running through [P]’s own mind and not the reasonable person’s (SVE [14.105]): [D] must prove she fully appreciated the risk of harm (Roggenkamp 300) and was ‘aware of the specific danger that materialised’, for where a plaintiff ‘was merely aware of the general class of perils to which the risk that materialised belonged, the defence would not apply’ (SVE [14.105]; Carey). Knowledge alone will not do in any event, ‘proof of knowledge is necessary but not sufficient’ (Carey [76]). She [had the specific danger in view, or knew the general danger only].”

A · full appreciation shown

  “[P] knew ‘[what P knew of the risk]’, and [why that was the specific danger] shows the specific danger that materialised was before her own mind, the test being subjective and her full appreciation proved (Roggenkamp 300; SVE [14.105]). That she knew is not alone enough, ‘proof of knowledge is necessary but not sufficient’ (Carey [76]), yet the knowledge here is of this very risk. The defence survives to consent.”

B · general danger only

  “[P] knew ‘[what P knew of the risk]’, which is knowledge of the general class of perils only: [what was never disclosed to her] was never before her mind, and where a plaintiff is ‘merely aware of the general class of perils to which the risk that materialised belonged, the defence would not apply’ (SVE [14.105]; Carey), the test being subjective and full appreciation unproved (Roggenkamp 300). The defence likely fails at knowledge.”

proved → B11 · If not proved, the defence fails, → 5 only where the facts give a recreational activity; otherwise the defences run ends

B11 Step 3: Consent · when knowledge is established

“Knowledge established, [P]’s consent asks more: ‘mere agreement’ doesn’t amount to voluntary acceptance of a risk (Carey), courts reluctant to infer it where the only alternative was to give up employment (Smith v Charles Baker), and passive participation is not enough (Rootes, whose waterskier, blinded by spray on a ‘crossover’ with no observer’s warning, collided with a stationary boat). Consent requires her actual perception and full appreciation of the risk, a conscious advertence to the possibility that the known risk might eventuate, and an unpressured, informed choice (Carey): ‘[t]o assume a risk, a plaintiff must know about the risk, he or she must turn their mind to it at the relevant time, and he or she must persist in the risky conduct regardless’ (Carey [108]), the claimant fully comprehending the extent of the risk and choosing to accept or ignore it (Woods). Carey itself failed here: its cyclist, riding an unfamiliar path, struck an obvious bollard of little practical purpose and was 50% contributorily negligent, yet not volens, because he never consciously adverted to it. ‘[P’s conduct]’ shows she [persisted regardless, or never adverted].”

A · persisted regardless

  “[P] ‘[her conduct]’ with ‘[the risk]’ before her mind: [what P saw or was told immediately before going on] shows she turned her mind to it at the relevant time and ‘persist[ed] in the risky conduct regardless’ (Carey [108]), fully comprehending the extent of the risk and choosing to accept or ignore it (Woods). This was no ‘mere agreement’ (Carey) nor passive participation (Rootes), and no alternative as onerous as giving up employment constrained her (Smith v Charles Baker). Consent is likely made out.”

B · never adverted

  “[P] never adverted to ‘[the risk]’ at the relevant time: [P’s conduct showing inattention or mere passivity] is at most passive participation (Rootes) or ‘mere agreement’ (Carey), and knowledge without conscious advertence does not make her volens. Carey is directly analogous, its cyclist striking an obvious bollard, 50% contributorily negligent yet not volens because he never consciously adverted to it; nor did she fully comprehend and accept the extent of the risk (Woods). Consent fails.”

P adverted and persisted → B29 (test whether her choice was truly free; if it was not shown to have been an unpressured choice or she may have believed the risk would not materialise → B30) · Otherwise the defence fails · Either way → 5 only where the facts give a recreational activity; otherwise the defences run ends

B12 s 5H No proactive duty to warn of obvious risk · when the risk was obvious and P’s case includes a failure to warn

“[P]’s case includes a failure to warn of ‘[the risk]’, yet [D] ‘does not owe a duty of care to another person … to warn of an obvious risk to the plaintiff’ (CLA s 5H(1)), unless [P] ‘has requested advice or information about the risk’, a written law requires the warning, or [D] is a professional and the risk is one ‘of the death of or personal injury to the plaintiff from the provision of a professional service’ (s 5H(2)). Even then, s 5H(2) ‘does not give rise to a presumption of a duty to warn’ (s 5H(3)), so any duty must be made out at common law. Dederer is analogous on obviousness, diving beside a ‘no diving’ sign into water nine metres below being obvious even to a fourteen-year-old boy. A duty to warn of ‘[the risk]’ accordingly [did not arise, or must be made out at common law].”

A · no duty to warn

  “‘[The risk]’ being obvious, [D] owed no duty ‘to warn of an obvious risk’ (CLA s 5H(1)), and no exception is engaged: [P] requested no advice or information, no written law required a warning, and [the nature of D’s service to P] involved no professional service carrying a risk of death or personal injury (s 5H(2)). Dederer is analogous, ‘[the risk]’ being as plain to [P] as the dive there was to its fourteen-year-old plaintiff. No duty to warn arose.”

B · an exception is engaged, but no duty is thereby presumed

  “[P] ‘requested advice or information about the risk’ from [D], [the subject of the request], or the risk was one of death or personal injury from [D]’s professional service, so s 5H(1)’s exclusion does not answer the claim (CLA s 5H(2)). Yet the exception ‘does not give rise to a presumption of a duty to warn’ (s 5H(3)): whether one arose is decided at common law, [what concealed or complicated the risk] bearing on it.”

C · Dederer both ways

  “Dederer is analogous because [P], like the boy for whom diving beside a ‘no diving’ sign was obvious at fourteen, [what P did in the face of plain danger]; yet [the request P made of D] tells against it, engaging the exception for requested advice or a professional service risk (CLA s 5H(2)). Therefore, the better view is that the exception is engaged and s 5H(1) does not answer the claim, though s 5H(3) means no duty to warn is presumed and any duty must be made out at common law.”

an exception applies → s 5H(1) does not answer this claim, though s 5H(3) means no duty to warn is presumed; it must be shown at common law · the risk was obvious → B9; if not obvious → B10

B13 s 5I No liability for materialisation of inherent risk · when the harm resulted from something that could not be avoided by reasonable care and skill

“‘[The risk]’ may be inherent: [D] ‘is not liable in negligence for harm suffered by another person as a result of the materialisation of an inherent risk’ (CLA s 5I(1)), an inherent risk being ‘a risk of something occurring that cannot be avoided by the exercise of reasonable care and skill’ (s 5I(2)). Being dumped while bodysurfing was inherent (at 482) and obvious (at 483) to an experienced surfer (Prast per Ipp J); but a plaintiff does not consent to risks eliminable by another’s proper care, a golfer accepting wet grass but not another player’s careless drive (Ollier). ‘[The risk]’ [could not, or could] be avoided by reasonable care.”

A · inherent risk materialised

  “‘[The risk]’ was inherent: [the feature of the activity that made the harm unavoidable] makes it ‘a risk of something occurring that cannot be avoided by the exercise of reasonable care and skill’ (CLA s 5I(2)), so [D] ‘is not liable in negligence’ for its materialisation (s 5I(1)). Prast is analogous, being dumped while bodysurfing inherent (482) and obvious (483) to an experienced surfer (per Ipp J), as [the activity]’s danger was. The claim is answered, s 5I(3) aside.”

B · risk was avoidable

  “‘[The risk]’ was avoidable by the exercise of reasonable care and skill: [the precaution that would have removed the risk] would have removed it, taking it outside ‘inherent risk’ (CLA s 5I(2)). Prast is distinguishable, the dumping wave there being the sea’s doing, whereas [D’s act or omission that created the risk] was [D]’s own; and a plaintiff does not consent to a risk eliminable by another’s proper care (Ollier). Section 5I(1) does not answer the claim.”

C · Prast both ways

  “Prast is analogous because ‘[the activity]’ carried, like being dumped while bodysurfing, a danger inherent (482) and obvious (483) to the experienced (per Ipp J); yet [the precaution that would have removed the risk] points the other way, reasonable care being able to avoid it (CLA s 5I(2)). Therefore, the better view is that the risk of harm was avoidable and s 5I(1), by which [D] ‘is not liable in negligence’ for an inherent risk’s materialisation, does not answer the claim, although finely balanced.”

the risk was inherent → D is not liable and the run ends · Otherwise, if the risk was obvious → B9; if not obvious → B10 · Note s 5I(3): it does not exclude liability in connection with a duty to warn

B14 Is the activity a ‘recreational activity’? (s 5K) · when p was engaged, when harmed, in a sport, or in a pursuit for enjoyment, relaxation or leisure, or at a beach, park or other…

“‘[The activity]’ is a ‘recreational activity’ if it answers any of three disjunctive limbs: ‘any sport (whether or not the sport is an organised activity)’; ‘any pursuit or activity engaged in for enjoyment, relaxation or leisure’; and ‘any pursuit or activity engaged in at a place (such as a beach, park or other public open space) where people ordinarily engage in sport or in any pursuit or activity for enjoyment, relaxation or leisure’ (CLA s 5K), the first directed to the characterisation of the activity, the second to the purpose of the participant, and the third to the location (Goode [190] per Leeming JA). Remuneration does not defeat the first limb, professional sport remaining ‘any sport’ (Goode, accepted in Singh [43]); and the relevant activity may be characterised very narrowly, the volunteer in Carter, operating ‘the catching pen gate’ for racing greyhounds, failing the second limb because he did it ‘to help out’, not for enjoyment ([50]). It [answers a limb, or none].”

A · recreational activity answered

  “‘[The activity]’ answers [the limb answered]: firstly, [the feature making the activity a sport] makes it ‘any sport (whether or not the sport is an organised activity)’, remuneration being irrelevant to that limb (Goode [190]; Singh [43]); or secondly, [P] engaged in it ‘for enjoyment, relaxation or leisure’, [her purpose], unlike the volunteer in Carter, who operated the catching pen gate ‘to help out’; or thirdly, it took place at [the place], ‘where people ordinarily engage in sport or … leisure’ (CLA s 5K). It is caught.”

B · outside the definition

  “‘[The activity]’ answers none of the three limbs: it is no ‘sport’ on its proper characterisation because [the feature going to the characterisation of the activity]; characterised narrowly as [the activity, so characterised], it was undertaken not ‘for enjoyment, relaxation or leisure’ but for [the task-like purpose], as the catching pen gate in Carter was operated ‘to help out’ ([50]); and [the place] is not one ‘where people ordinarily engage in sport or … leisure’ (CLA s 5K; Goode [190]). Division 5 is not engaged.”

Write only the limb answered · no limb is answered → Division 5 does not apply and the run ends · otherwise → B15

B15 Is the activity a ‘dangerous’ recreational activity that involves a ‘significant risk of physical harm’? (s 5K) · when the activity answers a limb of s 5K

“‘[The activity]’ is a ‘dangerous recreational activity’ if it is ‘a recreational activity that involves a significant risk of physical harm’ (CLA s 5K; Fallas; Jaber), which the courts have read as a significant risk of significant physical harm (Fallas), assessed objectively in the context of the particular circumstances: ‘[t]ime, place, competence, age, sobriety, equipment and even the weather may make dangerous a recreational activity which would not otherwise involve a risk of harm’ (Fallas per Ipp JA [36]). Fallas itself concerned one participant discharging a firearm and wounding another while both were engaged in ‘spotlighting and shooting kangaroo at night’ (Singh [45]). It therefore [carried such a risk, or not].”

A · dangerous recreational activity

  “‘[The activity]’ involved ‘a significant risk of physical harm’ (CLA s 5K; Fallas; Jaber), read as a significant risk of significant physical harm: [the feature of the activity or conditions relied on] made serious injury a real prospect, and [which of time, place, competence, age, sobriety, equipment or weather is in issue] made dangerous what might not otherwise involve a risk of harm (Fallas per Ipp JA [36]), as the night shooting in Fallas itself did. It was a dangerous recreational activity.”

B · no significant risk

  “‘[The activity]’ involved no ‘significant risk of physical harm’ (CLA s 5K), still less a significant risk of significant physical harm (Fallas; Jaber): [the feature of the activity or conditions relied on] made serious injury remote, and none of time, place, competence, age, sobriety, equipment or weather (Fallas per Ipp JA [36]) changed that in the particular circumstances, far from the night shooting in Fallas itself. Section 5L is not open.”

dangerous → B16 · not dangerous → s 5L is not open and the run ends

B16 No liability for harm from obvious risks of dangerous recreational activities (s 5L) · when the activity is a dangerous recreational activity

“[D] ‘is not liable in negligence for harm suffered by another person … as a result of the materialisation of an obvious risk of a dangerous recreational activity engaged in by the plaintiff’ (CLA s 5L(1)), and [P]’s ignorance is no answer, the section applying ‘whether or not the plaintiff was aware of the risk’ (s 5L(2)). In NSW the dangerous recreational activity defence has severed all links to the common law defence of volens (SVE [14.150]), so s 5G’s presumption of awareness plays no part here even though it governs Division 4, and the s 5L risk is determined only after a prima facie case of negligence, ‘the only consequence of a finding of breach of duty would be that s 5L was engaged’ (Singh [78] per Basten JA). The section [is engaged in form, or is not]; whether the risk of harm was obvious is decided next.”

A · s 5L engaged in form

  “Breach having been found against [D], s 5L is engaged in form, ‘the only consequence of a finding of breach of duty would be that s 5L was engaged’ (Singh [78] per Basten JA); [P]’s ignorance of ‘[the risk]’ does not matter, the section applying ‘whether or not the plaintiff was aware of the risk’ (CLA s 5L(2)), and s 5G’s presumption is irrelevant, the defence having severed all links to volens (SVE [14.150]). Whether it defeats the claim now turns only on obviousness.”

B · s 5L not engaged, breach not yet established

  “Breach is not yet established against [D], [why breach has not been established on these facts], so s 5L has nothing to answer: the s 5L risk is determined only after a prima facie case of negligence, ‘the only consequence of a finding of breach of duty would be that s 5L was engaged’ (Singh [78] per Basten JA). Until breach is made out, [P]’s awareness or ignorance under s 5L(2) is not in issue.”

→ B26 (test whether the risk was an obvious one under s 5F, on the Singh method) · gross negligence is on the facts → B27 as well · → B17 for the final characterisation and answer

B17 Tapp v Australian Bushmen’s Campdraft & Rodeo Association Ltd [2022] HCA 11 · when immediately after B26 or B27

“The s 5L risk, breach being established (Tapp (1); Singh [78]), is characterised at the same level of generality as the risk of the breach, on the same facts as established that risk but no more (Tapp (2)–(3)), the specification carrying ‘a degree of generality’ yet being ‘sufficiently precise to capture the harm which results from its materialisation’, with ‘a close causal connection between the harm suffered and the risk of harm’ (Singh [195], summarising Menz [70]–[74]), a balance between generality and specificity, striking it ‘between too broad and too narrow a specification of the risk’ (Singh [196]). Tapp’s own risk was ‘a substantially elevated risk of physical injury by falling from a horse that slipped due to the deterioration of the arena surface’, not obvious to a reasonable person in Ms Tapp’s position, so the respondent was liable in negligence. The Singh majority held obvious ‘the risk of a fall as a result of another jockey’s careless riding’ (Leeming JA [139]), an incident of the contest rather than a deteriorated surface. ‘[The risk]’ accordingly [is obvious as in Singh, or takes ‘[D’s specific default]’ into its description as in Tapp], and s 5L [answers the claim, or does not].”

A · obvious as in Singh

  “That holds at the proper level of generality: ‘[the risk]’, characterised with ‘a degree of generality’ yet precisely enough to capture ‘[the injury]’ (Singh [195]), remains obvious: [the ordinary hazard of the activity that P’s harm answers] is an incident of the activity itself, as ‘the risk of a fall as a result of another jockey’s careless riding’ was (Singh per Leeming JA [139]). Tapp is distinguishable, its deteriorated arena surface no ordinary incident of campdrafting and its risk not obvious to a reasonable person in Ms Tapp’s position. Therefore, the better view is that s 5L answers the claim.”

B · neglect goes into the description, as in Tapp

  “‘[D’s specific default]’ goes into the description of the risk of harm, as the deterioration of the arena surface did in Tapp, whose risk, framed to include that deterioration, was not obvious to a reasonable person in Ms Tapp’s position: the risk of harm is characterised on the same facts as the breach but no more (Tapp (2)–(3)), and no looser formulation captures ‘[the injury]’ (Singh [195]). Singh is distinguishable, its risk an incident of the contest itself (Leeming JA [139]). Therefore, the better view is that s 5L does not answer the claim.”

obvious → s 5L defeats the claim · not obvious → the claim survives · breach is unwalked → BREACH OF DUTY 1 first (Tapp (1) requiring breach before s 5L is tested; for a second defendant, run BREACH OF DUTY 1 again)

B18 s 5M No duty of care for recreational activity where risk warning · when the facts print a sign, notice, announcement or document given to P before she engaged

“‘[The words]’ were put before [P] before she engaged, and [D] does not owe a duty ‘to take care in respect of a risk of the activity if the risk was the subject of a risk warning to the plaintiff’ (CLA s 5M(1)), a risk warning being one ‘given in a manner that is reasonably likely to result in people being warned of the risk before engaging in the recreational activity’. [D] ‘is not required to establish that the person received or understood the warning or was capable of receiving or understanding the warning’ (s 5M(3)). A springboard sign warning that persons using the springboard did so at their own risk sufficed (Sharp); a horse-show notice headed ‘Participants Indemnity and Waiver RISK WARNING – HORSES’, advising only that participation ‘contains elements of risk, both obvious and inherent’, did not, its purpose being waiver and indemnity rather than warning of risk (Menz [126]–[127]). ‘[The words]’ accordingly [warned, as in Sharp, or gave waiver and indemnity, as in Menz].”

A · effective risk warning

  “‘[The words]’ warned: [how and when the warning was put before P] made them ‘reasonably likely to result in people being warned of the risk before engaging in the recreational activity’ (CLA s 5M(3)), and [D] need not show [P] received or understood them (s 5M(3)). Sharp is analogous, the springboard sign there warning that users did so at their own risk sufficing, unlike the waiver-and-indemnity notice condemned in Menz ([126]–[127]). No duty was arguably owed in respect of the risk (s 5M(1)).”

B · waiver, not warning

  “‘[The words]’ did not warn: [the wording of the notice] reads, like the notice headed ‘Participants Indemnity and Waiver RISK WARNING – HORSES’ advising only of ‘elements of risk, both obvious and inherent’, as waiver and indemnity rather than warning of risk (Menz [126]–[127]), and so was not ‘reasonably likely to result in people being warned of the risk’ (CLA s 5M(3)). Sharp is distinguishable, its springboard sign putting the risk of harm itself on the user. The duty likely stands.”

C · Sharp and Menz both ways

  “Sharp is analogous because ‘[the words]’, like the springboard sign warning that users did so at their own risk, told [P] that [the risk the words put on P] was hers; yet [the wording of the notice] reads closer to the Menz notice, headed ‘Participants Indemnity and Waiver RISK WARNING – HORSES’ and held waiver and indemnity rather than warning ([126]–[127]). Therefore, the better view is that they were ‘reasonably likely to result in people being warned of the risk’ (CLA s 5M(3)) and no duty was owed (s 5M(1)), though it is a close thing.”

P is a child or otherwise incapable → B28 · otherwise → B19, then B20, then B15

B19 A risk warning need not be specific to the particular risk (s 5M(5)) · when the warning printed is general rather than aimed at the risk that materialised

“‘[The words]’ are general rather than aimed at ‘[the risk]’, but a risk warning ‘need not be specific to the particular risk and can be a general warning of risks that include the particular risk concerned (so long as the risk warning warns of the general nature of the particular risk)’ (CLA s 5M(5)). It must, though, be given ‘by or on behalf of the defendant or by or on behalf of the occupier’ of the place (s 5M(6)). A sign reading ‘USE OF THIS FACILITY MAY BE HAZARDOUS’ did not sufficiently identify the risk of a child falling between rails to hard sand four metres below (Polglase [117]–[120]). ‘[The words]’ accordingly [warn of the general nature of ‘[the risk]’, or do not reach it, as in Polglase].”

A · general warning suffices

  “‘[The words]’ warn of the general nature of ‘[the risk]’: ‘[the injury]’ falls squarely within what they describe, unlike ‘USE OF THIS FACILITY MAY BE HAZARDOUS’, which did not sufficiently identify the risk of a child falling between rails to hard sand four metres below (Polglase [117]–[120]); and [who gave the warning and in what capacity] gave them ‘by or on behalf of’ [D] or the occupier (CLA s 5M(6)). Their generality does not defeat them (s 5M(5)).”

B · warning does not reach the risk

  “‘[The words]’ do not reach ‘[the risk]’: ‘[the injury]’ is of a different nature from anything they describe, just as ‘USE OF THIS FACILITY MAY BE HAZARDOUS’ did not sufficiently identify the risk of a child falling between rails to hard sand four metres below (Polglase [117]–[120]); or else [who gave the warning and in what capacity] shows they were not given ‘by or on behalf of’ [D] or the occupier (CLA s 5M(6)). That a warning ‘need not be specific to the particular risk’ (s 5M(5)) does not save them.”

→ B20

B20 When the risk warning does not assist (s 5M(7)–(9)) · when a written law, a contrary representation, or compulsion by D is on the facts

“Three independent exceptions decide whether the risk warning assists. Firstly, [D] is not entitled to rely on it if the harm ‘resulted from a contravention of a provision of a written law of the State or Commonwealth that establishes specific practices or procedures for the protection of personal safety’ (CLA s 5M(7)). Secondly, not ‘to the extent that the warning was contradicted by any representation as to risk made by or on behalf of the defendant’ (s 5M(8)). Thirdly, not ‘if the plaintiff was required to engage in the recreational activity by the defendant’ (s 5M(9)). [The exceptions the facts raise] [are engaged, or none is].”

A · one or more exceptions engaged, reliance disqualified

  “Section 5M(7) is engaged: [the written law and the practice it prescribes] establishes ‘specific practices or procedures for the protection of personal safety’, and the harm ‘resulted from a contravention’ of it on the balance of probabilities. Further, [what D said that contradicted the warning] left the warning ‘contradicted by any representation as to risk’ (s 5M(8)), and [the compulsion] shows [P] was ‘required to engage in the recreational activity’ by [D] (s 5M(9)). Any one of these disqualifies reliance on the warning.”

B · none of the three exceptions engaged

  “None of the exceptions is made out: the harm did not result ‘from a contravention of a provision of a written law’ establishing safety practices or procedures, because [why the harm did not result from a contravention] (CLA s 5M(7)); nothing [D] said left the warning ‘contradicted by any representation as to risk’ (s 5M(8)), because [why no contrary representation was made]; and [P] was not ‘required to engage in the recreational activity’ (s 5M(9)), her participation voluntary because [what shows P’s participation was voluntary]. Reliance on the warning is not disqualified.”

Write only the exceptions the facts raise; each disqualifies reliance independently of the others · → B15

B21 s 5N Waiver of contractual duty of care for recreational activities · when the facts print a signed waiver or a term of a contract for the supply of recreation services

“‘[The words of the contractual term]’ is a term of a contract for the supply of recreation services, and such a term ‘may exclude, restrict or modify any liability to which this Division applies that results from breach of an express or implied warranty that the services will be rendered with reasonable care and skill’ (CLA s 5N(1)). A term to the effect that [P] engages in the activity ‘at his or her own risk’ operates to exclude that liability (s 5N(3)). It fails where the harm ‘resulted from a contravention of a provision of a written law … that establishes specific practices or procedures for the protection of personal safety’ (s 5N(6)); and in Insight Vacations the exclusion failed because it did not apply outside New South Wales and was restricted to when passengers were seated. The term [reaches what happened, or stops short].”

A · term reaches the harm

  “‘[The term]’ reaches what happened: it provides that [P] engages in the activity ‘at his or her own risk’ and so excludes liability for breach of the warranty of reasonable care and skill (CLA s 5N(1), (3)), and [why the term’s scope covers P’s circumstances], unlike the term in Insight Vacations, confined to New South Wales and to seated passengers. No written law prescribing safety practices or procedures was contravened (s 5N(6)). The liability is arguably excluded.”

B · term stops short, as in Insight Vacations

  “‘[The term]’ stops short: [the scope limit in the term that P’s circumstances fall outside] leaves her position outside it, as the Insight Vacations exclusion did not apply outside New South Wales and was restricted to passengers who were seated; or it fails because the harm ‘resulted from a contravention of a provision of a written law’ establishing safety practices or procedures (CLA s 5N(6)), [the written law relied on] being such a law. Section 5N does not answer the claim.”

→ B15

B22 Step 3 Apportionment for CN: Pennington v Norris · when the apportionment percentage must be defended, or one party was markedly more culpable

“The figure of [the percentage reduction] must be defended, and Pennington fixes the method, arriving at a ‘just and equitable’ apportionment of the ‘responsibility’ for the damage. There the plaintiff ‘simply did not look when a reasonably careful man would have looked’, ‘the very fact that his conduct did not endanger the defendant or anybody else’ being material, while the defendant drove at a speed that ‘could not on the evidence have been found to be less than 30 miles per hour’ on a misty night when the three nearby hotels ‘had closed a very short time previously’. The High Court reapportioned ‘as to eighty per cent to the defendant and, as to twenty per cent to the plaintiff’. The common law’s preference for pedestrians is gone under the statute: 75% against a pedestrian who crossed against a red pedestrian signal in the face of oncoming traffic (T and X v Chivas), 80% against a motorcyclist riding at twice the speed limit (Ayre v Swan); and apportionment is rarely disturbed on appeal unless plainly wrong (RTA v Dederer [168]–[169]). [P’s conduct] against [D’s conduct] is [which conduct was graver and which was causally more potent], so [the percentage reduction and the figure it produces] is arguably defensible.”

A · small reduction defensible

  “[P’s conduct] was mere inadvertence, she ‘simply did not look when a reasonably careful man would have looked’ and endangered nobody else (Pennington), while [D’s conduct] endangered others, as the misty-night driving at no ‘less than 30 miles per hour’ did there, the Court reapportioning ‘eighty per cent to the defendant’. The statutory removal of the pedestrian preference (T and X v Chivas, 75%) does not alter that comparison here, because [why P’s lapse remains the lesser]. [The small percentage] is defensible, apportionment rarely disturbed absent plain error (RTA v Dederer [168]–[169]).”

B · large reduction defensible

  “[P’s conduct] was a deliberate courting of the danger, closer to the pedestrian who crossed against a red signal into oncoming traffic (T and X v Chivas, 75%) or the motorcyclist at twice the speed limit (Ayre v Swan, 80%), while [D’s conduct] was a single lapse, less culpable than the misty-night speeding in Pennington; [what shows P deliberately courted the danger]. The weighting reverses, and [the large percentage] is defensible, apportionment rarely disturbed on appeal absent plain error (RTA v Dederer [168]–[169]).”

C · Pennington and the modern authorities both ways

  “Pennington is analogous because [P’s conduct], like the plaintiff’s who ‘simply did not look when a reasonably careful man would have looked’, was momentary inadvertence; yet [D’s conduct] falls short of driving at ‘30 miles per hour’ on a misty night near closing hotels, for which the Court gave the defendant eighty per cent, and the pedestrian preference is gone (T and X v Chivas; Ayre v Swan). On balance [the percentage reduction and the figure it produces] is likely defensible (RTA v Dederer [168]–[169]), although the point is arguable.”

D · a child plaintiff

  “[P], [P’s age at the date of injury], departs from the standard of the reasonable child of the same age (Doubleday, the seven-year-old on a trampoline in roller skates not unreasonable); Verryt is analogous, the adult driver who allowed a twelve-year-old to skateboard hanging on to the back of a moving vehicle bearing the greater responsibility, the child ten per cent. [P’s conduct] against [D’s conduct] is [which conduct was graver and which was causally more potent], the Court arriving at a ‘just and equitable’ apportionment (Pennington), not looking solely at [P]’s conduct. [The percentage reduction] is therefore defensible.”

→ 3B (where a total or near-total reduction is arguable) · otherwise → 4

B23 Multiple defendants: combining the defendants’ shares · when two or more defendants are liable and [P] was contributorily negligent

“Responsibility falls [D1’s share] to [D1], [D2’s share] to [D2] and [P’s share] to [P]. Where two defendants have each injured [P], their shares of responsibility are combined for the purposes of her action against each of them (Fitzgerald v Lane; Barisic): if D1 is 20% responsible, D2 30% and [P] 50%, her damages are diminished by 50% against either or both, not by D1’s 20% alone. Apportionment is enlivened only against a defendant who committed a ‘wrong’ (LR(MP)A s 8), and a plaintiff with concurrent claims in negligence and contract cannot avoid apportionment by suing only in contract, the common law rule to the opposite effect having been reversed (Astley).”

A · both defendants liable, shares combined

  “Responsibility falls [D1’s share] to [D1], [D2’s share] to [D2] and [P’s share] to [P], because [why responsibility falls in those shares]; both committed a ‘wrong’ within s 8, so their shares are combined for the purposes of [P]’s action against each of them (Fitzgerald v Lane; Barisic), as where D1’s 20% and D2’s 30% leave her damages diminished by her own 50% whichever she sues. Nor could she avoid the reduction by suing only in contract (Astley). Her damages fall by [P’s share] against either defendant or both.”

B · only one defendant committed a wrong

  “[D2] committed no ‘wrong’: [why D2’s act or omission gives rise to no liability in tort attracting the defence] leaves it outside s 8, so there is nothing to combine, the rule of Fitzgerald v Lane and Barisic not reaching it. [P]’s damages fall by [P’s share] against [D1] alone, and suing in contract instead would not avoid that reduction (Astley).”

→ 3 (enliven the apportionment provision → to 3A to compare the parties) · the percentage must be defended → B22

B24 Where volenti does not run at all · when the facts are a motor accident, or a workplace injury to which workers compensation legislation applies

“‘[The accident]’ may shut volenti out altogether: the defence is excluded for motor vehicle accidents except where the plaintiff was injured while involved in motor vehicle racing (Motor Accidents Compensation Act 1999 (NSW) s 140), and in cases where workers compensation legislation applies (Workers Compensation Act 1987 (NSW) s 151O), and it ‘has all but vanished’ from the workplace setting in any event, danger money alone being insufficient (Davies v Global Strategies). The exception is the injured employee instrumental in creating the danger, as where two brothers using explosives at work deliberately breached statutory safety regulations imposed on the employees and not the employer (Shatwell). ‘[The accident]’ [answers one of these exclusions, or does not].”

A · the defence is shut out

  “‘[The accident]’ is [a motor vehicle accident other than motor vehicle racing, or an injury to which workers compensation legislation applies], so volenti does not run at all (Motor Accidents Compensation Act 1999 (NSW) s 140; Workers Compensation Act 1987 (NSW) s 151O), the defence having ‘all but vanished’ from the workplace in any event, danger money alone being insufficient (Davies v Global Strategies). [D] is thus likely shut out of it whatever [P] knew; CN under s 5R may still be argued.”

B · neither exclusion reaches it

  “Neither exclusion reaches ‘[the accident]’: [why it is neither a motor vehicle accident nor a matter to which workers compensation legislation applies] (Motor Accidents Compensation Act 1999 (NSW) s 140; Workers Compensation Act 1987 (NSW) s 151O). Unlike Shatwell, where the brothers deliberately breaching safety regulations imposed on them were themselves instrumental in creating the danger, [why P was not instrumental in creating her own danger] leaves that exceptional route closed too, but volenti itself remains open on the ordinary elements.”

If the defence is shut out, contributory negligence may still run → 1 · otherwise → B8 (test obviousness under s 5F)

[]

CONCURRENT LIABILITY: SCAFFOLD

0 What is concurrent liability?

“[D2] may be liable for ‘[P’s harm]’ alongside [D1], the liability shared between them so that [P] may sue one or more Ds in a single action or series of actions, subject to restrictions on the recovery of damages and costs in subsequent actions (Law Reform (Miscellaneous Provisions) Act 1946 (NSW) s 5(1)(a)(b)). Two situations arise: vicarious liability, where a blameless person is responsible for the damages resulting from the tortious conduct of another, and non-delegable duty, a duty on one person to ensure that another person takes reasonable care.”

A · vicarious liability the live issue

  “[The fact placing D1 in a relationship capable of generating vicarious liability] makes vicarious liability the live issue: [D2], although blameless, would answer for the damages resulting from [D1]’s tortious conduct. Whether that responsibility arises turns first on the relationship between [D1] and [D2].”

B · non-delegable duty the live issue

  “[The fact placing D2 under such a duty] makes the non-delegable duty the live issue: [D2] would owe a duty to ensure that [the delegate] took reasonable care, not a duty to take reasonable care itself. Whether it arises turns on [D2]’s control and responsibility and on [P]’s dependence (Burnie).”

C · both routes open

  “[The fact opening both routes] opens both routes: [D2] may be vicariously liable for [D1]’s tort, and [P], injured by the tort of an employee, may use both vicarious liability and a non-delegable duty; had the wrongdoer been an independent contractor, only the non-delegable duty would be available (SVE [13.115]). Each route is taken independently.”

[D1] is or may be an employee, agent or business partner of [D2] → 1 for vicarious liability · the claim is that [D2] itself owed [P] a duty to ensure reasonable care → 4 for non-delegable duty · both are pleaded → run each route independently; only one defendant can be vicariously liable for the same tort (Day [23]–[33])

1 Is there an employer/employee relationship?

“[D1] [serves [D2]’s business, or carries on a trade or business of his own], because [the fact showing whose business the work advances], the line being ‘rooted fundamentally in the difference between a person who serves his employer in his, the employer’s, business, and a person who carries on a trade or business of his own’ (Marshall 217, in Hollis 39). Of the three relationships generating vicarious liability, employer/employee, the relationship in issue here, principal/agent and business partners, an employer answers for an employee’s tort, not a contractor’s (Sweeney); the inquiry runs on the rights and obligations under contract (primary test) (CFMMEU), then the multi-factor test (secondary test) on the totality of the relationship (Hollis), and those tests decide whether [D1] is [D2]’s employee.”

A · employee relationship made out

  “[D1] serves [D2]’s business, not any trade of his own: [the fact showing whose business the work advances], and [the control fact] shows the work done under [D2]’s direction, the line being ‘rooted fundamentally in the difference between a person who serves his employer in his, the employer’s, business, and a person who carries on a trade or business of his own’ (Marshall 217, in Hollis 39). An employer answers for an employee’s tort, not a contractor’s (Sweeney), and the rights and obligations under contract (primary test) (CFMMEU), then the totality of the relationship (Hollis), decide whether [D1] is one.”

B · independent contractor

  “[D1] carries on a trade or business of his own: [the fact showing whose business the work advances], and [the independence fact] shows [D2] took the result of his work, not his service, ‘a contract for services’, not a contract of service (SVE [13.50]). The line falls between serving the employer’s business and carrying on one’s own (Marshall 217, in Hollis 39), and an employer answers for an employee’s tort, not a contractor’s (Sweeney). [D1] thus is likely not [D2]’s employee, and only a non-delegable duty could answer.”

C · Hollis both ways

  “Hollis is analogous because [the fact both cases share], [D1] presented to the world as [D2]’s emanation as the couriers did Vabu’s (Hollis 42–43); yet [the fact that distinguishes this case] tells against it, marking an independence the couriers lacked (Hollis 41). The line falls between serving the employer’s business and carrying on one’s own (Marshall 217, in Hollis 39). On balance [D1] serves [D2]’s business and is his employee, although not without difficulty.”

D1’s earnings may cross the contractor high income threshold → B3 first for the opt-out, then continue below on whichever status results · there is a written contract, its terms are given and no sham is pleaded → B1 · the terms point the other way, to a contract for services → B2 · there is no written contract, or its terms are not given, or a sham, variation or waiver is pleaded → B4 · a contractor is concluded → B7 · an employee is concluded → add B7 in one clause in the alternative: were D1 a contractor, no vicarious liability would arise and only a non-delegable duty could answer, going to 4 where the question raises it · a non-delegable duty only → 4

2 Was the tortious act done in the course of Employment (Bugge, Deatons)?

“Firstly, [D1] committed ‘[the tort]’. Secondly, the wrongs are taken one at a time, an act in the course of employment being an authorised act or ‘an improper mode of performing an authorised act’ (Bugge; Deatons), unauthorised acts falling within employment where ‘so connected’ with authorised acts ‘that they may be regarded as modes, although improper modes, of doing them’ (Salmond, in PAC [42]).”

A · improper mode, within employment

  “Firstly, [D1] committed ‘[the tort]’. Secondly, for ‘[the negligent wrong]’, [D1] was authorised to [the authorised act], and [the fact making the act a mode of doing the assigned task] makes the wrong an improper mode of that authorised act rather than an independent act (Bugge; Deatons). This wrong thus was arguably within the course of employment.”

B · too far removed

  “Firstly, [D1] committed ‘[the tort]’. Secondly, for ‘[the negligent wrong]’, [D1] was authorised to [the authorised act], yet [why the act was not a mode of doing the assigned task] makes the wrong too far removed from any authorised act: it was not [that task] done badly but a thing [D2] never engaged him to do, not ‘so connected’ with any authorised act as to be a mode, even an improper mode, of doing it (Salmond, in PAC [42]). This wrong thus was likely not within the course of employment.”

C · the wrongs divide

  “Firstly, [D1] committed ‘[the tort]’. Secondly, the wrongs divide. ‘[The first wrong]’ was an improper mode of [the authorised act], [D1] authorised to [the task D1 was engaged to do], though not [the forbidden manner], and stays within the employment (Bugge; Deatons); ‘[the second wrong]’, by contrast, [why it is too far removed], and is too far removed from any authorised act to be a mode, even an improper one, of doing it (Salmond, in PAC [42]). [D2] accordingly answers for the first and arguably not for the second, each wrong carrying its own answer.”

Run A or B for each wrong; where the answers differ, C states the split; where A or B is run again for a further wrong, drop the ordinals and open “Turning to ‘[the next wrong]’, …” · D1 exercised an independent statutory discretion or authority → B20 · a forbidden method or the careless doing of the assigned task → B8 · a personal quarrel or frolic → B9 · a criminal act → B10 · child abuse → B11 · → 3

3 Was the victim injured by the tort of the employee? (causation)

“Thirdly, [D1]’s tort [caused, or did not cause] ‘[P’s injury]’, the standard rules of causation applying (CLA s 5D) and yielding the same conclusion as the causation analysis above for each wrong; if [D1] committed no tort, [D2] is not liable, an employer answering only for a tort its employee actually committed. Therefore, [D2] [is, or is not] vicariously liable.”

A · tort caused injury

  “Thirdly, [D1]’s tort caused ‘[P’s injury]’: [the causal fact] makes the wrong a necessary condition of the harm on the standard rules of causation (CLA s 5D(1)(a)), the same conclusion as the causation analysis above. The three steps, employee, course of employment, causation, are answered. Therefore, [D2] is arguably vicariously liable, [D1] remaining personally liable as a joint tortfeasor.”

B · no tort, or no causation

  “Thirdly, [the fact showing no tort, or the fact breaking causation] defeats the claim: either [D1]’s conduct was no tort at all, an employer answering only for a tort its employee actually committed, or the tort did not cause ‘[P’s injury]’ on the standard rules of causation (CLA s 5D), [the intervening fact] standing between act and harm. Therefore, [D2] is likely not vicariously liable for this wrong.”

C · tort caused injury, but D1 a contractor

  “Thirdly, [D1]’s tort caused ‘[P’s injury]’ on the standard rules of causation (CLA s 5D), so the third question is answered in [P]’s favour; yet [D1] being an independent contractor, no vicarious liability arises, an employer answering for an employee’s tort and not a contractor’s (Sweeney). Therefore, [D2] is likely not vicariously liable, and only a non-delegable duty could answer.”

a CLA provision relieves D1 → B12 · D1 remains personally liable as a joint tortfeasor notwithstanding D2’s vicarious liability · the question asks about a non-delegable duty, or the wrongdoer is a contractor so vicarious liability cannot answer → 4 · Otherwise the run ends

4 Does the D have control and responsibility for the safety of others? (Burnie)

“Firstly, [D2] [had, or lacked] control and responsibility as to the safety of the person or property of others (Burnie), [the fact placing safety in D2’s hands] showing where that responsibility sat. The duty, where it arises, is ‘a duty to ensure that reasonable care is taken’, not a duty to take reasonable care (Burnie 550), and it arises where [D2] undertakes the care, supervision or control of [P] or [P]’s property, is in control of premises, or allows the situation of foreseeable risk of danger through a dangerous substance or activity, so assuming responsibility for [P]’s safety.”

A · control and responsibility established

  “Firstly, [the fact placing safety in D2’s hands] left [P]’s safety in [D2]’s hands: it undertook the care, supervision or control of [P] or [P]’s property, being ‘so placed in relation to that person or his property as to assume a particular responsibility for his or its safety’ (Kondis, in Burnie 551), as the Authority’s control over the building placed General Jones’s safety in its hands. [D2] thus likely has the control and responsibility the duty requires, though [the contrary fact] is arguable.”

B · no control and responsibility

  “Firstly, [why control sat elsewhere] shows [D2] lacked control: it undertook no care, supervision or control of [P] or [P]’s property, [the party who did control the activity] deciding how [the work] was done and when, and no situation of foreseeable risk of danger was allowed on anything [D2] controlled. The ‘central element of control’ generating the special responsibility to see that care is taken (Burnie 551) is absent. [D2] accordingly likely lacks control and responsibility, although [the contrary fact] tells against that.”

C · Burnie both ways

  “Firstly, Burnie is analogous because [the fact both cases share], [D2] taking advantage of its control of premises to allow [the dangerous substance or activity] there as the Authority allowed welding beside flammable material; yet [the fact that distinguishes this case] points the other way, the ‘central element of control’ (Burnie 551) arguably sitting with [the party who did control the activity]. The duty, where it arises, is ‘a duty to ensure that reasonable care is taken’ (Burnie 550). The better view is that [D2] had control and responsibility, though it is a close thing.”

To test the relationship against a recognised category → 4A, then 4B (state the scope of the duty); otherwise → 4B directly. If a dangerous substance or activity on premises D2 controls → B13 · a school, holiday program or other body in charge of children → B15 · a landlord, road authority or occupier of premises open to the public → B16

4A Categories of non-delegable duties

“‘[D2’s relationship]’ [answers one of the categories, or stands outside them]: employer to employee (Kondis), hospitals to patients (Ellis v WDH), schools to students (Introvigne), and occupiers to visitors and third parties (Burnie; Northern Sandblasting). Membership is not itself the test: ‘Reasoning by analogy suggests, but does not compel, a conclusion that that common element gives rise to such a duty of care’ (Burnie 551–552), the common element being control.”

A · within a category

  “[The fact placing the relationship in that category] puts [D2] and [P] squarely within [the category relationship], Mason J’s principal categories being ‘master and servant in relation to a safe system of work; hospital and patient; school authority and pupil; and (arguably), occupier and invitee’ (Kondis, in Burnie 550). The label alone decides nothing, Northern Sandblasting sits in the occupier category although the landlord’s duty to the tenant’s family was held not non-delegable (Jones v Bartlett), but the category confirms what control shows.”

B · outside the categories, reached by analogy

  “[Why the relationship falls outside every category] leaves ‘[D2’s relationship]’ outside the Kondis categories, yet that is not fatal: the common element is control, and ‘Reasoning by analogy suggests, but does not compel, a conclusion that that common element gives rise to such a duty of care’ (Burnie 551–552). [The fact supplying the analogy] leaves [P] as dependent on [D2] as a pupil on a school authority (Introvigne). The better view is that the duty arises outside the categories, though the High Court has been reluctant to extend them (SVE [13.125]).”

C · Introvigne both ways

  “Introvigne is analogous because [the fact both cases share], [D2] having taken charge of [P] as a school authority takes charge of its pupils; yet [the fact that distinguishes this case] points the other way, [the party who did control the activity] retaining the charge [D2] is said to have taken. The categories are illustrations, not a gateway: ‘Reasoning by analogy suggests, but does not compel’ the duty (Burnie 551–552). The better view is that the schools to students category is answered, although finely balanced.”

→ 4B (state the scope of the duty, whether or not a category is answered) · a school, holiday program or other body in charge of children → B15 · a landlord, road authority or occupier of premises open to the public → B16 · an occupier sued for a contractor’s negligence → B17 · the facts do not say what D2 is → do not force a category: run 4 and 5 directly on Burnie’s two criteria

4B Scope of duty: What could D have done to ensure reasonable care is taken?

“The duty required [D2] to ensure that reasonable care was taken in [the work or task entrusted] (Burnie 550), and its scope turns on what [D2] could have done to ensure reasonable care was taken: [the step D2 could have taken] [was, or was not] open to it.”

A · the duty extended to the precaution

  “[D2] could have [the step D2 could have taken to ensure care was taken]: warned [the delegate] of [the risk of harm created], written [the precaution P alleges] into the engagement, or checked [the work] before [P] was exposed to it. In Burnie the Authority had control over the building and could have warned the welder about the flammable material or had it moved away from where he was working. Its scope thus arguably covers ‘[the harm]’.”

B · the duty did not extend that far

  “[The fact limiting what D2 could do] left [D2] nothing useful to do: it knew nothing of [the risk of harm created], [the delegate] alone chose how [the work] was performed, and no term of engagement would have reached ‘[the harm]’. Unlike Burnie, where the Authority could have warned the welder about the flammable material or had it moved away, no comparable step was open. The duty is to ensure that reasonable care is taken, not to insure against harm (Burnie 550), thus its scope likely stops short of ‘[the harm]’.”

→ 5 (for the special dependence or vulnerability of [P]) · the wrong was an intentional criminal act → B18 first · the duty is made out → B19 states the extent of liability

5 Is there a special dependence or vulnerability of the P? (Burnie)

“Secondly, it is ‘special dependence or vulnerability on the part of’ [P] that must be shown (Burnie 551), considering the steps [P] could have taken to prevent the damage, [P]’s awareness of the risk creating activity or substance, and [P]’s control over it. [P] could have taken [the step P could have taken], knew [what P knew of the risk creating activity or substance] and had [P’s control over the activity]; ‘[P’s position]’ accordingly leaves [P], or [P]’s property, [dependent, or not].”

A · special dependence shown

  “Secondly, [P] could have done nothing to prevent the damage, [why the steps were unavailable or futile] making even [the step P could have taken] futile; nor did [P] know of [the risk creating activity or substance], and [the fact denying P control] left no control over it, such a person commonly having ‘neither the right nor the opportunity to exercise control over, or even to have foreknowledge of, what is done’ (Burnie 551). As in Burnie, where General Jones did not know the welder was welding, ‘[P’s position]’ leaves [P] dependent.”

B · no special dependence

  “Secondly, [P] could have taken [the step P could have taken], [why that step was open to P] showing it open; [P] knew of [the risk creating activity or substance] because [the fact giving P foreknowledge], and [the fact giving P control] gave control over it. That is not the plaintiff with ‘neither the right nor the opportunity to exercise control over, or even to have foreknowledge of, what is done’ (Burnie 551); unlike General Jones, [P] could have protected himself. ‘[P’s position]’ accordingly leaves [P] not dependent.”

C · Burnie both ways

  “Secondly, Burnie is analogous because [the fact both cases share], [P] standing to [D2] as General Jones stood to the Authority, unable to prevent the damage, unaware the welder was welding, without control over him; yet [the fact that distinguishes this case] points the other way, giving [P] the foothold that plaintiff lacked. [P] stands as the factory owner in AF Textile stood, Burnie applied there to a contractor using a high pressure water hose on an asbestos roof, damaging the factory beneath (AF Textile [72]). The better view is that ‘[P’s position]’ leaves [P], or [P]’s property, dependent, though it is a close thing.”
