Consider the steps P could have taken to prevent the damage, P’s awareness of the risk creating activity or substance, and P’s control over it · the duty is made out → B19 · Only one defendant can be vicariously liable for the same tort (Day [23]–[33]) · there is a second wrongdoer whose liability rests on a separate vicarious relationship, a non-delegable duty, or its own negligence → 1 for that separate claim

B20 Independent discretion or statutory authority · when [D1] exercised an independent discretion or authority conferred directly by statute or the common law, such as a police officer’s discretion to arrest, or…

“[D1] exercised [the independent discretion or statutory function] under [the statute or appointment conferring it], and an employer is not vicariously liable where the employee exercises an independent discretion or authority at common law or under statute (Enever; Oceanic Crest). Whether [D2] answers turns on whether the choice was [D1]’s alone or made under [D2]’s direction.”

A · independent discretion, no vicarious liability

  “[The fact showing the discretion was D1’s own] shows the choice was [D1]’s alone: as with the police officer’s independent discretion to arrest (Enever) and the ship’s pilot whose negligence arose in the exercise of an independent function under a statutory appointment for compulsory pilotage (Oceanic Crest), the authority came from [the statute], not from [D2]. [D2] accordingly likely escapes vicarious liability for this act, though a non-delegable duty may still be pressed.”

B · not an independent discretion, vicarious liability open

  “[The fact showing D2 directed or controlled the act] shows [D2] retained the decision: [D1]’s act was no exercise of a discretion conferred directly on him by statute or the common law. Enever and Oceanic Crest are distinguishable, the officer’s discretion to arrest and the pilot’s compulsory pilotage each coming from an independent statutory source rather than the employer, whereas [D1] acted under [D2]’s own direction and for its purposes. The exception likely does not apply, and the course of employment inquiry resumes.”

If the independent discretion exception applies, no vicarious liability arises for that act, though a non-delegable duty may still be pressed → 4 · Otherwise return to 2

B1 Is there a contract (if not a sham)? What does it says? (CFMMEU) i.e. Rights and obligations under contract (primary test) · when the facts set out the terms of a written contract and no sham, variation or waiver is pleaded

“[D1] signed ‘[the agreement]’, and where there is a written contract in place with no challenge to its validity, the contract must be given primacy and is determinative of the relationship between the parties (CFMMEU; ZG). Characterisation weighs the rights and obligations under the contract: control, and whether the work was done in [D1]’s own business or [D2]’s; if those two factors indicate an employment relationship, it is unlikely that other criteria will produce a different result. On [the decisive terms], [D1] thus [is, or is not] an employee.”

A · employee on the contract

  “[D1] signed ‘[the agreement]’, an unchallenged written contract given primacy and determinative of the relationship (CFMMEU). Its terms give [D2] the right to control [the work], and the work was done in [D2]’s business, not [D1]’s own, because [the fact showing whose business the work is done in]; where those two factors indicate an employment relationship, it is unlikely that other criteria will produce a different result, the multi-factorial approach retaining some relevance, the totality of the parties’ relationship considered as it pertains to the contract. [D1] is accordingly likely an employee.”

B · contractor on the contract

  “[D1] signed ‘[the agreement]’, an unchallenged written contract given primacy and determinative of the relationship (CFMMEU). Its terms leave [D1] free to [what the terms leave D1 free to do], and the work was done in his own business because [the fact showing the work is done in D1’s own business]; control and own-business-versus-employer’s-business are the two factors weighed, and both point away from employment, other criteria rarely producing a different result. [D1] is therefore likely not an employee, and no vicarious liability can arise.”

C · CFMMEU both ways

  “CFMMEU is analogous because [the fact both cases share], [D1] engaged under a written contract as Mr McCourt, a British backpacker working as a labourer, was, found an independent contractor under the multi-factorial approach at trial yet an employee once the contract was given primacy; yet [the fact that distinguishes this case] points the other way, [why these terms yield a different result]. The contract being determinative where unchallenged (CFMMEU; ZG), [D1] is on balance arguably [an employee, or a contractor], the terms and not the parties’ conduct deciding it.”

Weigh control and whose business the work was done in; if those two factors indicate an employment relationship, it is unlikely that other criteria will produce a different result · an employee → 2 · a contractor → B7 · the terms are not given or a sham is pleaded → B4

B2 CFMMEU v Personnel Contracting; ZG v Jamsek · when the terms of the contract point to a contract for services, or D2 argues the multi-factorial result should stand against the contract

“‘[D1’s contract]’ [points to a contract for services, or does not], and in ZG v Jamsek truck drivers held below to be employees were found independent contractors, the relationship determined by the contract rather than by how the parties in fact conducted themselves. Whether the same result follows here turns on [the term of D1’s contract that governs the relationship].”

A · Jamsek, a contractor

  “[The term of D1’s contract that governs the relationship] gives the written terms primacy over the evidence of how the work was actually carried out, as in ZG v Jamsek, where truck drivers held to be employees below were found independent contractors once the contract, not their day-to-day conduct, was treated as determinative. On that approach [D1] is likely a contractor, and the multi-factorial impression the other way cannot stand against an unchallenged written contract (CFMMEU).”

B · not Jamsek, an employee

  “[Why D1’s contract points to employment despite Jamsek] shows the written terms here point to employment rather than away from it: the drivers in ZG v Jamsek were found contractors only because the contract governed, and the same method of reasoning, applied to these terms, [the decisive terms], yields the opposite result. [D1] is thus likely an employee despite Jamsek, the contract itself doing the work.”

C · Jamsek both ways

  “ZG v Jamsek is analogous because [the fact both cases share], written terms governing as they did for the drivers, employees below yet contractors in the High Court; yet [the fact that distinguishes this case] points the other way, [why that term withholds the Jamsek result]. The contract determines the relationship on Jamsek’s own approach, and the High Court did not address the case where the contract’s validity is challenged, where review of the day-to-day activities of the parties is likely to be relevant. [D1] is on balance arguably a contractor.”

a contractor → B7 · Otherwise, if the contract’s terms are given → B1; if they are not → B4

B3 High income contractor opt-out · when an opt-out notice is printed, or the facts state [D1]’s own annual income and it may exceed the contractor high income threshold

“‘[D1’s earnings]’ [cross, or do not cross] the contractor high income threshold, currently $175,000: contractors earning above it may give a prospective employer a formal ‘opt out’ notice under the Fair Work Act 2009 (Cth), retaining independent contractor status rather than being deemed employees under the Act’s ordinary inquiry into the ‘real substance, practical reality, and true nature of the relationship’. The notice may be withdrawn at any time.”

A · opt-out preserved status

  “[D1’s stated annual income] crosses the contractor high income threshold, currently $175,000, and an opt-out notice was given, so [D1] retains independent contractor status rather than being deemed an employee under the Fair Work Act 2009 (Cth) inquiry into the ‘real substance, practical reality, and true nature of the relationship’. A prospective employer may inform a worker of that right, and the notice, though it may be withdrawn at any time, was not. [D1] keeps contractor status.”

B · no opt-out available

  “[D1’s stated annual income] does not cross the contractor high income threshold, currently $175,000, or no formal ‘opt out’ notice was given; only contractors above the threshold who give the notice displace the ordinary Fair Work Act 2009 (Cth) inquiry into the ‘real substance, practical reality, and true nature of the relationship’. The opt-out cannot assist [D2] here, and the ordinary characterisation, contract first, then the multi-factor test on the totality of the relationship, resumes.”

If a valid opt-out notice was given, D1 is an independent contractor, → B7 · otherwise → B1, or if the terms are not given → B4

B4 Multi-factor test (Vabu) (secondary test) · when there is no written contract, or its terms are not in evidence, or a sham, variation or waiver is pleaded

“No written contract wholly containing the terms is before the court, so the primary test on the rights and obligations under contract (CFMMEU) yields to the secondary multi-factor test (Vabu). The multi-factor test considers the totality of the relationship (Hollis). Pointing to employment are [the facts sorted to the employee side, for example, equipment supplied by the employer, doing as much work as is offered, a uniform with insignia, years of engagement]; pointing to independent contracting are [the facts sorted to the contractor side, for example, working for others, managing his own tax, no sick pay, no holidays, determining the route or manner of the work, deciding whether conditions allow it, not paid if no work is done, no fixed shifts, responsible for his own licence]. Control ‘is not the sole criterion’ but ‘one of a number of indicia’ (Stevens v Brodribb 24, per Mason J); decisively, ‘it is the totality of the relationship between the parties which must be considered’ (Stevens v Brodribb, in Hollis). Weighed, the totality [does, or does not] indicate employment.”

A · totality indicates employment

  “Weighing the totality of the relationship (Hollis; Stevens v Brodribb), the employee side outweighs the contractor side: viewed as a practical matter [D1] was not conducting any enterprise of his own (Hollis 41). The totality thus likely indicates employment, though [the strongest contrary indicium] is not nothing.”

B · totality indicates his own business

  “Weighing the totality of the relationship (Hollis; Stevens v Brodribb), the contractor side outweighs the employee side. Where workers determine their own hours, provide their own equipment, can delegate and are paid on completion, they point to an independent contractor (SVE [13.50]), and [the fact showing D1 ran his own enterprise] is the independence Hollis found wanting in the couriers (Hollis 41). The totality thus likely does not indicate employment, though [the strongest contrary indicium] tells against that.”

C · the columns near balance

  “The columns sit close. No single indicium decides (Stevens v Brodribb 24), and Hollis warns against making too much of any one fact, its Court of Appeal there ‘fell into error in making too much of’ the couriers owning their own bicycles (Hollis 41). Because [the indicium that tips the totality], the better view is that the totality indicates employment, although not without difficulty.”

Run the indicia the facts supply, sorting each into its column, then return here for the totality: control → B4A; skill → B4B; delegation → B4C; equipment → B4D; whether the task is on-going or one-off → B4E; remuneration → B4F; income tax and superannuation → B4G; hours, holidays and sick leave → B4H; the method of termination → B4I; presentation to the public and deterrence → B5 · Hollis on the couriers owning their own bicycles is made once, at B4D; at B4 and B5 refer back to it rather than retelling it · an employee → 2 · a contractor → B7

B4A The nature and degree of control the employers has · when the facts say who directed how the work was done, or who had the right to direct it

“[D2] [had, or lacked] the right to control how [the work] was done, [the control fact] showing where that right sat. The indicium is whether the employer has the right to control, not whether they exercise it (Hollis), asking whether ‘ultimate authority over (the person) resided in the employer so that [the person] was subject to the [employer’s] order and directions’ (Humberstone 404–405). Control accordingly points [to employment, or to a contract for services].”

A · the right to control sat with D2

  “[The control fact] shows [D2] directed how [the work] was done, [D1] subject to its ‘order and directions’ (Humberstone 404–405); that [D2] rarely intervened is immaterial, the indicium being the right to control, not its exercise (Hollis). In Hollis the couriers ‘had little control over the manner of performing their work’ (Hollis 42). Control therefore points to employment.”

B · no right to control

  “[The independence fact] shows [D1] chose how [the work] was done, and [the fact showing D2 could not give orders] leaves no ‘ultimate authority’ subjecting him to [D2]’s ‘order and directions’ (Humberstone 404–405). Unlike Hollis, where the couriers ‘had little control over the manner of performing their work’ (Hollis 42), [D1] took the work on his own judgment and could decline it, and occasional requests do not supply a right to control. Control therefore points to a contract for services.”

C · Hollis both ways on control

  “Hollis is analogous because [the fact both cases share], [D1] rostered and directed as couriers who ‘had little control over the manner of performing their work’ (Hollis 42); yet [the fact that distinguishes this case] shows [D1] answering to nobody on the manner of [the work]. The indicium remains the right to control rather than its exercise (Hollis; Humberstone 404–405), and a bare right suffices. The better view is that control sits on the employee side, though it is a close thing.”

→ B4B. If the work is skilled → B6 first · Sort the finding into its column and return to B4 for the totality

B4B The nature of the task undertaken – skilled or unskilled · when [D2] says the work needed no qualifications, or [D1] says it was skilled, artistic or professional

“‘[The task]’ was [skilled, or unskilled], [the qualification fact] showing what it required; skill alone does not defeat employment (Zuijs 571, in Stevens v Brodribb). The nature of the task accordingly points [to employment, or to a contract for services].”

A · unskilled work, employment indicated

  “[The qualification fact] shows ‘[the task D1 was engaged to do]’ required no special qualification, and [the fact showing no independent career or goodwill] shows [D1] could build nothing of his own upon it: in Hollis the couriers ‘were not providing skilled labour or labour which required special qualifications’, and a courier ‘is unable to make an independent career as a free-lancer or to generate any “goodwill”’ (Hollis 41–42), the notion that such a worker runs his own enterprise being ‘intuitively unsound’. The unskilled character of the work tells for employment.”

B · skilled work, independence indicated

  “[The qualification fact] shows ‘[the task D1 was engaged to do]’ required [the qualification or licence held by D1], and [the fact showing an independent career or goodwill] shows a practice of [D1]’s own built upon it, unlike the couriers who could generate no ‘goodwill’ (Hollis 41–42). Yet skill does not itself defeat employment, the control test being ‘unhelpful for professional and skilled employees, such as trapeze artists’ (SVE [13.50]). The skill the task required tells for a contract for services, though weakly.”

the work is skilled and [D2] says nobody could direct it → B6, the trapeze artists point (SVE [13.50]) being made once, here or at B6 and never both · otherwise → B4C. Sort the finding into its column and return to B4 for the totality

B4C The freedom of action given to the worker to perform the task – can the worker delegate? · when the facts say whether [D1] could send somebody else, or had to do the work personally

“[D1] [could, or could not] delegate ‘[the task D1 was engaged to do]’, [the delegation fact] showing which, ‘the delegation of work by the putative employee’ being one of the indicia (Stevens v Brodribb 24). The freedom to delegate accordingly points [to employment, or to a contract for services].”

A · no freedom to delegate

  “[The delegation fact] shows [D1] could send nobody else to perform ‘[the task D1 was engaged to do]’, [D2] engaging him and not a substitute; the employee’s duty is to render personal service (SVE [13.50]). In Hollis the evidence did not disclose whether the couriers could delegate, though the Court thought it unlikely Vabu would have permitted it (Hollis 42). The absence of any freedom to delegate tells for employment.”

B · free to delegate

  “[The delegation fact] shows [D1] could send [the substitute D1 could send] in his place, [D2] taking the work rather than the man; the ability to delegate the job to someone else points to an independent contractor, and where the worker employs others to carry out the work there is usually no contract of employment (SVE [13.50]; Stevens v Brodribb 24). The freedom to delegate tells for a contract for services.”

→ B4D. Sort the finding into its column and return to B4 for the totality

B4D Provision and maintenance of equipment by the worker or the employer · when the facts say who supplied, maintained or paid for the tools, vehicle, uniform or equipment

“[The equipment supplied by D2] came from [D2] and [the equipment supplied by D1] from [D1], ‘the provision and maintenance of equipment’ being an indicium Mason J listed in Stevens v Brodribb (24). The equipment point accordingly favours [employment, or a business of D1’s own].”

A · equipment indicates employment

  “[D2] supplied [the equipment supplied by D2] and bore [the cost or risk it carried]; that [D1] supplied [the equipment supplied by D1] decides little, for in Hollis the couriers owned their own bicycles yet ‘the situation in respect of tools and equipment also favours, if anything, a finding that the bicycle couriers were employees’, the capital outlay being relatively small and bicycles not being tools inherently capable of use only for courier work (Hollis 44–45). The equipment point favours employment.”

B · equipment indicates his own business

  “[D1] supplied [the equipment supplied by D1], and [the fact showing D1 bore the cost or risk of the equipment] shows the capital and its upkeep were his, the case Hollis reserved: ‘A different conclusion might, for example, be appropriate where the investment in capital equipment was more significant, and greater skill and training were required to operate it’ (Hollis 41–42). Workers who provide their own equipment point toward independent contracting (SVE [13.50]). The equipment point favours a business of [D1]’s own.”

C · the equipment point both ways

  “[D1]’s ownership of [the equipment supplied by D1] is [D2]’s strongest fact, yet Hollis holds ownership decides nothing on its own, the Court of Appeal below having made too much of the couriers owning their own bicycles (Hollis 41); ‘a different conclusion’ suits only investment ‘more significant’ needing ‘greater skill and training’ (Hollis 41–42), which [the fact showing the scale of D1’s capital investment] [is, or is not]. The better view sorts equipment to the employee side, finely balanced.”

→ B4E. Sort the finding into its column and return to B4 for the totality

B4E Is the task ‘on-going’ or ‘one-off’? · when the facts say how long the engagement ran, or that it was for a single job

“[The length and pattern of the engagement] shows ‘[the task D1 was engaged to do]’ was [on-going, or one-off], the on-going or one-off character of the task being among the indicia (Hollis), and that character accordingly points [to employment, or to a contract for services].”

A · an on-going task

  “[The length and pattern of the engagement] shows [D1] returned to the same work for [D2] over [the period of engagement], the employee providing ongoing service where the contractor ‘contracts to provide services in much the same way as a plumber goes to a domestic home to fix the hot water’ (SVE [13.50]). As in Hollis, the engagement ‘left the couriers with limited scope for the pursuit of any real business enterprise on their own account’ (Hollis 44). The on-going character of the task tells for employment.”

B · a one-off task

  “[The length and pattern of the engagement] shows [D1] engaged for [the single job or fixed project] and nothing beyond it, the contractor who ‘contracts to provide services in much the same way as a plumber goes to a domestic home to fix the hot water’ (SVE [13.50]), and [the fact showing D1 could pursue his own enterprise] left him free to pursue an enterprise of his own, unlike the Hollis couriers (Hollis 44). The one-off character of the task tells for a contract for services.”

→ B4F. Sort the finding into its column and return to B4 for the totality

B4F The amount of remuneration and how it is paid · when the facts state the rate, the fee or the way [D1] was paid

“[D1] was paid [the rate or fee] on [the basis of payment], ‘the mode of remuneration’ being among the matters Mason J listed (Stevens v Brodribb 24), and whether [D1] could bargain for it matters. The mode of remuneration accordingly points [to employment, or to a contract for services].”

A · remuneration indicates employment

  “[The fact showing whether D1 could bargain for the rate] shows the rate was set for [D1]: in Hollis, Vabu ‘superintended the couriers’ finances’ and there was ‘no scope for the couriers to bargain for the rate of their remuneration’ (Hollis 43–44), and payment ‘per delivery and not per time period engaged’ was still ‘a natural means to remunerate employees whose sole duty is to perform deliveries’ (Hollis 44), so payment by output does not make a contractor. The mode of remuneration tells for employment.”

B · remuneration indicates his own business

  “[The fact showing whether D1 could bargain for the rate] shows [D1] priced [the task D1 was engaged to do] himself, and payment made on completion of the job points to an independent contractor (SVE [13.50]). Unlike Hollis, where Vabu ‘superintended the couriers’ finances’ and left ‘no scope for the couriers to bargain for the rate of their remuneration’ (Hollis 43–44), [D1] kept the commercial terms of his own work, not paid unless he worked. The mode of remuneration tells for a business of [D1]’s own.”

C · a set fee both ways

  “[The rate or fee] taken as a set fee is [D2]’s strongest fact; yet a fixed rate no more makes a contractor than payment by output makes one, payment ‘per delivery and not per time period engaged’ being ‘a natural means to remunerate employees’ (Hollis 44). Decisive is bargaining power: there was ‘no scope for the couriers to bargain for the rate of their remuneration’ (Hollis 43–44), and [the fact showing whether D1 could bargain for the rate]. The better view sorts remuneration to the employee side, not without difficulty.”

→ B4G. Sort the finding into its column and return to B4 for the totality

B4G Does the employer deduct income tax and superannuation · when the facts say what [D2] withheld, deducted or contributed, or that [D1] invoiced and paid his own tax

“[D2] [deducted, or did not deduct] income tax and superannuation from [the rate or fee], [the deduction fact] showing which, ‘the deduction of income tax’ being one of Mason J’s indicia (Stevens v Brodribb 24). The deduction point accordingly favours [employment, or a business of D1’s own].”

A · deductions indicate employment

  “[The deduction fact] shows [D2] deducted income tax and superannuation before paying [D1], and [the other sums D2 deducted or withheld] shows it managed his pay generally, as Vabu did in deducting insurance from the couriers’ wages (Hollis 43–44). Deductions for taxation mark the employment relationship (SVE [13.50]). The deductions tell for employment.”

B · no deductions, independence indicated

  “[The deduction fact] shows [D2] deducted nothing, [D1] rendering [the invoice or statement D1 issued] and managing his own tax obligations, with no sick pay and no holidays provided; ‘the deduction of income tax’ is Mason J’s indicium (Stevens v Brodribb 24) and its absence tells the other way. Unlike the couriers, whose insurance Vabu deducted from their wages (Hollis 43–44), [D1]’s finances were his own. The absence of deductions tells for a contract for services, although a label chosen for tax purposes cannot decide the relationship.”

→ B4H. Sort the finding into its column and return to B4 for the totality

B4H The hours of work and provision of holiday and sick leave · when the facts state rostered hours, minimum hours, leave entitlements or that [D1] chose when to work

“[D1] worked [the hours or roster] and had [the leave], ‘the hours of work and provision of holidays’ being among the indicia (Stevens v Brodribb 24). The hours and leave accordingly point [to employment, or to a contract for services].”

A · hours and leave indicate employment

  “[D2] set [the hours or roster], and [the leave fact] shows [D1] had to ask before taking time off: in Hollis the couriers ‘were required to be at work by 9.00 am’, were rostered by the order in which they signed on, and leave requests had to be made (Hollis 42, 44). The hours and leave tell for employment.”

B · hours and leave indicate his own business

  “[D1] chose [the hours or roster] himself, worked no fixed number of shifts, and [the leave fact] shows no sick pay or holidays and nobody to answer to for time away; workers who determine their own hours point to an independent contractor (SVE [13.50]). Unlike Hollis, where couriers ‘were required to be at work by 9.00 am’ and leave requests had to be made (Hollis 42, 44), [D1] worked when it suited him. The hours and leave tell for a business of [D1]’s own.”

→ B4I. Sort the finding into its column and return to B4 for the totality

B4I The method of termination · when the facts say how the engagement could be ended, by notice, by dismissal, or on completion of the job

“[The termination fact] states how the engagement could be brought to an end, the right to hire and fire being among the relevant factors (SVE [13.50]). The method of termination accordingly points [to employment, or to a contract for services].”

A · termination at D2’s will

  “[The termination fact] shows [D2] could end the engagement at will, and [the fact showing the consequence of refusing work] shows refusal carried the same result: in Hollis couriers were not able to refuse work, Vabu stating that any driver who did so ‘WILL NO LONGER WORK FOR THIS FIRM’ (Hollis 42), dismissal in all but name. [D1] held his engagement on the same footing. The method of termination tells for employment.”

B · termination on completion

  “[The termination fact] shows the engagement ended when [the single job or fixed project] was finished, or on the notice either party could give; nothing resembling dismissal was available to [D2], and the right to hire and fire that marks employment (SVE [13.50]) was absent. Unlike Hollis, where couriers could not refuse work on pain of no longer working for the firm (Hollis 42), [D1] could decline [the task D1 was engaged to do] and keep the relationship. The method of termination tells for a contract for services.”

Sort the finding into its column and return to B4 for the totality · For presentation to the public and deterrence → B5

B5 Hollis v Vabu Pty Ltd (2001) 207 CLR 21 applied · when the multi-factor test is running and the facts give the worker’s public presentation, or a danger the employer knew its workers presented

“Viewed as a practical matter, [D1] [was, or was not] running a business or enterprise of his own: in Hollis, couriers who owned their own bicycles ‘were not running their own business or enterprise, nor did they have independence in the conduct of their operations’ (Hollis 41). [How D1 appeared to the public] shows how [D1] was presented, and [the danger D2 knew its workers presented] what deterrence adds.”

A · presented as D2’s emanation

  “[How D1 appeared to the public] shows [D1] wearing [the uniform or insignia] and known to customers as [D2]: in Hollis the couriers ‘were presented to the public and to those using the courier service as emanations of Vabu’, wearing ‘uniforms bearing Vabu’s logo’ (Hollis 42–43), training, discipline and attire directed by Vabu, so that ‘to its customers they were Vabu’ (Hollis 45). This finding sits on the employee side.”

B · deterrence favours liability

  “[The danger D2 knew its workers presented] was plain to [D2], and [the step D2 could have taken to identify or restrain its workers] was open and untaken: in Hollis the Court relied on ‘the knowledge of Vabu as to the dangers to pedestrians presented by its bicycle couriers and the failure to adopt effective means for the personal identification of those couriers by the public’ (Hollis 43), deterrence of future harm supporting vicarious liability. Holding [D2] answerable would give it reason to take that step; this finding sits on the employee side.”

C · his own enterprise despite the engagement

  “[The fact showing D1’s own business] shows an enterprise of [D1]’s own, [the name, livery or goodwill of his own], unlike couriers presented ‘as emanations of Vabu’ (Hollis 42–43); and no comparable knowledge of danger or failure to identify workers engages deterrence (Hollis 43). [The fact showing the scale of D1’s capital investment] is arguably the more significant investment, needing greater skill and training, that Hollis reserved (Hollis 41–42). These findings sit on the contractor side.”

Sort the findings into their columns and return to B4 for the totality · an employee → 2 · a contractor → B7

B6 Skilled work does not defeat employment · when [D2] argues nobody could direct the manner of [D1]’s work because it is skilled, artistic or professional

“That ‘[the task D1 was engaged to do]’ is skilled assists [D2] little: the control test is ‘unhelpful for professional and skilled employees, such as trapeze artists’ (SVE [13.50]; Zuijs), the emphasis shifting from the actual exercise of control to the right to exercise it, ‘so far as there is scope for it’, even if ‘only in incidental or collateral matters’ (Zuijs 571, in Stevens v Brodribb). Employment accordingly [is, or is not] indicated.”

A · right to control retained

  “[Why D2 retained the right to direct incidental matters] shows [D2] kept the right to control [the incidental or collateral matter D2 could direct], and that the work is skilled does not negate it: the emphasis shifts from exercising control to the right to exercise it, ‘so far as there is scope for it’, even if ‘only in incidental or collateral matters’ (Zuijs 571), the control test being ‘unhelpful for professional and skilled employees, such as trapeze artists’ (SVE [13.50]). Employment is thus arguably indicated.”

B · no right to control

  “[The fact showing D2 could not direct even incidental matters] left even incidental and collateral matters to [D1], and the work being skilled, no scope for a right to control remained: the right must exist ‘so far as there is scope for it’ (Zuijs 571), and here there was none, [why the right to control was absent]. The skilled-work qualification cannot supply a right the engagement withheld. Employment is accordingly likely not indicated on this indicium.”

C · Zuijs both ways

  “Zuijs is analogous because [D1], like the skilled performer, worked beyond [D2]’s practical direction in the doing of ‘[the task D1 was engaged to do]’; yet [the fact showing D2 retained the right to direct incidental matters] does not sit with that, the emphasis resting on the right to exercise control, ‘so far as there is scope for it’, even if ‘only in incidental or collateral matters’ (Zuijs 571). The better view is that employment is indicated, although not without difficulty.”

there is no written contract or its terms are not given → B4 · otherwise → B1

B7 Liability for agents and independent contractors · when d1 is an independent contractor

“[D1] being an independent contractor, no vicarious liability arises: in Sweeney the mechanic did what he did for the respondent’s benefit and in attempted discharge of its contractual obligations, yet ‘not as an employee of the respondent but as a principal pursuing his own business’ (Sweeney 173). Nor does agency assist unless [D1] was engaged ‘to solicit the bringing about of legal relations’, and then only for slanders uttered to persuade the third party (Colonial Mutual, in Sweeney 170).”

A · contractor, no vicarious liability

  “[The fact showing D1 pursued his own business] shows [D1] did ‘[the task D1 was engaged to do]’ on his own account, so no vicarious liability arises: as in Sweeney, where the mechanic acted for the respondent’s benefit and in attempted discharge of its contractual obligations, yet ‘not as an employee of the respondent but as a principal pursuing his own business’ (Sweeney 173). Agency does not assist, Colonial Mutual reaching only a contractor engaged ‘to solicit the bringing about of legal relations’ (Sweeney 170), which ‘[the task D1 was engaged to do]’ was not. Only a non-delegable duty could answer.”

B · agency, confined to Colonial Mutual’s holding

  “[The fact showing D1 was engaged to solicit legal relations] shows [D1] engaged to bring about legal relations between [D2] and [the third party], which is Colonial Mutual’s case: the agent canvassing proposals for life insurance made his principal answer ‘for slanders uttered to persuade the third party to make an agreement with the principal’ (Sweeney 170). The rule reaches no further, the wider proposition that if A ‘represents’ B, B is vicariously liable having been rejected (Sweeney 171–172). [D2] thus arguably answers, but only for words uttered in that solicitation.”

C · Sweeney both ways

  “Sweeney is analogous because [the fact both cases share], [D1] doing ‘[the task D1 was engaged to do]’ for [D2]’s benefit and in discharge of its contractual obligations as the mechanic did, yet ‘as a principal pursuing his own business’ (Sweeney 173); yet [the fact that distinguishes this case] tells against it, suggesting an engagement ‘to solicit the bringing about of legal relations’ (Colonial Mutual, in Sweeney 170). The better view is that no vicarious liability arises, agency reaching only slanders uttered in that solicitation, though [P] will press the point.”

the question asks only about vicarious liability → still run 2 and 3 in the alternative, then the run ends · it asks whether D2 is liable at all → 4 for the non-delegable duty · the finding could go the other way → 2 in the alternative and then to 3

B8 An improper mode of performing an authorised act · when [D1] did the assigned task by a method [D2] forbade, carelessly, or in service of [D2]’s own interests by unauthorised means

“‘[D1’s method]’ [was a forbidden way of doing the task assigned, or was outside it], and a forbidden method is still within the employment. In Cattermoles a garage hand without a licence, instructed to move cars by pushing them, got tired of doing this and drove one instead, damaging another. That was an improper mode of performing the authorised act of moving cars, and so within his course of employment.”

A · improper mode, still employment

  “‘[D1’s method]’ was forbidden but was still the task assigned, [the fact showing D1 was doing the assigned task] showing him doing [the work] set for him. [D1]’s departure from the method he was told to use is no different in kind from the garage hand’s driving of the very cars he was set to move, and [D2] likely answers for it.”

B · outside the employment

  “‘[D1’s method]’ was outside the task assigned, [the fact showing the act was outside the task assigned] showing him doing what [D2] never engaged him to do rather than that work badly. Cattermoles is distinguishable: the garage hand drove the very cars he was set to move, a forbidden method of his own task, whereas [D1] was never set ‘[the task D1 was engaged to do]’ at all, so there is no authorised act for his method to be an improper mode of.”

C · Cattermoles both ways

  “Cattermoles is analogous because [D1], like the garage hand told to push cars who drove one instead, performed the very task he was set by a forbidden method; yet [the act D2 forbade] tells against it, serving [D1]’s own ends rather than the task. The better view is that the method remains within the employment, the departure being one of manner rather than of task, an ‘improper mode of performing an authorised act’ (Bugge; Deatons).”

D · Poland, protecting the employer’s property

  “[D1] acted to protect [D2]’s [property or business interest], as in Poland, where an employee walking home for lunch struck a boy he thought was stealing his employer’s sugar and was held authorised to protect his employer’s business, his blow an improper mode of doing so and within his course of employment. [The fact showing D1 was authorised to protect D2’s interests] supplies the same implied authority, and [the act D1 took] was an improper, though connected, way of exercising it; the act thus arguably stays within the course of employment.”

  “[D1] misused an authorised access, as in French, where a carer of a person with quadriplegia stole $33,350 by misusing her client’s credit card while doing the shopping and was held within her employment, being authorised to use the card when shopping, an improper mode even though it was a criminal act. [D1] was authorised to [the authorised use of D2’s property or system], and [the fact showing D1 misused that authorised access] shows the same improper mode of an authorised act; the act thus arguably stays within the course of employment.”

E · French, an authorised use turned criminal

within employment → 3 · outside → B9 · an intentional criminal act → B10

B9 Deatons v Flew: the limit · when the act looks like a personal quarrel, horseplay, or a frolic of [D1]’s own, or resembles a bouncer or security guard maintaining order

“[D1]’s act [was, or was not] one of personal ‘passion and resentment’, not done in furtherance of the employer’s interests, under his express or implied authority or as an incident to, or in consequence of, anything he was employed to do (Deatons, in PAC [54]): the barmaid who threw a glass at a patron ‘did not throw the beer or glass in the course of maintaining discipline or order, for which she was not in any event authorised’. ‘[The act complained of]’ accordingly [answers that description, or does not].”

A · personal passion, outside employment

  “[The fact showing a private quarrel] shows a private quarrel: [D1]’s act was one of personal ‘passion and resentment’, no incident of anything he was employed to do (Deatons, in PAC [54]). Like the barmaid, who ‘did not throw the beer or glass in the course of maintaining discipline or order, for which she was not in any event authorised’, [D1] held no authority over [the incident giving rise to the act]. The act falls outside the course of employment and [D2] is likely not liable for it.”

B · incident of the work

  “[The fact showing D1 was doing the assigned task when the act occurred] shows the act an incident of the work, not personal ‘passion and resentment’ (Deatons, in PAC [54]). Deatons is distinguishable: the barmaid was ‘not in any event authorised’ to maintain discipline or order, whereas [D1] was authorised to [the disciplinary or protective function D1 was employed to perform] and acted in the course of it. The act stays within the course of employment.”

C · Deatons both ways

  “Deatons is analogous because ‘[the act complained of]’, like the glass thrown at the patron, looks like ‘passion and resentment’ personal to [D1]; yet [the fact tying the act to the assigned task] points the other way, the act done while [doing the task] and unlike the barmaid, who was ‘not in any event authorised’ to maintain order (PAC [54]). The better view is that the act was neither in furtherance of the employer’s interests nor an incident of the employment, although the point is arguable.”

Whether outside or within employment → 3, where the verdict is written

B10 A criminal offence does not preclude vicarious liability · when the act complained of is an intentional criminal act

“A criminal offence does not preclude vicarious liability: for employer liability the conduct must be done in intended performance of the job or in apparent execution of the authority given to [D1] by [D2], and ‘the relevant approach is to consider any special role that the employer has assigned to the employee’, whether its apparent performance gave the ‘occasion’ for the wrongful act, the features including ‘authority, power, trust, control and the ability to achieve intimacy with the victim’ (PAC [81]). Weighed here, it [did, or did not].”

A · role gave the occasion

  “[D2] assigned [D1] [the role], and [the fact showing the role’s apparent performance gave the occasion] shows its apparent performance gave the ‘occasion’ for the wrongful act, not mere opportunity: the role carried ‘authority, power, trust, control and the ability to achieve intimacy with the victim’ (PAC [81]), and [D1] took advantage of the position in which it placed him vis-à-vis [P]. The criminal character of the act does not preclude vicarious liability, and the act was arguably within the course of employment.”

B · no occasion supplied

  “[The fact showing the role gave only opportunity, not occasion] shows [the role] merely put [D1] where [P] was: employment that ‘affords an opportunity’ for the wrongful act is not of itself sufficient, the role’s apparent performance having to give the ‘occasion’ for it (PAC [80]–[81]), and the role carried no relevant ‘authority, power, trust, control and the ability to achieve intimacy with the victim’. The act was likely not within the course of employment, though a non-delegable duty may still be pressed.”

C · Prince Alfred College both ways

  “PAC is analogous because [D2] assigned [D1] [the role], as the College assigned Bain the role of housemaster, placing him ‘in a position of power and intimacy vis-à-vis the respondent’ (PAC [84]); yet [the fact that distinguishes this case] points the other way, the role giving no comparable position over [P]. Whether the apparent performance of the role gave the ‘occasion’ turns on authority, power, trust, control and intimacy (PAC [81]). The better view is that it did, although the point is arguable.”

Whether the role gave occasion or only opportunity → 3 · the victim is a child → B11

B11 Statutory vicarious liability for child abuse in NSW · when [P] is a child and the wrong is sexual or physical abuse by someone working for an organisation

“[D2] is an organisation and [P] a child, so the statutory route runs alongside the common law: liability requires that [D1] be an employee or akin to one (CLA s 6G) and both limbs of s 6H(1), the occasion and the taking of advantage. The section ‘does not affect, and is in addition to, the common law as it applies with respect to vicarious liability’ (CLA s 6H(3)), so the PAC inquiry runs alongside it.”

A · statutory liability arises

  “[D2] is an organisation, [P] a child, and [D1] its employee or akin to one (CLA s 6G). Firstly, the apparent performance of [the role D2 placed D1 in] supplied the occasion for the perpetration of the abuse (s 6H(1)(a)); secondly, [D1] took advantage of that occasion to perpetrate it (s 6H(1)(b)). The limbs are conjunctive and both are met, so [D2] is likely vicariously liable under the statute, the common law claim unaffected (s 6H(3)).”

B · statutory liability does not arise

  “[The fact defeating that limb] defeats [the limb of s 6H(1) that fails]: the apparent performance of the role must supply the occasion (s 6H(1)(a)) and the employee must take advantage of that occasion (s 6H(1)(b)), and the limbs are conjunctive. Statutory vicarious liability accordingly likely does not arise; yet the common law is unaffected (s 6H(3)), so the PAC inquiry remains open.”

→ B11A (for the s 6G threshold → B11B and B11C. → 3) · a non-delegable duty is also pleaded → 4, and if the abuse is an intentional criminal act, to B18

B11A 6G Employees include persons exercising functions akin to employees · when [D1] is not an employee on ordinary principles, or [D2] says he was a contractor or volunteer

“[D1] [is, or is not] akin to an employee of [D2]: an individual is akin to an employee if he ‘carries out activities as an integral part of the activities carried on by the organisation and does so for the benefit of the organisation’ (CLA s 6G(2)), but not if ‘the activities are carried out for a recognisably independent business’ of his own (s 6G(3)(a)). [The activities D1 carried out for D2] decide it.”

A · akin to an employee

  “[The activities D1 carried out for D2] were the very activities [D2] exists to carry on, and [the fact showing the benefit to D2] shows them done for its benefit, so [D1] ‘carries out activities as an integral part of the activities carried on by the organisation and does so for the benefit of the organisation’ (CLA s 6G(2)). Nor were they carried out for ‘a recognisably independent business’ of his own (s 6G(3)(a)), [the fact showing D1 had no recognisably independent business]. He is likely an employee for this Division.”

B · a recognisably independent business

  “[The fact showing D1 had his own independent business] shows [the activities D1 carried out for D2] carried out for a recognisably independent business of [D1]’s, and an individual is not akin to an employee if ‘the activities are carried out for a recognisably independent business of the individual …’ (CLA s 6G(3)(a)). That [D2] benefited is not enough, the definition requiring activities integral to the organisation’s own and done for its benefit (s 6G(2)). [D1] is accordingly likely outside the Division.”

[D1] is an employee or akin to one → B11B. If not, s 6H cannot apply: go back to 2 and B10 for the common law, and to 4 if a non-delegable duty is pleaded

B11B 6H(1)(a): the role supplies the occasion · when [D1] is an employee or akin to one, and the facts describe the role [D2] placed him in

“The apparent performance by [D1] of [the role D2 placed D1 in] [supplies, or does not supply] ‘the occasion for the perpetration of the child abuse’ (CLA s 6H(1)(a)), the court taking into account whether the organisation placed him in a position with ‘authority, power or control over the child’, ‘the trust of the child’ or ‘the ability to achieve intimacy with the child’ (s 6H(2)).”

A · the role supplied the occasion

  “[D2] placed [D1] in [the role D2 placed D1 in], and its apparent performance is what put him with [P] at all: [the fact giving D1 authority] gave ‘authority, power or control over the child’ (s 6H(2)(a)), [the fact giving D1 the child’s trust] ‘the trust of the child’ (s 6H(2)(b)), and [the fact giving D1 intimacy] ‘the ability to achieve intimacy with the child’ (s 6H(2)(c)). The role thus arguably supplied ‘the occasion for the perpetration of the child abuse’ (s 6H(1)(a)).”

B · the role supplied only opportunity

  “[The fact showing mere proximity] shows the role gave [D1] only the chance to be near [P]: it carried no ‘authority, power or control over the child’ (s 6H(2)(a)), not ‘the trust of the child’ (s 6H(2)(b)), and no ‘ability to achieve intimacy with the child’ (s 6H(2)(c)), [the facts denying each]. Mere opportunity is not occasion, so s 6H(1)(a) is likely not met and the statutory claim ends.”

the role supplied the occasion → B11C. If it supplied only opportunity, s 6H(1) fails and the statutory claim ends → B11 for the verdict, then to 3

B11C 6H(1)(b): the employee takes advantage of that occasion · when the role supplied the occasion at B11B, and the facts describe how the abuse was perpetrated

“[D1] [took, or did not take] advantage of that occasion to perpetrate the child abuse on the child (CLA s 6H(1)(b)), [the fact showing how D1 used the occasion] showing how; the limb is separate from s 6H(1)(a) and must be established as well.”

A · advantage taken of the occasion

  “[The fact showing how D1 used the occasion] shows [D1] using the access, authority and privacy [the role] gave him to reach [P], the abuse occurring in the course of [the occasion D1 turned to account], which the role supplied and nothing else did. The employee must take advantage of that occasion ‘to perpetrate the child abuse on the child’ (s 6H(1)(b)); both limbs being met, [D2] is thus arguably vicariously liable under the statute.”

B · no advantage taken

  “[The fact showing the abuse was unconnected to the occasion] shows the abuse owed nothing to [the role]: it occurred at [the place and time of the abuse], away from any occasion the role supplied, and [D1] used nothing the role gave him to bring it about. A role that merely put the two in the same place cannot satisfy s 6H(1)(b), the limbs being conjunctive, so statutory liability likely does not arise.”

→ B11 (for the verdict → to 3) · a non-delegable duty is also pleaded → 4 (B18)

B12 The Act’s limits pass through to the employer · when a CLA provision excludes or limits [D1]’s own liability, such as the volunteer provisions or the professional standards defence: s 5O

“‘[The provision]’ [relieves both, or is not engaged]: ‘Any provision of this Act that excludes or limits the civil liability of a person for a tort also operates to exclude or limit the vicarious liability of another person for that tort’ (CLA s 3C), though s 3C does not apply to non-delegable duties (Woodhouse [249]).”

A · limit passes to employer

  “[The fact satisfying the provision’s own test] brings [D1] within ‘[the provision]’, and the limit passes through: ‘Any provision of this Act that excludes or limits the civil liability of a person for a tort also operates to exclude or limit the vicarious liability of another person for that tort’ (CLA s 3C). No non-delegable duty being pleaded against [D2], ‘[the provision]’ accordingly relieves [D2] as it relieves [D1].”

B · provision not engaged

  “[The fact showing the claim is pleaded as a non-delegable duty] means s 3C cannot relieve [D2]: although any provision excluding or limiting [D1]’s liability also excludes or limits vicarious liability for that tort (CLA s 3C), the section does not apply to non-delegable duties (Woodhouse [249]), and the liability pressed against [D2] is exactly that. ‘[The provision]’ is accordingly not engaged against the non-delegable duty claim.”

→ 3

B13 Control of premises used for a dangerous substance or activity (Burnie) · when the wrong occurred on premises [D2] occupies or controls and the work or thing was dangerous

“[P] was [outside, or on, the premises], and a party in control of premises who takes advantage of that control to introduce a dangerous substance, undertake a dangerous activity, or allow another to do so owes the duty to a person ‘lawfully in a place outside the premises’ (Burnie 551, 557). Whether a lawful visitor is owed it was reserved, although analogy ‘would prima facie indicate that it is’. The claim rests in negligence, not the old rule in Rylands v Fletcher, so no escape from the premises is required (SVE [13.125]).”

A · outside the premises, duty owed

  “[P] was lawfully at [the place], outside the premises, when ‘[the harm]’ occurred, and [D2] took advantage of its control of the premises to [introduce the dangerous substance or undertake the dangerous activity], so the duty is owed to a person ‘lawfully in a place outside the premises’ (Burnie 551, 557). That [the dangerous thing] never in fact escaped the premises does not matter, the claim resting in negligence rather than the old rule in Rylands v Fletcher (SVE [13.125]).”

B · lawful visitor, duty owed

  “[P] was on the premises as a lawful visitor when ‘[the harm]’ occurred, and [D2] took advantage of its control to [introduce the dangerous substance or undertake the dangerous activity]; whether a lawful visitor is owed the duty was reserved in Burnie, but analogy ‘would prima facie indicate that it is’ (Burnie 557), and [the fact exposing P to the danger as a neighbour would be] leaves [P] no less exposed than a neighbour outside. The better view is that the duty is owed, no escape being required in negligence (SVE [13.125]).”

For whether the thing was dangerous → B14 · → 5

B14 What is a dangerous substance or activity · when [D2] says the substance or activity was ordinary

“‘[The thing or activity]’ [answers, or does not answer] the description of a dangerous substance or activity: ‘dangerous’ is not confined to the inherently dangerous, and it suffices that ‘the combined effect of the magnitude of the foreseeable risk of an accident happening and the magnitude of the foreseeable potential injury or damage … is such that an ordinary person acting reasonably would consider it necessary to exercise special care or to take special precautions in relation to it’ (Burnie 558–559).”

A · dangerous, special care required

  “[The magnitude of the foreseeable risk of an accident] together with [the magnitude of the foreseeable injury or damage] called for special precautions, so ‘[the thing or activity]’ is dangerous: the combined effect is ‘such that an ordinary person acting reasonably would consider it necessary to exercise special care’ (Burnie 558–559). A reasonably prudent person then exercises a higher degree of care, the standard, depending on the magnitude of the danger, sometimes ‘a degree of diligence so stringent as to amount practically to a guarantee of safety’ (Burnie 554–555); collateral negligence is no answer.”

B · ordinary care sufficed

  “[The magnitude of the foreseeable risk of an accident] together with [the magnitude of the foreseeable injury or damage] falls short: ordinary care sufficed because [why ordinary care sufficed], and although ‘dangerous’ is not confined to the inherently dangerous, the combined effect must still be one ‘an ordinary person acting reasonably would consider it necessary to exercise special care’ against (Burnie 558–559). The heightened standard verging on ‘a guarantee of safety’ (Burnie 554–555) is not engaged, and ‘[the thing or activity]’ likely does not answer the description.”

→ 5

B15 Schools to students (Commonwealth v Introvigne) · when [D2] is a school, a holiday program, a camp or another body that has taken charge of children

“[D2] having taken charge of ‘[the children]’, the schools to students category applies (Introvigne), and the duty is personal and cannot be discharged by delegation (Lepore 527–528 per Gleeson CJ), the characterisation tracing to the employer’s own ‘personal’ duty requiring ‘the provision of competent staff, adequate material, and a proper system of effective supervision’ (Wilsons & Clyde Coal 84 per Lord Wright, in Lepore).”

A · schools category applies

  “[The fact placing the children in D2’s charge] placed ‘[the children]’ in [D2]’s care, and the schools to students category applies directly (Introvigne). The duty is personal and cannot be discharged by delegation (Lepore 527–528 per Gleeson CJ), requiring ‘the provision of competent staff, adequate material, and a proper system of effective supervision’ (Wilsons & Clyde Coal 84, in Lepore); [D2] cannot discharge it by entrusting supervision to [the delegate], the duty being to see care taken, not to hand it on.”

B · no charge taken

  “[The fact leaving the children with the other party] left ‘[the children]’ with [the other party], so [D2] never took the charge on which the schools to students category rests (Introvigne). A duty of that kind, where owed, is personal and cannot be discharged by delegation (Lepore 527–528 per Gleeson CJ), but that duty was never [D2]’s to begin with, and no non-delegable duty likely arises on this category.”

→ 5 · an intentional criminal act → B18

B16 The relationships the courts have refused · when d2 is a landlord, a road authority, or the occupier of premises the public is admitted to

“‘[D2’s relationship]’ [answers one of the refused relationships, or is distinguishable from all three]: a landlord’s duty to the tenant’s family on the leased property (Northern Sandblasting; Jones v Bartlett), a statutory road authority for work done on a highway or public road on its behalf (Leichhardt), and an occupier’s duty to a visitor to a shopping centre (Bevillesta [53]). Kirby J reasoned that a contractor is normally as well placed as the council to insure and compensate an injured plaintiff, and deterrence of doubtful value where the negligent conduct is an independent contractor’s (Leichhardt [112]–[113]).”

A · a refused relationship

  “[The fact placing D2’s relationship within that refused category] puts ‘[D2’s relationship]’ within [the refused category it matches], and that relationship has been held not to give rise to a non-delegable duty: landlord to the tenant’s family (Northern Sandblasting; Jones v Bartlett), road authority for highway work done on its behalf (Leichhardt), occupier to a shopping-centre visitor (Bevillesta [53]). A contractor is normally as well placed to insure and compensate, and deterrence of doubtful value (Leichhardt [112]–[113]), so no non-delegable duty arises.”

B · distinguishable from all three

  “‘[D2’s relationship]’ is distinguishable from all three refusals. Firstly, [the fact distinguishing D2 from a landlord] shows no landlord’s duty to the tenant’s family (Northern Sandblasting; Jones v Bartlett). Secondly, [the fact distinguishing D2 from a road authority] shows no road authority’s duty for highway work done on its behalf (Leichhardt). Thirdly, [the fact distinguishing D2 from a shopping-centre occupier] shows no occupier’s duty to a shopping-centre visitor (Bevillesta [53]). None answering, the Burnie criteria decide the duty.”

C · Bevillesta both ways

  “Bevillesta is analogous because [P] entered [D2]’s premises as a visitor, and an occupier’s duty to a shopping-centre visitor is not non-delegable (Bevillesta [53]); yet [the fact putting P in D2’s care] does not sit with that, putting [P] in [D2]’s care as no shopping centre takes its visitors. Courts should be reluctant to create exceptional cases of liability for a contractor’s negligence (Leichhardt [98] per Kirby J). The better view is that Bevillesta answers it and no duty arises, although not without difficulty.”

D2’s relationship answers one → no duty and the run ends · an occupier → B17 for the ground of distinction · distinguishable → 5

B17 Occupiers to visitors and third parties: the limit · when [D2] is an occupier sued for a cleaning, security or maintenance contractor’s negligence

“The duty here [is an ordinary occupier’s, or answers Burnie]: occupiers to visitors and third parties is a category, yet an occupier’s duty to a shopping centre visitor is not itself non-delegable (Bevillesta [53]). What Burnie makes non-delegable is not occupation but control of premises taken advantage of to introduce a dangerous substance or undertake a dangerous activity (Burnie 551), courts being reluctant to create exceptional liability for a contractor’s negligence (Leichhardt [98] per Kirby J).”

A · ordinary occupier’s duty

  “[Why D2 merely occupied the premises] shows an ordinary occupier: [the fact showing D2 introduced no dangerous thing or activity] means [D2] introduced no dangerous substance and undertook no dangerous activity, and an occupier’s duty to a shopping centre visitor is not itself non-delegable (Bevillesta [53]). Burnie makes non-delegable not occupation but control taken advantage of to introduce danger (Burnie 551); and courts should be reluctant to create exceptional liability for a contractor’s negligence (Leichhardt [98]). No non-delegable duty arises.”

B · Burnie control answered

  “[Why D2 took advantage of its control] shows more than occupation: [D2] took advantage of its control of the premises to [introduce the dangerous substance or undertake the dangerous activity], which is what Burnie makes non-delegable (Burnie 551). Bevillesta does not reach it, [the dangerous thing or activity D2 introduced] taking the case outside an ordinary occupier’s duty to a visitor, and the reluctance to create exceptional liability (Leichhardt [98]) does not protect a defendant who introduced the danger itself. The duty answers Burnie.”

C · Bevillesta both ways

  “Bevillesta is analogous because [P] came onto the premises as a visitor, and such a duty is not itself non-delegable (Bevillesta [53]); yet [the dangerous thing or activity D2 introduced] tells against it, Burnie making non-delegable the control of premises taken advantage of to introduce a dangerous substance or activity (Burnie 551). The better view is that the duty answers Burnie notwithstanding the reluctance to create exceptional liability (Leichhardt [98] per Kirby J), though it is a close thing.”

an ordinary occupier’s duty → no non-delegable duty and the run ends · a dangerous substance or activity → B13

B18 Does the non-delegable duty extend to intentional criminal acts · when the act said to breach the non-delegable duty is an intentional criminal act

“‘[The act complained of]’ was an intentional criminal act, and that no longer takes it outside the duty: Lepore was overturned on that point (AA), a non-delegable duty being capable of breach by an intentional criminal act where there is a special relationship and a foreseeable risk. The duty accordingly [does, or does not] reach ‘[the act complained of]’.”

A · the duty reaches the act

  “Firstly, [P] had a particular vulnerability and [D2] had assumed a special responsibility with respect to ensuring [P]’s safety in its care, supervision or control. Secondly, the risk of ‘[the harm]’ was reasonably foreseeable to [D2] such that reasonable care could have been taken to guard against it. The duty accordingly arguably reaches ‘[the act complained of]’.”

B · the duty does not reach the act

  “Although Lepore was overturned on that point (AA), the two requirements fail: [the fact denying the special relationship] shows no particular vulnerability and no special responsibility assumed for [P]’s safety in [D2]’s care, supervision or control, and [the fact denying foreseeability of the harm] shows the risk of ‘[the harm]’ was not reasonably foreseeable, ‘there can be no non-delegable duty to prevent harm that is not itself reasonably foreseeable’ (AA [30]). The duty likely does not reach ‘[the act complained of]’.”

C · AA both ways

  “AA is analogous because [the fact both cases share], [P] standing to [D2] as the child stood to the Diocese, whose duty was ‘to ensure that reasonable care was taken to prevent reasonably foreseeable personal injury to the child while under the care, supervision or control of a Priest of the Diocese’ (AA); yet [the fact that distinguishes this case] points the other way, [P] arguably never in [D2]’s care, supervision or control. The better view is that the duty reaches ‘[the act complained of]’, though the point is arguable.”

→ B18A and B18B (for the two requirements → B18C for the breach → 5) · not foreseeable → no duty to prevent it and the run ends

B18A Special Relationship · when b18 is running and the facts describe [P]’s vulnerability and what [D2] had taken on

“[P] [was, or was not] particularly vulnerable, and [D2] [had, or had not] assumed a special responsibility with respect to ensuring the safety of [P] in its care, supervision or control (AA); [the fact establishing or denying the special relationship] decides it.”

A · special relationship made out

  “[What made P particularly vulnerable] made [P] particularly vulnerable, and [the fact showing D2 assumed responsibility for P’s safety] shows [D2] assumed a special responsibility with respect to ensuring [P]’s safety in its care, supervision or control (AA). In AA the Diocese’s duty was to ensure reasonable care was taken to prevent reasonably foreseeable personal injury to the child while under the care, supervision or control of a Priest of the Diocese, and [the fact both cases share] places [P] the same. The special relationship is likely made out.”

B · no special relationship

  “[The fact denying the special relationship] shows [P] stood to [D2] as any [ordinary member of P’s class] does, without particular vulnerability, and [why D2 took on no responsibility for P’s safety] shows no special responsibility assumed for [P]’s safety in its care, supervision or control (AA). Unlike the Diocese in AA, which had the child under the care, supervision or control of its Priest, [D2] had charge of nothing, so the special relationship is likely not made out and the duty does not reach the act.”

the special relationship is made out → B18B for the foreseeable risk · not → no non-delegable duty reaches the act and the run ends

B18B Foreseeable Risk · when the special relationship is made out at B18A, and the question is whether the risk of the harm was foreseeable to [D2]

“The risk of ‘[the harm]’ [was, or was not] reasonably foreseeable to [D2] such that reasonable care could have been taken to guard against that risk (AA), and ‘there can be no non-delegable duty to prevent harm that is not itself reasonably foreseeable’ (AA [30]).”

A · foreseeable risk established

  “[The fact making the risk foreseeable to D2] made the risk of ‘[the harm]’ reasonably foreseeable to [D2], and [the precaution D2 could have taken] was open and untaken: a duty-holder without personal fault is not incapable ‘of taking steps to minimise the risk that the delegate might fail to take reasonable care including by intentional criminal acts’, for ‘[i]f harm is reasonably foreseeable the risk of that harm usually can be avoided or minimised’ (AA [30]). The requirement is likely met.”

B · risk not reasonably foreseeable

  “[The fact denying foreseeability of the harm] put nothing before [D2] to notice: the risk of ‘[the harm]’ was not reasonably foreseeable, and ‘there can be no non-delegable duty to prevent harm that is not itself reasonably foreseeable’ (AA [30]), so no reasonable care could have been called for to guard against it. Foreseeability is the gateway; the requirement is likely not met, and no non-delegable duty reaches ‘[the act complained of]’.”

the risk was reasonably foreseeable → B18C for the breach, then to 5 · not → no duty to prevent it and the run ends

B18C AA v Trustees of the Roman Catholic Church [2026] HCA 2 · when a non-delegable duty is owed and the breach alleged is the delegate’s own intentional or criminal conduct

“[The delegate] [failed, or did not fail] to take reasonable care, and ‘a non-delegable duty of care can be breached either by the duty-holder personally failing to take reasonable care or by the delegate not taking reasonable care’ (AA [30]); it is nevertheless ‘not right to conceive of the non-delegable duty as imposing absolute liability’ (AA [30]).”

A · breach through the delegate

  “[The delegate] committed ‘[the act complained of]’ against [P] while performing [the work or task entrusted], and that is itself the breach: ‘an intentional criminal act of a delegate which injures the person to whom the duty-holder owes the non-delegable duty is necessarily a failure by the delegate to take reasonable care and therefore a failure by the duty-holder to ensure that reasonable care is taken’ (AA [30]). That [D2] was not personally at fault is no answer, although liability is not absolute; [D2] is thus arguably in breach.”

B · no default by delegate or duty-holder

  “[The fact showing no default by delegate or duty-holder] shows no default: [the delegate] performed [the work or task entrusted] with care and ‘[the harm]’ came from [the source of the harm]. It is ‘not right to conceive of the non-delegable duty as imposing absolute liability’ (AA [30]): the duty-holder cannot be liable unless either it personally or the delegate ‘has defaulted in the taking of reasonable care’ (AA [30]). [D2] is accordingly likely not in breach.”

→ B18A (B18B for the two requirements → to 5) · the duty is made out → B19 states the extent of liability

B19 5Q Liability based on non-delegable duty · when a non-delegable duty is made out, whether the wrongdoer was an employee, a contractor or another delegate: s 5Q(1) speaks of ‘any work or…

“Therefore, the better view is that [D2] owed [P] a non-delegable duty, both of Burnie’s criteria being answered, and its liability for ‘[P’s injury]’ is determined ‘as if the liability were the vicarious liability of the defendant for the negligence of the person in connection with the performance of the work or task’ (CLA s 5Q(1)), applying ‘whether or not it is an action in negligence, despite anything to the contrary in section 5A’ (s 5Q(2)). Even where the delegated work is dangerous enough to found the duty, it must still be shown that it was negligent to do it in the circumstances (Bootle [34]), though the delegate need not itself be liable (Woodhouse [223]–[243], [258]).”

A · liable under s 5Q

  “[What the delegate did or failed to do] on [the task D1 was engaged to do] fell below the standard, and that showing is made even for dangerous work such as crop spraying (Bootle [34]). That [the delegate] may itself escape liability does not matter: in Woodhouse the Rural Fire Service was statutorily immune for its authorised burnoff, yet the landowners remained liable on their non-delegable duty (Woodhouse [223]–[243], [258]). Liability is determined ‘as if the liability were the vicarious liability of the defendant’ (CLA s 5Q(1)), so [D2] is likely liable for ‘[P’s injury]’.”

B · delegate not shown negligent

  “[Why the delegate’s conduct met the standard] means no negligence is shown, and even dangerous delegated work, such as crop spraying, founds no liability unless it was negligent to do it in the circumstances (Bootle [34]). Had negligence been shown, liability would have been determined ‘as if the liability were the vicarious liability of the defendant’ (CLA s 5Q(1)), applying whether or not the action is in negligence (s 5Q(2)); it is not, so [D2] is likely not liable for ‘[P’s injury]’.”

Only one defendant can be vicariously liable for the same tort (Day [23]–[33]) · there is a second wrongdoer whose liability rests on a separate relationship, a non-delegable duty, or its own negligence → 1 · Otherwise the run ends

BREACH OF STATUTORY DUTY

Approach to BSD problem questions

The flow chart as given in Class 14.

+---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| 1. Does the legislation intend to confer on the P a right to sue?                                                   |
|                                                                                                                     |
|   ▪ Is there a penalty imposed? (O’Connor, Cutler)                                                                  |
|                                                                                                                     |
|   ▪ Is the legislation for the benefit of a defined class or the public at large? (Cutler)                          |
|                                                                                                                     |
|   ▪ Is the duty specific or general? (Cutler)                                                                       |
|                                                                                                                     |
|   ▪ Are there adequate alternative remedies? (see textbook)                                                         |
|                                                                                                                     |
|   ▪ What is the subject matter of the statute? e.g related to health, education or welfare or policy (see textbook) |
|                                                                                                                     |
| 2. Is the P a member of a class of persons protected by the legislation? (Pask)                                     |
|                                                                                                                     |
| 3. Is the statute directed at the kind of harm suffered by the P? (Mummery)                                         |
|                                                                                                                     |
| 4. Was the statutory duty imposed on the D? (Darling Island, Cubillo)                                               |
|                                                                                                                     |
| 5. Was the statute breached? (Galashiels)                                                                           |
|                                                                                                                     |
| 6. Did the breach cause the injury? (John Pfeiffer)                                                                 |
+=====================================================================================================================+
+---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

[]

BREACH OF STATUTORY DUTY: SCAFFOLD

1 Step 1. Does the statute confer on P a private right to sue?

“The provision saying nothing of damages, an action ‘will lie’ at the suit of one ‘for whose benefit and protection’ the duty was imposed, ‘prima facie, and, if there be nothing to the contrary’ (Groves 415–16 per Vaughan Williams LJ), where the obligation is ‘intended to provide a ground of civil liability when the breach of the obligation causes injury or damage of a kind against which the statute was designed to afford protection’ (Byrne 424). Firstly, intention is ‘ascribed to the legislature as a result of presumptions or by reference to matters governing the policy of the provision rather than the meaning of the instrument’ (O’Connor 477–8 per Dixon J), so the presumptions raised on these facts must each be weighed before question 1 is answered. An earlier failed negligence claim on the same facts does not bar this one, Darling Island itself following a failed negligence action against the same employer (SVE [15.10]).”

A · the Groves presumption is engaged

  “Firstly, the provision commands [the act or state of affairs the provision commands] for the protection of ‘[the words in the provision naming the class P belongs to]’, and [P] belongs to that class, so the Groves presumption is engaged. The presumptions must nonetheless be weighed, and the tally decides question 1.”

B · the implication is not readily made

  “Firstly, construction ‘rarely yield[s] a necessary implication positively giving a civil remedy’ (O’Connor 477–8 per Dixon J). Groves is distinguishable, its fencing duty cast for the very employee the machinery injured, whereas [the words showing the provision serves everyone] casts this one wider. The tally decides question 1.”

the duty is in a regulation, an award or another instrument → B7 first · Then the presumptions in order, writing only those the facts answer, each run to a yes or no: if a penalty is stated → B1; if no penalty is stated → B1B; if the duty touches health, welfare or education → B5D; if prosecution, appeal, review or complaint machinery is shown → B4; if a defined class or the public at large is shown → B2; if the duty relates to safety → B5A; if specific or general is shown → B3; if wider subject matter is shown → B5 · an object clause is printed → B6 · → 1B

1A Does the statute expressly state that there is a private right to sue?

“[P] sues [D] in breach of statutory duty (‘BSD’) upon ‘[the provision P relies on, quoted verbatim from the facts]’, which [does, or does not] expressly state that there is a private right to sue. Express conferral is straightforward but rare, statutes ‘almost never state an intention to give a private remedy for damages for breach’ (SVE [15.20]), the Competition and Consumer Act 2010 (Cth) s 82(1) and the Strata Titles Management Act 2015 (NSW) s 106(5) being the exceptions. Where the words do not say so, question 1 turns on the presumptions.”

A · expressly conferred

  “[P] sues [D] in breach of statutory duty (‘BSD’), and ‘[the words of the provision conferring the right to sue, quoted verbatim]’ expressly confers a private right to sue, naming both the person who may recover and the loss recoverable, so question 1 is arguably answered yes without any presumption. Section 82(1) is analogous, likewise saying in terms who may recover, as is s 106(5) for an owners corporation’s failure to repair. [P] must still answer questions 2 to 6.”

B · no express conferral

  “[P] sues [D] in breach of statutory duty (‘BSD’) upon ‘[the provision P relies on, quoted verbatim from the facts]’, which commands [the act or state of affairs the provision commands] and says nothing of damages, which is the ordinary case (SVE [15.20]). Section 82(1) is distinguishable, naming the person who may recover, whereas [the Act P relies on, by short title] leaves the remedy to construction. Question 1 turns on the presumptions.”

If the words expressly confer a right to sue, question 1 is answered yes; record it → 2 · otherwise → 1

1B Presumptions of statutory intention

“The presumptions are tallied, each yes or no toward a private right: a penalty imposed [yes, or no] (Cutler, O’Connor). A duty relating to health, welfare or education [yes, or no] (SVE [15.60]). Adequate alternative remedies [yes, or no] (SVE [15.40]). A defined class rather than the public at large [yes, or no] (Cutler). A duty relating to safety [yes, or no] (O’Connor). A specific rather than general duty [yes, or no] (Cutler). None is decisive, ‘the various presumptions are not particularly helpful in determining legislative intent’ (SVE [15.25]), much of it ‘pure fiction’ (Prosser, in SVE [15.20]). What governs is ‘what inference really arises, on a balance of considerations, from the nature, scope and terms of the statute, including the nature of the evil against which it is directed, the nature of the conduct prescribed, the pre-existing state of the law’ (Sovar 405 per Kitto J). Question 1 is accordingly [answered yes, or no].”

A · a private right is conferred

  “The tally runs [the count, yeses to noes], [the presumptions favouring a private right, named] carrying the construction and [the presumptions telling against, named] not displacing them (SVE [15.25]). On Kitto J’s balance of considerations, [the feature of this statute that most strongly indicates a private right] weighs heaviest. Question 1 is arguably answered yes.”

B · no private right is conferred
