  “The tally runs [the count, noes to yeses], [the presumptions telling against a private right, named] carrying the construction and [the presumptions favouring one, named] unable to outweigh them (SVE [15.25]). On Kitto J’s balance of considerations, [the feature of this statute that most strongly indicates a public scheme] weighs heaviest, leaving enforcement to [the enforcement machinery the Act provides]. Question 1 is likely answered no.”

C · the presumptions pull apart

  “The tally divides, [the presumptions favouring a private right, named] pointing to a remedy and [the presumptions telling against a private right, named] pulling the other way. Sovar’s balance of considerations (405 per Kitto J) resolves it. Therefore, the better view is that question 1 is answered yes, [the feature of this statute that most strongly indicates a private right] outweighing the rest, though it is a close thing.”

record the answer → 2 · Never stop at a ‘no’ here; → 2 whatever the answer

2 Step 2. Is P a member of a class of persons protected by the legislation? (Pask)

“Secondly, [P] ‘must show membership of that class’ (SVE [15.105]), and [P], ‘[P’s status in the statute’s own words]’, [answers that description, or stands one step removed]. Pask asks whether the benefit runs to someone other than the person the prohibition names, ‘a person shall not knowingly supply any firearm … or ammunition to or for the use of a prevented person’ being pressed by the 13 year old friend of the son to whom the gun was supplied, rather than by the prevented person himself, the action being among the rare instances held available (SVE [15.05]). Read v Croydon poses the same question for someone standing behind the person the statute names, ‘pure and wholesome water to all the inhabitants of the town who shall be willing to pay a water rate for the same’ being pressed by the ratepayer’s 15 year old daughter, who paid nothing. Whether she was within the class is the very issue the statute’s words must answer.”

A · within the protected class

  “Secondly, [the fact placing P within the statute’s own description of the class] makes [P] one the duty served. Pask is analogous, the benefit there likewise running past the person the prohibition named (SVE [15.05]). [P] answers the statutory description directly, standing closer to the words than that friend did, so question 2 is arguably answered yes.”

B · outside the protected class

  “Secondly, [the fact showing the duty was cast for others, not P] leaves [P] outside the protected class. A fireman cannot rely on safety regulations concerning electricity switches passed for the benefit of persons employed in a building (Hartley v Mayoh; SVE [15.105]), nor a sub-contractor on regulations protecting a ‘person employed’ (Herbert v Harold Shaw). [P] stands to this duty as they stood to those, [the person or capacity standing between P and the statutory class] intervening, so question 2 is likely answered no.”

C · Pask both ways

  “Secondly, Pask is analogous because [the feature P shares with the injured friend in Pask], [P] standing to ‘[the words of the provision naming the class]’ as that friend stood to the prohibition on supplying ‘a prevented person’. Yet [the feature separating P from that friend] tells against it, raising the question Read v Croydon poses of one standing behind the ratepaying ‘inhabitants of the town’. Therefore, the better view is that ‘[P’s status in the statute’s own words]’ answers the description and [P] is within the class, although not without difficulty.”

P’s own conduct put D in breach → B8 · record the answer → 3

3 Step 3. Is the statute directed at preventing the kind of harm suffered by the P? (Mummery)

“Thirdly, [P] ‘must prove that the damage suffered was within the risk at which the statute is directed’ (SVE [15.105]), and ‘[P’s injury, in the words the statute uses for the mischief]’ [is that risk, or is not]. Gorris is the illustration, an order under the Contagious Diseases (Animals) Act 1869 (UK) requiring ‘sheep pens are required on deck’, the sheep being washed overboard instead, and the claim failing because the provisions aimed ‘not at protection from perils of the sea, but prevention of the spread of disease’ (SVE [15.105]).”

A · harm within the risk

  “Thirdly, ‘[P’s injury, in the words the statute uses for the mischief]’ is the very evil the provision names, [why that injury is the mischief the statute names]. Gorris is distinguishable, its pens guarding against disease rather than the sea, whereas [the immediate physical cause of P’s injury] is what these words guard against. Question 3 is arguably answered yes.”

B · harm outside the risk

  “Thirdly, ‘[P’s injury, in the words the statute uses for the mischief]’ is not the evil the provision names, [why that injury is not the mischief the statute names]. Gorris is analogous, drowned sheep founding nothing under pens aimed at preventing the spread of disease. So too Heyman, protection from ‘mere economic loss’ being ‘no part of the purpose for which the relevant legislative powers and functions were conferred’ (Heyman 509 per Deane J). Question 3 is likely answered no.”

C · Gorris both ways

  “Thirdly, Gorris is analogous because [the feature of P’s loss that falls outside the statute’s evil], the pens there being aimed at disease rather than the sea, so a loss the words never contemplated founded nothing. Yet [why that injury is the mischief the statute names] weakens this, bringing ‘[P’s injury, in the words the statute uses for the mischief]’ back within the risk at which the statute is directed. Therefore, the better view is that [P]’s harm is the statute’s mischief, though it is a close thing.”

the harm answers the statute’s evil but the injury arrived indirectly, through an act of a third party, or otherwise by a route the words may not reach → 3B. record the answer → 4

3B Step 3. Is the statute directed at preventing the kind of harm suffered by the P?

“[the manner in which P’s injury came about] matters at this step because ‘the construction of the statute may itself provide the court with a means of introducing a limitation on the manner in which the injury must occur for liability to arise’ (SVE [15.105]). Mummery is the illustration, ‘Every occupier of a factory shall provide guards for … all dangerous parts of the machinery of the factory …’ (Factories and Shops Act 1928 (Vic) s 59(1)(a)) being held, 4:1, not to ‘impose an obligation to guard against dangerous materials being ejected from the machine while in motion’ (SVE [15.105]), the duty being directed at contact with the dangerous parts themselves rather than at less direct risks of the equipment, so wood flung from an unguarded circular saw fell outside it. The manner of occurrence [is within the provision, or is not].”

A · the manner is within the provision

  “[the manner in which P’s injury came about] is the manner the provision addresses, [the mechanism the words name]. Mummery is distinguishable, the harm there arriving by a mechanism the fencing words never touched, the guarding duty not reaching materials ejected from the machine. Nothing here is so indirect; the manner of occurrence is arguably within the provision.”

B · the manner falls outside

  “[the manner in which P’s injury came about] is not the manner the provision addresses, construction introducing exactly that limitation on the manner of injury. Mummery is analogous, the injury there also arriving at one remove, the fencing duty directed at contact with the dangerous parts themselves, not at wood flung from the saw. As in Mummery, where there was no direct evidence of how the accident happened, [what is unproved about how the injury came about] is unproved. The manner of occurrence likely falls outside.”

C · Mummery both ways

  “Mummery is analogous because [the feature making P’s injury an indirect one], its fencing duty reaching contact with the dangerous parts themselves rather than materials ejected from the machine (SVE [15.105]). Yet [the feature bringing P’s injury back within the words] tells against it, and Mummery was a 4:1 decision on a record with no direct evidence of how the accident happened, unlike this one. Therefore, the better view is that the manner of occurrence is within the provision, although finely balanced.”

record the answer → 4

4 Step 4. Is the statutory duty imposed on the nominated D? (Darling Island; Cubillo)

“Fourthly, ‘[the words of the provision naming who must act]’ [name D, or name another], the words being read against each defendant separately. Darling Island is the illustration, ‘person-in-charge’, defined by reg 4 as ‘any person directly or indirectly in control’, being ‘quite inapt to make such a person include the employer of the person actually in control of the work’ (per Williams J), reg 31’s precautions falling ‘aptly to be observed by the person actually on the spot and in control of the operations’. Statutes differ in whom they name, the duty lying variously on the ‘building owner’ (Anderson), the ‘person causing the excavation to be made’ (Pantalone), or the builder rather than the owner of the land (Stoneman) (SVE [15.100]). The duty accordingly [lies on this D, or on another].”

A · the duty lies on D

  “Fourthly, ‘[the words of the provision naming who must act]’ name [D], [the fact showing D answers the statutory description], no one narrower being on the spot and in control. Darling Island is distinguishable, its definition inapt to reach the employer of the person actually in control, whereas these words name [D] itself. The duty arguably lies on [D].”

B · the words name another

  “Fourthly, ‘[the words of the provision naming who must act]’ name [the person the provision names, and their relationship to D] rather than [D], each defendant being read against the words separately. Darling Island is analogous, the precautions there falling to the person actually on the spot and in control rather than to the employer (per Williams J); Cubillo likewise, its duty lying on officials ‘personally and by virtue of their office’. That does not end the claim, [D] possibly answering vicariously for the duty holder’s breach.”

C · Darling Island both ways

  “Fourthly, Darling Island is analogous because [the feature making another person, not D, the one on the spot], its precautions falling to the person actually on the spot and in control (per Williams J). Yet [the fact showing no person narrower than D is on the spot and in control] does not sit with that, and Cohen shows the opposite vice, no action lying where a statute imposes a duty on everyone (Cohen [44]). Therefore, the better view is that the words name [D], although finely balanced.”

the words confer a power, a discretion, an object or a statement of principle only → 4B. If the provision names D’s servant, officer or foreman rather than D → 4C. record the answer → 5

4B There must be an express statutory duty

“‘[the words said to impose the duty]’ [command D, or do no more than empower or declare], and ‘[t]here must be an express statutory duty’ (SVE [15.15]; Stuart [109]–[110] per Gummow, Hayne and Heydon JJ). AS v Minister is the contrary illustration, the detention of children as a last resort under s 4AA of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth) being a statement of principle not a statutory duty (SVE [15.15]). So is Cubillo, no express duty being imposed on the Director as Guardian of Aboriginal Children, and Lazarus, there being no mandatory duty to investigate (SVE [15.15]).”

A · an express statutory duty

  “‘[the words said to impose the duty]’ command [the act or state of affairs the provision commands] and leave [D] no choice about it. AS v Minister is distinguishable, its words setting a policy to pursue where these prescribe the act itself; Cubillo likewise. The words arguably impose an express statutory duty.”

B · a power, a discretion or a statement of principle

  “‘[the words said to impose the duty]’ do no more than [what the words actually authorise or declare], leaving [D] to choose. AS v Minister is analogous, its words likewise setting an aspiration rather than a command, as is Lazarus. Question 4 is likely answered no, although a negligence claim on the same facts is unaffected.”

C · Stuart and AS v Minister both ways

  “‘[the words said to impose the duty]’ read as a command because [the feature of the words that makes them mandatory], satisfying Stuart’s requirement ([109]–[110]). Yet [the feature of the words that leaves D a choice] points the other way, as in AS v Minister. Therefore, the better view is that the words impose an express statutory duty, though the point is arguable.”

If the words impose no express duty, question 4 is answered no; record it → 5 · otherwise → 4

4C Vicarious liability for breach of statutory duty

“The duty lying on [the person the provision names, and their relationship to D], ‘the employer could not be made liable for the breach by his servant of a duty imposed by a statute or regulation on the servant and not on the employer’ (Darling Island per Williams J). Yet in New South Wales ‘the Law Reform (Vicarious Liability) Act 1983 (NSW), s 7 reverses the effect of Darling Island Stevedoring v Long … on the issue of vicarious liability’, although ‘the common law position continues to apply in other states’ (SVE [15.100]). A statutory duty of [D]’s own is by contrast non-delegable if unqualified in its terms, liability not being avoided ‘by showing that the conduct constituting the breach was the act of another’ (SVE [15.10]; Lochgelly; Kirk [10]). [D] accordingly [answers for the breach, or does not].”

A · D answers for the servant’s breach

  “Section 7 of the Law Reform (Vicarious Liability) Act 1983 (NSW) governs here, and [the fact placing the breach within the course of that person’s employment] places the breach in the course of employment. [D] arguably answers for the breach.”

B · the duty lies on the servant alone

  “The breach occurring in [the State whose law governs the claim], where the common law position continues to apply, s 7 does not assist [P]. [D] likely does not answer for it; [P] may sue the duty holder.”

record the answer → 5

5 Step 5. Has the statute been breached? (Galashiels)

“Fifthly, ‘[the verb and any qualifier the provision uses]’ settles the standard: whether a defendant is liable for a breach, regardless of intention or negligence, depends on the statute, and strict liability applies in many cases (Galashiels; SVE [15.110]). Part 1A of the Civil Liability Act 2002 (NSW) (‘CLA’) applies only to ‘any claim for damages for harm resulting from negligence, regardless of whether the claim is brought in tort, in contract, under statute or otherwise’ (CLA s 5A(1)), and not to civil liability ‘excluded from the operation of this Part by section 3B’ (CLA s 5A(2)), s 3B(1)(f) excluding liability relating to an award to which Division 3 of Part 5 of the Workers Compensation Act 1987 applies. Where [P] is an employee suing an employer that exclusion often decides the point, ‘[t]ypically employee claims against the employer are not subject to the civil liability legislation’ (SVE [15.80]). The standard governing breach is accordingly [the terms of the statute, or ss 5B and 5D].”

A · the statute’s own terms govern

  “Fifthly, ‘[the verb and any qualifier the provision uses]’ commands [the state of affairs the provision commands] whatever care [D] took, a statute of the kind imposing strict liability regardless of intention or negligence (Galashiels; SVE [15.110]). This is accordingly no claim for damages for harm resulting from negligence, and [P] suing as an employee would put it beyond Part 1A anyway (SVE [15.80]). Breach is tested on the terms of the statute.”

B · ss 5B and 5D govern

  “Fifthly, ‘[the verb and any qualifier the provision uses]’ asks what care [D] took rather than commanding a result, and [what P says D failed to do] is want of that care, which s 5A(1) answers in substance. Nothing in s 3B excludes it (CLA s 5A(2)). Breach and causation are assessed under ss 5B and 5D.”

the claim is in substance one for damages for harm resulting from negligence → B14 · D is a public or other authority → B13 · → 5B

5B Step 5. Has the statute been breached?

“The command [was obeyed, or was not], [the fact showing the commanded state of affairs did not exist]. Galashiels is the illustration, s 22(1) of the Factories Act 1937 (UK) requiring every hoist or lift to be ‘properly maintained’ and s 152(1) defining ‘maintained’ as ‘maintained in an efficient state, in efficient working order, and in good repair’. The words were ‘imperative’, nothing in the Act leading ‘your Lordships to infer any qualification upon that absolute obligation’ (per Lord Morton), so an unexplained brake failure was breach, the lift not in efficient ‘state’, the workman needing ‘only prove that the mechanism failed to work efficiently and this failure caused the accident’. The statute accordingly [was, or was not] breached.”

A · the statute was breached

  “[the fact showing the commanded state of affairs did not exist] answers this question, the words commanding a state of affairs and admitting no exception. Galashiels is analogous, the failure there likewise unexplained yet the obligation absolute. [P] need show no step [D] failed to take. The statute was arguably breached.”

B · the statute was not breached

  “[the fact showing D did what the qualified words required] answers this question, ‘[the words in the provision that qualify the command]’ conditioning what [D] had to do. Galashiels is distinguishable, its words unqualified and absolute, whereas these words leave room those did not. The command being satisfied, no breach arises on the statute’s terms, and [what P says D failed to do] is in truth an allegation of want of care. The statute was likely not breached.”

C · Galashiels both ways

  “Galashiels is analogous because [the fact showing the commanded state of affairs did not exist], the words commanding a state of affairs as ‘shall be properly maintained’ did. Yet ‘[the words in the provision that qualify the command]’ is the strongest counter, leaving [D] room the Galashiels words did not, Austral Bronze reading ‘all floors … shall be of sound construction and properly maintained’ with qualification (SVE [15.110]). Therefore, the better view is that the statute was breached, though it is a close thing.”

the words are imperative and unqualified → B12 · ‘dangerous’ → B9 · ‘permitted’ or ‘allowed’ → B10 · ‘practicable’ or ‘reasonably practicable’ → B11 · ‘safe’ → B11B. record the answer → 6

6 Step 6. Did the breach of the statute cause the harm? (John Pfeiffer)

“Sixthly, [P] ‘must make it appear at least that on a balance of probabilities the breach of duty caused or materially contributed to his injury’ (Bonnington 620), and [the fact tying the breach to the injury] [answers that, or does not]. Where the claim is in substance one for damages for harm resulting from negligence, causation is instead determined under s 5D of the CLA (SVE [15.85]). Causation is accordingly [made out, or not].”

A · causation made out

  “Sixthly, [the fact tying the breach to the injury] shows the breach caused or materially contributed to [P]’s injury, proved ‘by the ordinary standard of proof in civil actions’ (Bonnington 620), and the same fact would answer the factual causation limb of s 5D were the claim in substance one for negligence. Causation is arguably made out.”

B · causation not made out

  “Sixthly, [the step between D’s breach and P’s injury that the breach does not explain] stands between breach and injury, and arguably [the precaution the provision commanded] might not have prevented the accident, leaving the Bonnington standard unmet. John Pfeiffer is distinguishable, the unqualified operator there being the very thing that produced the injury, whereas the injury here would have occurred whatever [D] did. Causation is likely not made out.”

the accident is of the kind the statutory duty was designed to prevent → 6B for the causal inference · D disputes the foreseeability of the particular injury → B16 · → 6C

6B Step 6. Did the breach of the statute cause the harm?

“‘[i]n practice at least an inference of causality is easily drawn when the accident is one which the statutory duty was designed to prevent’ (SVE [15.115]; Quigley 584), and [the accident, in the words of the statute’s mischief] [is such an accident, or is not]. John Pfeiffer is the illustration, ‘no person shall employ, instruct or allow any person to use a tool in any work without first ensuring that such person is a qualified operator and is not unfit to use such tool’ being breached when a 16 year old workmate, no qualified operator, fired the Ramset explosive-powered gun that struck the plaintiff. Such provisions found a prima facie presumption that an unqualified operator has caused the injury (per Mason J), and Murphy J saw the causal link wherever damage results from the use of the tool, negligently used or not. The inference [is drawn, or is not].”

A · the inference is drawn

  “[the accident, in the words of the statute’s mischief] is exactly the accident ‘[the provision, by number and its opening words]’ was designed to prevent, so the inference of causality is easily drawn (Quigley 584). John Pfeiffer is analogous, the breach there consisting in the very thing that produced the injury, founding a prima facie presumption of causation (per Mason J). The inference is arguably drawn.”

B · no inference arises

  “[the accident, in the words of the statute’s mischief] is not the accident ‘[the provision, by number and its opening words]’ was designed to prevent, [why the accident falls outside the mischief the duty addresses], the inference being ‘easily drawn’ only for such an accident (SVE [15.115]). John Pfeiffer is distinguishable, the unqualified operator there being the whole of the breach and the whole of the cause, whereas [the step between D’s breach and P’s injury that the breach does not explain] intervenes here. Causation must be proved without any inference.”

C · John Pfeiffer both ways

  “John Pfeiffer is analogous because [the feature of the breach that makes it the very thing that produced the injury], the unqualified operator there founding a prima facie presumption of causation (per Mason J). Yet [the step between D’s breach and P’s injury that the breach does not explain] tells against it, and arguably [the precaution the provision commanded] might not have prevented the accident. Murphy J’s wider view, the link arising wherever damage results from the use of the tool, favours [P]. Therefore, the better view is that the inference is drawn.”

D disputes the foreseeability of the particular injury → B16 · → 6C

6C We need a ‘YES’ response to EACH question to establish BSD

“Establishing BSD needs a yes to each of the six questions, the language of the statute being central at every step. Questions 1 to 6 having been answered [the answers to questions 1 to 6, in order], [D] [is liable to P, or is not, the claim failing at the question named].”

A · all six answered yes

  “Questions 1 to 6 are each answered yes. [D] is accordingly arguably liable to [P] for ‘[P’s injury, in the words the statute uses for the mischief]’, the strongest limb being [the question on which P’s case is strongest, by number and in a phrase] and the weakest [the question on which P’s case is weakest, by number and in a phrase], where [D] will press. ‘[the words of the provision that carry P’s case]’ carries [P] through each step.”

B · the claim fails at one question

  “Questions [the questions answered yes, listed] are answered yes, yet [the question answered no, by number and in a phrase] is not, and a yes is needed to each question. The claim likely fails at that question alone, [why that question is answered no, in a phrase] defeating it, though the same facts found the negligence claim, the statutory breach standing as evidence that [D] has been negligent (SVE [15.10]).”

a defence is raised against the claim → B15 · a second provision or a second defendant is not yet run → 1A

B16 Remoteness: no further foreseeability rule once the statute’s harm is shown · when causation is made out at 6 or 6B, and D argues that the particular way or extent of P’s injury could not have been…

“Once damage of the kind the statute contemplates is shown, ‘in the case of safety statutes at any rate, there is no further remoteness of damage rule to be applied which requires that the damage must be of a foreseeable kind’ (SVE [15.120]; Grant; Littler). [D]’s argument that [the extent or manner of P’s injury D says was unforeseeable] could not have been foreseen accordingly [fails, or must be answered on its own footing].”

A · the rule answers D’s argument

  “[D] argues that [the extent or manner of P’s injury D says was unforeseeable] could not have been foreseen, but no such showing is required once damage of the statute’s own kind is shown (SVE [15.120]; Grant; Littler). [P]’s injury being that kind, established at question 3, [D]’s argument fails.”

B · the qualification does not reach this statute

  “The relaxed rule is stated only for safety statutes (SVE [15.120]), and ‘[the provision, by number and its opening words]’ is [not a safety statute, or a provision D disputes that characterisation of]. Ordinary remoteness principles accordingly still apply, and [the extent or manner of P’s injury D says was unforeseeable] must independently be shown to have been a foreseeable kind of harm before [D] answers for it.”

→ 6C

B1 Is there a penalty imposed? (Cutler, O’Connor) · when the facts state a penalty for breach

“‘[the maximum penalty in figures, and what it penalises]’ may presume itself the only remedy, an Act which creates an obligation and enforces the performance in a specified manner being, on the view derived from Lord Tenterden CJ, unenforceable in any other manner (Doe d Murray 859; SVE [15.35]). Yet that presumption ‘has not prevented the courts finding an appropriate legislative intention when dealing with industrial safety statutes’ (SVE [15.35]): ‘the comparative smallness of the statutory penalty’ does not operate ‘as a factor against the possibility of an action’, it being ‘hardly to be supposed’ that a breach ‘of so fundamental a safety provision … should be visited only by liability to a penalty not exceeding £20’ (O’Connor). This limb of the tally accordingly runs [yes toward a private right, or no].”

A · the penalty tells for a right to sue

  “‘[the maximum penalty in figures, and what it penalises]’ is trifling beside [the harm the provision exists to prevent], and O’Connor is analogous, a £20 maximum there not defeating the action upon so fundamental a safety provision. Groves succeeded although its Act let the penalty be applied for the injured person’s benefit (SVE [15.35]). The penalty accordingly favours a private right of action.”

B · the penalty is the only remedy

  “‘[the maximum penalty in figures, and what it penalises]’ is substantial and answers the breach on its own. Cutler is analogous, its Act imposing substantial penalties upon a duty found public. O’Connor is distinguishable, its £20 maximum attaching to a fundamental safety provision, whereas [the provision’s subject: the safety of whom, or the regulation of what] is not of that kind. The penalty accordingly tells against one.”

C · Cutler both ways

  “Cutler is analogous because ‘[the maximum penalty in figures, and what it penalises]’ is substantial as the penalties there were. Yet [the provision’s subject: the safety of whom, or the regulation of what] tells against it, Cutler’s duty being public where this one is cast for safety, and the comparative smallness of the penalty did not defeat O’Connor. Therefore, on balance this limb tallies yes, though not without difficulty.”

→ B5D, or to the next presumption the facts answer

B1B Statute contains no penalty for breach · when the facts state no penalty for breach

“‘[the provision, by number and its opening words]’ carries no penalty, and a person injured by the breach was presumed to have ‘a right to recover damages unless it could be established by considering the Act as a whole, that no such right was intended to be given’ (SVE [15.30]). Without this presumption the statute was a mere ‘pious aspiration’ (Cutler 407 per Lord Simonds). Yet ‘[t]he stream of cases rejecting claims for breach of statutory duties imposed by welfare statutes suggests the principle now holds little weight as establishing a statutory intention to grant a civil remedy’ (SVE [15.30]). The silence accordingly tallies [yes, or only weakly].”

A · the silence tells for a right to sue

  “Nothing in [the Act’s short title and its object] read as a whole withholds a remedy, so the presumption of recovery stands (SVE [15.30]; Cutler 407). The caveat that the principle ‘now holds little weight’ bites on welfare statutes, which this is not. The statute’s silence accordingly favours a private right of action.”

B · the silence carries little

  “Yet little follows from the silence, [the Act’s subject matter, in a phrase] being of the welfare kind for which the principle now holds little weight (SVE [15.30]), and the aspiration Lord Simonds feared is answered by [the enforcement machinery the Act provides] rather than by an action in [P]’s hands. The silence favours a private right only weakly, and the remaining presumptions carry the question.”

→ B5D, or to the next presumption the facts answer

B2 Is the statute for a defined class or the public at large? (Cutler) · when where the facts show who the provision benefits: a defined class, or the public at large

“The class [tallies yes, or no], a duty ‘enacted for the benefit of the public rather than for the protection of a defined class’ presuming that Parliament did not intend a private right, a limited class presuming that one exists (SVE [15.45]). Cutler is the illustration, ‘The occupier of a track shall take such steps as necessary to secure that… there is available space for bookmakers on the track…’ (Betting and Lotteries Act 1934 (UK)) being held a public duty, not one owed to a specific class, so the bookmaker had no private right to sue.”

A · a limited class

  “‘[the words of the provision naming the class]’ casts the duty for those persons alone, and [P] is one of them, ‘industrial regulations enacted for the protection of a limited class, such as factory workers’ giving rise to actions (SVE [15.45]). Cutler is distinguishable, its duty serving the betting public rather than bookmakers as a class, whereas this provision names its beneficiaries. The defined class accordingly favours a private right of action.”

B · a general public duty

  “‘[the words showing the provision serves everyone]’ casts the duty for everyone alike, presuming no private right (SVE [15.45]). Cutler is analogous, the duty to secure space for bookmakers serving the betting public, not bookmakers as a class. Traffic regulations are the paradigm, enacted for the public at large and giving rise to no action (Tucker; Abela 490–1). [P] stands as any member of the public does. A duty owed to the public at large accordingly tells against one.”

C · Cutler both ways

  “Cutler is analogous because ‘[the words showing the provision serves everyone]’, like the duty there, is cast for the public rather than for a class, presuming no private right (SVE [15.45]). Yet ‘[the words of the provision naming the class]’ tells against it, marking out those for whose protection the duty exists, a limited class presuming a private right of action. Therefore, the better view is that the defined class favours a private right of action, though it is a close thing, [P] not suing as a member of the public.”

→ B5A, or to the next presumption the facts answer

B3 Is the duty specific or general? (Cutler) · when where the facts give a provision prescribing a specific precaution, or only a general duty

“The duty being [specific, or general], ‘a provision prescribing a specific precaution for the safety of others in a matter where the person upon whom the duty laid is, under the general law of negligence, bound to exercise due care’ will ‘give rise to a correlative private right, unless from the nature of the provision or from the scope of the legislation of which it forms a part a contrary intention appears’ (O’Connor per Dixon J, in Darling Island). McDonald v Girkaid is the contrast, a blanket prohibition that ‘prescribes the end but not the means’ and identifies no ‘specific precaution or measure’ conferring no ‘correlative private cause of action’ ([177] per McColl J). The specificity or generality of the duty accordingly [favours a private right of action, or tells against one].”

A · a specific precaution

  “The duty is specific, ‘[the provision, by number and its opening words]’ requiring [the exact step the provision commands] and nothing broader, so the correlative private right arises, no contrary intention appearing (O’Connor per Dixon J). McDonald v Girkaid is distinguishable, its blanket prohibition leaving the means at large ([177] per McColl J). The specificity of the duty accordingly favours a private right of action.”

B · cast in general terms

  “The duty is general, ‘[the provision, by number and its opening words]’ doing no more than requiring [the open-ended result the provision commands, leaving the means to D], no specific precaution being prescribed (O’Connor per Dixon J). McDonald v Girkaid is analogous, its regulation likewise prescribing the end but not the means ([177] per McColl J). The generality of the duty accordingly tells against one.”

C · McDonald v Girkaid both ways

  “McDonald v Girkaid is analogous because ‘[the provision, by number and its opening words]’ is cast in general terms, [the open-ended result the provision commands, leaving the means to D] being left to [D], such a duty prescribing the end but not the means ([177] per McColl J). Yet [the exact step the provision commands] weakens this, a specific precaution giving rise to the correlative private right (O’Connor per Dixon J). Therefore, the better view is that the specificity of the duty favours a private right of action, although finely balanced.”

→ 1B, or to the next presumption the facts answer

B4 Are there adequate alternative remedies? (textbook) · when the facts print prosecution, appeal, review or complaint machinery

“‘[the remedial machinery the facts print, quoted verbatim]’ supplies only [a prosecution, or machinery answering this loss], and an action might be refused if there are adequate alternative remedies (Phillips 842; SVE [15.40]). It might be sufficient that general tort remedies are adequate, as in Dennis v Brownlee, where they answered breach of local government ordinances requiring safety precautions on road repairs. And the presumption may run in reverse, an available negligence claim counting against the statutory action (Jane Doe [138]) and no available common law action also counting against it. Yet ‘unless the statute expressly provides otherwise, these avenues of redress do not normally provide compensatory damages to the plaintiff’ (SVE [15.40]). The adequacy of those remedies accordingly [tells against a private right of action, or leaves one open].”

A · no adequate alternative

  “‘[the remedial machinery the facts print, quoted verbatim]’ supplies only a prosecution, which leaves [P’s loss, and whether damages alone answer it] where it fell, an action being refused only where alternative remedies are adequate (Phillips 842; SVE [15.40]). Olotu is distinguishable, public law remedies sufficing there because the Crown had failed to bring the plaintiff before the court in time while bail remained open, whereas these avenues do not deliver compensatory damages, and nothing but damages answers this loss. The want of an adequate alternative remedy accordingly favours a private right of action.”

B · adequate remedies exist

  “‘[the remedial machinery the facts print, quoted verbatim]’ supplies machinery answering this loss, [the machinery the facts print and the relief it delivers] restoring [P] to the position the provision secured, and an action might be refused if there are adequate alternative remedies (Phillips 842; SVE [15.40]). Dennis v Brownlee is analogous, general tort remedies there answering breach of safety ordinances on road repairs, as here; Olotu likewise, public law remedies sufficing for the Crown’s failure to produce the plaintiff while bail remained open. The adequacy of those remedies accordingly tells against one.”

C · Olotu both ways

  “Olotu is analogous because ‘[the remedial machinery the facts print, quoted verbatim]’ leaves [P] the public law remedies that sufficed there, bail and review remaining open to the plaintiff the Crown failed to produce in time. Yet [P’s loss, and whether damages alone answer it] does not sit with that, no compensatory damages flowing from those avenues (SVE [15.40]), and an action is refused only where the alternatives are adequate (Phillips 842). Therefore, the better view is that the alternatives do not displace a private right of action, though the point is arguable.”

→ B2, or to the next presumption the facts answer

B5 What is the subject matter of the statute? · when where the facts disclose subject matter beyond the safety and health limbs at B5A and B5D

“The subject matter [tallies for, or against] the action, actions having ‘been common for duties involving workers safety, but exceptional in other areas’ (SVE [15.05]). The categories in which courts are unlikely to find the requisite intention are political decisions and priorities, general duties, health, education and welfare duties, investigatory, regulatory and administrative functions, and the accountability of public bodies (SVE [15.50]–[15.70]). Each category the facts answer is weighed in the tally on question 1.”

A · the subject matter tells for the action

  “[the duty, in the provision’s words] is cast for safety and nothing else, and actions ‘have been common for duties involving workers safety, but exceptional in other areas’ (SVE [15.05]). It answers none of the categories in which the intention is unlikely to be found, being no political decision, no general duty, no health, education or welfare duty, no investigatory, regulatory or administrative function and no question of the accountability of a public body (SVE [15.50]–[15.70]). The subject matter tallies for the action.”

B · the subject matter tells against the action

  “[the duty, in the provision’s words] is not cast for safety but for [the character of what the provision requires], and actions are ‘exceptional in other areas’ than workers safety (SVE [15.05]). It answers [the categories below that the facts engage, named], each a category in which courts are unlikely to find the requisite statutory intention (SVE [15.50]–[15.70]). The subject matter tallies against the action.”

C · the categories pull apart

  “[the duty, in the provision’s words] is one of safety, for which actions ‘have been common’ (SVE [15.05]), yet it is also [the character of what the provision requires], bringing it within [the categories below that the facts engage, named]. Sovar resolves it on the balance of considerations. Therefore, the better view is that the subject matter tallies for the action, the evil being [the mischief the provision names], although finely balanced.”

Where the duty relates to safety → B5A. For political decisions and priorities → B5B. For general duties → B5C. For health, education and welfare duties → B5D. For investigatory, regulatory and administrative functions → B5E. For the accountability of public bodies → B5F. If an object clause is printed → B6 · → 1B

B5A Does the duty relate to safety? (O’Connor) · when the duty is one of safety, of workers or others, or D says it is not

“[the duty, in the provision’s words] [relates to safety, or does not], and safety is where the action lives, actions having ‘been common for duties involving workers safety, but exceptional in other areas’ (SVE [15.05]), indeed ‘even if the statute appears to be enacted to protect a restricted class, except for workers safety statutes, it is rare for courts to find that an action for breach of statutory duty is available’ (SVE [15.60]). Even there opportunities are diminishing, such claims being frequently argued alongside negligence with courts usually finding it unnecessary to rule (SVE [15.05]). The safety character of the duty accordingly [favours a private right of action, or is absent].”

A · the duty relates to safety

  “[the duty, in the provision’s words] protects [the persons the duty protects] against [the physical danger the provision addresses], a safety provision of the O’Connor kind. Groves shows the category in operation, an injured employee recovering for unfenced dangerous machinery (SVE [15.20]). The safety character of the duty accordingly favours a private right of action.”

B · not a safety duty

  “[the duty, in the provision’s words] protects [the interest the duty in fact protects] rather than anyone’s safety, and outside safety the action is rare even for a statute protecting a restricted class (SVE [15.60]). Wynn Tressider makes the like point of a member of the public suing on work safety legislation, such an action seeming ‘prima facie improbable’ ([91] per McColl JA). The absence of any safety character accordingly tells against one.”

C · safety both ways

  “[the duty, in the provision’s words] is a safety provision on its face, for which actions ‘have been common’ (SVE [15.05]), and [the feature making the duty one of safety] supports that reading. Yet [the feature showing the duty serves persons or interests beyond safety] tells against it, Wynn Tressider thinking an action at the suit of ‘members of the public’ on such legislation ‘prima facie improbable’ ([91] per McColl JA). Therefore, the better view is that the duty is a safety duty and favours a private right of action, though not without difficulty.”

→ B3, or to B6 if an object clause is printed, and → 1B

B5B Political decisions and priorities · when the provision commits a decision about policy, priorities or the allocation of resources to D

“[the character of what the provision requires] [calls for an evaluation of political decisions, or does not], and ‘in the absence of clear statutory language, courts will not allow actions which require an evaluation of what are really political decisions, those that involve policy making or quasi-legislative functions’ (SVE [15.50]). Such decisions ‘involve setting political priorities for government, the allocation of resources and balancing of competing societal demands’, and ‘[i]t is not the role of the courts in damages claims to review these functions or “second guess” these decisions’ (SVE [15.50]). A political decision of that kind accordingly [tells against the action, or is not engaged].”

A · a political decision

  “[the character of what the provision requires] is in substance a decision about priorities, [the resource or priority decision the provision commits to D], and to try this claim would be to ‘second guess’ exactly that (SVE [15.50]). A political decision of that kind accordingly tells against the action.”

B · not a political decision

  “[the character of what the provision requires] commits no political decision to [D], the provision prescribing [the exact step the provision commands] and leaving nothing to be weighed against competing demands. Nothing here requires an evaluation of political decisions or the weighing of priorities against competing demands (SVE [15.50]). The court is asked only whether [D] did the thing commanded, not to ‘second guess’ any decision. This being no political decision, the subject matter accordingly tells for the action.”

→ B6 if an object clause is printed, and → 1B

B5C General duties · when the duty is expressed in broad general terms rather than prescribing a specific act

“[the duty, in the provision’s words] [is expressed in broad general terms, or prescribes a specific duty], courts having ‘often rejected claims where duties are expressed in broad general terms rather than imposing specific duties’ (SVE [15.55]). Gorringe is the illustration, a duty on ‘each local authority (to) prepare and carry out a programme of measures designed to promote road safety’ founding no action ([18]–[20] per Lord Hoffmann); so is Kirkup, on a duty that ‘Australia Post shall, as far as practicable, perform its functions in a manner consistent with sound commercial practice’ (SVE [15.55]). General performance duties are now excluded outright, s 19(1) of the Work Health and Safety Act 2011 (NSW) requiring that ‘a person conducting a business or undertaking must ensure, so far as is reasonably practicable, the health and safety of … workers’ and s 267 excluding actions upon such duties, although ‘[t]his exclusion does not affect breaches of duties imposed by regulations which may be still actionable’ (SVE [15.75]). The generality of the duty accordingly [tells against the action, or gives way to its specificity].”

A · a general duty

  “[the duty, in the provision’s words] does no more than set an end, leaving the means to [D], and courts have ‘often rejected claims where duties are expressed in broad general terms rather than imposing specific duties’ (SVE [15.55]). Gorringe is analogous, its duty likewise programmatic, founding no action ([18]–[20] per Lord Hoffmann), as in Kirkup. A general performance duty of the s 19(1) kind would be excluded outright by s 267. A duty of that generality accordingly tells against the action.”

B · a specific duty

  “[the duty, in the provision’s words] requires [the exact step the provision commands] and leaves [D] no choice of means, taking it outside duties ‘expressed in broad general terms’ (SVE [15.55]). Gorringe is distinguishable, its duty setting a programme rather than an act ([18]–[20] per Lord Hoffmann), and Kirkup likewise turned on ‘sound commercial practice’. Nor does s 267 reach it, the exclusion not affecting ‘breaches of duties imposed by regulations’ (SVE [15.75]). The specificity of the duty accordingly tells for the action.”

C · general or specific both ways

  “Gorringe is analogous because [the feature making the duty programmatic], such a duty founding no action ([18]–[20] per Lord Hoffmann). Yet [the exact step the provision commands] tells against it, prescribing an act rather than an end, outside duties ‘expressed in broad general terms’ (SVE [15.55]). Therefore, the better view is that the duty is specific enough to tell for the action, s 267 not reaching a duty imposed by regulation.”

→ B6 if an object clause is printed, and → 1B

B5D Does the duty relate to health, welfare or education? (textbook) · when the duty is imposed on a public authority in the areas of health, education or welfare

“[the duty, in the provision’s words] [is a health, education or welfare duty, or is not]. ‘General duties imposed on public authorities for the benefit of the general public in the areas of health, education and welfare are not intended to be enforceable by damages claims brought by individuals’ (SVE [15.60]). Lord Browne-Wilkinson put it in X v Bedfordshire that welfare legislation ‘is not to be treated as being passed for the benefit of those individuals but for the benefit of society in general’, the cases allowing a private right being ‘all cases in which the statutory duty has been very limited and specific as opposed to general administrative functions imposed on public bodies’ (X v Bedfordshire 731–2). In such a context ‘it would require exceptionally clear statutory language’ to show a contrary intention (747). A duty of that kind accordingly [tells against a private right of action, or falls outside the category].”

A · a health, education or welfare duty

  “[the duty, in the provision’s words] is imposed on [the authority the duty is imposed on] in [health, education or welfare, as the Act frames it], the very field in which damages claims by individuals fail (SVE [15.60]). X v Bedfordshire is analogous, its child protection legislation likewise passed for the benefit of society in general (731–2 per Lord Browne-Wilkinson), and nothing here approaches the exceptionally clear statutory language needed to displace that (747). A duty of that kind accordingly tells against a private right of action.”

B · not a welfare duty

  “[the duty, in the provision’s words] commands [the exact step the provision commands] and is very limited and specific rather than a general administrative function, the description of the duties that do found a private right (X v Bedfordshire 731–2). X v Bedfordshire is accordingly distinguishable, its child protection legislation passed for the benefit of society in general, as were the duties in O’Rourke, Cubillo [1183] and Williams v Minister (SVE [15.60]). No exceptionally clear statutory language is needed where the duty is this specific. The duty falling outside that category, this presumption accordingly favours a private right of action.”

C · welfare or specific both ways

  “X v Bedfordshire is analogous because [the feature making the duty a general welfare function], welfare legislation being passed for the benefit of society in general (731–2 per Lord Browne-Wilkinson). Yet [the exact step the provision commands] tells against it, the successful cases being confined to very limited and specific duties. Therefore, the better view is that the welfare character of the duty tells against a private right of action, exceptionally clear statutory language being required to hold otherwise (747), though the point is arguable.”

→ B4, or to the next presumption the facts answer

B5E Investigatory, regulatory and administrative functions · when d is a government body or official exercising investigative powers or regulatory functions, or carrying out administrative duties

“[the character of what the provision requires] [is an investigatory, regulatory or administrative function, or is not]. The policy reasons denying a duty of care ‘apply with greater force to an action for breach of a statutory duty, which may impose strict liability’, and ‘[t]here is reluctance to allow actions for breach of statutory duties in relation to government bodies or officials exercising investigative powers, regulatory functions, or carrying out administrative duties, particularly where decisions may be subject to judicial review’ (SVE [15.65]). So too ‘duties to ensure timely performance and administrative efficiency are unlikely to give rise to actions’, lest such bodies ‘become excessively cautious and defensive with scarce resources being diverted to protect against claims’ (SVE [15.65]). A regulatory or administrative function of that kind accordingly [tells against the action, or is not engaged].”

A · an investigatory, regulatory or administrative function

  “[the character of what the provision requires] is what [the authority the duty is imposed on] does in regulating [the activity regulated], and the reluctance falls squarely on it, [D]’s decision moreover being subject to judicial review (SVE [15.65]). A regulatory or administrative function of that kind accordingly tells against the action.”

B · not a regulatory or administrative function

  “[the character of what the provision requires] is not the exercise of any investigative or regulatory function but a command that [D] do [the exact step the provision commands]. No decision of [D]’s is under review, nor is this a duty to ensure timely performance and administrative efficiency (SVE [15.65]). This being no such function, the subject matter accordingly tells for the action.”

→ B6 if an object clause is printed, and → 1B

B5F Accountability of public bodies · when the duty is imposed on government, a government officer or an instrumentality, and the claim is in substance about holding it to account

“[the duty, in the provision’s words] is imposed on [the authority the duty is imposed on], and this claim involves ‘the intersection between public and private law’, courts having ‘considered whether statutory duties imposed on government, government officers or instrumentalities can give rise to claims for damages for breach’, attempts which have ‘on the whole’ been ‘unsuccessful’ (SVE [15.70]). Civil liability legislation has run the same way, its impact being ‘to limit the liability of government, its instrumentalities and public authorities’ (SVE [15.70]). A claim about the accountability of a public body accordingly [tells against the action, or is not made].”

A · a claim about the accountability of a public body

  “[the duty, in the provision’s words] is imposed on [the authority the duty is imposed on], and the claim asks the court to make it answer in damages for [the function the claim is really about]. That is exactly the attempt that has on the whole been unsuccessful, and civil liability legislation points the same way (SVE [15.70]). A claim about the accountability of a public body accordingly tells against the action.”

B · not a claim of that kind

  “[the duty, in the provision’s words] binds [D] as [D’s character under the Act: licensee, occupier, employer or authority], and [P] asks nothing of the court beyond whether [D] did the thing commanded. Nothing here involves the intersection between public and private law, the duty holder being [D] and the obligation [the exact step the provision commands] (SVE [15.70]). This being no such claim, the subject matter accordingly tells for the action.”

→ B6 if an object clause is printed, and → 1B

B6 The object of the Act · when the facts print an object, purpose or long-title clause

“‘[the object, purpose or long-title clause, quoted verbatim]’ is a strong indicator rather than a trump, the inference arising ‘on a balance of considerations, from the nature, scope and terms of the statute, including the nature of the evil against which it is directed’ (Sovar 405 per Kitto J). Because it names [the general public, or ‘[the words of the object clause naming the class]’] and fixes the mischief as ‘[the mischief the object clause names, in the clause’s own words]’, it [confines the benefit to that class, or serves the public at large]; and it is the natural starting point for the risk at question 3 (cf Gorris).”

A · benefit confined to the class

  “‘[the object, purpose or long-title clause, quoted verbatim]’ names ‘[the words of the object clause naming the class]’ and fixes the mischief as ‘[the mischief the object clause names, in the clause’s own words]’, the very harm [P] suffered, so it confines the benefit to that class, of which [P] is one. It is a strong indicator rather than a trump, the inference arising ‘on a balance of considerations’ (Sovar 405 per Kitto J), and it is the natural starting point for the risk at question 3 (cf Gorris).”

B · benefit to the public at large

  “‘[the object, purpose or long-title clause, quoted verbatim]’ names the general public and fixes the mischief as ‘[the mischief the object clause names, in the clause’s own words]’, a harm [P] shares with everyone in [P’s position], so it serves the public at large rather than confining the benefit to any class. It is a strong indicator rather than a trump, the inference arising ‘on a balance of considerations’ (Sovar 405 per Kitto J), and it is the natural starting point for the risk at question 3 (cf Gorris).”

→ 1B

B7 Where the duty is in a regulation, an award or another instrument · when p relies on something made under an Act rather than the Act itself

“The delegation carries much: a regulation ‘must create the same correlative civil right as the same provision in the statute would create unless the delegate is expressly confined by the statute to making regulations which can only be enforced in a particular manner’ (Darling Island per Williams J). Yet where the statute does not ‘expressly confer’ on the Executive ‘a power by regulation to create an action for damages’, ‘it must be difficult to construe the statute and the delegated legislation’ as impliedly doing so (Byrne 462 per McHugh and Gummow JJ). The regulation therefore [does, or does not] carry the correlative right.”

A · regulation carries the right

  “‘[the regulation or award clause, by number and its opening words]’ leaves the delegate nowhere expressly confined, and Darling Island itself proceeded on a regulation. The statute’s silence (Byrne 462) does not unsay this clause’s specific safety command. The regulation arguably carries the correlative right.”

B · no right from the delegate

  “The delegation carries little here, ‘[the enabling section, by number, and the words of its regulation-making power]’ going no further than [the subject matter the enabling section names], and the statute nowhere conferring a power to create an action for damages (Byrne 462). Darling Island’s rule cannot outrun that silence. The regulation likely does not carry the correlative right.”

the enabling section names the subject matter → B1, or where no penalty is stated → B1B. If it is silent, the presumption tells strongly against, though not conclusively, it being ‘difficult to construe the statute and the delegated legislation’ as impliedly conferring the action (Byrne 462 per McHugh and Gummow JJ); contrast Darling Island, where s 425(c) of the Navigation Act 1912-1953 (Cth) named ‘the protection of the health and the security from injury of persons engaged in the loading or unloading of ships’; then → B1

B8 Where P’s own conduct put D in breach · when p disobeyed an express instruction, or P controls D

“The class may exclude an employee whose own conduct put the employer in breach, the protected class excluding ‘employees whose actions have put the employer in breach, where nothing done or omitted by the employer itself has contributed to the breach’ (Millington per Ipp JA). Millington requires five things together: [P] injured doing what [D] expressly forbade; that disobedience causing the breach; ‘[a]part from the employee’s own conduct in disobeying the instructions, nothing done or omitted to be done by the employer’ bringing the breach about; [P]’s own act itself constituting the breach; and nothing [D] could have done to stop the disobedience (SVE [15.115]). ‘It will, however be a rare case where an employer has not been guilty of some neglect, such as provision of training and information about regulatory requirements’ (SVE [15.115]; Boaden; Texcrete), exclusion arising only in the clearest of cases, where [P] ‘can be regarded as exclusively responsible for the breach’ (SVE [15.115]). Shedlizki cannot be relied on as it stands, its majority rejected in favour of Mason JA’s dissent (Andar [49]–[50]). The better view is that [P] [is, or is not] so placed.”

A · not exclusively responsible

  “Millington excludes an employee only where nothing the employer did or omitted contributed to the breach and nothing could have stopped the disobedience; not so here. [D] left [the system, instruction, training or equipment D set, which P was following] unaltered, exactly the neglect expected in all but the rarest case (SVE [15.115]; Boaden; Texcrete). [P]’s act was one step in a breach already begun, not exclusive responsibility. [P] is likely not so placed.”

B · exclusively responsible

  “Millington excludes an employee where [P] was injured doing what [D] expressly forbade, that disobedience alone caused the breach, and nothing could have stopped it; each is met, [P’s act said to have produced the breach] alone producing what the provision forbids, with no neglect of [D]’s own behind it. This is the clearest of cases, [P] exclusively responsible for the breach (SVE [15.115]). The better view is that [P] is so placed.”

C · Shedlizki both ways

  “[D] says [P], like the ‘one man’ company director in Shedlizki, controlled [the task P controlled] so that [P]’s act produced the breach; yet Shedlizki’s majority was disapproved, Andar approving Mason JA’s dissent at 386, ‘it matters not that the plaintiff had control or the capacity to control the defendant’s activities’ (Andar [49]–[50]). And [what D did or omitted that contributed to the breach independently of P] does not sit with Millington, which excludes [P] only where nothing the employer did or omitted contributed. Therefore, the better view is that [P] is not so placed.”

→ 3

B9 ‘Dangerous’ · when the operative words turn on danger

“The duty to ‘securely fence all dangerous parts … of machinery’ (Factories, Shops & Industries Act 1912 (NSW) s 33) operates on machinery that is dangerous, which Dixon CJ took it to be if it may reasonably be foreseen to be a source of injury to people in the vicinity, taking them with all the ordinary infirmities to which human nature can be prone (Dunlop Rubber 320; SVE [15.110]). ‘[the machine]’ therefore [answers that description, or does not].”

A · machinery dangerous

  “[the moving part that injured P] turned unguarded within reach of [P] at work, on Dixon CJ’s test foreseeably a source of injury to people in the vicinity (Dunlop Rubber 320). Dairy Farmers is analogous, the High Court adopting the same view of the duty to fence dangerous machines under s 27(1) of the Factories, Shops and Industries Act 1962 (NSW) (SVE [15.110]). ‘[the machine]’ answers that description.”

B · not dangerous machinery

  “[the guard, distance or enclosure putting the part beyond reach in ordinary operation] put [the moving part that injured P] beyond reach in the ordinary course, no injury being reasonably foreseeable even taking people with all their ordinary infirmities (Dunlop Rubber 320 per Dixon CJ). The duty to ‘securely fence all dangerous parts … of machinery’ does not operate; ‘[the machine]’ does not answer that description.”

C · Dunlop Rubber both ways

  “Dunlop Rubber is analogous because ‘[the machine]’, like the machinery there, might on Dixon CJ’s test be foreseen as a source of injury to people in the vicinity, [P] working beside [the moving part that injured P] (Dunlop Rubber 320). Yet [the guard, distance or enclosure putting the part beyond reach in ordinary operation] points the other way, the part being guarded in ordinary operation. Therefore, the better view is that the duty operates, taking people with all the ordinary infirmities to which human nature can be prone.”

→ 6

B10 ‘Permitted or allowed’: permission presupposes knowledge · when the operative words permit, allow or suffer

“Waugh is analogous, the Factories and Shops Act 1960 (Qld) providing that an adult male employee ‘shall not be permitted or allowed to … carry by hand any object so heavy as to be likely to cause risk of injury’, words which ‘presupposed awareness, actual or constructive on the part of the employer’ (SVE [15.110]). Unknown to the employers there, the plaintiff had existing back trouble, and it was ‘unreasonable to construe the clause as imposing an obligation on the employer to protect the employee from a risk of injury of which the employer neither knew nor ought to have known’, although ‘likely to cause risk of injury’ required only ‘a real or not remote chance or possibility regardless of whether it was less or more than 50%’ (SVE [15.110]). The better view is that [D] [knew, or did not].”

A · awareness constructive at least

  “[what put the hazard before D: the instruction, the roster, the complaint, the inspection, or P’s known condition] put [the object and its weight] before [D], the operative words presupposing awareness and the risk needing only a real or not remote chance of injury (SVE [15.110]). [D] had at least constructive awareness. On balance, [D] knew.”

B · no awareness, no real chance

  “[the fact showing D neither knew nor ought to have known of the weight] leaves [D] as Waugh’s employer, to whom the plaintiff’s existing back trouble was unknown, the clause not being construed to reach risks of which [D] neither knew nor ought to have known (SVE [15.110]). Nor was the danger ‘a real or not remote chance or possibility’, whatever the odds. The better view is that [D] did not.”

C · Waugh both ways

  “Waugh is analogous because [D], like the employer there, set [the task D set], the operative words presupposing awareness, actual or constructive (SVE [15.110]). Yet [the fact showing D neither knew nor ought to have known of the weight] points the other way. Therefore, the better view is that [D] knew, though the point is arguable.”

If D neither knew nor ought to have known, question 5 is no; record it → 6

B11 The statutory standard: ‘practicable’ and ‘reasonably practicable’ · when the operative words are ‘practicable’, ‘all practicable steps’ or ‘so far as is reasonably practicable’

“‘[the words of practicability the provision uses]’ [reach, or stop short of] [the precaution P says D should have taken]. A duty to ‘take all practicable steps’ ‘can be the basis of an action for breach of statutory duty even though it does not prescribe particular conduct’ (SVE [15.110]). Slivak confines the qualified form, a designer’s duty to ‘ensure so far as is reasonably practicable’ that a structure is safe applying only ‘to matters which are within the power of the designer to perform or check’, and ‘there will be no breach of this duty if the construction does not comply with the design’ (SVE [15.110]). Under the uniform legislation ‘the tests for what is reasonable practicable are similar to the standard tests for negligence requiring risk, damage, knowledge and cost of avoiding the risk to be taken into account’, though ‘not necessarily the same as the negligence standard where the statutory duty is to “ensure” workers safety’ (SVE [15.110]). Nor does the onus shift to [D] ‘unless the statute requires this’ (Slivak; Chugg; SVE [15.15]).”

A · the words reach the step

  “[the fact showing the step was within D’s power to perform or check] puts [the precaution P says D should have taken] squarely within ‘[the words of practicability the provision uses]’. Slivak is distinguishable, the qualified duty there reaching only what the designer could perform or check, whereas this step was [D]’s to take. That [P] bears the onus changes nothing (Slivak; Chugg). The words reach the step.”

B · the words stop short

  “[the fact putting the step beyond D’s power to perform or check] puts [the precaution P says D should have taken] outside ‘[the words of practicability the provision uses]’. Slivak is analogous, this step lying beyond [D]’s power to perform or check as the builder’s non-compliance lay beyond the designer’s (SVE [15.110]). Nor does the onus shift to [D] ‘unless the statute requires this’ (Slivak; Chugg). The words stop short.”

C · Slivak both ways

  “Slivak is analogous because [the fact putting the step beyond D’s power to perform or check]. Yet [the fact showing the step was within D’s power to perform or check] tells against it, such a duty founding an action even without prescribing particular conduct (SVE [15.110]). Therefore, the better view is that the words reach the step, although the onus stays on [P] (Chugg).”

→ 6

B11B The statutory standard: ‘safe’ · when the operative word is ‘safe’, as of access, plant or a place of work

“‘[the words of safety the provision uses]’ ask whether [the access, plant or place the provision requires to be safe] was safe, and Andriolo, on the Scaffolding and Lifts Ordinance 1957 (ACT), which required ‘safe’ means of access, held that access ‘would not be safe if it was a possible cause of injury to a worker acting in a way in which a worker can reasonably be expected to act’ (SVE [15.110]). [P]’s conduct [answers that description, or does not], and the thing accordingly [was not safe, or was].”

A · the thing was not safe

  “[the access, plant or place the provision requires to be safe] was a possible cause of injury to [P], and [P] was doing no more than [what P was doing when injured, in a phrase]. Andriolo is analogous, the standard being set by ordinary working conduct rather than ideal conduct, access not being safe ‘if it was a possible cause of injury to a worker acting in a way in which a worker can reasonably be expected to act’ (SVE [15.110]). Possibility, not probability, is the measure; the thing was not safe.”

B · the thing was safe

  “[the access, plant or place the provision requires to be safe] answered ‘[the words of safety the provision uses]’, being no cause of injury to anyone working in the ordinary way. Andriolo is distinguishable, the measure being the worker ‘acting in a way in which a worker can reasonably be expected to act’ (SVE [15.110]), and [P’s act said to fall outside what a worker can reasonably be expected to do] is not that. The provision does not require [D] to guard against every course [P] might take; the thing was safe.”

C · Andriolo both ways

  “Andriolo is analogous because [the access, plant or place the provision requires to be safe] was ‘a possible cause of injury’ to [P] acting as a worker can reasonably be expected to act (SVE [15.110]). Yet [P’s act said to fall outside what a worker can reasonably be expected to do] tells against it, no worker being expected to act so. Therefore, the better view is that the thing was not safe, possibility rather than probability being the measure.”

→ 6

B12 The statutory standard: strict liability and its qualification · when the operative words command a state of affairs, such as that a thing be maintained, and D says the failure was unexplained

“‘[the words of the provision requiring the thing to be maintained]’ [command a state of affairs absolutely, or admit qualification]. Austral Bronze is the contrast to Galashiels, s 34(a) of the Factories, Shops and Industries Act 1962 (NSW) requiring that ‘all floors … shall be of sound construction and properly maintained’ and s 4(1) defining ‘maintained’ as ‘maintained in good order, condition and repair and in an efficient state’, yet Barwick CJ ‘could not accept a construction of the section that placed the defendants in breach of it in the circumstances’ (SVE [15.110]), the plaintiff there having slipped on an oil slick of unknown origin. Breach accordingly [is, or is not] made out.”

A · the obligation is absolute and breach is made out

  “[the fact showing the thing itself was out of order] shows [the plant, floor or fitting the provision requires to be maintained] was not in an efficient ‘state’ when [P] used it. Galashiels, above, is analogous here too, the failure unexplained yet the obligation absolute. Doval is analogous too, ‘maintain’ indicating ‘that the obligation is not complied with simply by the provision of a system’ (per Clarke JA). Breach is arguably made out.”

B · the words are qualified and no breach is made out

  “[the fact showing nothing more than a substance or object on an otherwise sound thing] is all that is shown, [the plant, floor or fitting the provision requires to be maintained] being itself in good order until [the external event D says produced the failure]. Austral Bronze is analogous, the slick there likewise unexplained yet founding no breach. Galashiels is distinguishable, the mechanism there having itself failed. Breach is likely not made out.”

C · Galashiels and Austral Bronze both ways

  “Galashiels is analogous because [the fact showing the thing itself was out of order], the thing itself failing to work efficiently. Yet [the fact showing nothing more than a substance or object on an otherwise sound thing] tells against it, Austral Bronze treating an unexplained oil slick on a sound floor as no breach (SVE [15.110]). Therefore, the better view is that the obligation is absolute and breach is made out.”

→ 6

B13 Where D is a public or other authority: CLA s 43(2) · when d is the Crown, a Government department, a public health organisation, a local council, an authority constituted by or under an Act, or a…

“Section 43 applies only to the extent the liability is ‘based on a breach of a statutory duty by a public or other authority in connection with the exercise of or a failure to exercise a function of the authority’ (CLA s 43(1)). [D] being a ‘public or other authority’, an act or omission is then a breach of statutory duty only if ‘in the circumstances so unreasonable that no authority having the functions of the authority in question could properly consider the act or omission to be a reasonable exercise of its functions’ (CLA s 43(2)), a Wednesbury standard that ‘drastically reduces the rights of persons to a remedy by very significantly lowering the standard of care owed by public or other authorities’ (Hamcor [196] per Dalton J). ‘[the act or omission complained of, in the words of the enabling Act]’ [meets it, or does not].”

A · Wednesbury standard met

  “[the fact putting the decision beyond what any authority with that function could think reasonable] left no authority charged with [the authority’s function under the enabling Act, in that Act’s words] able to think ‘[the act or omission complained of, in the words of the enabling Act]’ reasonable, which is what s 43(2) demands. Even that drastically reduced standard (Hamcor [196]) is met.”

B · short of Wednesbury

  “[the fact showing the decision was one an authority with that function could think reasonable] shows a course an authority with [the authority’s function under the enabling Act, in that Act’s words] could properly consider reasonable, so ‘[the act or omission complained of, in the words of the enabling Act]’ is no breach of statutory duty under s 43(2). Even that drastically reduced standard (Hamcor [196]) is not met.”

Where D’s function is instead a special statutory power, apply the same standard under s 43A. Where D’s function is to prohibit or regulate an activity, s 43 applies in addition to s 44 (CLA s 43(3)) · → 5B

B14 Where the claim is in substance one for damages for harm resulting from negligence · when the duty is not strict, s 3B is not engaged, and the CLA is to be run

“This claim [is, or is not] in substance one for negligence because [whether the duty asks what care D took, or commands a result whatever care D took], breach and causation being assessed under ss 5B and 5D where the claim is ‘in substance a claim for damages for harm resulting from negligence’ (SVE [15.85]). Where the duty is strict, Wynn Tressider [96] left open whether the civil liability provisions are impliedly repealed, the claim arguably not then being one for damages for harm resulting from negligence at all (SVE [15.85]). Sections 5B and 5D accordingly [apply, or do not].”

A · in substance negligence

  “The claim being in substance one for negligence, ‘the CLA provisions for determining whether there is negligence apply’ (SVE [15.85]), and the statutory basis becomes irrelevant except in so far as the breach of statute provides evidence of negligence (SVE [15.85]).”

B · a strict duty, not negligence

  “The duty being strict, [what P says D failed to do] is the bare failure to achieve the commanded state of affairs, so ss 5B and 5D arguably do not apply (CLA s 5A(1)).”

→ 5B. Other parts of the CLA are unaffected either way, personal injury damages applying ‘more broadly’ and being ‘not limited to negligence’ (SVE [15.80])

B15 Contributory negligence · when contributory negligence is raised against the BSD claim

“Contributory negligence reaches this claim only so far as the CLA does: where the claim is ‘in substance a claim for damages for harm resulting from negligence’, ‘the CLA provisions for determining whether there is negligence apply as do the provisions relating to contributory negligence’ (SVE [15.85]). Where the duty is strict, that is left open (Wynn Tressider [96]; SVE [15.85]). [P]’s [P’s act said to fall short of the care P owed to P] accordingly [reduces the damages, or does not].”

A · the damages are reduced

  “[P]’s [P’s act said to fall short of the care P owed to P] falls short of the care [P] owed for [P]’s own safety, [the fact showing P knew of the risk and could readily have avoided it], and the claim being in substance one for negligence, ‘the provisions relating to contributory negligence’ apply (SVE [15.85]). A reduction is also preferable to a total defeat of the claim (SVE [15.115]). The damages are reduced rather than refused.”

B · no reduction is made

  “The duty being strict, the claim is not in substance one for negligence, and only in that case do ‘the provisions relating to contributory negligence’ apply (SVE [15.85]). Wynn Tressider [96] leaves the strict liability question open, and ‘defences to breach of statutory duty may be more restricted than at common law’ (SVE [15.10]). No reduction is to be made, [P]’s act going to causation instead.”

volenti is raised → B15B. If P’s own conduct put D in breach → B8 · P’s own unlawful conduct raised → the DEFENCES section · → 6C

B15B Volenti non fit injuria · when volenti is raised against the BSD claim

“Volenti rarely answers a breach of statutory duty, ‘defences to breach of statutory duty may be more restricted than at common law’ (SVE [15.10]). It is preferable ‘to allow the plaintiff’s claim for breach of statutory duty but reduce the plaintiff’s damages under the defence of contributory negligence’ (SVE [15.115]), a reduction being favoured over a complete defeat. [D]’s reliance on volenti accordingly [is weak, or is better argued as a reduction for contributory negligence instead].”

A · volenti fails

  “‘[D]efences to breach of statutory duty may be more restricted than at common law’ (SVE [15.10]), and volenti is not established as an answer to [D]’s own statutory breach; to allow it would let [D] contract out of a duty Parliament imposed for [P]’s protection. The preferable course is a reduction rather than a defeat of the claim (SVE [15.115]). [D]’s surer course is a reduction for contributory negligence, not a complete defence.”

B · volenti is left to be argued

  “[the fact showing P knew of the risk and freely accepted it] is what [D] presses, but the point goes no further than that ‘defences to breach of statutory duty may be more restricted than at common law’ (SVE [15.10]). The safer course for [D] is to press it in general terms only and to argue in the alternative for a reduction for contributory negligence, which the preference for reducing rather than defeating the claim supports (SVE [15.115]).”

→ B15 for the reduction, and → 6C

DAMAGES

Overview of steps for calculating economic loss

The flow chart as given in Class 16, Class 17 and Class 18.

+--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| Head 1 past loss of earnings = actual loss (s12) ➜ Cap = no more than 3x average weekly NSW wage                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
