| Head 2 future loss of earnings                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
|   ▪ S13(1) – ‘most likely’ future circumstances x remaining working years                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
|   ▪ Minus outgoings (Wynn)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
|   ▪ Minus lost years cost of living (Sharman)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
|   ▪ Plus or minus contingencies/vicissitudes of life ≈ 15% (eg, promotion, unemployment, marriage, maternity leave, sickness etc) (Wynn)                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
|   ▪ S12 – no more than 3 x average weekly wage in NSW                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| Head 3 medical expenses – ‘reasonable’ = cost v benefit (Sharman)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |

| Head 4 is there gratuitous care?                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
|   ▪ Services provided by another person without cost (s15(1))                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |

|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
|   ▪ More than 6 hours per week and 6 months per year (s15(3)) [CLA s 15(3): ‘at least 6 hours per week’ and ‘at least 6 consecutive months’, not six months per year; see B7]                                                                                                                                                                                |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
|   ▪ Not less than 40 hours per week at market rate (Van Gervan) [CLA s 15(4) sets a ceiling, not a floor, where the services are provided ‘for not less than 40 hours per week’; CLA s 15(5) sets an hourly rate of one-fortieth of that amount where they are provided ‘for less than 40 hours per week’; Van Gervan fixes the rate at market cost; see B8] |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| Head 5 discount rate on all future economic damages = 5% (s14)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| Then NON-ECONOMIC LOSS [composed from Class 16’s table of CLA limits and Class 18; the flowchart above is Class 16’s economic loss overview, which ends at Head 5]: s 16; s 16 threshold 15% of ‘most extreme case’ capped at $804,000 by order published on 1 October 2025 (CLA s 17(3)); Woolworths v Lawlor                                               |

DAMAGES: SCAFFOLD

1 General principles at common law for compensatory damages

“[P] claims ‘personal injury damages’, ‘damages that relate to the death of or injury to a person’ (CLA s 11), and restitutio in integrum governs: [P] is put ‘in the same position as … if he had not sustained the wrong’ (Livingstone 39), the compensation ‘fair’ rather than ‘perfect’, recovered once and for all as a lump sum (Murphy v Stone-Wallwork per Lord Pearce; Todorovic 412), [P] free to do with it as [P] likes, though ‘the burden lies on the plaintiff to prove the injury or loss for which he seeks damages’ (Todorovic 412). The court assesses heads of damages (Teubner); the heads the facts name are [the heads named, in the words and order the facts use], and they alone are assessed.”

Only [P]’s ‘[the heads named, in the words and order the facts use]’ are assessed · Short form, where the question names only one or two heads: “[P] claims ‘personal injury damages’ (CLA s 11), assessed head by head (Teubner); the heads named are [the heads named, in the question’s words], and they alone are assessed.” First sort those losses to special and general damages and map each to its provision → 1A; in the short form, skip 1A. Then open ONLY the heads the question’s own words name, in the question’s order · Do not walk a head the question does not name; where a fact plainly belongs to a head not named, relocate it in a single clause rather than assessing it · The order is Head 1 past loss of earnings → 2; Head 2 future loss of earnings → 3; Head 3 medical expenses → 4; Head 4 gratuitous care → 5; Head 5 discount rate → 6 · Where the question names non-economic loss, it is opened at B10, its losses are sorted head by head at B23A, B23B, B23C and B23D, its severity is fixed at B24A and B24B, and it is valued at B11; it is exempt from both the s 14 discount and the vicissitudes deduction · For services P can no longer give dependants → B9A. For pain, disfigurement, lost amenity or shortened life → B10 · For superannuation → B12 · For an event between injury and trial → B13 · For a head resting on P’s psychiatric condition → B17 · punishment is sought → B20 · For a second injured person → 1 and walk the run again for them · a loss answers no obvious head; fund management, home maintenance, house modifications, special equipment, or travel and accommodation for treatment → B25 · Where the question names a category rather than a head, ‘economic loss’, ‘non-economic loss’; open only the heads the facts actually raise within it, and say in one clause which heads the facts do not raise and why

1A Heads of Damages

“Windeyer J divides the field (Teubner 506): an injury may ‘destroy or diminish, permanently or for a time, an existing capacity, mental or physical’, may ‘create needs that would not otherwise exist’, and may ‘produce physical pain and suffering’. Special damages ‘can be calculated or estimated with some accuracy’ and ‘have to be specifically pleaded’; general damages are ‘incapable of mathematical calculation’ and ‘at large’, special damages being in modern usage ‘loosely equated with economic loss, both past and future’. ‘[The losses the question names]’ are accordingly sorted to those categories, and each head to its provision: past expenses and lost earnings to CLA s 12(1)(a); lost earning capacity to s 12(1)(b), capped at three times average weekly earnings (s 12(2)); future needs and the assumptions behind them to s 13; gratuitous attendant care to s 15; lost capacity to provide domestic services to s 15B; superannuation to s 15C; and non-economic loss to ss 16–17.”

A · special damages, the ascertained loss

  “‘[The losses the question names]’ are special damages: ‘[the ascertained figures the facts supply]’ show they ‘can be calculated or estimated with some accuracy’, and they ‘have to be specifically pleaded’ (Teubner 506), sitting in Windeyer J’s first and second ways, capacity diminished for a time and needs created that ‘would not otherwise exist’. Being ‘loosely equated with economic loss, both past and future’, each takes its provision: past expenses and lost earnings (CLA s 12(1)(a)), future needs (s 13), gratuitous care (s 15), superannuation (s 15C); the heads are stated as figures rather than at large.”

B · general damages, at large

  “‘[The losses the question names]’ are general damages: ‘[the unquantified losses the facts supply]’ are ‘incapable of mathematical calculation’ and ‘at large’ (Teubner 506), a figure arrived at rather than added up, within Windeyer J’s first and third ways, capacity destroyed and pain produced. Lost earning capacity accordingly takes CLA ss 12(1)(b) and 13, and pain and suffering, lost amenities, lost expectation of life and disfigurement take ss 16–17. Therefore, the better view is that the assessment proceeds on stated assumptions.”

Sort every named loss before opening any head, and say in one clause which heads the facts do not raise · For an economic head → 2 and take the question’s own order · For pain, disfigurement, lost amenity or shortened life → B10 · For a loss answering no obvious head → B25 · → 2

2 Head 1 Past loss of earnings = actual loss

“Step 1 is [P]’s actual loss of wages from the date of the injury until the start of trial (CLA s 12(1)(a); Graham): [P] earned [P’s weekly earnings before the injury] and earned [P’s weekly earnings after the injury, or nil] over [the weeks or months between injury and trial]. The loss is assessed at the capacity [P] was working at the time of injury and evidenced by [the pay slips or work rosters the facts supply], past economic loss being recoverable where ‘due to loss of earnings or the deprivation or impairment of earning capacity’ (CLA s 12(1)(a); Todorovic). Graham (347) sets the method for the whole of economic loss, calculated ‘by reference to the actual loss of wages which occurs up to the time of the trial and which can be more or less precisely ascertained’ before any assessment of future loss. The past head takes the ascertained figure, [that difference multiplied out over the period, or the shortfall stated], net of tax (Cullen, which decided the calculation is on net after-tax income, overruling Atlas Tiles). Step 2 applies the cap, no more than three times the average weekly NSW wage (CLA s 12(2)–(3)).”

A · full difference recovered

  “[P] earned [P’s weekly earnings before the injury] and earned [P’s weekly earnings after the injury, or nil] over [the period between injury and trial]; [the medical finding that stops P doing the pre-injury job] kept [P] from the old work throughout, the loss assessed at the capacity exercised at injury, evidenced by [the pay slips or rosters]. Step 1 recovers ‘the actual loss of wages which occurs up to the time of the trial’ in full (CLA s 12(1)(a); Graham 347), net of tax (Cullen).”

B · defendant’s trimmed calculation

  “[The post-injury job and its weekly wage] trims the loss: [P] earned [P’s weekly earnings before the injury], and what was in fact earned over [the weeks or months between injury and trial] comes off, the head reaching only loss ‘due to loss of earnings or the deprivation or impairment of earning capacity’ (CLA s 12(1)(a); Todorovic). Graham (347) fixes the measure at ‘the actual loss of wages … up to the time of the trial’, no more; Step 1 gives [the shortfall stated] less actual earnings, net of tax (Cullen).”

Step 2 is the cap: if gross weekly earnings may exceed three times average weekly earnings → B1, the ceiling operating on the weekly rate before that rate is multiplied out · Being a loss already suffered, the head takes no present value discount, s 14 speaking only of ‘future economic loss’; it remains open to a vicissitudes adjustment on [P]’s own employment history, because the question remains whether [P] would in fact have earned throughout the period · Then open the next head the question names, or → 6

3 Head 2 Calculating Future Economic Loss

“Step 1: [P]’s most likely circumstances but for the injury were [the job, weekly wage and retirement age P would most likely have had but for the injury], this head lying where the loss is ‘due to the deprivation or impairment of earning capacity’ (CLA s 12(1)(b)). No award may be made unless the assumptions ‘accord with the claimant’s most likely future circumstances but for the injury’ (CLA s 13(1)), the assumption multiplied over the remaining working years. The court values ‘the capital asset of the injured person, namely, his capacity to earn money’ (Cullen 7–8), and ‘[c]ompensation for loss of earning capacity is paid only because it is or may be productive of financial loss’ (Sharman, citing Graham). The assumption gives [the weekly shortfall in earning capacity] a week over [the years from trial to P’s likely retirement]. Foreseeability plays no part in fixing the figure: ‘[y]ou have to pay the actual loss to the man’, even one who ‘turn[s] out to be a millionaire engaged in a very profitable business’ (The Arpad per Scrutton LJ). Where [P] is a woman with a prospect of marriage, the prospect is disregarded, Sharman adopting ‘the expedient course of simply disregarding the prospect of marriage as a relevant factor in the assessment of such a plaintiff’s future economic loss’. Step 2 subtracts outgoings (Wynn); Step 3 subtracts the cost of living during any ‘lost years’ (Sharman); Step 4 adds or subtracts contingencies of about 15% (Wynn); Step 5 applies the cap (CLA s 12(2)–(3)).”

A · full future loss

  “[P]’s most likely circumstances but for the injury were [the job, weekly wage and retirement age]; [the qualification, job offer or promotion track in the facts] makes that path probable, so the assumptions ‘accord with the claimant’s most likely future circumstances but for the injury’ (CLA s 13(1)). The court values ‘the capital asset … capacity to earn money’ (Cullen 7–8), the lost capacity plainly ‘productive of financial loss’ (Sharman, citing Graham); Step 1 gives [the weekly shortfall] a week over [the years to retirement].”

B · defendant’s trimmed assumption

  “[The work P can still do and what it pays weekly] shows residual capacity remains, and the head reaches only loss ‘due to the deprivation or impairment of earning capacity’ (CLA s 12(1)(b)): compensation is paid ‘only because it is or may be productive of financial loss’ (Sharman, citing Graham), so what remains earnable comes off the s 13(1) assumption, no deduction made for any prospect of marriage (Sharman). Step 1 accordingly gives only [the weekly shortfall in earning capacity] a week over [the years from trial to P’s likely retirement].”

C · capacity revives in the future

  “[The different work P can resume, and when] revives the capacity: [P] cannot continue as [the pre-injury work] but can in time work as [the work P can resume], which pays [what that work pays] against [the pre-injury weekly wage], though only in the future, so there is a period for which the full shortfall still runs. The assumption that best ‘accord[s] with the claimant’s most likely future circumstances but for the injury’ (CLA s 13(1)) is therefore [the full weekly shortfall] over [the interim period] and [the residual shortfall, or nothing] thereafter.”

State [the weekly shortfall in earning capacity] a week over [the years from trial to P’s likely retirement] · Step 2: if there are outgoings P will no longer pay, or future hospital board → B2 · Step 3: if life expectancy is shortened → B3; if not, say so in a clause · Step 4: the vicissitudes percentage, mandatory wherever future economic loss is awarded, is fixed at B14 · Step 5: if a high earner → B1, the ceiling operating on the gross weekly rate before it is multiplied out · there is no work history → B4 · For superannuation → B12 · The arithmetic order qualifies the step order in one respect: the present value discount is struck first at 6 and the vicissitudes percentage is then applied to the discounted sum, never to the undiscounted figure · Then open the next head the question names, or → 6

4 Head 3 Economic loss- medical costs

“‘[The treatment, aid, modification or journey claimed]’ costs [the price of that treatment, or that it is costly] and offers [the benefit, stated as benefit to health or benefit only to amenity]. The head takes medical, hospital, nursing, physiotherapy and ongoing care costs, past and future: past outlay is recoverable ‘upon proof of payment or liability to pay’ by [P], being both a need the injury created, an injury ‘may create needs that would not otherwise exist’ (Teubner 506), and an act of mitigation. For the future, [P] may expect to be compensated only for the level of care that is reasonable in all the circumstances, ‘not what are the ideal requirements but what are the reasonable requirements of the respondent’ (Arthur Robinson per Barwick CJ, adopted in Sharman). Where there is more than one option, the touchstone weighs ‘financial cost against relative health benefits to the plaintiff’ (Sharman): its quadriplegic and epileptic plaintiff, aged 20 and expected to live twenty to thirty years more, sought care at home at a present value of about $390,000 when hospital care cost about $108,500, and because the home benefit was ‘not any benefit to her health but rather to her future enjoyment of life’ the defendant paid only the hospital cost. On that footing ‘[the treatment, aid, modification or journey claimed]’ [is, or is not] reasonable.”

A · treatment reasonable

  “[The medical evidence that the treatment improves health, not merely enjoyment] shows ‘[the treatment claimed]’ meets a need the injury created (Teubner 506), its cost, [the price], proportionate to that health benefit, recovery reaching ‘the reasonable requirements of the respondent’ (Arthur Robinson per Barwick CJ). Sharman is distinguishable: its plaintiff was held to hospital cost only because home care bought ‘not any benefit to her health but rather to her future enjoyment of life’, whereas this benefit is to [P]’s health itself; it is reasonable.”

B · treatment unreasonable

  “[The medical evidence on what the treatment achieves] leaves the gain from ‘[the treatment, aid, modification or journey claimed]’ uncertain at [the price of that treatment]: ‘[i]f cost is very great and benefits to health slight or speculative, the cost-involving treatment will clearly be unreasonable’, the more so where there is ‘an alternative and relatively inexpensive mode of treatment’ (Sharman). Sharman is analogous, home care of about $390,000 against $108,500 in hospital buying amenity and no health benefit; weighing ‘financial cost against relative health benefits to the plaintiff’ (Sharman), it is not reasonable.”

C · Sharman both ways

  “Sharman cuts both ways on ‘[the treatment, aid, modification or journey claimed]’. It confined recovery to hospital cost where home care bought only ‘future enjoyment of life’, and [the respect in which the benefit here is only to amenity] matches that; yet it also allowed $20,000 for nursing and transport on periodic outings, the amenity there proportionate to the cost, as [the respect in which this claim is proportionate in the same way] is here, the measure being ‘the reasonable requirements of the respondent’ (Arthur Robinson). Therefore, the better view is that it is reasonable, though arguable.”

a treatment was recommended and not taken → B5 · care was given free of charge → 5 · Otherwise open the next head the question names, or → 6

5 Head 4 Economic loss – gratuitous care

“Step 1: ‘[the carer, named, and their relationship to P]’ provides [hours of care per week, and whether the care is domestic, nursing, or aimed at alleviating the injury] a week for [the months over which that care runs] without charge, care of a kind that would otherwise have to be provided ‘in a hospital or nursing home, or by a paid nurse or team of nurses working in the plaintiff’s home’ (Griffiths per Gibbs J). ‘[A]ttendant care services’ means ‘services of a domestic nature’, ‘services relating to nursing’ or ‘services that aim to alleviate the consequences of an injury’, and they are ‘gratuitous’ where ‘the claimant has not paid or is not liable to pay’ (CLA s 15(1)), which ‘[the words in the facts about payment]’ [answers, or does not]. Griffiths is the foundation: its quadriplegic plaintiff recovered for housework and nursing-type care given gratuitously by his fiancée and members of his family, ‘the value of the services so provided should not reduce the damages payable to the plaintiff’. Van Gervan (333) governs the rate, the market cost, because that compensates [P] ‘whether or not the gratuitous care provider continues to provide that care’, the carer’s forgone income answering nothing.”

A · gratuitous care established

  “Step 1: ‘[the carer, named, and their relationship to P]’ provides [hours of care per week, and the kind of care] for [the months that care runs]; nothing is paid and nothing owed, so the services are ones ‘for which the claimant has not paid or is not liable to pay’ (CLA s 15(1)). Griffiths is analogous, ‘[the domestic or nursing task performed]’ being care otherwise provided ‘in a hospital or nursing home, or by a paid nurse’, its value not reducing [P]’s damages (Griffiths per Gibbs J).”

B · not gratuitous

  “[The wage, fee or reimbursement P pays or is liable to pay the carer] leaves [P] liable to pay, so ‘[the carer, named]’s’ services are not ones ‘for which the claimant has not paid or is not liable to pay’ (CLA s 15(1)); the head fails, and the paid cost is instead claimed as a medical expense. Griffiths is distinguishable, its fiancée and family owed nothing for their housework and nursing care; Van Gervan (333) would govern only the rate, the market cost, the carer’s forgone income answering nothing.”

Step 2 is s 15(2) → B6 · Step 3 is s 15(3) → B7 · Step 4 is the s 15(4)–(5) cap → B8 · Where time is short, write the short form of B6 and B7 in one sentence, “Secondly, [the task P cannot do without help] shows a reasonable need arising ‘solely because of the injury’, services that ‘would not be … provided … but for the injury’ (CLA s 15(2)), and thirdly, [the hours and months] runs ‘at least 6 hours per week’ for ‘at least 6 consecutive months’ (CLA s 15(3))”, and put the cap in a clause: “Fourthly, under forty hours, the cap is an hourly rate of one-fortieth of average weekly NSW earnings (CLA s 15(5)), at market cost (Van Gervan 333).” If the facts dwell on what the carer gave up → B26 · Where the need for care might in any event have arisen from causes unconnected with the injury, the award is discounted for that chance (Malec) → B16 · Then open the next head the question names, or → 6

6 Head 5 Economic loss – discount rate

“[The head being discounted, named] runs on after trial for [the number of future years the head runs], and where an award includes ‘any component, assessed as a lump sum, for future economic loss of any kind’, its present value ‘is to be determined by adopting the prescribed discount rate’ (CLA s 14(1)), the rate prescribed by the regulations or, absent one, ‘a discount rate of 5%’ (CLA s 14(2)(b)). The reason is the time value of money: [P] takes now, as investable capital, what would have come week by week, so the lump sum’s interest and investment potential is balanced against inflation eroding its future value. The 5% accordingly applies to future loss of earnings, future medical expenses and future gratuitous care alike, giving [the present value of that head, or that it is materially cut].”

A · present value struck

  “[The continuing need or incapacity the facts give] carries [the head being discounted, named] well beyond judgment, for [the number of future years the head runs]; the award for it is ‘a lump sum, for future economic loss’, so its present value is fixed by ‘the prescribed discount rate’ (CLA s 14(1)), 5% where none is prescribed (s 14(2)(b)), because the sum’s investment potential would otherwise overcompensate [P]. The head is accordingly discounted to [the present value of that head, or that it is materially cut].”

B · discount bites hard

  “[The length of that future period, stated in years] makes the 5% discount compound against [P] (CLA s 14(1), (2)(b)): [the weekly shortfall] a week over [the years the head runs] is [the undiscounted total] undiscounted, which falls to [the present value at 5%] and, after the vicissitudes deduction, to [the figure after the vicissitudes deduction] (CLA s 13(2)). [D] accordingly presses the compounding, and [the head being discounted, named] is cut to [the present value of that head, or that it is materially cut].”

Where no head runs past trial, say so in one clause and → 7 · Where time is short, one clause serves: “All future components are reduced to present value at 5% (CLA s 14(2)(b)), the vicissitudes percentage then applied to the discounted sum.” The two discounts differ in kind, present value discounting being equivalent to negative compound interest and the vicissitudes allowance to negative simple interest; the present value discount is calculated first and the vicissitudes percentage is then applied to the discounted sum · Non-economic loss is exempt from both discounts · the head turns on a chance rather than a certainty → B16 · To fix the vicissitudes percentage, which is mandatory wherever future economic loss is awarded → B14

7 Stating the award: Part 2 as a prohibition

“Stating them head by head, [each head assessed, named with its figure], [P] accordingly recovers [the sum of those heads], Part 2 operating as a prohibition: a court ‘cannot award damages, or interest on damages, contrary to this Part’ (CLA s 11A(3)). Sharman governs the form, the award stated head by head so that the parties obtain ‘a proper insight into the process of assessment’ and ‘an adequate opportunity of seeking the correction of error on appeal’ (George v Pinnock 934 per Sachs LJ, adopted in Sharman). Where future loss exceeds $100,000, the court ‘must first notify all the parties to the proceedings of the terms of the award it proposes to make’ (CLA s 23(2)), enabling a structured settlement (CLA s 22).”

A · award stated, recovered

  “Stating them head by head, [each head assessed, named with its figure], [P] recovers [the sum of those heads], each head within Part 2, which operates as a prohibition: a court ‘cannot award damages, or interest on damages, contrary to this Part’ (CLA s 11A(3)); [the source in the facts for each figure: payslips, invoices, medical reports] supplies each figure, the components set out to give ‘a proper insight into the process of assessment’ (George v Pinnock 934 per Sachs LJ, adopted in Sharman). Where future loss exceeds $100,000, s 23(2) notice enables a structured settlement (CLA s 22).”

B · head cut down

  “[The head that exceeds a Part 2 limit] cannot stand as claimed: [the statutory limit that head exceeds, and by how much] exceeds what Part 2 permits, and Part 2 is a prohibition, a court unable to ‘award damages, or interest on damages, contrary to this Part’ (CLA s 11A(3)); [P] accordingly recovers [the sum of those heads], reduced. The components are still set out head by head, giving ‘a proper insight into the process of assessment’ and ‘an adequate opportunity of seeking the correction of error on appeal’ (George v Pinnock 934 per Sachs LJ, adopted in Sharman).”

Short form: “Stating them head by head, [each head with its figure], [P] recovers [the total], Part 2 a prohibition (CLA s 11A(3)); interest runs on past economic loss only, none on non-economic loss or gratuitous care (CLA s 18(1)); [where contributory negligence is found, the total is reduced by [the percentage] (CLA s 5S)].” If a past economic component is valued → B21 for the full interest treatment · there are two defendants → B18 · contributory negligence is found → B19 before the total · The run ends

B1 The s 12(2) cap: three times average weekly earnings · when p’s gross weekly earnings may have exceeded three times average weekly earnings

“[P]’s ‘[P’s gross weekly earnings but for the injury]’ [exceeds the ceiling, the excess disregarded, or falls below it]: the court disregards the amount by which ‘the claimant’s gross weekly earnings would … have exceeded an amount that is 3 times the amount of average weekly earnings at the date of the award’ (CLA s 12(2)), that average being ‘the average weekly total earnings of all employees in New South Wales’ (CLA s 12(3)(a)). Operating on the weekly rate, the ceiling is struck on gross weekly earnings before that rate is multiplied out over the period, notwithstanding that the award itself compensates net earnings (Cullen).”

A · below the ceiling

  “[P’s gross weekly earnings but for the injury] sit under three times ‘the average weekly total earnings of all employees in New South Wales’ (CLA s 12(3)(a)), so nothing is disregarded: the section reaches only the amount by which gross weekly earnings ‘would … have exceeded an amount that is 3 times the amount of average weekly earnings at the date of the award’ (CLA s 12(2)). The ceiling is noted on the weekly rate before it is multiplied out, though it takes nothing here.”

B · cap bites, excess disregarded

  “[P]’s gross weekly earnings stood at [P’s gross weekly earnings but for the injury], above three times average weekly earnings, so the excess is disregarded: the court is to disregard the amount by which they ‘would … have exceeded an amount that is 3 times the amount of average weekly earnings at the date of the award’ (CLA s 12(2), (3)(a)). The ceiling is applied to the gross weekly rate before that rate is multiplied out over the years, thus only three times that average survives, however high the true loss.”

back to the head in issue → 2, or → 3 · → 6

B2 Deducting outgoings from lost earning capacity · when step 2 of the future head

“Step 2: [the named work expenses: fares, tools, uniform, work clothing] [are, or are not] deducted, because [P] ‘will not normally continue to incur all of the outgoings necessary for the realization of that capacity’, and to ignore that is ‘to compensate for a gross loss when it is only the net loss that is in fact suffered’ (Sharman). Sharman applies twice over: where future hospital expenses include board and lodging, ‘some no doubt fairly arbitrary proportion of the present value of future hospital expenses regarded as attributable to board and lodging must be taken and deducted from the present value of lost earning capacity’, so that [P] is not compensated twice. Wynn fixes the scope: only outgoings ‘necessarily incurred in or in connection with the employment or undertaking by which earning capacity is realised’ come off, so child care and domestic help escape, there being ‘simply no basis for treating domestic help as necessary’ to earning.”

A · outgoings not deducted

  “Step 2 takes nothing: [P] will incur [the named expenses: child care, domestic help, or another cost paid regardless of work] whether or not she works, so this is no case of outgoings ‘necessary for the realization of that capacity’ (Sharman), and the award compensates net loss without them. Wynn is analogous, only outgoings ‘necessarily incurred in or in connection with the employment or undertaking by which earning capacity is realised’ being deductible, which is why child care and domestic help escaped there. The outgoings are arguably not deducted.”

B · outgoings deducted

  “Step 2 bites: [the named work expenses: fares, tools, uniform, work clothing] were spent only in earning, so [P] ‘will not normally continue to incur all of the outgoings necessary for the realization of that capacity’, and to ignore them is ‘to compensate for a gross loss when it is only the net loss that is in fact suffered’ (Sharman); where future hospital costs include board and lodging, a ‘fairly arbitrary proportion’ also comes off to avoid double compensation (Sharman). They are deducted, within Wynn’s scope, being expenses of earning itself.”

C · Wynn both ways

  “Step 2 cuts both ways on [the named work expenses]. Sharman supports deduction, the expenses, like the hospital board and lodging proportion deducted there, being spent only in earning, the award otherwise compensating ‘a gross loss when it is only the net loss that is in fact suffered’; yet [the reason P would incur the expense whether or not working] points the other way, Wynn deducting only what is ‘necessarily incurred in or in connection with the employment’, as child care and domestic help were not. Therefore, the better view is that they are deducted, though not without difficulty.”

Short form: “Step 2: [the named work expenses], spent only in earning, come off, the award otherwise compensating ‘a gross loss when it is only the net loss that is in fact suffered’ (Sharman; Wynn).” If life expectancy is shortened → B3, where this deduction has ceased · otherwise → 6

B3 The ‘lost years’ · when step 3 of the future head

“Step 3: [the medical evidence shortening P’s life expectancy] shortens [P]’s life expectancy by [the years by which expectancy is shortened], and [P] is compensated ‘in respect of lost earning capacity during those years by which her life expectancy has been shortened’, though only ‘to the extent that they are years when she would otherwise have been earning income’ (Sharman, citing Skelton). Against that sum goes ‘at least the amount that the plaintiff would have expended on his own maintenance during those lost years’ (Sharman, citing Skelton per Taylor J 121–2), and no more, because by then ‘no outgoings whatever will be involved in respect of that period since it is assumed that the plaintiff will then be dead’, the board and lodging deduction taken from lost earning capacity having by then ceased to operate. The lost years accordingly [are, or are not] brought to account.”

A · lost years brought in

  “Step 3 bites: [the medical evidence shortening P’s life expectancy] cuts [P]’s expectancy by [the years by which expectancy is shortened], years in which she would otherwise have been earning, so she recovers ‘in respect of lost earning capacity during those years by which her life expectancy has been shortened’ (Sharman, citing Skelton). Off it comes ‘at least the amount that the plaintiff would have expended on his own maintenance during those lost years’ (Sharman, citing Skelton per Taylor J 121–2), and no more; the balance would have been hers. The lost years are brought to account.”

B · no shortened expectancy

  “Step 3 takes nothing: [the medical evidence that life expectancy is unaffected] leaves [P]’s expectancy untouched, so nothing is recovered ‘in respect of lost earning capacity during those years by which her life expectancy has been shortened’ (Sharman, citing Skelton). Sharman is distinguishable, its quadriplegic plaintiff facing twenty-four lost years; were it otherwise, against the sum would go ‘at least the amount that the plaintiff would have expended on his own maintenance during those lost years’ (Skelton per Taylor J 121–2). The lost years are not brought to account, and one clause says so.”

→ 6

B4 What about a child plaintiff? · when p had no salary or wage at the date of injury, or was a child, student, unemployed, part-time, casual or an unpaid domestic carer

“‘[What P was doing at the date of injury]’ leaves no wage to measure, yet the want of wages is no answer: the distinction between loss of earnings and loss of earning capacity ‘is real and radical’, and ‘[a] capacity to earn which is not being exercised nor presently intended to be exercised has a value which can be estimated’ (Atlas Tiles 210 per Barwick CJ), the distinction mattering most for plaintiffs ‘unemployed or under-employed at the date of the accident, for example, part-time or casual workers, students, children, and unpaid domestic carers’. Where there is ‘no work history nor educational attainment to guide the courts’ on most likely circumstances, evidence is usually parental occupations and education, using tables of average weekly earnings, and ‘[the parental occupations and education, and the average weekly earnings figure]’ supplies the assumption s 13(1) requires. Mann v Ellbourn is the counter, a capacity to earn not fully exploited not being compensated on the basis of full exploitation.”

A · capacity valued

  “‘[What P was doing at the date of injury]’ assists nothing against [P]: ‘[a] capacity to earn which is not being exercised nor presently intended to be exercised has a value which can be estimated’ (Atlas Tiles 210 per Barwick CJ), and ‘[the parental occupations and education, and the average weekly earnings figure]’ builds the s 13(1) assumption in the usual way where there is no work history. Mann v Ellbourn is distinguishable, its capacity unexploited by choice, whereas ‘[the reason P was out of the paid market]’ kept [P] from it. The capacity is valued.”

B · valued only as it would have been exercised

  “‘[The job or course P had before her and did not take]’ carries [P] only so far: a capacity to earn not fully exploited is not compensated on the basis of full exploitation (Mann v Ellbourn), and ‘[why P would not have exercised the capacity fully]’ leaves full exercise doubtful, the average-earnings figure an average and not a promise; yet the capacity still ‘has a value which can be estimated’ (Atlas Tiles 210 per Barwick CJ). Therefore, the better view is that it is valued at what [P] would in fact have exercised, discounted for the chance.”

Build the weekly figure before any cap or discount is taken · gross weekly earnings may be above three times average weekly earnings → B1 · To fix the contingency the assumption carries → B16 · → 6

B5 Mitigation · when a treatment was recommended and P has not undertaken it, or D says P should

“‘[The treatment recommended to P]’ is [unproven and costly, or well established and inexpensive], and plaintiffs in all types of actions must mitigate their losses (British Westinghouse), mitigation requiring, where the loss is personal injury, the acceptance of reasonable medical or other treatment. Sharman supplies the measure: ‘[i]f cost is very great and benefits to health slight or speculative, the cost-involving treatment will clearly be unreasonable’, the more so if there is ‘an alternative and relatively inexpensive mode of treatment, affording equal or only slightly lesser benefits’; [P] thus [must accept it, damages being assessed on that footing, or need not].”

A · refusal reasonable

  “[The evidence the treatment is unproven or carries its own risk] makes refusing ‘[the treatment recommended to P]’ no failure to mitigate, though plaintiffs in all types of actions must mitigate their losses (British Westinghouse): its benefits to health are ‘slight or speculative’ at [the cost of the recommended treatment], the very case Sharman marks unreasonable, and there is no ‘alternative and relatively inexpensive mode of treatment’ open to [P]. [P] therefore need not accept it, and damages stand on her present condition.”

B · treatment must be accepted

  “[The evidence about the alternative treatment] shows ‘[the cheaper alternative treatment]’ open to [P] at [its cost], an ‘alternative and relatively inexpensive mode of treatment, affording equal or only slightly lesser benefits’ (Sharman), and mitigation requires, where the loss is personal injury, the acceptance of reasonable medical treatment (British Westinghouse). [P] must therefore accept it, and damages are assessed on that footing, the dearer course unrecoverable.”

C · Sharman both ways

  “Sharman cuts both ways on ‘[the treatment recommended to P]’. It is ‘relatively inexpensive’ at [the cost of the recommended treatment] and mitigation requires the acceptance of reasonable medical treatment (British Westinghouse); yet [the evidence the treatment is unproven or carries its own risk] leaves its benefits ‘slight or speculative’, which Sharman marks unreasonable. Therefore, the better view is that [P] must accept it and damages are assessed on that footing, although the point is arguable.”

→ 4 → 6

B6 Step 2 – 15(2) · when always with 5

“Step 2: [the three s 15(2) conditions, named: reasonable need; need arising solely from the injury; services not otherwise provided] [are or are not] met, no damages being awarded unless ‘there is (or was) a reasonable need for the services to be provided’, the need ‘has arisen (or arose) solely because of the injury to which the damages relate’, and the services ‘would not be (or would not have been) provided to the claimant but for the injury’ (CLA s 15(2)). Where [P] is a child, only the excess over the care needed in any event answers (b) and (c).”

A · three conditions met

  “Step 2: [the specific task P can no longer do without help] shows a ‘reasonable need for the services’, arising ‘solely because of the injury’, services that ‘would not be … provided … but for the injury’ (CLA s 15(2)); all three conditions are met, [the care P received before the injury, or none] leaving nothing that would have been provided in any event. Where [P] is a child, only the excess over ordinary care is counted.”

B · conditions not met

  “Step 2 fails: [the care this carer provided before the injury] shows the care would continue regardless, so the need has not ‘arisen … solely because of the injury’ and the services cannot be ones that ‘would not be … provided … but for the injury’ (CLA s 15(2)); no damages may be awarded for them, however reasonable the need, one unmet condition being enough. Only ‘[any extra care the injury alone requires]’ survives as the excess.”

→ B7

B7 Step 3 – 15(3) · when always with B6

“Step 3: ‘[hours of care per week]’ and ‘[the number of consecutive months]’ [meet the threshold, or fall short], because no damages may be awarded unless the services are provided ‘for at least 6 hours per week’ and ‘for a period of at least 6 consecutive months’ (CLA s 15(3)), the threshold on which recovery turns.”

A · threshold met

  “Step 3: [the hours per week and the consecutive months, both stated] clears both limbs, the services provided ‘for at least 6 hours per week’ and ‘for a period of at least 6 consecutive months’ (CLA s 15(3)); the threshold on which recovery turns is met, and the head proceeds to the cap.”

B · threshold not met

  “Step 3 fails on [the actual hours per week, or the actual number of consecutive months]: however heavy the weekly load, both limbs must hold, the services provided ‘for at least 6 hours per week’ and ‘for a period of at least 6 consecutive months’ (CLA s 15(3)); care at six hours a day for four months recovers nothing, while the same care over six months recovers, and this care sits on the failing side. The head likely fails, though the consecutive months may yet accrue.”

Put s 15(3) as D’s argument and answer it; do not assume it against P. → B8

B8 Step 4 – 15(4) -cap · when always with B7

“Step 4: the ceiling is statutory, and ‘[the hours of care per week, stated as at or above 40, or below 40]’ decides which limb applies. Where the services ‘are provided or are to be provided for not less than 40 hours per week’, the damages ‘must not exceed’ ‘the amount per week comprising the amount estimated by the Australian Statistician as the average weekly total earnings of all employees in New South Wales’ (CLA s 15(4)(a)). Where they ‘are provided or are to be provided for less than 40 hours per week’, they ‘must not exceed the amount calculated at an hourly rate of one-fortieth of’ that amount (CLA s 15(5)). The Act caps the award, not the hours. Van Gervan (333) governs only the rate at which the capped hours are valued: ‘the market cost of services is ordinarily the reasonable and objective value of the need for those services’, not what the carer earned.”

A · under forty hours, the s 15(5) hourly rate

  “Step 4: ‘[the hours of care per week, below 40]’ puts the services under the line, so the damages ‘must not exceed the amount calculated at an hourly rate of one-fortieth of’ the average weekly total earnings figure (CLA s 15(5)), s 15(4) reaching only services ‘for not less than 40 hours per week’. The hours are valued at that hourly rate, Van Gervan (333) fixing the rate at ‘the market cost of services’, ‘the reasonable and objective value of the need’, and not at what the carer gave up.”

B · forty hours or more, the s 15(4) weekly ceiling

  “Step 4: ‘[the hours of care per week, at or above 40]’ takes the care to forty hours or more, so ‘[the weekly sum claimed]’ is cut to ‘the amount per week comprising the amount estimated by the Australian Statistician as the average weekly total earnings of all employees in New South Wales’ (CLA s 15(4)(a)), s 15(5) reaching only services ‘for less than 40 hours per week’. The Act caps the award, not the hours; the rate within the cap is market cost (Van Gervan 333), not the carer’s income.”

Where the need is permanent, value a future component and → 6 for the present value discount · Where the facts dwell on what the carer gave up → B26 · Then open the next head the question names, or → 6

B9A Loss of capacity to provide domestic services: the dependants, s 15B(1)–(2)(b) · when the facts say P can no longer cook, clean, drive, garden or care for a member of P’s family or household

“Section 15B reverses CSR v Eddy, which held there could be no recovery for the loss of a capacity to provide domestic services to others, and reinstates Sullivan v Gordon, where an injured mother recovered the value of her family’s care of the two children she could no longer care for herself. Firstly, ‘[the dependants the facts name, and their relationship to P]’ must be dependants, ‘wholly or partly dependent on the claimant’ and within a list running from spouse and de facto partner through ‘a child, grandchild, sibling, uncle, aunt, niece, nephew, parent or grandparent’ to ‘any other person who is a member of the claimant’s household’ (CLA s 15B(1)). Secondly, [P] must have ‘provided the services to those dependants before the time that the liability in respect of which the claim is made arose’ (CLA s 15B(2)(a)), and they must not be ‘capable of performing the services themselves by reason of their age or physical or mental incapacity’ (CLA s 15B(2)(b)).”

A · limbs (a) and (b) met

  “‘[The dependants the facts name, and their relationship to P]’ answer s 15B(1), ‘wholly or partly dependent on the claimant’ and within its list. Firstly, ‘[the domestic services P performed before the injury]’ shows [P] ‘provided the services … before the time that the liability … arose’ (CLA s 15B(2)(a)); secondly, ‘[the dependant’s age, or incapacity]’ shows they are not ‘capable of performing the services themselves by reason of their age or physical or mental incapacity’ (CLA s 15B(2)(b)). Sullivan v Gordon is analogous, its two children incapable by age alone. Both limbs are met.”

B · a limb fails

  “‘[The reason the dependants can perform the services themselves]’ defeats the head at the second limb: damages lie only where the dependants ‘were not (or will not be) capable of performing the services themselves by reason of their age or physical or mental incapacity’ (CLA s 15B(2)(b)), and age or incapacity is the only qualifying reason, mere convenience none. CSR v Eddy left no common law route back, so s 15B is the sole path, and Sullivan v Gordon is distinguishable, its two children plainly incapable by reason of age. The head accordingly arguably fails.”

C · the second limb both ways

  “The second limb cuts both ways. ‘[The dependant’s age, or physical or mental incapacity]’ supports [P], Sullivan v Gordon’s children being incapable by reason of age alone; yet ‘[the reason the dependants can perform the services themselves]’ points the other way, s 15B(2)(b) reaching only dependants not ‘capable of performing the services themselves’. Therefore, the better view is that the limb is met for the tasks the dependants cannot manage and the value is trimmed to those, s 15B(11) requiring the court to take into account [P]’s capacity before the injury.”

Answer each limb separately and never in one chain · For the 6 hours and 6 consecutive months, and for the reasonableness of the need → B9B

B9B Loss of capacity to provide domestic services: the 6 hours and 6 consecutive months, s 15B(2)(c)–(d) · when always with B9A

“Thirdly, there must be ‘a reasonable expectation that, but for the injury to which the damages relate, the claimant would have provided the services to the claimant’s dependants’ ‘for at least 6 hours per week’ and ‘for a period of at least 6 consecutive months’ (CLA s 15B(2)(c)). Fourthly, and separately, ‘there will be a need for the services to be provided for those hours per week and that consecutive period of time and that need is reasonable in all the circumstances’ (CLA s 15B(2)(d)). ‘[The hours per week and consecutive months of services the dependants will need]’ is measured against both, short-term and occasional assisted care disregarded up to four weeks in total (CLA s 15B(3)).”

A · limbs (c) and (d) met

  “Thirdly, ‘[the hours per week and consecutive months of services the dependants will need]’ grounds ‘a reasonable expectation’ that [P] would have provided the services ‘for at least 6 hours per week’ and ‘for a period of at least 6 consecutive months’ (CLA s 15B(2)(c)); fourthly, ‘[the reason the need for those hours is reasonable]’ makes the need ‘reasonable in all the circumstances’ (CLA s 15B(2)(d)). Sullivan v Gordon is analogous, the care of two young children plainly running past both limbs. Both are likely met.”

B · limb (c) falls short

  “Thirdly, ‘[which limb falls short: under 6 hours a week, or under 6 consecutive months]’ leaves s 15B(2)(c) unsatisfied, the expectation having to run ‘for at least 6 hours per week’ and ‘for a period of at least 6 consecutive months’, one limb failing being enough; the four-week disregard for short-term assisted care (CLA s 15B(3)) does not save it. Fourthly, (d) is answered on its own footing, ‘[the reason the need for those hours is or is not reasonable]’ deciding whether the need ‘is reasonable in all the circumstances’. The head accordingly arguably fails.”

C · the reasonableness of the need both ways

  “Thirdly, paragraph (d) cuts both ways. ‘[The reason the need for those hours is reasonable]’ supports [P], the dependants requiring the services for those hours over that period; yet ‘[the alternative provision already available to the dependants]’ points the other way, (d) requiring that the need be ‘reasonable in all the circumstances’. Sullivan v Gordon is the template, two young children needing care well past six hours a week for six consecutive months. Therefore, the better view is that the need is reasonable to the extent the dependants are not otherwise provided for.”

Answer (d) separately; it does not fall with (c) · For the cap and the exclusions → B9C

B9C Loss of capacity to provide domestic services: the s 15B(4) cap and the s 15B(5), (10) exclusions · when always with B9B, once the four limbs are answered

“The award ‘must not exceed the amount calculated at the same hourly rate as that provided by section 15(5) regardless of the number of hours involved’ (CLA s 15B(4)), thus the hourly rate governs however many hours are needed. A court ‘may only award damages for that loss in accordance with the provisions of this section’ and ‘must not include in any damages awarded to the claimant for non-economic loss a component that compensates the claimant for the loss of that capacity’ (CLA s 15B(5)). Damages are not awarded where [P] ‘could recover damages for gratuitous attendant care services (within the meaning of section 15) in respect of the same injury that caused the loss’ and those services would themselves supply the dependants with the lost domestic services (CLA s 15B(10)). In valuing, the court ‘must take into account’ [P]’s capacity before the injury, the benefit to persons for whom damages could not be awarded, and ‘the vicissitudes or contingencies of life’ (CLA s 15B(11)).”

A · a component survives the cap

  “‘[The hours of domestic services the dependants need]’ is valued at the s 15(5) hourly rate, the award capped ‘at the same hourly rate … regardless of the number of hours involved’ (CLA s 15B(4)), so extra hours lift the total, never the rate. Section 15B(10) does not bite: ‘[the reason the s 15 attendant care would not itself supply these services]’ shows the two claims cover different services. The court weighs [P]’s pre-injury capacity and ‘the vicissitudes or contingencies of life’ (CLA s 15B(11)); a component survives, none counted again as non-economic loss (s 15B(5)(b)).”

B · s 15B(10) denies the head

  “‘[The attendant care services under s 15 that would themselves supply these domestic services]’ denies the head: damages do not lie where [P] ‘could recover damages for gratuitous attendant care services (within the meaning of section 15) in respect of the same injury’ and their provision ‘also resulted (or would also result) in the claimant’s dependants being provided with the domestic services that the claimant has lost the capacity to provide’ (CLA s 15B(10)). Nothing is added to non-economic loss for it either (CLA s 15B(5)(b)). The head fails on that overlap.”

No component for this loss goes into non-economic loss (CLA s 15B(5)(b)), so do not count it again at B11 · → 6

B10 Non-economic loss: the s 16(1) threshold · when the facts give pain, scarring, a lost limb, organ or sense, a shortened life, or a pursuit P has given up

“[P]’s [the pain, scar, lost limb, organ or sense, shortened life or abandoned pursuit] answers ‘non-economic loss’, which means, exhaustively, ‘pain and suffering’, ‘loss of amenities of life’, ‘loss of expectation of life’ or ‘disfigurement’ (CLA s 3), the third limb governed by Skelton. ‘No damages may be awarded for non-economic loss unless the severity of the non-economic loss is at least 15% of a most extreme case’ (CLA s 16(1)), a threshold rather than a starting point.”

A · threshold crossed

  “[P]’s [the pain, scar, lost limb, organ or sense, shortened life or abandoned pursuit] answers ‘non-economic loss’, exhaustively ‘pain and suffering’, ‘loss of amenities of life’, ‘loss of expectation of life’ or ‘disfigurement’ (CLA s 3); because [the residual symptom that persists, and for how long] persists, its severity is ‘at least 15% of a most extreme case’ (CLA s 16(1)), a threshold rather than a starting point, and the head is open.”

B · below the threshold

  “[P]’s [the pain, scar, lost limb, organ or sense, shortened life or abandoned pursuit] is ‘non-economic loss’ (CLA s 3), yet because [the symptom that has resolved, and when] resolved, its severity falls short of ‘at least 15% of a most extreme case’, and ‘[n]o damages may be awarded’ below that line (CLA s 16(1)); the threshold is a bar, not a starting point, and the head closes however real the pain was.”

Non-economic loss is exempt from both the s 14 discount and the vicissitudes deduction, and bears no interest (CLA s 18(1)(a)) · Each loss is then put to its own head before any percentage is fixed → B23A. Where time is short, write each of B23A to B23D as one applied sentence, fold B24A into B24B as a clause, “quadriplegia certainly fulfils the requirement (Southgate), and [P] sits [below, or within] that class (Dell v Dalton)”, and value with the B11 short form: “The Table converts [the severity percentage] to [the Table proportion] of the $804,000 maximum declared by order published on 1 October 2025 (CLA ss 16(3), 17(3)), rounded to the nearest $500 (s 16(4)).”

B11 Non-economic loss: working the s 16(3) Table · when always with B24B, converting the severity percentage fixed there

“The ceiling and the Table fix what severity yields, the Note to s 16(3) setting the method in three steps: determine the severity as a percentage of a most extreme case, confirm the indexed maximum, then apply the Table’s percentage to that maximum. The statutory ceiling, ‘$350,000 … only in a most extreme case’ (CLA s 16(2)), gives way to the amount last declared under s 17, which ‘applies to the exclusion of the amount under section 16(2)’ (CLA s 17(3)), that amount being $804,000 by order published on 1 October 2025. The Table rises slowly to 25% and steeply thereafter, 15% severity giving 1%, 16% 1.5%, 17% 2%, 18% 2.5%, 19% 3%, 20% 3.5%, 21% 4%, 22% 4.5%, 23% 5%, 24% 5.5%, 25% 6.5%, 26% 8%, 27% 10%, 28% 14%, 29% 18%, 30% 23%, 31% 26%, 32% 30%, 33% 33%, and above 33% the proportion equals the severity percentage. Accordingly, [the severity percentage fixed at B24B] yields [the Table proportion for that percentage] of the maximum, rounded ‘to the nearest $500 (with the amounts of $250 and $750 being rounded up)’ (CLA s 16(4)), earlier decisions citable to establish the appropriate award (CLA s 17A(1)). The head is accordingly valued at [that proportion of $804,000, rounded].”

A · steep range, substantial

  “[The permanent limitation the facts give] places [P] past the bend of the s 16(3) Table, which rises slowly to 25% of a most extreme case and steeply thereafter, 27% severity yielding 10%, 30% 23%, 33% 33%, and above 33% the proportion equalling the severity percentage. [The severity percentage fixed at B24B], on the decided cases there stated, yields [the Table proportion for that percentage] of the $804,000 maximum, that being the amount last declared under s 17 by order published on 1 October 2025 (CLA ss 16(3), 17(3)), rounded to the nearest $500 (CLA s 16(4)); the head is substantial.”

B · slow range, modest

  “[The permanent limitation the facts give] leaves [P] on the slow rise of the s 16(3) Table, where severity between 15% and 25% of a most extreme case yields only 1% to 6.5% of the maximum. [The severity percentage fixed at B24B], on the decided cases there stated, yields only [the Table proportion for that percentage] of the $804,000 maximum, that being the amount last declared under s 17 by order published on 1 October 2025 (CLA ss 16(3), 17(3)), rounded to the nearest $500 (CLA s 16(4)); the head is modest, and that modesty is the scheme working, not an error.”

Damages for non-economic loss are awarded only where severity is at least 15% of a most extreme case, and then in accordance with the Table (CLA ss 16, 17) · The head is exempt from both discounts and bears no interest (CLA s 18(1)(a)) · The s 16 maximum is $804,000, by order published on 1 October 2025 · → 7

B12 Loss of superannuation entitlements: s 15C · when the facts say P was an employee on a salary or wage

“[P] being ‘[P’s job and employer at the date of injury]’, superannuation is a component of the capacity that earned it, recoverable as economic loss but capped at ‘the relevant percentage of damages payable (in accordance with this Part) for the deprivation or impairment of the earning capacity on which the entitlement to those contributions is based’ (CLA s 15C(1)), that percentage being ‘the minimum percentage required by law to be paid as employer superannuation contributions’ (CLA s 15C(2)). Wynn shows the mechanic in the opposite direction, Handley JA in the Court of Appeal reducing the future economic loss calculation itself to allow for superannuation contributions, so the sum is stated as a proportion of the earning-capacity award and never as a fresh figure. Section 15C accordingly [operates, or does not].”

A · s 15C bites

  “[The employment record in the facts] shows contributions paid on [P’s weekly wage], so superannuation follows the lost capacity, capped at ‘the relevant percentage of damages payable … for the deprivation or impairment of the earning capacity on which the entitlement to those contributions is based’ (CLA s 15C(1)), ‘the minimum percentage required by law’ (s 15C(2)). Wynn shows the mechanic, the Court of Appeal there adjusting the future economic loss calculation for superannuation contributions; here the sum is added as that proportion of the earning-capacity award, no more. Section 15C applies.”

B · no superannuation head

  “[The reason P was outside any scheme: self-employed, unpaid, or casual below the threshold] left [P] with no employer contributions, and s 15C caps a head that must first exist: recovery is ‘the relevant percentage of damages payable … for the deprivation or impairment of the earning capacity on which the entitlement to those contributions is based’ (CLA s 15C(1)), and no entitlement was based on [P]’s capacity. Wynn’s adjustment for superannuation contributions has nothing here to adjust. Section 15C does not operate.”

State it as a proportion of the earning-capacity award as already reduced under ss 12–14, not as a fresh figure, and do not discount it again · → 7

B13 Date of assessment: the verdict, not the accident · when the facts give an event or medical development between injury and trial

“‘[The event or medical development between accident and trial, and its date]’ displaces the prognosis: damages for personal injury, wrongful birth and death actions are assessed at the date of the verdict, facts preferred to prophecies. It is ‘proper to lead evidence at the trial as to any events or developments between the date of the accident and the date of the trial’, and ‘always proper to take account of developments with regard to the injuries which were caused by the defendant’s tort’ (Baker v Willoughby 490–491 per Lord Reid). For non-personal-injury matters the date is instead when the cause of action is complete. Latham CJ, taking a widow’s re-marriage into account, held that ‘where actual facts are known, speculation as to the probability of those facts occurring is surely an unnecessary second-best’ (Willis 109). The assessment thus proceeds on the facts as they stand at the verdict.”

A · deterioration proved before trial

  “‘[The event or medical development between accident and trial, and its date]’ has overtaken the prognosis, and [P] stands worse than [the prognosis given at the time of injury] allowed. It is ‘always proper to take account of developments with regard to the injuries which were caused by the defendant’s tort’ (Baker v Willoughby 490–491 per Lord Reid), and ‘actual improvement or deterioration in the medical condition of the injured person is taken into account’ (Thompson v Faraonio 7); ‘where actual facts are known, speculation … is surely an unnecessary second-best’ (Willis 109 per Latham CJ). The assessment proceeds on the facts at the verdict.”

B · recovery better than predicted

  “‘[The recovery in fact made before trial]’ displaces the prognosis in [D]’s favour: the gloomier prediction cannot be paid for, because facts are preferred to prophecy even against a plaintiff, ‘actual improvement or deterioration in the medical condition of the injured person is taken into account’ rather than ‘prognosis made immediately after the accident’ (Thompson v Faraonio 7), and ‘where actual facts are known, speculation as to the probability of those facts occurring is surely an unnecessary second-best’ (Willis 109 per Latham CJ). The assessment proceeds on [P]’s actual condition.”

Carry the fact into every head · → 1

B14 Fixing the vicissitudes percentage · when step 4 of the future head, whenever future economic loss is awarded

“Step 4: [P]’s [the named contingency: ill health, insecure industry, promotion track, maternity leave, industrial disputes] adjusts the allowance for the vicissitudes of life, the court required to ‘adjust the amount of damages … by reference to the percentage possibility that the events might have occurred but for the injury’ and to ‘state the assumptions on which the award was based and the relevant percentage’ (CLA s 13(2)–(3); Macarthur DMS, where a 20% allowance ‘sufficiently complied with s 13(2) and s 13(3)’). Contingencies are weighed for this plaintiff, not the workforce generally: death apart, ‘sickness, accident, unemployment and industrial disputes are the four major contingencies which expose employees to the risk of loss of income’ (Luntz, adopted in Wynn), and earning capacity lost while in jail draws nothing (Leschke, approved in Faulkner). The New South Wales practice is 15%, ‘subject to adjustment up or down to take account of the plaintiff’s particular circumstances’ (Wynn), and not all cuts go down: ‘all contingencies are not adverse: all vicissitudes are not harmful’ (Bresatz 544 per Windeyer J). Wynn itself moved from 5% at trial to 28% on appeal to 12.5% in the High Court, the ‘real possibility’ of promotion balanced against maternity leave and an earlier spinal injury. The percentage is [the vicissitudes percentage], because [why that contingency moves the 15% up or down for this plaintiff].”

A · adjusted below 15%

  “Step 4: [P]’s [the positive contingency: promotion track, secure industry, good health] moves the allowance below the 15% NSW practice, ‘subject to adjustment up or down to take account of the plaintiff’s particular circumstances’ (Wynn), for ‘all contingencies are not adverse: all vicissitudes are not harmful’ (Bresatz 544). Wynn itself settled at 12.5%, promotion balanced against maternity leave and prior injury, and [why P’s risks run lower] places [P] near it; the percentage is [the vicissitudes percentage], stated with its assumptions (CLA s 13(2)–(3)).”

B · adjusted above 15%

  “Step 4: [P]’s [the adverse contingency: ill health, insecure industry, industrial disputes] moves the allowance above 15%: sickness, accident, unemployment and industrial disputes are ‘the four major contingencies’ (Luntz, adopted in Wynn), and [why that contingency made P’s working life precarious] exposes [P] to them beyond the ordinary, the practice figure being ‘subject to adjustment up or down’ (Wynn); Macarthur DMS held 20% compliant with s 13(2)–(3). Bresatz’s reminder that not all vicissitudes are harmful does not meet [the adverse contingency]. The percentage is [the vicissitudes percentage], stated with its assumptions (CLA s 13(3)).”

C · Wynn and Bresatz both ways

  “Step 4 cuts both ways. [The adverse contingency] presses the 15% practice up, the figure ‘subject to adjustment up or down to take account of the plaintiff’s particular circumstances’ (Wynn); yet [the contingency that might as easily have improved P’s position] presses it down, ‘all contingencies are not adverse: all vicissitudes are not harmful’ (Bresatz 544), Wynn itself settling at 12.5% after balancing promotion against maternity leave and prior injury. Therefore, the better view is that the percentage is [the vicissitudes percentage], the court stating the assumptions and the percentage (CLA s 13(2)–(3); Macarthur DMS).”

The present value discount is taken first; the vicissitudes percentage is then applied to the discounted sum, never to the undiscounted figure · Past economic loss takes no present value discount, yet it remains open to a vicissitudes adjustment on P’s own employment history · D relies on s 13 to exclude a positive contingency → B15 · otherwise → 7; where a head’s working remains unstated → B22 first

B15 Does s 13 allow positive vicissitudes? · when p’s case depends on an uncertain but promising career path and D relies on s 13

“[P]’s ‘[the promotion, course or career step P says she would have had]’ is weighed in, Bresatz surviving s 13: all that need be established is ‘a real possibility’ of the advancement (Wynn). In Macarthur DMS Hodgson JA thought s 13 ‘may not allow for positive vicissitudes’, yet Giles JA approved Professor Luntz’s view that s 13 ‘enshrines in legislation the method for asserting an uncertain career path that was adopted in Norris v Blake (No. 2)’ (Nominal Defendant v Lane [63]–[65]). Therefore, the better view is that the positive contingency is weighed in.”

A · positive contingency weighed in

  “[The evidence of the promotion track in the facts] establishes ‘a real possibility’ of ‘[the promotion, course or career step]’, the standard Wynn sets, making it a prospect rather than speculation, and prospects of advancement are balanced into the percentage as Wynn balanced the vice-presidency against maternity leave. Hodgson JA’s doubt that s 13 ‘may not allow for positive vicissitudes’ (Macarthur DMS) is met by Giles JA’s approval of Luntz, s 13 enshrining ‘the method for asserting an uncertain career path’ from Norris v Blake (No 2) (Nominal Defendant v Lane [63]–[65]); it is weighed in.”

B · prospect too speculative

  “[The absence of evidence for that advancement] leaves ‘[the promotion, course or career step]’ short of ‘a real possibility’, the standard Wynn requires, and speculation rather than the prospect Bresatz would recognise; Wynn’s plaintiff had rapid promotion, ambition and an employer that promoted from within, and [P] shows nothing comparable. Hodgson JA’s doubt that s 13 ‘may not allow for positive vicissitudes’ at all (Macarthur DMS) is the better guide without that evidence. The contingency is not weighed in.”

State the percentage at B14 if it is not already stated, then → 7

B16 Valuing a chance: Malec · when a head turns on whether something would have happened, or the facts give a percentage prospect

“Malec operates because ‘[the hypothetical event and the percentage prospect the facts give it]’ is hypothetical, not a past fact: the notional full sum is discounted by the percentage chance of the future contingency eventuating (Malec), for ‘[d]amages founded on hypothetical evaluations defy precise calculation’ (Malec per Brennan and Dawson JJ, adopted in Wynn). Past facts, by contrast, are proved on the balance of probabilities. Gratuitous care awards are reduced the same way for the chance that causes unconnected with the negligence would have necessitated similar care (Malec). It is thus worth [the percentage prospect] of [the sum recoverable had the event come about]; the chance is valued, not decided.”

A · chance valued at a percentage

  “Malec operates: [the event that had to come about first, and when it would have] must first have come about before [P] could earn [the sum recoverable had the event come about], so the sum is hypothetical and is discounted by the percentage chance of the contingency eventuating, ‘[d]amages founded on hypothetical evaluations defy[ing] precise calculation’ (Malec per Brennan and Dawson JJ, adopted in Wynn). It is worth [the percentage prospect] of that sum; the chance is valued, not decided.”

B · a past fact, proved or not

  “Malec does not operate: ‘[the event said to be a chance]’ had already come about before [the date of the accident], a past fact proved on the balance of probabilities, and only a hypothetical or future event is discounted by the percentage chance of its eventuating (Malec per Brennan and Dawson JJ, adopted in Wynn). Proved, [the sum recoverable had the event come about] is recovered whole; unproved, none of it is; there is no middle percentage.”

the chance goes to a future economic component → B14 for the vicissitudes percentage · otherwise → 7

B17 Economic loss for consequential mental harm: s 33 · when a head rests on P’s psychiatric condition following a physical injury

“[P]’s ‘[P’s psychiatric condition as the facts name it]’ is consequential mental harm, and ‘[a] court cannot make an award of damages for economic loss for consequential mental harm resulting from negligence unless the harm consists of a recognised psychiatric illness’ (CLA s 33). Thus ‘[P’s psychiatric condition as the facts name it]’ [is a recognised psychiatric illness, so the economic components stand, or is not, and they fail].”

A · a recognised psychiatric illness

  “[The clinical evidence in the facts] shows ‘[P’s psychiatric condition as the facts name it]’ diagnosed by [the treating clinician], a recognised psychiatric illness, so s 33 does not bar it: an award of damages for economic loss for consequential mental harm lies where ‘the harm consists of a recognised psychiatric illness’ (CLA s 33). The economic components resting on the condition accordingly stand.”

B · not a recognised illness

  “[The absence of any diagnosis in the facts] leaves ‘[P’s psychiatric condition as the facts name it]’ [the lay word the facts use] rather than a diagnosis, so ‘[a] court cannot make an award of damages for economic loss for consequential mental harm resulting from negligence unless the harm consists of a recognised psychiatric illness’ (CLA s 33) bars the economic components resting on it; they fail, whatever the physical-injury heads recover.”

back to the head in issue → 2 → 3 → 4 → 5 → B9A or → B12 · Where the condition is not a recognised psychiatric illness, the non-economic loss claim survives the s 33 bar → B10

B18 Two defendants · when the facts name a second defendant, or the question asks what is owed if one and/or the other were liable

“[P] may sue one or more of [D1] and [D2] in a single action or series of actions, proportionate liability not reaching ‘any claim arising out of personal injury’ (CLA s 34(1)(a)). Sued alone, [D1] answers [the whole of P’s damage]; sued alone, [D2] answers [the part of the damage D2 caused]; and sued together, they answer [the whole of P’s damage]. Where instead [P]’s award against [D1] falls within Part 2 but the award against [D2] does not, s 19 fixes [D1]’s contribution to [D2] as a percentage the court thinks ‘just and equitable’, applied to damages determined as if Part 2 governed both awards (CLA s 19(2)); [P]’s recovery from [D2] is then reduced by the difference between the contribution [D2] would otherwise have been entitled to from [D1] and that figure (s 19(3)).”

A · indivisible harm, each answers all

  “[The injury said to be indivisible] cannot be divided between them, so sued alone [D1] answers the whole of [P]’s damage, sued alone [D2] answers the whole, and sued together they answer the whole, proportionate liability not reaching ‘any claim arising out of personal injury’ (CLA s 34(1)(a)). Where [D2]’s award falls outside Part 2, s 19 instead fixes [D1]’s contribution to [D2] as a ‘just and equitable’ percentage of damages determined as if Part 2 governed both awards, and reduces [P]’s recovery from [D2] by the difference between the ordinary contribution and that figure (s 19(2), (3)).”

B · divisible damage

  “[D2]’s want of care produced only [the further injury D2 alone caused], so the damage divides: sued alone, [D1] answers the whole of [P]’s damage; sued alone, [D2] answers only that further part; sued together, the whole is answered between them, proportionate liability not reaching ‘any claim arising out of personal injury’ (CLA s 34(1)(a)). Where [D2]’s award falls outside Part 2, s 19 fixes [D1]’s contribution to [D2] as a ‘just and equitable’ percentage of damages determined as if Part 2 governed both awards, and reduces [P]’s recovery from [D2] by the difference between the ordinary contribution and that figure (s 19(2), (3)).”

Value each head once; do not reassess it against the second defendant · → 7
