B19 Reduction for contributory negligence · when p has been found contributorily negligent

“[P] being contributorily negligent, the court fixes a single percentage by comparing each party’s departure from the standard of care and the causal potency of their acts (Podrebersek), taken once from the total of the heads assessed above and not from any head separately. [P] accordingly recovers the total assessed less [P’s percentage share of responsibility], and ‘a court may determine a reduction of 100% if the court thinks it just and equitable to do so, with the result that the claim for damages is defeated’ (CLA s 5S).”

A · modest share, taken once

  “[P’s own conduct, as the facts describe it] was mere inadvertence, so the comparison of culpability and causal potency (Podrebersek) yields a modest percentage, taken once from the total of the heads assessed and not from any head separately. [P] recovers the total less [P’s percentage share of responsibility], the 100% reduction s 5S permits nowhere in view.”

B · substantial share, taken once

  “[P’s own conduct, as the facts describe it] courted [the danger P courted], so the comparison of culpability and causal potency (Podrebersek) yields a substantial percentage, taken once from the total of the heads assessed and not from any head separately. [P] recovers the total less [P’s percentage share of responsibility], and the reduction may reach 100%, ‘with the result that the claim for damages is defeated’ (CLA s 5S).”

→ 7 (state the total)

B20 Exemplary, punitive and aggravated damages: s 21 · when the facts show an act done deliberately to injure, or the question asks whether D can be punished

“‘[The act or omission that caused the injury]’ being negligence, [nothing lies beyond compensation, or s 21 is not engaged]: at common law exemplary and aggravated damages may be awarded, yet both are excluded where the civil liability legislation applies, for ‘[i]n an action for the award of personal injury damages where the act or omission that caused the injury or death was negligence, a court cannot award exemplary or punitive damages or damages in the nature of aggravated damages’ (CLA s 21).”

A · nothing beyond compensation

  “[The conduct the facts describe] shows want of care, not design to injure [P], so nothing lies beyond compensation: exemplary and aggravated damages, available at common law, are excluded where the CLA applies, a court unable to ‘award exemplary or punitive damages or damages in the nature of aggravated damages’ where ‘the act or omission that caused the injury or death was negligence’ (CLA s 21).”

B · intentional act, s 21 not engaged

  “[The words showing the act was done with intent to injure] make ‘[the act or omission that caused the injury]’ no negligence but [the intentional tort the facts disclose], so s 21 does not reach the award: its bar operates only ‘where the act or omission that caused the injury or death was negligence’ (CLA s 21), and at common law exemplary and aggravated damages remain available for such conduct.”

→ 7

B21 Interest: what s 18 does and does not bar · when a past economic component has been valued, or the question names non-economic loss or gratuitous attendant care, on which s 18(1)(a)–(b) bars interest outright

“Firstly, no interest runs on ‘non-economic loss’ (CLA s 18(1)(a)); secondly, none on ‘gratuitous attendant care services as defined in section 15’ (s 18(1)(b)); thirdly, none on lost capacity to provide gratuitous domestic services (s 18(1)(c)). Interest otherwise runs ‘for the period from when the loss to which the damages relate was first incurred until the date on which the court determines the damages’ (CLA s 18(2)(a)), at ‘the relevant interest rate’ (s 18(3)(b)), ‘the rate representing the Commonwealth Government 10-year benchmark bond rate as published by the Reserve Bank of Australia’ (s 18(4)). Accordingly, [interest runs, or is barred].”

A · interest runs

  “[The reason the component is past economic loss] makes [P]’s [the past economic component valued] past economic loss, and none of the three bars reaches it, being neither ‘non-economic loss’, nor ‘gratuitous attendant care services as defined in section 15’, nor lost domestic-services capacity (CLA s 18(1)). Interest runs ‘from when the loss … was first incurred until the date on which the court determines the damages’ (s 18(2)(a)), at the Commonwealth 10-year benchmark bond rate (s 18(3)(b), (4)).”

B · interest barred

  “[The words in the facts describing the claim] make [P]’s [claimed head] [the s 18(1) head it answers], and s 18(1) bars interest on ‘non-economic loss’, on ‘gratuitous attendant care services as defined in section 15’, and on lost capacity to provide gratuitous domestic services (CLA s 18(1)(a)–(c)). Interest would otherwise have run from when the loss was first incurred at the Commonwealth 10-year benchmark bond rate (s 18(2)(a), (4)); it does not.”

→ 7

B22 Calculating economic loss: stating the figures · when only where a head’s working was not already stated inside the head itself, or the question asks for the assessment gathered in one place

“Each head is stated as a calculation, not asserted; where the facts do not supply a figure, the assumption is stated, shown to ‘accord with the claimant’s most likely future circumstances but for the injury’ (CLA s 13(1)), s 13(3) itself requiring the assumptions to be stated. Past economic loss is [the past weekly shortfall] a week over [the weeks or months between injury and trial], net of tax (Cullen). Future economic loss is [the future weekly shortfall] a week over [the years from trial to P’s likely retirement], reduced to present value at ‘a discount rate of 5%’ (CLA s 14(2)(b)), then reduced by [the vicissitudes percentage] for vicissitudes (CLA s 13(2)). Gratuitous care is [hours of care per week] a week at the s 15(5) rate, capped by s 15(4). Where non-economic loss is among the heads named, it is [the severity percentage] of a most extreme case, which the Table converts to [the Table proportion] of the maximum, rounded ‘to the nearest $500’ (CLA s 16(4)).”

A · plaintiff’s full calculation

  “Past economic loss runs [the past weekly shortfall] a week over the whole of [the period between injury and trial], [the medical finding] keeping [P] out of work throughout, net of tax (Cullen). Future economic loss runs [the future weekly shortfall] a week over [the years to retirement] on stated s 13(1) assumptions, discounted at 5% (CLA s 14(2)(b)) and reduced by [the vicissitudes percentage] (s 13(2)), no further. Gratuitous care takes [hours per week] at the s 15(5) rate; non-economic loss takes [the severity percentage], converting to [the Table proportion] of the maximum (CLA s 16(3)–(4)).”

B · defendant’s calculation

  “Past economic loss is [the past weekly shortfall] a week over [the period] less what was in fact earned, [the post-injury job and its weekly wage] coming off, net of tax (Cullen). Future economic loss is trimmed for remaining capacity to [the future weekly shortfall] a week on the s 13(1) assumptions, discounted at 5% (CLA s 14(2)(b)) and cut by the full [the vicissitudes percentage] (s 13(2)). Gratuitous care is capped by s 15(4)–(5), the cap taken; non-economic loss is [the severity percentage], yielding only [the Table proportion] of the maximum (CLA s 16(3)–(4)).”

→ 7 · there are two defendants → B18 first; if contributory negligence is found → B19 first

B23A Head 1 Pain and suffering · when the facts give physical pain, worry, anxiety, frustration, inconvenience or discomfort, or P has been rendered unconscious

“Pain and suffering is subjective: ‘[the pain, worry, anxiety, frustration, inconvenience or discomfort the facts describe]’ answers the head only where [P] actually endures it, the head including physical pain as well as worry, anxiety, frustration, inconvenience and discomfort (Sharman). No damages are awarded where [P] has been rendered permanently unconscious, awards being made only where pain and suffering are subjectively experienced (Skelton). Skelton’s plaintiff, aged 19, suffered severe brain damage and was likely to remain unconscious in hospital for six months until his expected death. He could not recover for pain and suffering, though some compensation was given for loss of amenities and loss of expectation of life. The anguish of mind caused by the subjective experience of loss of life expectancy is taken into account under this head, not under expectation of life (Sharman), and nothing is awarded for pain and suffering during the ‘lost years’, as they are not subjectively experienced.”

A · head engaged, P conscious of the pain

  “‘[The pain, worry, anxiety, frustration, inconvenience or discomfort the facts describe]’ engages the head: ‘[the evidence P consciously experiences that pain]’ shows [P] actually endures it, and the head reaches physical pain as well as worry, anxiety, frustration, inconvenience and discomfort (Sharman). Skelton is distinguishable, its plaintiff unconscious and expected never to wake, whereas [P] feels each of these; any anguish at a shortened life expectancy is counted here too (Sharman), though nothing is counted for the lost years themselves.”

B · no pain and suffering, P unconscious

  “‘[The finding of permanent unconsciousness, and its source]’ defeats the head: awards are made only where pain and suffering are subjectively experienced, and Skelton is analogous, its plaintiff, aged 19 with severe brain damage, likely to remain unconscious until his expected death, recovering nothing for pain and suffering, though some compensation was given for loss of amenities and loss of expectation of life. [P] stands in that position, realising nothing; those two heads survive objectively, and modestly.”

C · Skelton both ways

  “Skelton cuts both ways on ‘[the pain the facts describe]’. It is analogous because ‘[the finding of impaired or intermittent consciousness, or memory loss, and its source]’ leaves [P] often unaware, and awards are made only where pain and suffering are subjectively experienced, so memory loss may lessen the damages; yet ‘[the evidence P consciously experiences that pain]’ points the other way, [P] plainly enduring it in the periods of clarity (Sharman). Therefore, the better view is that the head lies, valued for those periods alone, though the point is arguable.”

Value nothing for pain and suffering during the lost years · Where the anguish of a shortened life expectancy is claimed, it is assessed here and not at B23C. → B23B

B23B Head 2 loss of amenities · when the facts name a pursuit, pastime, occupation or domestic role P can no longer do or enjoy

“Loss of amenities is a loss of the capacity of the injured person to consciously enjoy life to the full (Skelton), and ‘[the activities and pastimes the facts say P has lost]’ are put to it; its character is ambiguous, courts having awarded for both subjective and objective loss. The examples are the lost opportunity for a fulfilling marriage, parenthood and the realisation of ambitions, and the lost enjoyment of life, the everyday pleasures of living in one’s own home and the capacity to engage in hobbies, sport, work, marriage and child-bearing (Sharman), and the loss of an active and fulfilling life (Woolworths v Lawlor). Where the mind is willing but the body incapable there is a much higher degree of loss than where the victim is insensible to the lost capacity; an unconscious plaintiff receives only a small sum for the objective loss (Skelton), and no amounts are awarded during the ‘lost years’.”

A · conscious loss, head substantial

  “‘[The activities and pastimes the facts say P has lost]’ engage the head, ‘[the evidence P is conscious of each loss]’ showing [P] knows what has gone, and where the mind is willing but the body incapable the loss is much higher than where the victim is insensible to it (Skelton). Muzic is analogous: $40,000 where bocce twice a week, Italian cooking, knitting, helping at an old persons’ home, the domestic work at home, and swimming and fishing were lost, and [P] is deprived [as widely, or more widely, stated how]. The head is substantial.”

B · no consciousness of the loss, modest objective sum

  “‘[The finding that P is insensible to the loss]’ holds the head to a modest figure: an unconscious plaintiff can receive damages for loss of amenities, but only a small sum for the objective loss, the higher degree of loss belonging to the mind willing and the body incapable (Skelton). Muzic is distinguishable, its plaintiff consciously deprived of bocce, cooking and helping at an old persons’ home for $40,000. The head stands objectively and modestly, and nothing is awarded for the lost years.”

C · Muzic both ways

  “Muzic cuts both ways as the decided case. It is analogous because ‘[the activities lost that answer Muzic’s bocce, cooking, knitting and swimming]’ match the pursuits lost there for $40,000, that court treating the head as primarily subjective; yet ‘[the loss P has that Muzic’s plaintiff did not]’ points the other way, [P]’s deprivation running past pastimes into the everyday pleasures of one’s own home and the capacity for work, marriage and child-bearing (Sharman), an active and fulfilling life lost (Woolworths v Lawlor). Therefore, the better view is that the head is valued above Muzic.”

No amounts are awarded for loss of amenities during the lost years · Where P’s life expectancy is reduced → B23C; where it is not, say so in one clause and → B23D

B23C Head 3 Loss of expectancy · when the facts reduce P’s life expectancy

“The head is awarded where [P] has a reduced life expectancy, and ‘[the years by which the facts reduce P’s life expectancy]’ is what it measures: loss of expectation of life ‘bears no relationship to lost earning capacity during “lost years” but is rather the loss of a measure of prospective happiness’ (Skelton per Taylor J, adopted in Sharman). The amount for the objective loss is ‘a quite conventional sum, very moderate in amount’ (Sharman), the High Court there cutting the $6,000 allowed below to not more than $2,000. An amount is awarded for the objective loss of life expectancy, this being the lost years, but the anguish of mind which the appreciation of that loss causes is pain and suffering, valued under that head (Sharman).”

A · head engaged, a conventional sum

  “‘[The medical evidence reducing life expectancy]’ engages the head, ‘[the years by which the facts reduce P’s life expectancy]’ measuring ‘the loss of a measure of prospective happiness’ (Skelton per Taylor J, adopted in Sharman). Sharman is analogous, its plaintiff facing twenty-four lost years, and the objective amount is ‘a quite conventional sum, very moderate in amount’, the $6,000 below cut there to not more than $2,000. The anguish of appreciating the shortening is valued as pain and suffering, not here; a modest objective figure stands for the lost years.”

B · no reduced expectancy, head not opened

  “‘[The medical evidence that life expectancy is unaffected, or the silence of the facts]’ closes the head, which is awarded only where [P] has a reduced life expectancy: Sharman is distinguishable, its quadriplegic plaintiff’s expectancy cut by twenty-four years, and Skelton further still, its plaintiff expected to die within months. Were it otherwise, the amount would be ‘a quite conventional sum, very moderate in amount’ (Sharman). Nothing falls here, and any anguish at mortality is in any event pain and suffering, not this head.”

C · objective or subjective, both ways

  “The head’s character cuts both ways, objective and subjective at once (Sharman). An amount is awarded for the objective loss of life expectancy, the lost years, and it is held to ‘a quite conventional sum, very moderate in amount’; yet ‘[the evidence P appreciates the shortening]’ shows [P] feels it. Sharman resolves the tension: the anguish of mind counts under the pain and suffering head, this head remaining ‘the loss of a measure of prospective happiness’ (Skelton per Taylor J). Therefore, the better view is that a conventional sum lies here and the anguish is valued under that head.”

Value the anguish of appreciating the shortening at B23A, not here · Where earning capacity in the lost years is also claimed → B3 · → B23D

B23D Head 4 Disfigurement · when the facts give scarring, the loss of one or more limbs, or the loss of one or both eyes

“Disfigurement is the mental impact of the loss of one or more limbs, scarring, or the loss of one or both eyes, awarded for the objective loss, and ‘[the scarring, lost limb or lost eye the facts give]’ is put to it; the ‘lost years’ are not relevant here. It is one of the four exhaustive limbs of ‘non-economic loss’ (CLA s 3), thus it is stated and then carried into the single severity percentage rather than earning a separate sum.”

A · disfigurement engaged

  “‘[The scarring, lost limb or lost eye the facts give]’ engages the head, disfigurement being the mental impact of the loss of one or more limbs, scarring, or the loss of one or both eyes, awarded for the objective loss, so ‘[the evidence of how the disfigurement appears to others]’ carries it whether or not [P] dwells on it. Jopling shows it is no makeweight, knee scarring counting at 28% alongside chronic pain and lost sport. It lifts the single severity percentage (CLA s 3) rather than earning a separate sum.”

B · disfigurement minor

  “‘[The reason the disfigurement is minor or concealed]’ holds the head down, disfigurement being awarded for the objective loss rather than for [P]’s distress, which is pain and suffering. Khan is analogous at 20%, a necrotic testicle removed with work capacity unchanged and no ongoing physical disability, the disfigurement unseen and the percentage modest, and [P] stands similarly. It is stated as one of the four s 3 limbs and not ignored, although it arguably adds little to the severity percentage.”

C · objective or subjective, both ways

  “The head’s character cuts both ways. It is awarded for the objective loss, so ‘[the evidence of how the disfigurement appears to others]’ governs; yet it is defined as the mental impact of the loss of limbs, scarring or eyes, and ‘[the evidence of P’s own distress at the disfigurement]’ is mental impact. Therefore, the better view is that the objective disfigurement fixes this head and the distress is valued as pain and suffering, the two not counted twice within the single severity percentage (CLA s 3).”

Add nothing under this head for the lost years · Once all four heads are stated → B24A

B24A ‘A most extreme case’ · when always with B23D

“The phrase fixes the top of the scale, not [P]’s place on it, and ‘[P’s injury, stated at its worst]’ is measured against that top. ‘Most’ means very, not in the greatest quantity, amount, measure, degree or number, and the indefinite article provides for the creation of a class of most extreme cases, which means the cases may be different and some may be worse than others (Dell v Dalton). Opinions on what constitutes a most extreme case will vary, ‘but quadriplegia certainly fulfils the requirement’ (Southgate v Waterford); total blindness accompanied by loss of limbs was a most extreme case (Mason v Demasi); and brain damage with a restricted ability to walk but an exceptionally limited ability to maintain an independent life would also qualify (Quintano). Sharman’s quadriplegic, ‘fully aware of her plight’, sits in that class.”

A · P below the class

  “‘[The function or faculty P retains that the class members had lost]’ keeps [P] below the class: the indefinite article creates a class of most extreme cases, different and some worse than others, so [P] need not be the worst imaginable, yet ‘most’ still means very (Dell v Dalton), and Southgate’s quadriplegia, Mason v Demasi’s total blindness with lost limbs and Quintano’s exceptionally limited independent life each lost what [P] retains. Therefore, the better view is that [P] falls below the class, though well within the scale.”

B · P within the class

  “‘[The respect in which P’s condition matches quadriplegia, total blindness with lost limbs, or brain damage with an exceptionally limited independent life]’ puts [P] within the class, which Dell v Dalton makes a class and not a single worst case, its members different and some worse than others. Quintano is analogous, brain damage with restricted walking and an exceptionally limited ability to maintain an independent life qualifying; Southgate puts quadriplegia in the class and Mason v Demasi total blindness with lost limbs. [P] is arguably within it, though some members are worse.”

This block fixes the top of the scale only, never the figure · For the percentage → B24B

B24B ‘Most extreme case’: the decided ladder · when always with B24A

“The percentage is fixed by comparison, and ‘[P’s loss, stated as those cases state theirs]’ is set against four decided cases. Khan gives 20% for a testicle removed after a delayed diagnosis, work capacity unchanged and no ongoing physical disability. Jopling gives 28% for a fractured patella from a defective balcony, with chronic leg pain, restricted movement, snowboarding lost, surfing impaired, knee scarring and a risk of early arthritis. Wighton gives 45% for negligent surgery producing intense neck pain, shoulder damage, an inability to walk more than twenty minutes and difficulty with nappy-changing, bathing, breast-feeding and driving. George v Survery gives 65% for a heart transplant after a negligent failure to diagnose, with fatigue, muscle wasting, cognitive impairment, depression and lifelong immunosuppressants. Earlier decisions may be cited (CLA s 17A(1)), thus ‘[the severity percentage]’ follows from that comparison.”

A · above the mid decided cases

  “‘[The two nearest decided cases, and the respect in which P sits between them]’ place [P] above the middle of the ladder: worse off than Jopling’s 28%, chronic leg pain, restricted movement, snowboarding lost and knee scarring, and approaching Wighton’s 45%, intense neck pain, shoulder damage and an inability to walk more than twenty minutes, while George v Survery’s 65%, a heart transplant with lifelong immunosuppressants, stays above. ‘[The severity percentage]’ accordingly follows, earlier decisions citable (CLA s 17A(1)).”

B · below the mid decided cases

  “‘[The function P retains that the higher decided case had lost]’ holds [P] below the middle of the ladder: Khan is analogous at 20%, its work capacity unchanged and no ongoing physical disability, while Jopling’s 28%, chronic leg pain, restricted movement and snowboarding lost, is not reached, function largely intact, and Wighton at 45% and George v Survery at 65% stand far above. ‘[The severity percentage]’ arguably follows, earlier decisions citable (CLA s 17A(1)).”

C · two decided cases pulling opposite ways

  “The ladder cuts both ways on ‘[P’s loss, stated as those cases state theirs]’: Jopling’s 28% is analogous because [P] shares its chronic pain, restricted movement and lost sport, yet Wighton’s 45% is analogous too, [P] sharing that plaintiff’s difficulty with nappy-changing, bathing and driving. Therefore, the better view is that ‘[the severity percentage]’ sits between the two, nearer the decided case whose functional loss [P] more closely matches (CLA s 17A(1)), the Table converting only what the comparison fixes.”

State the percentage and the decided cases it rests on, never the percentage alone · To convert it under the Table → B11

B25 The three types of recoverable loss (CSR v Eddy) · when the facts give a loss that answers no obvious head

“Losses divide into three types. Firstly, non-pecuniary losses ‘such as pain and suffering, disfigurement, loss of limbs or organs, loss of the senses: sight, taste, hearing, smell and touch; and loss of the capacity to engage in hobbies, sport, work, marriage and child-bearing’, recoverable ‘even if no actual financial loss is caused’. Secondly, ‘loss of earning capacity both before the trial and after it’, ‘awardable only to the extent that the loss has been or may be productive of financial loss’. Thirdly, ‘actual financial loss, for example, ambulance charges; charges for medical, hospital and professional nursing services; travel and accommodation expenses incurred in obtaining those services; the costs of rehabilitation needs, special clothing and special equipment; the costs of modifying houses; the costs of fund management; and the costs of professionally supplied home maintenance services’ (CSR v Eddy [28]–[31] per Gleeson CJ, Gummow and Heydon JJ). ‘[The expense or loss claimed]’ is put to one of the three.”

A · recoverable as actual financial loss

  “‘[The expense claimed]’ answers ‘[the Eddy item it answers, quoted]’ within the third type, actual financial loss (CSR v Eddy [28]–[31]), and ‘[the evidence the need arose from the injury and not otherwise]’ shows the need is one the injury created. Where fund management is claimed, ‘[the evidence P cannot manage her own affairs]’ supports it, the court notifying the NSW Trustee and Guardian where it ‘considers that the person may be a person in need of protection’ (CLA s 23(3)). It is recoverable at ‘[the cost claimed]’.”

B · not a recoverable loss

  “‘[The expense claimed]’ answers none of the Eddy items: not ‘travel and accommodation expenses incurred in obtaining those services’, nor ‘the costs of rehabilitation needs, special clothing and special equipment’, nor ‘the costs of modifying houses’, nor ‘the costs of fund management’ (CSR v Eddy [28]–[31]), and ‘[why the expense fails to answer a named Eddy item]’ is the reason. Nor is it lost earning capacity, ‘awardable only to the extent that the loss has been or may be productive of financial loss’. It is not recoverable here, although the first type may yet take it as non-economic loss.”

C · Eddy both ways on the item

  “Eddy cuts both ways on ‘[the expense claimed]’. It sits within the third type because it answers ‘[the Eddy item it answers, quoted]’, the list running from ambulance charges to ‘the costs of professionally supplied home maintenance services’; yet ‘[why the expense arguably fails to answer a named Eddy item]’ points the other way, and the second type is ‘awardable only to the extent that the loss has been or may be productive of financial loss’. Therefore, the better view is that it is recoverable as actual financial loss, though the characterisation is arguable.”

Route the type, do not merge them: for the first type, non-pecuniary loss → B23A; for the second, loss of earning capacity before and after trial → 3; for the third, actual financial loss, value it here and then → 6

B26 The claim is [P]’s, not the carer’s · when the facts dwell on what the carer gave up, a job left, income forgone, a life rearranged, or a defendant says the carer should…

“What [the carer, named] gave up founds nothing of [the carer, named]’s own: it is the need for the services which gives [P] the right to an award, so the damages ‘are not determined by reference to the actual cost to the plaintiff of having them provided or by reference to the income forgone by the provider of the services’ (Van Gervan 333), and ‘the case will be rare indeed where the income forgone by the care provider is ever an appropriate guide to the fair value of the services required by the injured person’. [P] is ‘beneficially entitled to the judgment he obtains without question of the imposition of any trust in respect of some part of his damages in favour of one who has rendered … gratuitous services to him’ (Griffiths per Stephen J).”

A · the carer’s forgone income is not the measure

  “[The carer, named] gave up ‘[the job, income or life the carer gave up]’, and that is not the measure: damages answer the need, not the provider, so they ‘are not determined … by reference to the income forgone by the provider of the services’, ‘the case … rare indeed’ where that income is any guide (Van Gervan 333); market cost governs instead. The award is [P]’s, ‘beneficially entitled to the judgment’ without any trust in the carer’s favour (Griffiths per Stephen J), ‘no legal obligation’ existing to pay it over (Grincelis [51] per Callinan J).”

B · the carer has no claim of her own

  “[The carer, named] has no action of her own for ‘[the job, income or life the carer gave up]’: the head belongs to [P], the definition reaching services ‘provided by another person to a claimant’ for which ‘the claimant has not paid or is not liable to pay’ (CLA s 15(1)), and [P] is ‘beneficially entitled to the judgment’ without any trust in the carer’s favour (Griffiths per Stephen J). That the carer is unlikely to be able to distribute the loss is why the claim runs through [P], the wrongdoer ‘a much better loss distributor’ (Griffiths per Stephen J).”
